# The Circle That Doesn't Need a Name

*A Kusakabe story*

---

# Part One: The Man Who Didn't Want the Job

## Chapter 1. Six Ten

The alarm goes off at 6:10.

He set it years ago, on the theory that ten extra minutes would make him a different person in the morning. It hasn't. He's kept it anyway.

Kusakabe Atsuya lies on his back and looks at the ceiling.

It's the ceiling of a flat that is neither nice nor bad. And in the corner above the wardrobe, the water stain. Brown at the edges, paler in the middle, roughly the shape of a country he's never been to.

He's been meaning to report it for two years. The landlord's name is Sato. He has the email address saved somewhere.

*Grade 1 sorcerer,* he thinks. *One of the strongest in the country, apparently. Can't be bothered to email the landlord.*

The stain hasn't grown. He checks most mornings. It's the closest thing he has to a hobby.

He gets up because the body decides to. It's the only reliable thing about him.

---

Kettle. Mug. One heaped spoon of instant coffee from a jar whose label promises *rich roast aroma.* It smells like a cardboard box that got rained on. He makes it anyway. Better coffee would mean a machine, and a machine would mean cleaning a machine.

He drinks it standing at the window.

Below, the street is waking up. Salarymen in dark suits, walking fast, briefcases swinging. One of them is already on the phone, apologising to someone with his whole body, bowing to the pavement.

Kusakabe watches them over the rim of the mug.

Ordinary people with ordinary problems. A boss who shouts. A train that's late. A mortgage, a quarterly target, a colleague who steals the good stapler. He envies them in a vague, tired way, the way you envy people in a warm café when you're standing outside in the rain.

Nobody ever asked a salaryman to walk into a building full of curses before breakfast.

*Then again,* he thinks, *nobody ever asked me either. I just never left.*

The man on the phone has stopped bowing. He stands at the crossing with his free hand pressed to his forehead. The light turns green. He doesn't move. The crowd parts around him like water round a post, and then the light turns red again, and he's still there.

Kusakabe finishes the coffee.

*Same.*

---

He has no innate technique.

In the jujutsu world, being born without a technique is like being born without a surname. The clans don't look at you. The higher-ups don't look at you. Nobody hands you a scroll or a family name or a seat at the table. You turn up with nothing, and if you want to stay, you build something with your hands.

What he built is the New Shadow Style. Years of drawing the same small circle around himself until his body could do it in its sleep. Simple Domain, the old men call it. The reach of one arm and one sword. Anything that crosses the line, he feels. His hands answer before his head has finished asking the question.

It's not glamorous. It's a craft, like plastering a wall. You practise until you can't get it wrong, and then you keep practising.

He leaves the mug in the sink.

*Later.*

---

He's late leaving. He's always late leaving.

It's the part in between. Shirt. Socks. The sword in its bag. His phone, which he forgets on the counter and goes back for. The window, which he didn't open, and goes back to check anyway.

By the time he's on the street, the salarymen have thinned out and the delivery vans have taken over.

The walk to Jujutsu High takes twenty-five minutes. He goes the long way, along the back of the shrine and up the hill through the trees. The short way has a crowded crossing, and he doesn't like crowds. Too many people inside the circle without asking.

It's late October. The ginkgo trees along the shrine wall have gone yellow all at once, the way they do, like someone flipped a switch overnight. The fallen leaves are already squashed on the path. They smell faintly of something gone off.

An old man is sweeping the leaves into a pile. The wind puts the top of the pile back on the path. The old man starts again.

*There's a lesson in that,* Kusakabe thinks. *I'm not going to learn it.*

---

The school sits at the top of the hill behind a long wall and a lot of trees, pretending to be a religious college.

He doesn't go in through the main gate. The main gate is for students and visitors and people who want to be seen arriving. He goes round the side, past the storehouse and the bins, to the staff yard where the cars are kept.

Somewhere off to the left, from the direction of the training grounds, there's a thud and a yell and a burst of laughter. First-years, probably. He hears a girl's voice say something sharp, and a much deeper voice say, "Ow, okay, fair." He doesn't look. Not his class. Not his problem.

Ijichi is waiting by the car.

He's standing very straight beside the black sedan with a folder pressed to his chest. He checks his watch as Kusakabe comes round the corner, then checks it again, as if the watch might have changed its mind.

"Kusakabe-san. Good morning."

"Morning."

"We were scheduled to leave at eight."

"Yeah."

Ijichi waits. Kusakabe doesn't add anything. It's eight twenty-six. They both know it's eight twenty-six. There's nothing to add that would make it eight o'clock.

"Right," Ijichi says. "That's fine. It's a forty-minute drive. We'll still be within the window."

He opens the back door. Kusakabe gets in.

Ijichi is one of the few people at the school Kusakabe doesn't mind. He's anxious, overworked, and polite to the point of pain, and he never asks a question he doesn't need the answer to. He drives carefully. He apologises to other drivers when they cut him up.

*If anyone in this place deserves a pay rise,* Kusakabe thinks, *it's the man who drives the people who deserve pay rises.*

He doesn't say it. Saying it would start a conversation about pay, and he can't afford that either.

---

The briefing happens at a red light.

Ijichi passes the folder back over his shoulder without turning round. "It's a Grade 2. Probably. The assessment team weren't certain."

Kusakabe opens the folder. A photograph of a five-storey apartment block, grey concrete, balconies with rusted rails. The kind of building that went up quickly in the seventies and has been coming down slowly ever since. A few windows have newspaper taped over them from the inside.

"Owner's been trying to clear it for demolition for three years," Ijichi says. "Most of the tenants have moved out. Four households left, all on the ground floor of the east block. They've been complaining about knocking."

"Knocking."

"At night. On the walls. On the ceilings. Sometimes on the doors. Always three times." Ijichi's hands adjust on the wheel. "One of the tenants is an elderly woman. She's been sleeping in her kitchen with the light on. The demolition crew went in last week to survey the upper floors and two of them walked off the job. One of them says something knocked on the inside of his hard hat."

Kusakabe looks at the photo. Then at the next one, a stairwell, taken with a flash. The walls are covered in small dents. Hundreds of them. Neat, evenly spaced, like a pattern on fabric.

*Three times,* he thinks. *Every time. Someone wants to be let in.*

He closes the folder.

"Anyone hurt? Badly."

"No. Not yet. Hence the probably."

"Okay."

The light turns green. Ijichi checks all three mirrors and pulls away as gently as if the car were full of eggs.

Kusakabe watches the city go past. Pachinko parlours. A shuttered bakery. A man walking six small dogs, all wearing the same small coat.

*Nobody's going to know about this one,* he thinks. *That's the job, mostly. Making sure nobody knows.*

He closes his eyes for the rest of the drive. He doesn't sleep. He just stops looking.

---

The building is worse in person.

It stands in a patch of weeds behind a chain-link fence with a demolition notice cable-tied to it. The notice has been there long enough to fade. The east block is the one on the right. On the ground floor, four windows have curtains. One has a potted plant on the sill, a small, stubborn geranium, the only thing in the whole lot that looks alive.

The rest of the building looks like it's holding its breath.

Ijichi stands by the gate with his hands together. "I'll lower the curtain once you're inside. The tenants have been told it's a gas inspection. They've been asked to stay in their flats."

"Fine."

"Do you need anything? Before you go in."

Kusakabe thinks about it. A different job. A different life. Ten more minutes in bed.

"No."

He unzips the bag and takes out the sword.

Behind him Ijichi says the words, low and fast, and the curtain comes down over the building. The sky goes dark the way a room goes dark when someone stands in the doorway. The traffic noise drops away. The geranium on the windowsill turns the colour of ash.

He walks to the stairwell door. It's propped open with a brick.

---

Inside, it smells of damp and old cooking oil and something sweeter underneath, like fruit left too long in a bag. The mailboxes in the entrance hall are all hanging open.

He stops at the bottom of the stairs and lets the circle out.

It isn't a thing he does, exactly. It's more like a thing he stops not doing. He lets his attention settle into his skin and then a little past it, the reach of one arm and one sword, all the way round. Floor. Air. The cold metal of the handrail. The draught on the back of his neck from the open door.

Nothing inside it but him.

He goes up.

First floor. Second. The dents start on the second-floor landing. Small round marks in the plaster, three at a time, three at a time, like someone patient with a hammer. They get closer together as he climbs. By the fourth floor they're almost touching.

On the fourth-floor landing, he stops.

Somewhere above him, something knocks.

Three times. Polite. Unhurried.

Then, from the wall to his left, three more. Closer.

*Okay,* he thinks. *You know I'm here. Good. Saves time.*

His palms are cold. They always are. He's done this more times than he can count and his palms are still cold every single time, as if the body is filing a formal complaint that nobody upstairs is reading.

The knocking moves. It goes along the wall, through the wall, round behind him. He doesn't turn. Turning is what it wants. He keeps his eyes on the stairs and his weight low and his hand on the sword, and he waits.

It's a long wait. Twenty seconds. Thirty.

He thinks, idly, about the man at the crossing this morning, standing still with his hand on his forehead while the light changed.

Then the wall behind him opens.

He doesn't see it. He feels it. Something crosses the line of the circle at the back of his left shoulder, fast and thin and wrong. His body has already turned and drawn and cut before the thought *behind* has finished forming. There's a sound like a wet branch breaking. Something long and pale with far too many knuckles hits the floor of the landing and curls up like a dead spider.

Then the rest of it comes out of the wall.

It's bigger than the photographs suggested. A hunched, grey thing, a body made mostly of arm, the arm made mostly of fist. It's got no face, only a sort of flat, listening patch where a face would go. It raises what's left of the fist and knocks on the air between them. Three times.

*No,* he thinks. *Not coming in.*

It lunges. It's quick, quicker than a Grade 2 should be, and for half a second he feels the old cold drop in his stomach. Then it's inside the circle, and the half-second doesn't matter, because the circle doesn't wait for him to be brave. The blade goes where the line says it should go. Once, across. Once, down.

The knocking stops.

The thing comes apart slowly, the way smoke does when the room is still. The dents on the walls stay where they are. Those were real. Plaster doesn't heal.

He stands on the landing with the sword held low. His heart is going faster than it needs to. His hands are fine. His hands are always fine. It's the rest of him that takes a minute to catch up.

He breathes in. Damp and oil, and nothing sweet any more.

He checks the fifth floor anyway. Empty rooms. A child's sticker on a door frame, a cartoon bear, faded almost white. A calendar on a kitchen wall, still on March of a year that ended a long time ago.

*Someone lived here,* he thinks. *Someone knocked on these doors and got let in.*

He doesn't stay with that thought. He wipes the blade, sheathes it, and goes back down the stairs, three dents at a time.

---

Ijichi is exactly where he left him, by the gate, hands together. He lifts the curtain as Kusakabe comes out. The sky comes back. The geranium is red again.

Ijichi looks at his watch. Then at Kusakabe.

"That was eleven minutes."

"Yeah."

"Is it..."

"Done."

"Oh." Ijichi seems almost disappointed, the way people are when a film they braced for turns out to be short. "Good. That's good. I'll call it in."

He steps away to make the call. Kusakabe zips the sword into its bag and stands in the weeds with his hands in his pockets.

A curtain moves on the ground floor of the east block. The window with the geranium. An old woman in a cardigan looks out at him. She has the face of someone who hasn't slept properly in a month. She looks at him the way you look at a man from the gas company, which, as far as she knows, is what he is.

She slides the window open a few centimetres.

"Are you the inspection?"

"Yes."

"Did you find it? The noise."

He thinks about what to say. The truth would take an hour and she wouldn't believe any of it.

"It was the pipes," he says. "It'll stop now."

She looks at him for a moment longer. She doesn't thank him. She doesn't seem to think it's the kind of thing you thank anyone for. She just nods, once, and slides the window shut, and the curtain falls back.

He prefers it, honestly. Thanks would mean standing there while someone was grateful at him.

*She's going to sleep in her bed tonight,* he thinks. *That's the whole report, really.*

It won't be the whole report. The report has eleven sections and a box for *Additional Observations.*

On the drive back, Ijichi says, "Well done, Kusakabe-san," into the rear-view mirror, quietly, the way you'd say it to someone who might not want to hear it.

Kusakabe grunts.

It's as close to *thanks* as he gets before lunch.

---

Back at the school, the corridor outside the administrative office smells of floor polish and the radiator that clanks. He's on his way to nowhere in particular. Then a voice says his name the way people say the name of a parcel they've been waiting for.

"Kusakabe-san. A moment."

Okabe.

Okabe works in administration. Mid-level. Not a sorcerer, or not much of one; Kusakabe has never asked and never will. He's in his forties, with a neat side parting and a cardigan the colour of weak tea. He likes forms. Not in an ironic way. He likes it when the boxes are filled in on time, and he takes it personally when they aren't.

He has a clipboard with a printout on it. He holds it up.

"The Saitama assignment. Tuesday last week. The warehouse."

"Yeah."

"The report was due on Friday at five."

Kusakabe looks at the printout. It's a list of outstanding reports. His name is on it, alone, highlighted in yellow. Someone has gone to the trouble of highlighting it in yellow.

"Is there a reason it's late?" Okabe asks.

The true answer arrives in his head, whole and simple. The warehouse was fine. The curse was fine. What wasn't fine was the security guard. He'd been living in the back office for a month because he had nowhere else to go. When Kusakabe walked in, he was sitting on the floor in the middle of it all, knees pulled up, not screaming, just very quiet. Writing the report means writing that down in the box marked *Civilian Involvement.* Writing it down means remembering it properly. And on Friday at five he'd been sitting in his flat with the laptop open and the box empty, and then it was dark, and then it was Saturday.

That's the reason. It's not a reason Okabe has a box for.

"No," Kusakabe says.

Okabe waits. Kusakabe doesn't fill the gap. The radiator clanks.

"No?" Okabe says.

"No."

Okabe's mouth tightens, very slightly, at one corner. "Kusakabe-san, the reports aren't a formality. They go into the assessment records. If one report is missing, the whole picture is..." He searches for the word. "Incomplete."

"Okay."

"I'm not trying to be difficult."

*You're not trying,* Kusakabe thinks. *It comes naturally. I respect that.*

"I know," he says.

"So when can I expect it?"

"Tonight."

Okabe writes something on the clipboard. Kusakabe can see the pen moving, small and precise. It's not the word *tonight.* It's longer.

"I'll have to make a note of the delay," Okabe says. "On your record. It's procedure."

"Fine."

Okabe looks at him, waiting. An apology, maybe. A sign that Kusakabe understands the seriousness of the yellow highlighter. Some people need the other person to be sorry before they can stop being annoyed. Okabe is one of those people, and Kusakabe can see it, the annoyance sitting there in the tight corner of his mouth with nowhere to go.

He lets it sit there.

It isn't his job to make Okabe feel better about Okabe's feelings. Explaining never works with people like this. It only gives them more to hold onto.

"Well," Okabe says, after a moment. "Tonight, then."

"Tonight."

Okabe turns and walks back into the office. The door closes behind him a little harder than it needs to. Not slammed. Just firm. A door with opinions.

Kusakabe stands in the corridor.

*There it is,* he thinks. *A line on a file somewhere. Kusakabe Atsuya. Late. No reason given.*

It's small. It's nothing. Nobody will read it for years, probably. But he knows how files work in this place. Nobody reads them, until somebody does, and then everything that's ever been written down gets read at once.

He goes to the vending machine and buys a can of coffee. It's warm and too sweet and tastes faintly of the can.

It's better than the stuff at home. He doesn't like what that says about the stuff at home.

---

He spends the afternoon doing what most of the job actually is: nothing that looks like anything. He sharpens the sword in the equipment room. He sits in the staff room for forty minutes with a newspaper open at the same page. Two other teachers argue about a curse in Chiba and whether it was really a Grade 1 or whether someone in assessment panicked.

Nobody asks his opinion. He doesn't offer one.

At one point Yaga walks past the open door, big and square in his dark glasses, talking low on his phone. He doesn't look in. Kusakabe doesn't look up. He hears the footsteps go down the corridor, steady and heavy, until they're gone.

At half past five, he leaves.

He goes out the side way, past the bins and the storehouse, past the car yard where Ijichi is bent over the open boot of the sedan, sorting folders into other folders. Ijichi straightens up and raises a hand. Kusakabe raises one back.

The sun is already down behind the trees. Down below, the city is turning its lights on, a window at a time.

---

On the way home, he stops at the convenience store by the crossing.

It's the bright one with the music that never changes. He picks up a karaage bento without looking at the others, because looking at the others would mean choosing, and he's done enough choosing for one day. Then a bottle of tea. Then, at the till, without really meaning to, a small packet of chocolate biscuits.

The boy at the till has dyed hair and a name badge that says *Trainee.* He scans the bento, the tea, the biscuits.

"Would you like it warmed?"

"Yes."

"Chopsticks?"

"One pair."

"Bag?"

"No."

The boy puts the bento in the microwave and watches it turn, with the expression of someone watching the sea. Kusakabe watches it too. For forty seconds they are united in a total lack of interest in each other.

The microwave pings.

"Thank you very much," the boy says, to the till.

"Thanks," Kusakabe says, to the door.

*Best conversation I've had all day,* he thinks. *No notes.*

---

The flat is exactly as he left it. The mug in the sink. The bed unmade. The water stain in the corner, watching.

He takes off his shoes. He puts the sword bag in its place against the wall, where the light from the window falls across it in the mornings. He sits on the sofa with the bento on his knees and turns on the television, because that's what you do when you get home.

A variety show. Six celebrities in a studio built to look like a school classroom. They're answering quiz questions about vegetables, and the audience laughs in big rehearsed waves.

He eats the bento. The karaage is fine. The rice is a little dry at the edges where the microwave missed.

He isn't watching the show. He's facing it. There's a difference, and he's very good at the difference. The sound is just there, filling the room, so the room isn't empty. The colours move across his face. Someone on the screen wins a giant radish, and there's applause.

He thinks about nothing.

Not quite. He thinks about the grey thing in the wall, knocking three times, wanting to be let in. He thinks about the dents on the stairs. The old woman in the cardigan sliding the window shut.

Then he stops thinking about them. Or he puts them somewhere he doesn't have to look at them, which is the same thing, more or less, if you don't look too closely.

His phone buzzes on the arm of the sofa.

A message from Mrs. Fujita, his sister's carer. *Sunday as usual? She had a good day today. Ate all her lunch.*

He reads it twice. The second time, he reads only the last line.

*Yes,* he types. *Thanks.*

He puts the phone face down.

On the screen, the celebrities have moved on to fruit.

---

At ten to eleven, he opens the laptop.

The Saitama report is still there in a window he never closed. Eleven sections. Most of them are done. The box marked *Civilian Involvement* is empty, the cursor blinking in it with great patience, the way it's been blinking since Friday.

He looks at it for a while.

Then he types: *One civilian present (security guard, male, 50s). Unharmed. Referred to local services.*

He reads it back. It's all true. It doesn't say anything about the man's knees, or how quiet he'd been, or how he kept saying *sorry* to Kusakabe, for some reason, all the way out to the car.

The box doesn't need that. The box has what it needs.

He fills in today's report too, while he's there. *Grade 2. Exorcised. No civilian injuries.* Under *Additional Observations,* he thinks for a moment and writes: *Tenant has a geranium.* Then he deletes it.

He sends both reports at 11:04.

Somewhere in the administration building, he imagines, there's a clipboard with a note on it that says *late* in small, precise handwriting. Sending the report won't remove the note. It'll just sit next to it.

*Fine,* he thinks. *Let it sit.*

---

He turns off the television. The room goes quiet all at once, the way a room does when you remove the thing that was keeping the quiet out.

He stands up. Something in his lower back clicks.

The mug is still in the sink from this morning, a brown ring dried in the bottom.

He runs the tap until it's warm. He rinses the mug, rubbing the ring out with his thumb. The water runs down his wrist and he lets it. Outside, a car goes past, slowly, its headlights moving across the ceiling and over the stain in the corner, and then away.

He turns off the tap. He shakes the mug once.

He puts it upside down on the rack.

*Another day of pretending I'm not tired.*

---

## Chapter 2. Sunday

On Sundays, he visits his sister.

The train is almost empty at nine. A grandmother with a shopping trolley. Two boys in baseball uniforms, asleep against each other. A man in a suit who has clearly not been home since Friday. Kusakabe sits by the door with a carrier bag between his feet and watches the stations go past.

Twenty minutes. Six stops. He doesn't need to count them anymore. His body gets up at the right one the way it gets up in the mornings.

In the bag: rice crackers, the soft kind. A packet of the tea she likes. Hand cream, unscented, because the scented one made her sneeze for a week. Two persimmons from a stall near the school that he walked past twice before going back for.

*Grade 1 sorcerer,* he thinks. *Fruit shopping.*

He doesn't mind. That's the strange part. Six days a week he minds almost everything. Sunday morning, on this train, with this bag, he doesn't mind anything at all.

It's the one appointment he never misses. Never complains about. Never mentions at school. Ijichi doesn't know. Okabe doesn't know. Gojo doesn't know, which is a small miracle, because Gojo knows everything that's none of his business.

It isn't a secret, exactly. It's just his.

---

Her building is four storeys of tired cream tile on a street with a laundrette and a dentist. Ground floor, because of the chair. He chose it for the ramp and the bus stop. He didn't choose it for the view. The view is a car park and, past the car park, a strip of other people's balconies with futons hung out to air.

She likes it anyway. She likes watching the futons.

He rings the bell, then lets himself in with his own key, which is the arrangement. Ring so she's not startled. Key so she doesn't have to wait.

The smell reaches him before anything else. Lavender. The cheap kind, from a little bottle with a wooden stick in it that stands on the shoe cabinet by the door. He buys a new one every two months. It's the first thing in the flat and the last thing when he leaves. Some weeks it's the only thing he remembers clearly about the visit.

"Kusakabe-san." Mrs. Fujita comes out of the kitchen drying her hands on a towel tucked into her apron. "You're early."

"Train."

She doesn't ask what that means. She's in her fifties, short, square, with reading glasses pushed up into grey hair and the forearms of someone who lifts people for a living. She has been coming four mornings a week for almost three years, plus Sunday mornings until he arrives. She talks fast, works faster, and has never once asked him what he does for a job. He's grateful for that in a way he would never be able to say out loud.

"She's in the window," Mrs. Fujita says. "She's had her bath. She ate half. Better than Thursday."

He nods, takes his shoes off, and goes through.

---

His sister sits by the window in her chair.

Not the wheelchair. The other one, the high-backed armchair with the wooden arms. He carried it up from a recycling shop and had it re-covered in a blue she picked out three years ago and has since forgotten picking. The wheelchair waits folded by the wall. Mrs. Fujita moves her between the two with a sort of practised hug that Kusakabe has learned to do too, badly.

The light from the window is pale and flat. October getting tired. It falls across the blanket on her knees and her hands on top of it, and the side of her face.

She's thinner. She's always a little thinner. He tells himself he imagines it.

"Hey," he says.

She turns her head. It takes a moment. Her eyes go over him the way they go over the car park, the futons, the sky, taking it in without hurry, deciding where it goes.

He waits. He's good at waiting. He's had practice.

"Oh," she says. "You're back."

Then she says their father's name.

Not a question. A greeting. Warm, a little reproachful, the way you'd speak to someone who has been away too long on business and brought nothing home.

He answers to it.

"Yeah," he says. "I'm back."

He sits on the stool beside the chair. She looks at him for a while longer, satisfied, and then back out of the window.

*Some days she knows me straight away,* he thinks. *Some days it takes a minute. Some days I'm a dead man.*

He doesn't correct her. He tried that, once, early on. She got frightened, and then upset, and then she cried for an hour because she couldn't work out why she was upset. He decided afterwards that it costs him nothing to be someone else for an afternoon. Lots of people would like that deal.

"The futons are out," she says.

"Good drying weather."

"Mm. That one's crooked."

He looks. One of the futons on the third balcony across is hanging at a slant, half a metre lower on one side.

"Yeah," he says. "It is."

She seems pleased to have been agreed with. He would agree with her about anything. That's another part of the arrangement.

---

In the kitchen, Mrs. Fujita runs through it.

She does this every Sunday. A notebook on the counter, the kind with a cartoon cat on the cover that she bought at a hundred-yen shop. She writes everything in it, in neat pencil, in a hand that slopes uphill. Meals. Medicine. Bowels. Mood. Anything odd. He reads it every week, standing at the counter, and she watches him read it to make sure he does.

"Tuesday she didn't want the lunch. Wednesday fine. Thursday she was confused in the evening, she thought it was morning, she wanted to go to the station. I talked her out of it." Mrs. Fujita taps the page. "Friday the bruise."

"Bruise."

"Her left wrist. From the transfer. It's nothing, it's yellow already, but I wrote it down." She looks at him over the glasses. "I write everything down."

"I know."

"In case."

"I know."

She nods, as if that settles something between them. It probably does. He's never asked in case of what. He suspects the answer is *in case you ever decide I'm the one who did it*, and he suspects some family, somewhere, once did, and that's why the notebook exists.

The rest is routine. The pill organiser for the week, seven little plastic doors with the days printed on them, filled and checked. The physio exercises she did on Wednesday, the ones she refused on Friday. A note that the toilet handle sticks. A note that the lady upstairs has a new dog and it barks at four, and his sister doesn't like it. Mrs. Fujita says all of it in the same quick flat voice, like reading out a train timetable, and he listens to all of it.

This is the part he's good at. Tasks. Lists. Small things that can be fixed by a man with a screwdriver and a bank account.

There's a pile of envelopes by the kettle. He takes them without being told. Electricity. Gas. Water. The building's management fee. The pharmacy. The agency's invoice, which comes from the agency, not from Mrs. Fujita, because she doesn't like handling money and neither does he.

He folds them all in half and puts them in his inside pocket.

He pays them on the Monday. He always pays them on the Monday. He does it at the convenience store near the school, at seven in the morning, before anyone he knows is awake, and then he throws the receipts away. Not hiding it. There's nobody to hide it from. He just doesn't see the point of anyone else knowing a number that isn't theirs.

*Grade 1 pay,* he thinks. *Grade 1 bills.*

It works out. More or less. Most months.

---

Mrs. Fujita is putting her coat on when she says it.

She does it the way people do it when they've practised. Coat first. Bag on the shoulder. Hand on the door. Then, as if she's only just remembered:

"Kusakabe-san. One thing."

He waits.

"The agency's changing the rates. From next month." She isn't looking at him. She's looking at the lavender bottle on the shoe cabinet. "It's not me. Well. It's partly me. I asked. They wouldn't do it unless they did it for everyone, so they're doing it for everyone. It's another fifteen percent. For the weekday hours and the Sunday. I'm sorry. I know it's a lot. I wanted to tell you myself before the letter."

There's more coming. He can see it lined up behind her teeth. The cost of the train. Her mother. The other client in Nerima who cancelled. Her knees. All the reasons, in order, ready to be read out like the notebook.

He doesn't want to hear any of it. Not because he doesn't care. Because if she explains, he'll have to respond to the explanation, and then they'll both be standing in a narrow hallway explaining things to each other, and he'd rather be under a curse.

"Fine," he says.

She stops.

"Fine?"

"Yeah."

"You don't want to... the agency can send you the breakdown, if you want to look at..."

"It's fine."

She looks at him properly then. Her face does something complicated. Relief, mostly. Something under the relief that might be suspicion, or might be pity. He can't tell. He doesn't try.

"All right," she says. "Thank you. Really. Thank you."

"Mm."

She opens the door. Then she leans back in, and calls past him into the flat, much louder and slower, in the voice she uses for his sister. "I'm off now! See you Tuesday!"

From the window, no answer. Then, after a while, a small hand lifted off the blanket.

The door closes.

He stands in the hallway for a moment, in the lavender, doing the sum he should have done before he said the word.

He gets as far as *fifteen percent of* and stops.

*Next month,* he thinks. *Next month's problem.*

He goes to make the tea.

---

The tea she likes is cheap green tea in a bag, and she likes it weak. Not a little weak. Badly weak. The bag goes in, the water goes in, and the bag comes out again almost immediately, so the water is the colour of a pale green wall in a hospital.

He learned this the hard way. The first time he made it properly, she took one sip and put the cup down and looked at him with an expression of deep, patient disappointment, like he'd done it on purpose.

Now he dips the bag. Counts to three. Takes it out.

He makes his own in the same pot, afterwards, with the same bag. It tastes of almost nothing. He drinks it anyway.

*Bad coffee six days a week,* he thinks. *Bad tea on the seventh. Very balanced life.*

He carries both cups through and puts hers on the little table by the chair, turned so the handle faces her right hand. She can lift with the right. Not far. Not always. She has a lid she can use, a plastic one with a spout, like a child's cup. Mrs. Fujita uses it. He doesn't, on Sundays, unless she asks. He holds the cup for her instead, and she drinks, and some of it goes down her chin, and he wipes her chin with the cloth, and neither of them says anything about it.

"Too strong," she says.

It isn't. He's made it exactly the same way for two years.

"Sorry."

"It's all right. You never could make tea."

He doesn't know if she means him, or their father. He doesn't ask. Both are probably true.

---

The afternoon goes the way Sunday afternoons go.

He does the blinds in the bedroom, which have a slat that keeps slipping. He fixes the toilet handle, which takes two minutes and a coin. He trims her fingernails, carefully, one hand on the blanket, her fingers held still in his, the little clipper going *tick, tick* in the quiet. Her nails are soft. They tear if you're not careful. He's careful.

She talks while he does it. Not to him exactly. To the room.

She talks about a summer at the sea. A guest house with a red roof and a woman who gave them watermelon. A beach where the sand burned your feet so you had to run. She tells it the way she always tells it, the same three pieces in the same order. When she reaches the end she starts again from the beginning, and he listens to it again from the beginning.

He doesn't go with her into it. He keeps himself here. On the stool. In the flat. With the clippers in his hand and her fingers in his palm. He can listen to the sea without going to the sea.

*Some parts she can still reach,* he thinks. *I'm not taking any of them away from her by being bored.*

At two he does the medicine. Seven little doors. Sunday's door. Three white, one yellow, one that's half a pill because the doctor cut the dose in the summer. He gives them to her one at a time with water, and she takes them one at a time, and frowns at the yellow one the way she always frowns at the yellow one.

"That one tastes," she says.

"I know."

"Of metal."

"I know. Last one."

She swallows it. She looks pleased with herself. He tells her she did well, which feels ridiculous to say to a grown woman, and is also the right thing to say.

By three she's tired. Her head goes down a little at a time, like a flower closing, and then she's asleep in the chair with her mouth slightly open and her hand still on the blanket. He tucks the blanket higher. He takes her cup into the kitchen. He sits back down on the stool.

And because she's asleep, and the flat is quiet, and there's nothing left on the list, the other thing comes in.

---

It always comes on Sundays. He's stopped being surprised by it.

Years ago, her son died.

Takeru. Small, sudden. An accident. The kind of thing you can turn over a thousand times and it never makes any more sense than it did the first time. One afternoon there was a boy and then there wasn't. That's all it was. That's all it ever is, for the people it happens to.

She broke.

Not loudly. People think breaking is loud. Hers was quiet and permanent, like a building settling on a bad foundation, one crack and then another and then a door that won't shut anymore. Memory gone in patches. Then a body that stopped doing what she asked. Doctors said things with long words in them. Kusakabe nodded at the long words and went home and looked them up and understood them perfectly and it made no difference at all.

Somebody had to take it on. There wasn't anybody else. So he did.

Nobody asked him. Nobody would have blamed him if he'd found her a place somewhere with a long corridor and visited at New Year. People did that. Reasonable people. He'd have understood it if someone else had done it.

He just didn't.

He found the flat. He signed the lease. He arranged the carers, interviewed four of them in a café with his hands around a coffee he didn't drink. He picked the one who asked the fewest questions and wrote the most things down. He came on Sundays. He sat with her. He made tea wrong, and then right.

*It's not kindness,* he tells himself, now, on the stool, while she sleeps. *It's just what you do. She's my sister.*

He doesn't push it further than that. Pushing it further would mean feeling it, all of it, at once, and he isn't built to hold that much. He knows his own limits the way he knows the reach of his own arm. So he does tasks instead. Blinds. Nails. Medicine. Toilet handle. The bills folded in his pocket. The tasks get the same thing done as the feelings would. They just don't wreck him on the way.

That's the first thing.

The second thing nobody knows.

---

At the worst of it, she stopped eating.

She stopped talking, except sometimes to say her son's name, and then she'd look at Kusakabe like he'd know where the boy had gone, like he was hiding it from her. He sat with her through that. He doesn't let himself think about how long. He went to work during the week and came back and sat with her and went to work, and somewhere in there he started to understand that one day she would just stop. Not die, exactly. Stop.

Then Masamichi Yaga came to see him.

The principal. The big man with the sunglasses and the beard and the little felt dolls. The man who made Panda. A craftsman of something the higher-ups would very much like to pretend doesn't exist, the kind of knowledge that gets you executed if the wrong person decides it should.

Yaga sat on a chair too small for him in Kusakabe's kitchen and listened. He didn't say much. He looked at the photographs. He asked to see her. He came here, to this flat, and stood in the doorway for a long time looking at a woman in a chair by a window.

Then he offered.

Takeru's soul, given form again. An independent cursed corpse. Not the boy back. Yaga was very clear about that. Kusakabe remembers him being clear about it, almost stern. Not the boy back, exactly. But the boy, in a way. Something that would know her. Something she could hold.

Kusakabe said yes.

He didn't think about it. He didn't weigh it. There wasn't anything to weigh. Of course he said yes.

And there was a day, after, when a small boy climbed up into his mother's lap in this chair, by this window. He settled there with his head under her chin, as if he'd only been out. And she put her arms around him. The arm that worked and the arm that didn't. She held him the way she couldn't hold a cup.

Kusakabe stood in the kitchen doorway. He had to go and stand in the bathroom for a while afterwards. He ran the tap so nobody would hear anything.

That's all he lets himself remember of it. That much, and no more. It's enough.

---

It's a debt.

He knows that. Yaga has never once mentioned it, never asked for anything, never so much as looked at him in a corridor in a way that said *you owe me*. That almost makes it worse. A debt somebody calls in, you can pay. A debt nobody mentions just sits there.

It's too big to pay back. He worked that out a long time ago. There isn't a currency for it. So he doesn't try. He just carries it.

He thinks of it as a coin.

Small. Heavy for its size. Sitting in his pocket, always, and every so often his hand goes to it without being told, just to check it's there. In a meeting. On a train. Standing in a corridor while Yaga walks past with a box of dolls under one arm, nodding to him the way he nods to everyone.

Kusakabe nods back. Checks the coin.

Still there.

It's also a secret. Nobody at school knows. Nobody can. What Yaga did for her is exactly the kind of thing that could get them both killed. It's exactly the kind of thing that the people who'd do the killing are always looking for. So Kusakabe keeps it inside the circle with everything else. The flat. The tea. The bills. Her.

His circle isn't large. Arm and sword. That's how he likes it. Anything inside it, he can feel. Anything inside it, his hands answer for before his head has to decide.

She stirs in the chair. Not awake. Just shifting, her fingers moving on the blanket like she's looking for something.

He puts his hand where she can find it. She finds it.

He sits like that until the light goes orange.

---

She wakes at half past four, all at once, the way she does.

She looks at him. Her eyes are clear. The difference is so sharp it's like a window being cleaned.

"Atsuya," she says.

Just his name. His actual name.

"Hey."

"You came."

"It's Sunday."

"Is it." She thinks about this. "You look tired."

"I'm fine."

"You always say that." She looks at him a moment longer, the way she used to look at him across a table when they were both adults with jobs and he'd said something stupid. Then she looks back out of the window. "Someone should take that futon in. It'll get damp."

He looks. The crooked futon is still out. The sun is off the balconies now, and the air coming in round the window frame has a cold edge to it.

"Yeah," he says. "Someone should."

They sit a little while longer. He doesn't try to keep her. He's learned that too. If you try to hold onto the clear part, it goes faster. You just let it be there, and then you let it go.

By five she's somewhere else again. Not unhappy. Just elsewhere. She tells him the woman with the watermelon had a dog. That's new. He files it.

He makes her dinner, the soft rice and the fish Mrs. Fujita left in the fridge with a note on the lid in uphill pencil. He sits with her while she eats half. He writes in the cat notebook, in his own handwriting, which is worse than Mrs. Fujita's: *Sun. Ate half. Medicine 2pm, fine. Toilet handle fixed. Knew me at 4:30.*

He looks at the last line for a while. Then he leaves it.

At seven the night carer from the agency comes, a young man who says almost nothing and that suits them both. Kusakabe hands over. He puts his coat on. He says goodbye to his sister, who says goodbye to him without looking away from the dark window. There is nothing in it now to see but the two of them reflected and the lamp.

"Bye," she says. Then, after a moment, their father's name again, softly, like an afterthought.

"Bye," he says.

The lavender, on the way out. First thing in, last thing out.

---

The train back is fuller. Sunday evening. People coming home from things. A family with a sleeping toddler and too many bags. Students with their earphones in. A couple not talking to each other in the specific way of a couple who've just had an argument and are now going to be polite about it for the rest of the evening.

Nobody looks at him. That's the nice thing about trains.

He gets a seat. He sits with his hands in his coat pockets. The bills are in the inside one, folded in half, waiting for Monday. In the other pocket, nothing. In the other pocket, the coin.

He checks it.

Still there.

*Fifteen percent,* he thinks, from nowhere. *Fifteen percent of what, though.*

He could work it out. He could take his phone out right now and open the calculator and do it. He knows roughly what the agency costs, and roughly what he earns, and roughly what's left at the end of most months, which is not much. He could know, in about thirty seconds, exactly how bad it is.

He doesn't take his phone out.

He'll find out next month. When the letter comes. When the money goes out and the number at the bottom is smaller than it should be. He'll stand at the convenience store at seven in the morning and look at it and think *ah*. That's when he'll know. Not now. Now is Sunday.

*I owe him everything,* he thinks, watching the stations go past in the other direction. *And I don't even like him that much.*

It's true. Yaga is stubborn and proud and has never once in his life made a joke Kusakabe found funny. He runs the school like a man holding a door shut in a high wind. He makes stuffed animals in meetings. He looks at Kusakabe sometimes like he's waiting for him to be more than he is.

None of that matters. That's how debts work. You don't have to like the person. You just have to remember.

The train goes up onto the elevated section. Outside, the city opens out: lit windows stacked on lit windows, red lights blinking on the tops of buildings, a highway like a river of headlights going somewhere he isn't.

The carriage lights are bright, and the night outside is dark, and so the window turns into a mirror.

He's in it. Sitting there, collar up, hands in pockets. His own face laid over the city like a sheet of glass. The lights go straight through him. Office towers through his forehead. A neon sign through his cheek.

He looks tired. Not Sunday tired. The other kind. The kind that's been there long enough to become a feature of the face, like a nose.

Usually he'd look away. Look at the floor, or the advertisements, or the back of his own hands. He doesn't like looking at himself for long. Nothing wrong with the face. It's just that it always seems to be about to ask him something.

Tonight he doesn't look away.

He sits there and looks at the man in the window, with the city running through him, all the way to his stop.

---

## Chapter 3. The Hurricane with Sunglasses

The corridor outside the old archive room is the quietest place in the school after four o'clock.

Kusakabe has tested it. Nobody comes down here except to look for old files, and nobody looks for old files. There's a bench under the window, and a radiator that works on one side. If you sit at the warm end with your back to the wall and a folder open on your knees, you look like a man reviewing something important.

He has been reviewing the same page for twenty minutes. It's a list of approved suppliers of paper towels.

*Critical work,* he thinks. *The country depends on it.*

He feels it before he hears it. Not through the circle. The way you feel the air change before a storm. A sort of brightness at the far end of the corridor, coming closer. Cheerful. Enormous.

Then footsteps. Long ones, unhurried, with a little bounce in them.

He doesn't look up.

*If I don't look up,* he thinks, *it might be someone else.*

"Ku-sa-ka-be."

It isn't someone else.

Satoru Gojo stops in front of the bench. Kusakabe would have to tip his head right back to see his face, so he looks at the knees instead. A paper bag from the bakery by the station. The edge of the blindfold at the top of his vision. Gojo wears it at school the way other people wear a lanyard, and it has never once stopped him seeing exactly what you'd rather he didn't.

There's powdered sugar on the bag. There's powdered sugar on Gojo.

"Found you," Gojo says.

"I wasn't lost."

"No, you were hiding. Different thing." He sits down on the bench, which is not long enough for two grown men when one of them is Gojo, and reads the folder upside down, blindfold and all. "Paper towels. Wow. Gripping stuff."

"It's for a report."

"It's from three years ago. The supplier went bust." He holds out the bag. "Want one?"

"No."

"They're really good."

"No, thanks."

"Your loss." He eats half of one in a single bite, and talks through it. "So. I need a favour."

*Here it comes.*

Kusakabe has seen this happen to other people. They walk into a conversation with Gojo holding a plan for their evening, and they walk out holding Gojo's.

"There's a hospital in the east of the city," Gojo says. "Children's ward. Something's been growing in it. Started with bad dreams, kids waking up crying at the same time every night. Now it's fevers nobody can explain, and a night nurse who says the corridor lights keep going out." He licks sugar off his thumb. "The Window called it Grade 2 last week. Grade 1 this week. It's eating well."

"Eating what."

"Fear. Obviously. Kids in hospital at night? They're terrified. It's a buffet." He says it lightly. He says everything lightly. "Anyway, it was mine. And I can't. I'm in Kyoto tonight. Meeting. Old men. You know." He pulls a face. "So I thought of you."

"Why."

"Because you're good. And because you're here."

Kusakabe looks at the paper towels.

There are three ways out of this. He has been getting out of things for most of his adult life.

"I'm busy," he says.

"You're not. You're reading about paper towels in a corridor nobody uses. That's the least busy a person can be without lying down."

Fair. He tries the second way.

"There are other Grade 1s on the board. Send one of them."

"There are two this week. One's in Hokkaido with a broken collarbone. The other one I don't like."

"That's not a reason."

"It's a great reason. Also, he'd go in loud. You need somebody quiet on a children's ward. Somebody boring." Gojo points at him with the other half of the pastry. "You are the most boring man I know. That's a compliment. I don't give many."

The third way is the honest one, which is why he keeps it for last.

"I don't want to."

Gojo tilts his head. For a moment he's genuinely still, which with Gojo is like a fountain stopping.

"Oh, I know," he says. "Nobody wants to. Ijichi's picking you up at ten, by the way. I already told him it was you. The file's on your desk."

"You went to my desk?"

"I went to everybody's desk. Yours was the tidiest. Very suspicious." He stands, brushing sugar onto the floor, and onto Kusakabe. "The night nurse is called Mori. She's apparently a nightmare. You'll love her."

And somewhere in there, after the third way and before any fourth, Kusakabe hears his own voice say:

"What time does it start?"

He doesn't know where it came from. He was there the whole time.

"Around three, the nurse says." Gojo is already walking away, the bag swinging. Without turning round, he lifts a hand. "You're the best, Kusakabe!"

Then he's round the corner, and the brightness goes with him, and the corridor is the quietest place in the school again.

Kusakabe sits.

There's powdered sugar on the bench where Gojo was. A little on his own sleeve. He doesn't brush it off. It feels like evidence.

*Three ways to say no,* he thinks. *Used all three. Nobody told me you needed a fourth.*

Some of the staff call Gojo a hurricane, when he isn't in earshot. Not because of the damage. Because there's no point arguing with one. You go indoors, and you wait, and afterwards you go outside and find out where your things ended up.

His things, it turns out, have ended up in a children's ward, at three in the morning.

---

Ijichi is in the car yard at ten exactly, with the engine running and the heater on.

"Kusakabe-san. Thank you for doing this." He hesitates, his hand on the gearstick. "Gojo-san said you volunteered."

"Did he."

Ijichi looks at him in the mirror. Then at the road, and keeps looking at it.

"I'm sorry," he says.

"Not your fault."

"No. It's quite often not my fault." Ijichi pulls out of the gate. "I find I'm still quite often sorry."

Kusakabe reads the file under the little light in the back. Eleven children, ages two to nine. Four with fevers of no known cause, all in the rooms at the far end, near the playroom. Corridor bulbs replaced twice.

At the bottom, in someone's careful handwriting, copied from the nurse's own notes: *They wake up and say something was under the bed. All of them say the same thing. Same time. They don't know each other.*

He reads that line twice and closes the file.

*Kids,* he thinks. *Of course it's kids.*

He'd rather it were an empty warehouse. Not because children are worse to save. Because if something goes wrong with an empty warehouse, it's just a warehouse.

---

The hospital is big and grey and mostly asleep. A man from the night office meets them there and holds out a lanyard at arm's length, as if it might bite. It says CONTRACTOR on it. Kusakabe puts it on.

The lift smells of floor polish. The fifth floor smells of everything else.

Disinfectant first, sharp, the kind that gets into the back of your throat. Then warm plastic. Then something sweet: fruit juice from the little cartons with the bent straws. And underneath all of it, the smell hospitals have at night, which isn't really a smell. It's the sound of people trying not to make any noise.

The lights are dimmed to half. The walls have animals painted on them, a little faded. A row of ducks. A giraffe whose neck goes round the corner, so you can only see its body from the lift and you have to walk to the end to find its face.

The doors to the rooms are open a hand's width. Through each gap, a bed.

The beds are small.

He knew they would be. Knowing doesn't help. The beds are small and the blankets are small and in one of them, a hand is lying outside the blanket, and it's about the size of the bottom of a teacup.

He keeps walking.

The nurses' station is a curved desk under the only full-strength light on the ward. Behind it sits a woman in her forties in blue scrubs with a grey cardigan over them, her hair in a clip that's slowly losing the argument. A mug of tea at her elbow with a skin on it. A chart in front of her. The name badge says MORI.

She doesn't look up.

"You're the one admin called."

"Yes."

"I don't know what you are. I don't want to know. I've got eleven children, me, and one agency nurse who's on her break." She turns a page. "Don't wake them. Don't go in the rooms unless you have to. If you have to, knock first. Quietly. They're not animals in a zoo."

"Fine."

"Don't touch the drip stands. Don't touch the trolleys."

"Okay."

Then she does look up. At his face, briefly, and then at the long bag on his shoulder, a second longer. Something goes across her face and she decides not to have it.

She looks back at the chart.

"Room four's night light is broken," she says. "If she wakes up and cries, that's why. It's not you. Facilities said Monday." She writes something. "It's been Monday for two weeks."

He waits to see if there's more. There isn't.

He doesn't take it personally. Eleven children, a cold cup of tea, and now a man with a long bag, the latest in a long line of things that turn up on her shift without asking. He'd be curt too. He usually is.

"The lights," he says. "Around three?"

The pen stops.

"Who told you that?"

"It's in the file."

"Nobody reads what I write."

"Someone did."

She looks at him again. Not warmer. Just a little more carefully, the way you'd look at a step you'd been told was loose.

"Around three," she says. "Ten to, sometimes. Never here. Only down there."

She points with the pen. Down the corridor, past the giraffe's body, to where the light is thinnest.

"Thanks."

She's already writing again.

---

He walks the ward once with the circle open.

Not fully. Just let out a little, the way you'd crack a window. The reach of an arm and a sword, close and quiet.

What he feels is residue. A faint chill at the threshold of each door, like the draught that comes off a fridge. It's thickest at the far end. Room four. Room five. The playroom, with its door shut and a paper sign on it with a smiling sun.

Room four's door has a name card in a little plastic sleeve. *Yui.* There's a strawberry sticker on it, slightly peeling.

He doesn't go in. Through the gap he can see the end of a bed, a small hump under a blanket, and a plug-in night light in the wall socket by the floor. A plastic rabbit, sitting up. It isn't lit. Room four is darker than every other room on the ward.

The hump under the blanket breathes fast and shallow. Not awake. Somewhere near it.

There's a plastic chair by the drinking fountain, the kind made for a parent to sleep badly in. He sits on it with the bag across his knees.

And he waits.

The ward at night has its own noises. A pump beeping until somebody's shoes squeak over and stop it. A child talking in their sleep, one clear word, *no*, and then nothing. The agency nurse back from her break, who sees him and stops dead, and then very carefully doesn't look at him for the rest of the night.

One forty. Two. Two thirty.

He thinks about nothing. He's good at it. It's the main thing the job has taught him. Not the sword. The waiting.

At two fifty-two the hair on his arms stands up.

---

The light at the far end of the corridor goes out.

Not a flicker. Just gone, like a mouth closing. Then the next panel. Then the next. The dark comes up the corridor towards him one ceiling panel at a time, over the giraffe, over the ducks.

Behind him, at the nurses' station, the light stays on.

Kusakabe stands. He doesn't remember deciding to. The bag is open. He doesn't remember that either.

The circle draws itself closed around him, tight and certain. Everything inside it goes very clear and very slow.

Under the playroom door, something pours out.

It's ugly. It's made of what children see at three in the morning. The shape of a coat on a hook. The gap under a bed. A face in the pattern of a curtain. All of it stitched together badly, too many elbows, legs that bend the wrong way at the wrong time. Where its middle should be there's a mouth, and in the mouth, very faintly, a monitor beeping out of time.

It doesn't come for him.

It goes for room four. The dark one. Where it's easiest.

It's fast. Faster than anything that shape should be. It moves along the floor like spilled ink finding the low side of a table.

It crosses the line of the circle.

His body is already there. That's all he knows about it. One moment the corridor, the next his shoulder against the doorframe of room four and the blade coming round, low, and the thing meeting it.

It makes a sound only he can hear, thin and high, like a kettle left on in another flat.

The second cut is the one that counts. He feels it go through. Something in the middle of it gives, the way a knot gives when you find the right loop.

Then it comes apart. Not dramatically. It just stops having a shape. A grey mist with a smell like rubber gloves, and then not even that.

Behind him, the corridor lights come back on, one panel at a time, from the near end.

He stands in the doorway of room four with a drawn sword, under a painted giraffe, at two fifty-four in the morning.

His heart takes longer to finish than the curse did.

*If anyone comes round that corner right now,* he thinks, *there's no story in the world.*

Nobody comes round the corner. He wipes the blade, sheathes it, zips the bag, and stands there with his hand on the zip until it stops shaking. It never takes long. It's still the longest part.

At the far end of the corridor, Mori has stood up behind the station. She's looking at the lights. Then at him. A man in a doorway with a bag.

She looks at him for a while. Then she sits back down.

---

In room four the breathing has changed.

Slower. Deeper. Like someone putting down something heavy.

He should go. That's the job. In, done, out. Nobody wakes. Nobody knows.

He looks at the rabbit.

He knocks on the door frame with one knuckle, very quietly, because she told him to. Then he goes in.

The night light is cheap. The plastic body is cracked down one side, and someone's strip of sticking plaster has come away. He eases it out of the socket. One of the metal prongs is loose in its housing and bent a little sideways, so it never quite meets the contact.

*Facilities,* he thinks. *Monday.*

There's a roll of white surgical tape on the trolley outside. Mori told him not to touch the trolleys. He touches the trolley.

He bends the prong straight with the tip of his door key. He presses the cracked body together and winds the tape round it, twice, tight. He tears the tape with his teeth, which he's fairly sure is against some rule. He pushes the rabbit back into the wall.

It lights.

Not much. A soft yellow, low down, the colour of the inside of a lemon. Enough to see the leg of the bed. A pair of small slippers with faces on them, side by side. The edge of the blanket.

The hump under the blanket turns over and sighs and doesn't wake.

He stands there for a moment, which he shouldn't.

Then he goes out, and puts the tape back on the trolley exactly where it was, label facing the same way.

---

"Done?" Mori says, when he passes the desk.

"Done."

"Will it come back?"

"Someone'll check. In a few days."

She nods. She doesn't say thank you. He doesn't wait for it. She has a chart to finish and three hours of shift left, and none of it is about him.

"Right," she says.

He's at the lift when he hears her shoes. He turns. She's walked halfway down the corridor, to check on room four the way she must check on it every hour. She stops at the door.

She stands there a second. Looking in.

Then she goes on to room five, and he gets in the lift.

---

He writes the report in the car, on his phone, before they've left the car park.

*Grade 1 curse, fifth floor, paediatric ward. Exorcised at 02:54. No civilian injuries. Residual check recommended within one week.*

That's it. He reads it once. He sends it.

Ijichi glances at the mirror. "That's very fast, Kusakabe-san."

"Yeah."

"It's usually..." Ijichi stops himself, kindly.

"Late," Kusakabe says. "I know."

He doesn't say why. He isn't sure why. Something about the hand outside the blanket, the size of the bottom of a teacup.

He puts his head back against the seat and closes his eyes and doesn't sleep.

---

Gojo comes back from Kyoto the next afternoon.

Kusakabe can hear him from the other side of the building.

He's in the staff room at his desk, the one with the dying plant. Ijichi is at the next one, eating rice crackers over mileage forms. Two auxiliaries are at the kettle. The dispatch coordinator is at the big table with her laptop, a woman in her thirties with a headset round her neck that nobody has ever seen her take off. By the photocopier, Okabe is watching each page come out as if it might try something.

The door opens and the weather comes in.

Gojo isn't wearing the blindfold. He's off the clock, or wants everyone to think so. Little round dark sunglasses, perched on his nose. A big box under one arm, wrapped in green paper with gold writing. Kyoto sweets.

"Souvenirs!" he announces, to the room. "Don't fight."

He puts the box down in front of the dispatch coordinator, who moves her laptop without looking up. Then he turns, and the sunglasses find Kusakabe, and Kusakabe knows, with complete calm, that something bad is about to happen to him.

"There he is!"

"Here I am," Kusakabe says. To his screen.

"Everybody. Everybody, listen." Gojo comes across the room and puts a hand on Kusakabe's shoulder. It's like having a bird land on you, a very large and very pleased bird. "Last night. Children's ward. Grade 1, getting bigger every night, sitting in a room full of sleeping kids. This man walks in. In and out. Not one kid woke up." He leans down to the room in general. "Do you know how hard that is? I wake children up just by walking past their house."

One of the auxiliaries laughs. Ijichi has stopped eating his rice cracker.

"It was one curse," Kusakabe says.

"See? Modest. I love it." Gojo straightens up and lifts his voice, so it carries all the way to the photocopier. "Best Grade 1 without a technique in the country. I'm serious. Somebody write that down."

By the photocopier, Okabe takes out a pen.

Kusakabe isn't going to look to see if he uses it.

*There's a word in that sentence,* he thinks, *doing a lot of work. And it isn't "best."*

That isn't the part that sits on him. He's heard the other part his whole working life, from people who meant it a lot less kindly.

The part that sits on him is the rest of it.

Because the room is looking at him now. Not long. Nobody looks at Kusakabe for long. But the dispatch coordinator lifts her eyes from her laptop, with the face of somebody moving a name a little further up a list.

And it's out now. Said out loud, by the strongest sorcerer alive, in a room with a dispatch coordinator in it. It'll be in people's heads the next time something ugly turns up in a building and they need a name.

Until today nobody at this school expected anything from him except that his reports would be late.

That was a manageable expectation. He was meeting it.

*Praise from Gojo,* he thinks, *is a loan. Nobody tells you the interest rate.*

Gojo has already moved on. He's tearing the gold paper off the box himself, offering the first sweet to Ijichi, who takes it with both hands like a certificate.

On his way out, he comes past Kusakabe's desk again and puts a wrapped sweet down next to the dying plant.

"You were going to say yes anyway," he says. Not loudly, this time.

"I wasn't."

"Sure." The sunglasses slide down his nose. Above them, for half a second, the eyes, very blue and very amused, looking all the way through him and out the other side. He pushes the glasses back up with one finger. "Eat that. It's the good kind."

Then he's gone, and the room goes back to its normal size.

Kusakabe looks at the sweet. Pale green, soft, folded into a triangle, in a little cellophane wrapper.

He doesn't eat it. He puts it in his drawer.

---

On Monday, the dispatch sheet has his name on it four times.

Usually it's once. Sometimes twice, if somebody's off sick.

He stands in front of the board and reads it. A Grade 1 in a closed-down school in Chiba, Wednesday. A multistorey car park near the docks that has already sent one Grade 2 home with a broken wrist, Thursday. A shrine in the hills outside the city, Saturday, with a note next to it in the coordinator's small square handwriting: *Requested. Per G.*

And the fourth one, which was already his. *Residual check, paediatric ward. Thursday night.*

Okabe comes past with his clipboard, looks at the board, then at Kusakabe.

"Reports by Friday," Okabe says. "All of them."

"Mm."

Okabe goes. Kusakabe stays a little longer, looking at the line that says *Per G.*

*Best Grade 1 without a technique,* he thinks. *Also, it turns out, the most available.*

He takes a photo of the board so he won't have to come back and look at it. Then he goes to find somewhere quiet. The bench outside the archive room has a box of old files on it now, and somebody's coat.

---

Thursday night, he goes back.

It's late. The car park by the docks took longer than it should have. He has a graze on the back of one hand from a concrete pillar that came closer than it looked, and a plaster on it from Ijichi's glove box, which came with an apology.

The fifth floor smells exactly the same. Disinfectant. Plastic. Juice cartons. The giraffe still hides its face round the corner.

Mori is at the station. Same cardigan. Different mug, same skin on the tea.

"You again," she says.

"Me again."

"Lights have been fine."

"Good."

She goes back to her chart. Whatever she saw the other night, a man standing in a doorway with a bag, she's decided not to carry it around with her. He respects that. It's what he'd do.

He walks the ward with the circle open.

Nothing. No chill at the doors. Just a ward at night, ordinary and tired. The playroom door is propped open now, and inside, in the half-light, there are small chairs round a table and a drawing taped to the wall of a very large green dog.

It's clean. It's done.

He comes back past room four.

The name card has gone from the door. Just the empty plastic sleeve, and a little tacky square where the strawberry sticker used to be.

He looks in.

The bed is stripped. The mattress is bare in its blue plastic cover. The slippers with faces on them are gone. The room is waiting for the next person, clean and patient and not anyone's.

*Went home,* he thinks. He doesn't ask. He doesn't need to.

Down by the floor, in the socket by the bed, the rabbit is still glowing.

Nobody has thought to unplug it. White tape round its middle, sitting up, giving off its small yellow light to a room with no one in it.

He doesn't unplug it either.

At the station, Mori doesn't look up when he says he's finished. She lifts a hand off the desk an inch, which might be goodbye, or might mean go away. Either way, he goes.

He walks back towards the lift. The lights are at half. Every door is open a hand's width, and behind each one somebody small is sleeping through the night.

At the lift he presses the button, and waits, and looks back.

The corridor is empty. Just the long pale floor, and the painted ducks, and halfway down, from the open door of room four, a little patch of yellow light lying on the tiles, the size of a folded handkerchief.

It doesn't need anybody to see it. It's just on.

The lift doors open behind him. He stays where he is a moment longer, looking at it.

Then he gets in, and the doors close, and the rabbit goes on glowing in the empty corridor without him.

---

## Chapter 4. Paperwork

The envelope is brown, A4, and has his name typed on a white label.

Not handwritten. Typed. Kusakabe has learned to be afraid of the typed ones.

It sits in his pigeonhole in the staffroom on a Monday in November, on top of two mission files he hasn't opened yet. He looks at it for a while without picking it up, the way you look at a dog you don't know.

*Could be nothing,* he thinks. *Could be a pension form.*

It is not a pension form.

NOTICE OF ATTENDANCE. Review of Grade 1 Field Deployment, Tokyo Region. Thursday, 2 p.m. Headquarters, third floor, Conference Room B. Attendance is mandatory. Below that, in smaller type, a list of names. His is the last one. Under the list, a line that says the panel will be "consulting with personnel on readiness, workload and conduct."

He reads the word *conduct* twice. Then he puts the notice back in the envelope, the envelope under the mission files, and the whole pile under his arm.

Out of sight. That's a start.

---

It's been a long fortnight.

Since Gojo stood in the middle of the staffroom and told everyone within shouting distance exactly what he thought of Kusakabe, the files have been arriving the way leaves arrive in a gutter. Three missions in nine days. A shrine in Chiba. A school gym in Kawasaki. A car park that turned out to be two car parks, which nobody had mentioned. Nobody told him the work would increase. The files just got thicker, with his name at the top.

*That's what praise is,* he thinks. *A longer queue.*

He sits at his desk in the corner of the staffroom, the one by the radiator that doesn't work, and opens the first file. Kanagawa. Probably Grade 2. Then the second. Saitama again.

He doesn't take the envelope out again.

He knows what's in it. He knows what Conference Room B looks like, too. A long table. Water jugs nobody drinks from. A row of men who have never drawn a blade in their lives, asking him to describe, in his own words, his approach to risk.

*My approach to risk,* he thinks, *is not to go to Conference Room B.*

---

On Thursday, he goes to Conference Room B.

Or he goes to the building. He gets as far as the third floor. That has to count for something.

The headquarters building is the kind of place that tries hard to look like nothing. Grey stone, a reception desk, a potted plant that may be plastic.

Outside Conference Room B there is a row of chairs. Four other Grade 1s are already sitting on them. He knows two by sight and one by reputation. Nobody talks. One of them is reading a newspaper so slowly he can't really be reading it.

Kusakabe sits on the end chair.

A young clerk comes out with a clipboard and a smile that has been told to be there. "The panel is running a little behind," she says. "About forty minutes. Thank you for your patience."

Through the half-open door, Kusakabe can see a slice of the room. The long table. The water jugs. A row of name cards, white, folded into little tents. He can see the backs of three heads. One of them is bald and very still. One of them is talking, not loudly, but in the voice of a man who has never once been interrupted.

At the far end of the table, a single chair faces the others. Empty. Waiting.

*That's where they put you,* he thinks. *So everyone can see your hands.*

His palms are cold. They're always cold before something. The men in there are only going to ask him questions, and he's going to have to answer them in sentences, with verbs and reasons, and at the end someone will write something down.

He'd rather be in the car park in Kawasaki. Both car parks.

He gets up.

Nobody looks at him. He walks down the corridor towards the toilets, because that's where people go when they get up from a row of chairs. Past the toilets, the corridor ends in a fire door. A cleaner's trolley has been parked in front of it, and someone has wedged it open a hand's width with a mop bucket. Presumably to air out the smell of whatever's in the bucket.

Cold air comes through the gap. A metal staircase. A slice of grey sky.

He stands there for perhaps three seconds.

*Nobody's ever been sacked for going to the toilet,* he thinks. *Probably.*

He moves the bucket six inches with his foot, steps through, and moves it back from the other side.

---

The fire stairs come out in a yard behind the building, between a row of air-conditioning units and a skip full of broken office chairs. Nobody's there. A crow on the skip watches him the way a crow watches anything, as if it's already decided what you're worth.

He walks out of the yard, round the block, and back to the station.

He does not feel free. That's the annoying part. He'd thought he might. Instead he feels like a man who has left the gas on, except the gas is a room full of old men, and there's no going back to check.

He doesn't go home. Home is where the sofa is, and the sofa would make it official. Instead he goes back to school, to an empty classroom on the second floor with the chairs up on the desks, takes one down, and sits at the window with his laptop.

He writes reports.

All three. The shrine in Chiba. The school gym. Both car parks. Times, locations, grades, outcomes. *Exorcised. No civilian casualties. Minor damage to the surface of level two.* He fills in every box. He even fills in the box that asks for "observations relevant to future assessment," which he has left blank for six years, and writes: *None.*

At 4:52 he presses send on the last one.

*Look at that,* he thinks. *On time.*

He sits there a while longer, in the dark classroom, with the screen glowing on his knees. Then he puts the chair back up on the desk where he found it, legs in the air, and goes home.

---

Okabe finds him the next morning, which is not difficult. Kusakabe is at his desk by the broken radiator, where he always is.

The cardigan today is grey. The clipboard is the same.

"Your reports came in," Okabe says. "All three."

"Yeah."

"On time."

"Yeah."

Okabe looks at the clipboard as if the clipboard might know what to do with this. Then he looks back up. "You weren't at the review."

Kusakabe says nothing. The radiator, which doesn't work, ticks anyway, the way old metal does when the building warms up around it.

"The clerk said you were in the corridor," Okabe says. "And then you weren't."

"Something came up."

"What came up?"

He could say it. *A fire door, a mop bucket, a crow.* He could say the truth, which is that he looked at an empty chair and his body decided for him. He could explain. Some people would.

"Something," he says.

Okabe breathes in through his nose. There's a long, careful pause, in which Kusakabe can see a whole speech being drafted, revised, and filed.

"The panel noticed," Okabe says at last. "One of them asked specifically about your attendance. Your name was on the list for a reason, Kusakabe-san. There were questions about your workload. Whether it was... appropriate." He holds out a file. It's thicker than the others. It has a red tab. "This was assigned this morning. By the panel. Directly."

Kusakabe takes it.

Gunma. A disused railway tunnel on a closed mountain line. Two road maintenance workers missing for nine days. A Grade 2 sent in last month came out with a broken arm and a statement that ran to half a page and didn't make much sense. Assessed Grade 1, possibly higher. Under *Personnel* there are two names.

His. And under it: *Kamo Tsubasa, Grade 2. Accompanying for field experience.*

"Kamo," Kusakabe says.

"A minor branch," Okabe says. "But still." He says *but still* the way some people say *bless you*. Automatically, and with a little bow in it. "The panel felt it would be valuable. For the young man. To observe a senior sorcerer in the field."

Kusakabe looks at the file. Then at Okabe.

Okabe doesn't smile. Okabe is not a man who smiles at things like this. But there's something about the way he tucks the clipboard back under his arm, very neatly, that is the closest he will ever get.

"Tomorrow," Okabe says. "Eight o'clock."

---

The kid is already at the car when Kusakabe gets there, which is the first bad sign.

Nineteen, maybe. Tall, narrow, with good posture he's clearly been told about since he could stand. Uniform pressed. Hair cut by someone who charges for it. Across his chest, on a leather strap, a row of small clear packs, each one filled with something dark red.

Blood. His own, presumably. Kamo clan. The family technique runs in the family's veins, quite literally, and they carry spares.

Ijichi is standing by the open back door of the black sedan, holding a paper cup of coffee out towards nobody in particular. He has the expression of a man who has been standing there for some time.

"Kusakabe-san," Ijichi says, with feeling. "Good morning."

"Morning."

The kid turns. He looks Kusakabe up and down. It doesn't take long. It's the look of someone checking a menu for a dish they already know isn't on it.

"You're Kusakabe," the kid says.

No *san*. Not even a little one.

"Yeah."

"Kamo Tsubasa." A nod, not a bow. "They told me you don't have a technique."

"That's right."

The kid waits, as if there must be more. As if Kusakabe is going to produce something from his sleeve. When nothing comes, he tilts his head slightly. "So what do you actually do?"

Kusakabe thinks about it. He thinks about the honest answer, which is: *I stand in one place and I hit whatever comes too close. I've done it for years. It's boring, and it works.*

"Sword," he says, and gets in the car.

---

It's a long drive. Three hours, the last one on roads that narrow and climb.
