The kid talks. Not to Kusakabe, exactly. Near him. He talks to Ijichi about the mission file, which he has clearly read several times. Every so often he says something like "of course, with blood manipulation, range isn't really an issue," and leaves a small space after it. Like a man leaving a door open in case anyone wants to follow him through.

Nobody does. Ijichi says "I see" at intervals. Ijichi could say "I see" to an earthquake.

Kusakabe looks out of the window.

The trees on the hills are past their best. The red has gone to brown and the brown has gone to nothing, and between the bare trunks there's frost still lying in the shadows at eleven in the morning. A farmer is burning something in a field. The smoke goes straight up. There's no wind at all.

The kid catches him looking at the blood packs.

"Problem?"

"No."

"You're staring."

"Thinking."

"About what?"

*About whether you know how much of that you can spend before you fall over.*

"Lunch," Kusakabe says.

The kid makes a small sound through his nose and turns back to Ijichi. Kusakabe hears him say, quite clearly, not bothering to lower his voice, "My uncle says the ones without techniques are basically auxiliaries with swords."

Ijichi's eyes flick up to the mirror. Find Kusakabe's. Flick away.

"I see," says Ijichi.

---

The tunnel is at the end of a road that stopped being a road some time ago.

Ijichi parks where the gravel gives out. Beyond it, a cutting in the hillside, steep and dark with old moss, and at the end of the cutting, the tunnel mouth. Brick arch, blackened. A rusted gate hanging off one hinge. The rails still run into it, red-brown and half buried in dead leaves, and disappear.

The air coming out of it is colder than the air outside. That's not unusual, for a tunnel. What's unusual is that it smells of iron, and the iron smell gets stronger when he breathes in, as if the tunnel is breathing out at the same time.

Ijichi raises the curtain. The sky goes the colour of a bruise. The birds stop.

Kusakabe takes the sword out of its bag. He does it slowly, the way he always does, because his hands like the routine. Bag. Strap. Hilt. The weight settling where it lives on his hip.

The kid is already at the gate.

"Wait," Kusakabe says.

The kid turns, impatient. "For what?"

"Me."

"Why?"

Kusakabe walks up to the gate and stops beside him. "Statement said it's not at the entrance. It's further in. It drops. From above." He nods at the arch, the dark beyond it. "We go in together. You stay inside my reach. If it comes, it comes to me first."

The kid stares at him.

Then he laughs. Not a big laugh. A short one, the kind you give a waiter who has told you a joke you didn't ask for.

"Inside your *reach*," he says. "You want me to stand next to you so you can protect me with your *sword*." He pulls one of the packs from the strap and turns it over in his fingers. "I can hit it from thirty metres. I don't need to be inside anyone's reach."

"Then hit it from thirty metres," Kusakabe says. "From here."

"I can't see it from here."

"No."

The kid's jaw tightens. He looks into the tunnel, then at Kusakabe, then into the tunnel again. Kusakabe can see the exact moment he decides. It's in his shoulders. They come up and back, like a boy on a diving board who has been told the water is cold and has decided to prove that it isn't.

"I'll find it," the kid says. "You can catch up."

He steps through the gate.

*Oh, good,* Kusakabe thinks.

---

He follows.

He doesn't run. Running in the dark is how you trip over a rail and break your nose in front of a nineteen-year-old. He walks, quickly, and as he walks he draws the circle.

It comes up around him the way it always does, without fuss. The reach of one arm and one sword. Inside it, the tunnel stops being dark and starts being shape. Sleepers under his feet, rotten and soft. Puddles between them, skinned with ice. The curve of the brick overhead, wet, close. Ten metres ahead, the kid's footsteps, too fast, and the faint slosh of the packs on his chest.

And above them both, something else.

It isn't moving. That's what's wrong with it. Everything in a tunnel moves a little: drips, air, small animals. This doesn't. It's spread along the roof like a stain spreads, thin and wide, and it's waiting with the patience of something that has caught two men already and expects to catch more.

"Tsubasa," Kusakabe says. "Stop."

The kid doesn't stop. The kid lifts his hand, and the pack in it bursts, and the blood comes out in a line. Kusakabe hears it rather than sees it: a hiss, a whip-crack against brick. Then another. Then three at once, the kid firing into the dark at the ceiling, at the walls, at the shape of his own fear.

One of them hits something.

The thing on the roof notices.

---

It comes down all at once, like a sheet pulled off a bed.

Kusakabe feels it the way you feel a lift drop. His stomach goes first. The kid is outside the circle, nine metres ahead, half turned, a fourth pack already open in his hand and his face gone white. Too much blood out, too fast. His knees are going. Kusakabe can hear it in his breathing, the little stagger in it.

*Nine metres,* he thinks. *Too far.*

He doesn't decide anything. There isn't time for deciding.

His feet move. Three steps, long and flat, and the circle comes with him, because the circle is him, it goes where he goes. On the third step, the kid is inside it.

So is the curse.

The line is crossed. His hands answer.

There's a sound like a cloth tearing. There's cold on his face and a weight that goes past his ear and keeps going, and a smell like old coins, and then there's nothing coming down anymore. Just pieces. Settling. Fading into the dark between the sleepers like breath on a window.

One cut.

He's standing with the blade out and his heel sunk an inch into rotten wood and his whole arm humming, wrist to shoulder.

His palms are freezing. His palms are always freezing. The sword doesn't care.

Behind him, the kid sits down on the rails, hard, the way a puppet sits when someone lets go of the strings.

---

For a while, nobody says anything.

Water drips somewhere. The kid's breathing is loud and ragged in the curved space. Kusakabe wipes the blade on his sleeve, because it's that or his trousers, and sheathes it. His arm is still humming. He lets it.

Further in, in the light from his phone, he finds the two maintenance workers. Or what there is of them. Two orange jackets. He doesn't look for long. He takes a photo, because the file will want one, and comes back.

The kid hasn't moved. He's holding the empty pack in one hand and staring at it. His lips have gone a strange colour. Grey at the edges.

Kusakabe crouches. He takes a bottle out of his jacket pocket, the sports drink he buys from the machine on the way to every job and almost never drinks, unscrews the cap, and holds it out.

The kid looks at it. Then at him.

"Drink."

"I'm fine."

"You're grey. Drink."

The kid takes it. His hand shakes so much the first mouthful goes down his chin. The second one goes in. He drinks half the bottle without stopping. Then he puts it down on the sleeper next to him and wipes his mouth with the back of his wrist. Kusakabe watches the colour start to come back into his face.

And with the colour, something else.

"I had it," the kid says.

Kusakabe doesn't say anything.

"I hit it. You saw. I hit it first. It was already..." He waves a hand at the dark. "It was coming apart. You just finished it."

The honest answer is simple. *It was coming for your head, and you were out of blood, and you were sitting down before I touched it.*

Kusakabe looks at him for a moment. Nineteen. Pressed uniform, filthy now at the knees. A strap of empty packs. An uncle somewhere who thinks men like Kusakabe are auxiliaries with swords. A whole family waiting at home to hear how it went.

"Okay," Kusakabe says.

He stands up. He offers a hand. The kid doesn't take it. He gets up on his own, slowly, using the wall, and walks out of the tunnel ahead of him, very straight.

---

In the car, the kid makes a phone call.

He makes it in the back seat, with his head turned towards the window, as if that makes it private. It doesn't. The car is small and Ijichi drives very quietly.

"Yes. Yes, it's done. Grade 1, they said, maybe higher." A pause. "No, I'm fine. A bit tired. I used a lot." A longer pause. A laugh, a real one this time, warm and relieved. "Yes. I did. The other one was there. He came in at the end."

Kusakabe looks out of the window.

The frost has gone from the shadows now. The farmer's fire has burned down to a grey smear in the field. A small truck passes them going the other way, stacked with firewood, an old woman at the wheel who doesn't look at them at all.

"I'll tell you everything tonight," the kid says. "Yes. Thank you, Uncle."

He hangs up. He puts the phone in his lap and closes his eyes, and inside ten minutes he's asleep, mouth slightly open, head against the glass.

Ijichi's eyes find Kusakabe in the mirror again. This time they stay.

"Kusakabe-san," he says, very low. "The report. For a joint mission, the junior officer drafts it. You countersign." He swallows. "I just... I thought you should know. That's the procedure."

"I know."

"He'll write..."

"I know."

Ijichi's hands shift on the wheel. Ten to two, then back. "If you wanted to submit a separate statement, there's a form. It's not common, but there's a form. I could find it for you."

Of course there's a form. There's always a form.

Kusakabe thinks about the form. He thinks about filling it in. He thinks about sitting across a table from someone who will read both versions and look up and say *So which is it?* and then wait, the way Okabe waits, for him to explain. About the kid's uncle, who will hear about it, and the kid's uncle's friends. About the kid, nineteen, who will have to sit on a chair at the end of a long table and say *I sat down. He saved me. I lied on the phone.*

He thinks about how many sentences all of that would take.

"It's fine," he says.

Ijichi drives for another kilometre in silence.

"Yes, Kusakabe-san," he says finally, in a voice that makes it clear he doesn't think so, and is sorry, and will never mention it again.

---

The draft comes on Tuesday.

It's in his pigeonhole in a plain brown envelope, his name handwritten on it this time, which is somehow worse. Two printed pages and a yellow sticky note in Okabe's careful hand. *Countersignature required. Return by Friday 5 p.m.*

He takes it to his desk by the radiator and reads it.

*Upon arrival, Grade 2 Kamo assessed the site and determined the target to be located within the tunnel's upper structure. Grade 2 Kamo advanced to engage. Grade 1 Kusakabe remained at the entrance.*

He reads on.

*Grade 2 Kamo located and engaged the target using inherited technique (blood manipulation), inflicting significant damage. Grade 1 Kusakabe entered the tunnel following the initial engagement and assisted in the final exorcism of the weakened target. Grade 2 Kamo sustained no injuries but experienced fatigue due to technique expenditure.*

*Remains of the two missing workers were located. See attached.*

That's it. Nothing false, exactly. That's the clever part. He did stay at the entrance. For three seconds. He did enter after the first engagement. He did assist.

*Assisted,* he thinks. *Fair enough. I assisted it into four pieces.*

He sits back.

There's a second sheet underneath. A copy of the draft, the same text, but this one has been somewhere before it came to him. Someone has stamped it with a received date, Monday, and initialled it. And in the margin, beside the line *Grade 1 Kusakabe remained at the entrance*, someone has written in pencil, very small, very neat, the way men write when they know the paper will be kept:

*Froze?*

Just that. One word and a question mark.

He looks at it for a long time.

He doesn't know whose handwriting it is. He could guess. A bald head, very still, at the end of a long table. A man who has never once been interrupted. A man who noticed, last Thursday, that one of the chairs in the corridor was empty when the clerk came out to call the last name on the list.

*Froze.*

It's almost funny. Two weeks ago, Gojo stood in a room and shouted that he was the best Grade 1 without a technique, and the work doubled. Now a man he's never spoken to has written one word in pencil, and it'll be in his file long after the shouting's forgotten. That's the thing about paper. It doesn't raise its voice. It doesn't need to.

He could go upstairs. He could find Okabe and ask for Ijichi's form. He could sit on the chair at the end of the table and say, in sentences, with verbs: *No. It came down from the roof. He was nine metres in and out of blood. I took three steps and cut it once. I did not freeze.*

He imagines how long the meeting would be.

*Somebody upstairs thinks I froze,* he thinks. *Somebody upstairs already thought I was an auxiliary with a sword. It's not much of a fall.*

It costs something. He knows it does. He can feel it, small and exact, like a splinter he's decided to leave in. One day someone will open that file looking for a reason, and there it'll be. In pencil. Waiting.

He takes a pen out of the drawer.

At the bottom of the second page there are two lines. The first one is signed already, in a quick confident hand that slopes a little forward, as if it's in a hurry to get somewhere. *Kamo Tsubasa.*

Under it, the second line. Empty.

He doesn't read it again. Once was enough.

He signs. Kusakabe Atsuya. Small, plain, perfectly legible, on the line under the kid's. The pen scratches once and stops.

It's Tuesday. It isn't due till Friday.

*Early,* he thinks. *Don't tell anyone.*

---

## Chapter 5. Days Off

The leave isn't his idea.

It arrives on a Friday at the end of November, as a printout with a yellow line through the middle of it. Okabe's highlighter. *Annual leave, unused: eleven days. Leave not taken by the end of the calendar year will be forfeited.* Under that, in Okabe's own careful pencil: *Please take at least five before December. The office is concerned about the figures.*

Not concerned about him. Concerned about the figures. Kusakabe appreciates the honesty.

"Monday to Friday," Okabe says, standing by his desk. "Dispatch has already moved your files."

"Fine."

"You could go somewhere."

"Could."

Okabe waits, as if Kusakabe might name a hot spring. Kusakabe looks at the radiator. Eventually Okabe goes away, and Kusakabe sits there with the printout, and five empty days open up in front of him like a field with no path across it.

*I'll do things,* he thinks. *Shoes. Laundry. The bank.*

He folds the printout in four and puts it in his pocket.

---

Sunday he goes to his sister's, the same as always. Lavender at the door. Tea too weak. Mrs. Fujita reads him the cat notebook in her quick flat voice and he listens. Nothing in it is new. That's the best kind of week.

Monday he wakes at six ten, because the body doesn't know about leave.

He lies there. Then he gets up, and makes coffee, and takes it to the sofa instead of the window. That's the first decision of the day, and it turns out to be the only one.

He sits.

He has a list. It's in his head, which is the safest place for a list, because nobody can check it. Shoes to the repair place by the station; the left heel has been going for a month. Laundry. The bank, about the card.

He'll do the shoes after the coffee.

The coffee goes cold in his hand. He drinks it cold. It's not worse. That's the most you can say for it.

There's a patch of sunlight on the floor. In late November it comes in low, across the boards, a pale square with the shadow of the window frame in it like a cross. When he sat down it was by the door. By eleven it's reached the leg of the low table. By one it's climbing the side of the sword bag against the wall.

He watches it go. He doesn't decide to. It's just the thing in the room that's moving.

At some point he eats crackers from the packet. At some point he goes to the toilet and comes back and sits in exactly the same dent. The sofa keeps his shape for him while he's gone.

The square of light thins, goes yellow, goes orange, slides up the wall and off it.

By half past four it's dark.

He doesn't turn the lamp on. Turning the lamp on would mean admitting it's evening, and then he'd have to admit what happened to the afternoon.

*Shoes tomorrow,* he thinks.

---

This isn't rest. He knows that much.

Rest is something you come back from. You rest, and then you get up a little more yourself than you sat down. This is the other thing. This is being switched off at the wall. The days don't go anywhere. They just stop being days. He sits inside them the way you sit in a waiting room, without a number, without a magazine, without anyone due to call his name.

He doesn't put the television on. In the evenings, usually, he puts it on so the room has a voice in it. This week he doesn't even do that. The flat is quiet enough to hear the fridge thinking. The neighbour's washing machine goes into its spin cycle through the wall, and the floor hums very faintly under his feet, and then stops.

He doesn't speak to anyone.

Not on purpose. There's just nobody to speak to, and he doesn't go looking. On Monday evening he goes down to the convenience store for rice and eggs and a can of coffee. At the till the boy says the total. Kusakabe holds up his card, and the boy taps it, and that's the whole exchange. He nods. The boy nods. Nobody has to use a voice.

On the way back up the stairs he tries to clear his throat, and it comes out cracked and strange, like a door that hasn't been opened since summer.

*Sunday,* he thinks. *Last word I said out loud was to the night carer. "Night."*

He finds he doesn't mind. That's the part that should worry him, probably.

---

The phone goes on Tuesday morning, at twelve minutes past ten.

It's on the floor by the sofa, face down, because face up it lights every time something wants him. It buzzes against the boards. It turns, very slowly, a few degrees each time, like a beetle on its back.

He leans over and looks.

*Fujita.*

He watches it ring.

There's no reason not to answer. He's awake. He's the least busy he's been in six years. He could pick it up and say "Yes," and it would take four minutes.

But answering means being a person on the phone. It means being Atsuya Kusakabe, the brother, the one who pays, with a voice and answers and decisions, at ten in the morning, in a week that was supposed to be empty. It means the field gets a path across it, and the path has somebody standing on it, waiting.

It rings out.

The screen says he has a missed call for a while, then goes dark.

*She'd text if it was serious,* he thinks. *She always texts.*

At eleven fifty she texts.

*Please call when you have a moment. Nothing urgent now. Fujita.*

He reads it twice. He notices the *now*. It's sitting at the end of the sentence like someone standing at the end of a corridor, not quite looking at you.

*Nothing urgent,* he thinks, and holds onto that part, and lets the other part go.

*After lunch.*

After lunch it's *after I've eaten something*. After he's eaten something it's *when it's dark, she'll be home by then*. When it's dark it's *too late now, she'll be tired, she has an early start*.

Every one of them is reasonable. That's how it works. None of them is a no. They're all just *not yet*, lined up one after another, until the day runs out of room.

---

On Wednesday an unknown number rings at nine.

He doesn't answer unknown numbers. Nobody does. It's a rule of modern life, like not making eye contact on trains. It rings out. No message.

It rings again at two. Same number. No message.

*Insurance,* he thinks. *Someone selling a better phone plan.*

He looks at the Fujita message again. It's still there. He sets a time this time, a real one, out loud in his head like a man setting an alarm. *Six o'clock.*

At six o'clock he's in the shower, which he started at five fifty-five without thinking about why.

At seven he has the phone in his hand, his thumb over the green button. She'll tell him whatever it is, and he'll have to answer it, and he doesn't know what it is, so he doesn't know the answer. He can't stand on a phone line without an answer.

He puts the phone face down on the floor.

*Tomorrow,* he thinks. *Properly. First thing.*

The sofa takes him back without comment.

---

He calls on Thursday at quarter past nine, standing up, in his coat, because sitting down felt too much like the last three days.

She answers on the first ring.

"Kusakabe-san."

"Sorry. I missed your call."

"Calls," she says. Not sharply. Just accurately.

"Yeah."

A pause. He can hear the flat behind her. A spoon on a cup. A radio low in the kitchen. Then a door closing, which means she's gone into the hallway so his sister won't hear.

"Tuesday morning she choked," Mrs. Fujita says. "On her tea. Not badly. She was fine. But it went on longer than it should, and she was frightened, and afterwards she didn't want to eat anything. And I didn't know if I should call the doctor or if you'd want to be the one who decided. So I called you."

He stands very still in his own entrance hall, one shoe on.

"I called the doctor in the end," she says. "He came at three. He says it's the swallowing, it happens, he's written it down. Thicker drinks. He'll come again next week. She's fine." She breathes in. "She's fine, Kusakabe-san. I want to say that first."

"Okay."

"The other thing."

He waits. He knows. Some part of him has known since the word *now*.

"I've given notice to the agency," she says. "I told them on Monday. They were supposed to tell you. Friday is my last day."

There it is.

He looks at the shoe in his hand. The left one. The heel he was going to get fixed.

"It's not only the calls," she says, quickly, the reasons lining up the way they always do, ready to be read out. "It's my knees. It's my mother, she's in Saitama now, I need to be closer. They've offered me a place nearer her, mornings, a lady who lives with her daughter. I've been thinking about it since the summer." A pause. "But it's the calls too. I'm sorry. I'm not going to pretend it isn't. Three years, I've never once rung you about anything that wasn't something. And Tuesday I stood in that kitchen with her coughing and I thought, if it goes wrong, it's just me. It's always just me."

He could tell her about the sofa. He could tell her he was on leave, that he was right there, that the phone was eighteen inches from his hand. That would make it worse. He doesn't.

"Understood," he says.

"I'm not angry."

"Okay."

"I am a bit," she says. "But I'm not."

"Yeah."

"Friday, then," she says. "I'll do the notebook properly. You'll need to talk to Mr. Hasegawa. At the agency. He's been trying to call you."

*Insurance,* he thinks. *Better phone plan.*

"I'll call him," he says.

There's a short silence in which they both hear exactly how much that's worth this week.

---

Midori Home Care is on the second floor of a building between a dry cleaner and a shop that sells nothing but umbrellas.

He goes in person on Thursday afternoon, because he called Mr. Hasegawa back at ten and Mr. Hasegawa said *It would be easier to do this face to face*, in the voice of a man who has learned what happens to families over the phone.

The stairwell smells of steam from the cleaner below. Upstairs is one room with four desks, two of them empty, and a whiteboard covering one whole wall. A grid of days and names in coloured marker. Small round magnets, red, blue, green. He finds his sister's surname near the bottom. Next to it, from Monday, the rows are blank.

Mr. Hasegawa is sixties, thin, with a grey cardigan and a mask pulled down under his chin, where it stays for the whole meeting like a second, smaller chin. He bows. He offers tea. Kusakabe says no, and gets it anyway, in a paper cup, green and much too strong.

"Kusakabe-san. Thank you for coming."

"Yeah."

Mr. Hasegawa sits, opens a folder, and turns it round so they can both see it. It's the care plan. His sister on four printed pages. Her medication. Her mobility. *Cognitive: variable.* Somewhere in a box near the bottom, his own name, his phone number, the word *Family*.

"When a lead carer leaves," Mr. Hasegawa says, "the plan goes back for review. That's our policy. It protects the client. We need to sit down with the family, agree the changes, and sign. Until it's signed, I can't put new staff on it." He turns a page. "We called you on Wednesday. Twice. Mrs. Fujita tried too, I believe."

Kusakabe looks at the four printed pages.

There are about forty things he could say. That he was on leave. That he doesn't answer unknown numbers. That his work is irregular, that he's sometimes somewhere a phone doesn't reach, that last month he was in a tunnel in Gunma. Some of it's even true. All of it would take a long time and none of it would change the whiteboard.

"That's on me," he says. "I should've called back."

Mr. Hasegawa stops with his hand on the page.

He looks at Kusakabe over his glasses. It's the look of a man who has sat across this desk from a great many sons and daughters-in-law and heard all forty things, often loudly. He had his own answers lined up.

He doesn't need them. He seems briefly unsure what to do with his hands.

"Well," he says. "Yes. Thank you." He clears his throat. "Let's see what we can do."

What they can do is this. The weekend is covered: the Sunday morning is already on the board, the night carer already rostered through Sunday. Mr. Hasegawa has a woman who could start Wednesday. Possibly someone else for later. But Monday and Tuesday are gone. Those slots went to other clients while the plan sat unsigned.

"I can try to find someone," he says. "But honestly. This time of year. Before Wednesday, I'd be lying to you."

"Fine," Kusakabe says. "I'll do it."

"Both days? The nights as well?"

"Yeah."

Mr. Hasegawa looks as if he'd like to ask what Kusakabe does for a living, and whether it can spare him. He doesn't ask. Kusakabe's grateful. It's the second decent thing anyone's done for him today, and the first was a woman telling him she was a bit angry.

"Then tomorrow," Mr. Hasegawa says, "you can meet the candidates. Here. Ten o'clock. I'll need you to tell them about her."

*Tell them about her,* he thinks.

He signs the plan where the sticky arrows point.

---

There are two.

The first is a woman in her forties called Mrs. Kubo, round-faced, with a scarf in three colours and a voice that fills the little room right to the corners. She's lovely. He can see it at once. She's the kind of person who brings biscuits on the first day and remembers your birthday on the third.

She asks everything.

How long has his sister been like this? What happened? An accident, how awful, what sort of accident? Does she have children? A son, oh, and is he? Oh. I'm so sorry. And what about him, is he married, what does he do for work?

"School," he says.

"Oh, a teacher! What do you teach?"

He thinks about it.

"P.E."

Mrs. Kubo beams. Mr. Hasegawa, at the end of the desk, writes something down that is probably not *P.E.*

Every answer he gives, she hands back with another question attached. She means it kindly. By the end of twenty minutes he feels like a coat that's been gone through for its pockets.

When she leaves, she squeezes his arm. "Your sister's lucky to have you."

He doesn't know what to say to that. So he says nothing. She doesn't seem to need him to.

---

The second is Ono Haruka.

Late twenties. Black puffer jacket she keeps on. Hair pulled back hard. A plain watch with the face on the inside of her wrist, the way nurses wear them. She bows the minimum, sits, and looks at him as though he's a form she needs to fill in correctly.

"Ono," she says. "I worked two years in a care home in Adachi. Before that, hospital. Ward assistant."

"Okay."

Mr. Hasegawa slides the care plan across. She reads it. All four pages. She doesn't skim. Her eyes go to the end of each line and back. He finds he's watching her read the way Mrs. Fujita used to watch him read the notebook.

Then she looks up.

"Her pills. Whole or crushed?"

"Whole. The half one you cut."

"After Tuesday, the doctor might want them crushed. I'll ask." She turns a page. "Which hand?"

"Right. Not far. Not always."

"Transfers?"

"There's a way. Mrs. Fujita does it." He tries to show her with his arms, the practised hug, and does it badly, the way he always has.

Ono watches. "I know it," she says. "I'll do it my way. It'll be the same thing."

She doesn't ask what happened to his sister. She doesn't ask about a son. She doesn't ask what he does for a living. She doesn't smile at him once. He realises halfway through that she isn't going to, and that he doesn't want her to, and that the room has gone very easy to sit in.

There are three things he has to tell her. He's been dreading them all morning, in order.

"Some days she doesn't know who people are," he says. "She'll call you something else. A name. Doesn't matter whose. Just answer to it."

Ono nods once. No face. Not *oh how sad*. Just the nod you give a fact.

"She likes her tea weak. Really weak. Count to three and take the bag out. It'll look wrong. Do it anyway."

Another nod. She writes *three* on the back of her hand in biro.

The third one is the hardest, and it's the smallest.

"She likes the window. The chair by the window. Even when there's nothing. Even at night." He stops. "Don't move her away from it to make the room easier."

Ono looks at him for slightly longer than before.

"I don't do that," she says.

That's all. Not a promise. A statement about herself, like her height.

When she's gone, Mr. Hasegawa takes his glasses off and rubs his nose. "Mrs. Kubo is very experienced," he says carefully. "Families tend to like her."

"Ono," Kusakabe says.

Mr. Hasegawa puts his glasses back on. He doesn't argue. He gets up, goes to the whiteboard, and puts a green magnet on the Wednesday square.

---

Friday he goes to the flat for Mrs. Fujita's last shift.

She's at the counter with the notebook. Not the cat one. A new one, the same kind, the same hundred-yen shop. This one has a cartoon rabbit on it. She's been copying things across. Medicines, the doctor's number, the exercises, the toilet handle that still sticks if you don't jiggle it. Every page in her neat uphill pencil.

"For the new one," she says. "Ono-san, is it?"

"Yeah."

"Good. Hasegawa said she's sensible." She closes the rabbit and puts it on top of the cat. "You keep the old one."

He takes it. It's soft at the corners. Three years of it. *Ate half. Ate all. Confused p.m.* Every Sunday, a few lines in his own worse handwriting.

The bills are by the kettle, the way they always are. He picks them up. The agency's envelope is on top.

He opens it. He doesn't usually open them here. He's not sure why he does today.

It's the first one at the new rate. There's the number, at the bottom, with a box round it. He does the sum this time, standing there, without the calculator. It doesn't take thirty seconds. It takes about four. It's roughly what he'd guessed, which isn't comforting. It just means the guess was right, and the guess was bad.

*Fine,* he thinks. *Still fine. A bit less fine.*

He folds it back into the envelope.

Mrs. Fujita is putting on her coat. She does it slowly, one arm, then the other, and stands in the hallway for a moment with her bag on her shoulder, looking at the lavender on the shoe cabinet.

"Thicker drinks," she says. "Don't forget. There's a powder. It's in the cupboard."

"Okay."

She goes to the window chair. She bends down and says something to his sister, low, that he doesn't hear. His sister nods, the polite nod she gives people she can't place. Mrs. Fujita squeezes her right hand and doesn't make a thing of it.

At the door she bows to him. He bows back, lower than he means to.

"Three years," she says.

"Thank you."

It comes out of him before he's decided to say it. Two words he's not good at. She looks almost as surprised as Mr. Hasegawa did.

"Answer your phone, Kusakabe-san," she says. And goes.

---

Monday, half past seven in the morning, the night carer hands over to him in the doorway, and goes, and then it's just the two of them.

He calls dispatch from the kitchen while the kettle boils. The coordinator with the headset answers on the second ring. He tells her he won't be in today or tomorrow.

"Reason?"

"Family."

A pause. Keys tapping. "I'll have to put it down as unscheduled."

"Fine."

"There were two jobs for you today. They'll go to someone else."

"Okay."

He can hear her moving his name. Not up a list this time. Sideways, into some other column.

Then he does everything Mrs. Fujita does, and does it worse.

He does the transfer, the hug, from the bed to the wheelchair and the wheelchair to the window chair. She grips his collar with her right hand as if he's a stranger who might drop her. He gets her washed, with the cloth and the basin, slowly, carefully, both of them looking at the wall. He mixes the powder into her tea until it goes the texture of thin custard. She tastes it and pulls a face he hasn't seen before.

"This is wrong."

"Doctor says."

"Doctors," she says, with contempt, and drinks it.

He does the exercises from the rabbit notebook. Lift the arm. Hold. Lower. Again. She does three and then looks out of the window and won't do the fourth, and he doesn't push.

At lunchtime she looks at him for a long time and asks, quite politely, "Are you the new one?"

He thinks about it.

"Yeah," he says. "Sort of."

"You're slower than the other lady."

"Yeah."

"She had a cat," his sister says. "On a book."

---

The days at the flat don't disappear.

That's what he notices, by Tuesday. Same grey light as the sofa, same early dark. But they don't go anywhere. They're full of small hard things. Pills at eight, at one, at seven. The toilet, which takes all of his patience and none of his dignity, and less of hers. They both get through it by not looking at each other. Lunch she won't eat. Tea she will. Each hour has a shape. Each hour needs him in it.

He's more tired than he's been all year. He doesn't once look at a clock wondering where the afternoon went.

At night he puts her to bed. Then he turns off the big light and leaves the little one, and sits in the window chair, the blue one, with a blanket from the cupboard over his knees.

The chair is too small for him. It was bought for her. His knees come up too high. His head finds the wing of it at an angle that's going to hurt tomorrow.

He doesn't draw the circle. There's nothing here that needs it. But he can feel the room the way he'd feel it if he did. The bed, three metres away, her breathing inside it. The kitchen, the fridge, the cupboard with the powder. The front door and the lavender on the cabinet beside it. Everything that matters, in one room and a hallway.

*Small,* he thinks. *Could reach all of it with one arm, nearly.*

The car park outside is orange under its one lamp. Upstairs, at four, the new dog barks twice and thinks better of it.

He sleeps in pieces.

---

He wakes because the light has changed.

Not much. The sky over the car park has gone from black to the dark grey of wet slate. The lamp is still on. His neck is wrong. His legs are in a shape legs shouldn't be. Somebody's balcony across the way has a towel on the rail, white, just starting to show.

Wednesday. He knows that before he knows anything else. Ono at eight.

He turns his head, slowly, because of the neck.

His sister is awake.

She's lying on her side in the bed, facing him, the blanket up to her chin, and she's watching him. He doesn't know how long she's been doing it. Her eyes are clear. Not the polite look. Not the stranger look, or the long, patient look she wears when she's talking to their father. It's a different one. He's not sure, for a second, that he remembers it.

"Nii-san," she says.

Her voice is rough with sleep. It's what she calls her brother. It's what she's calling him.

"You'll do your back in," she says. "In my chair."

"Probably," he says.

She keeps looking. She doesn't say anything else. She doesn't need to. He doesn't move, either. His neck is screaming. There's a kettle he could be putting on, a powder to measure, a rabbit notebook to fill in before eight.

*In a minute,* he thinks.

The grey comes up behind the towel on the balcony. The lamp goes thin and unnecessary. In the bed, his sister lets her eyes close, then opens them again to check he's still there.

He is. He shuts his own eyes, in her chair, by her window, and lets her watch.

---

## Chapter 6. The Night of a Hundred Demons

He isn't there when it happens.

That's the first thing he knows about the war. He's in Kawagoe that morning, in a primary school storeroom that smells of floor polish and old gym mats, cutting a small sad curse out of a pile of folded flags. By the time Ijichi's car brings him back through the gate, it's already happened, and everyone is telling it.

Suguru Geto came to the school. In the open. In daylight. With people behind him and a few things that weren't people.

He stood on the grounds and said it out loud. On the twenty-fourth of December, at sundown, he would release a thousand curses in Shinjuku and a thousand in Kyoto. A Night Parade of a Hundred Demons. He made it very clear who the curses were for. People who can't see them. Everyone else could choose a side.

Then he left. Nobody stopped him. Gojo was there for some of it. That part gets told the most.

Kusakabe hears it from four different people before he reaches the staff room. A first-year auxiliary by the gate, white in the face. A Grade 2 in the corridor, too loud, telling it like a film. The dispatch coordinator, headset round her neck, who says it in one breath and then answers her phone. Okabe, who says it as if it were a procedural problem, which it is, among other things.

Each version is a bit different. The thousand stays the same in all of them.

*A thousand,* he thinks. *They say it like a weather forecast.*

He remembers Geto as a name. Years back, a name on lists, the student everyone said would be the other half of Gojo. Then a village, and a number nobody liked to say, and the name came off the lists and went somewhere quieter, where people only said it in low voices in car parks.

Now it's back. Loud.

He puts his sword bag against the wall, sits at his desk in the corner, and looks at the radiator that doesn't work. It ticks.

*The twenty-fourth,* he thinks, and does the arithmetic without wanting to.

It's a Sunday.

---

The dispatch board goes up on Thursday. Normally it's a grid of small square handwriting, one job per box. This week there's one line across the whole of the twenty-fourth, in red.

*Shinjuku. All available. Kyoto: see Kyoto.*

Under it, a list of names. His is about halfway down. Nobody has written "Requested." Nobody has needed to.

He stands in front of it for a while. Other people come and stand next to him and read their names and go away again. Nobody talks much. A Grade 3 he doesn't know reads the board twice, then takes out her phone and types something with both thumbs, very fast, and puts it away without sending it.

On Friday night he rings Ono Haruka.

She answers on the second ring. "Ono."

"Kusakabe. Sunday. Can you do the afternoon?"

A pause. He can hear a television where she is, a game show, someone winning something.

"It's Christmas Eve," she says.

"Yeah."

Another pause. Not long. Long enough for him to understand she has a plan for her evening, and to understand that she's going to tell him the price instead of the plan.

"Double rate after twelve," she says. "Agency rule. Holidays."

"Fine."

"Until when?"

He thinks about it. *Sundown, and then however long a thousand takes.*

"Overnight," he says. "Maybe."

"Maybe," she says, as if she's writing it on her hand. "I'll tell the night girl to stay till I come. What do I tell your sister?"

"Work."

"She'll ask."

"Tell her work."

"Fine," Ono says, and hangs up. He stands in his kitchen with the phone. He has said the word "Fine" to a cost again, without doing the sum.

*Some habits you keep because they work,* he thinks. *Some you keep because you're tired.*

---

Sunday he goes to his sister's anyway, in the morning, early, before anyone is expecting him.

The night carer opens the door with her coat already on. Lavender. The window chair. His sister is awake and watching the car park, where a man is trying to fit a Christmas tree into the back of a very small car.

"You're early," she says.

She knows him. He takes that and doesn't look at it too hard.

"Yeah."

"It's not afternoon."

"Got work later."

"On a Sunday?"

"Yeah."

She thinks about that. Her right hand moves a little on the arm of the chair, the way it does when she's following something.

"You're always working," she says. Not a complaint. A fact about him, like the weather.

He makes the tea. Three, out. She drinks it with the powder in, slowly, and makes a face at the texture, and drinks it anyway. Outside, the man gets the tree in by putting it through the passenger window. The top sticks out into the street.

She laughs. It surprises both of them.

He stays an hour and a half. At ten past eleven he stands up, and she's already looking at the window again, and that's easier. He says, "See you." She says, "Mm."

At the door the lavender gets him on the way out, the way it always does.

On the train he checks his pocket. The coin is there. It's always there. Today he just wants to know it hasn't moved.

---

Shinjuku at four in the afternoon on Christmas Eve is the brightest place he's ever been asked to die in.

That's how he thinks it, and then he tells himself not to be dramatic, and then he looks around again and thinks it anyway.

The illuminations are already on, though the sky isn't dark yet. Blue lights strung through every bare tree along the avenue. White stars on wires across the side streets. A giant screen on a building showing a perfume advert, a woman walking slowly through snow that isn't real. Shop speakers playing a song about a white Christmas, a song about a lonely Christmas, a song about bells. Couples holding hands. People queuing out of a fried chicken place in a line that goes round the corner. Office workers carrying white cake boxes by the string, very carefully, level, like something that might spill.

Nobody here knows.

The higher-ups have done what they could. Some streets are closed for "gas works." A few blocks have been emptied with police tape and nervous constables. But you can't empty Shinjuku on Christmas Eve. Nobody can. There are too many people, and most of them can't see what's coming. There's no form that tells a city of ten million to go home early because of something it can't see.

His sector is a grid of side streets east of the station. Bars. A pachinko parlour. A ramen shop with a paper lantern. Narrow alleys between the buildings, full of bins and air-conditioning units and pipes.

The auxiliary who briefs him is young, younger than Ijichi, with a laminated map in a plastic sleeve that keeps fogging when she breathes on it. She points at the map. He nods. She points again. He nods again.

"Grade 2s there and there," she says. "Two Grade 3s on the corner. If anything special-grade turns up, don't engage. Call it in."

"Who comes?"

She opens her mouth. Closes it.

"Someone," she says.

"Okay."

"Gojo-san is somewhere west of the station," she says, as if it helps. It helps a little. It's like being told there's a fire station in the city. You don't know where. You know it exists.

She goes. He stands on his corner by the ramen shop with his sword bag over his shoulder. The lantern sways. The air smells of pork broth and cold exhaust. Somebody's phone is playing a ringtone that is also, for some reason, a Christmas song.

He looks at his hands. They're cold. It's December. Everybody's hands are cold. Nobody would notice. He puts them in his coat pockets, and his right hand finds the coin without being asked.

*I want to be on my sofa,* he thinks, *watching a bad drama. A doctor one. Where the surgeon is also somehow a detective.*

He'd watch the whole series. He'd watch the one after it. He wouldn't even complain about the ending.

The sun goes down behind the buildings at about half past four. There's no moment you could point to. The blue in the sky just gets thinner, and the blue in the trees gets brighter, until the trees are the only blue left.

Then the screaming starts.

---

It starts somewhere else first. Two streets away, maybe three. Not one scream. A lot of them, layered, the way traffic noise is layered, so you can't pick out a single voice.

Then the neon flickers. All of it at once, along the whole street, the way a light flickers in an old flat when someone runs the microwave. It comes back. It flickers again.

He feels the first ones before he sees them.

They come out of the alleys. Out of the gaps between buildings, where the bins are, where the pipes run down the walls. They come the way water comes up through a drain in a storm: not from anywhere, just up, all at once. Things with too many legs and things with none. A thing like a heap of wet coats. A thing with a long white neck and nothing at the top of it. They pour into the street under the Christmas lights and the lights shine on them and they don't care.

Most of the people on the pavement can't see them. That's the worst part. They see something. A man's hat blows off when there's no wind. A shop window cracks top to bottom. A woman stops and puts her hand on her chest as if she's forgotten something important. Then a car alarm goes, and another, and the crowd starts to move without knowing why, which is how crowds move.

He takes the sword out of the bag. He doesn't remember unzipping it.

He draws the circle. Or the circle draws itself. After this many years he isn't sure which comes first.

---

There's a family coming towards him down the pavement.

He notices them because they're the only people walking at a normal speed. A father with a little girl on his shoulders. A mother beside them with a cake box held level by the string. They're talking about something. The girl is pointing at the blue trees. They haven't understood yet that they're supposed to be frightened.

Behind them, out of the alley by the pachinko parlour, something unfolds.

It's long. It comes out of the gap between the buildings like a tape measure coming out of its case, flat and fast. At the front of it there's a mouth. The mouth is open, and it's level with the little girl's back.

He's already moving.

That's the strange part, every time. He's already moving, and the thought is still arriving. The thought is something like *that's a family* or *that's not my sector* or *wait*, and it never gets to the end. His feet have gone. His weight has gone. The edge of the circle has run ahead of him down the pavement because he's carried it there, and the thing crosses into it, and his hands answer.

One cut. A sound like wet cardboard tearing. Something cold sprays across his cheek.

The long thing falls apart in the air and is grey smoke before it reaches the ground.

The father has turned round. He's looking straight at Kusakabe. A man in a dark coat in the middle of the pavement, holding a sword, breathing hard. Behind him, nothing. The father can't see what was there. He can only see what's here.

He grips his daughter's legs and runs.

The mother runs with him. She drops the cake box. It lands on its corner, tips, and sits there on the pavement under the blue trees, the string still in a neat loop on top.

They don't look back. Not once. He watches them all the way to the end of the street, and they don't.

*Good,* he thinks. *Keep going. Don't look at the strange man with the sword.*

His heart is going like a hand slapping a table.

He picks up the cake box, because it's in the way, and puts it on the windowsill of the ramen shop, under the lantern. Then he turns round.

There are more coming out of the alley.

---

After that, the night goes methodical.

That's the only word for it. He's done this enough times to know there are two kinds of fight. There's the kind with a story, where something happens and you have to think. And there's this kind, where you don't think at all, because there's no time and no point. Curse, blade, next. Curse, blade, next. A thing comes into the circle. His hands answer. It goes. Another comes. He doesn't decide anything. He just stays where he is, and the street keeps sending things.

He counts, at first. Out of habit. He gets to fourteen.

Then he stops counting, because counting means thinking about how many are left, and nobody knows that.

The Christmas music keeps going. Nobody's turned it off. The shop speakers just carry on into the dark, a song about bells, a song about a lonely Christmas, a song about bells again. He fights through the whole playlist three times. By the third time he knows where the key changes are.

*If I die tonight,* he thinks, *the last thing I'll have heard is a woman asking for nothing but me for Christmas. I'd like to state for the record I didn't choose this.*

Around seven, a curse with a face like a crumpled paper bag gets through the edge of the circle on his left, where his attention has thinned, and something drags across his forearm. Not deep. It stings the way paper cuts sting, out of proportion. His sleeve goes dark and warm and then cold.

He doesn't look at it. Looking at it would be stopping, and stopping is how they get you.

The radio on his collar talks in pieces. Static. A voice. Static.

*"...Kyoto's the same. Same numbers..."*

*"...anyone near the south exit, we need..."*

*"...Gojo-san's gone. Gone where? He just went..."*

He hears that one and keeps cutting. The fire station has left the city. There's nothing to do with that information, so he does nothing with it.

At some point a Grade 2 he half knows, a woman with short hair and a long spear, ends up back to back with him for a while at the corner. She's crying. Not loudly. Just steadily, tears running down her face while she fights, perfectly well, her spear going in and out like a sewing machine. Neither of them mentions it. After twenty minutes the street thins, and she nods to him and goes north, still crying, to wherever she's needed. He never learns her name.

Around ten there's a man in a doorway.

A salaryman, fifties, tie gone, a paper Santa hat from some office party sitting crooked on his head. He's too drunk to run and too drunk to be frightened, and he can't see a thing. He sits in the doorway of a closed bar with his knees up, watching Kusakabe cut at empty air, with the patient interest of a man watching street theatre.

Something with a lot of fingers comes up the street towards the doorway. Kusakabe steps across in front of it. One cut. Smoke.

The man claps. Slowly. Three times.

"Very good," he says. "Very good. Is it for the tourists?"

"Yeah."

The man nods, satisfied. He digs in his coat and holds out a mandarin.

Kusakabe looks at it. There's another thing coming out of the alley behind him, and his circle is already turning towards it.

He takes the mandarin and puts it in his pocket, next to the coin, and turns round.

*Paid in fruit,* he thinks. *Better rate than usual.*

The neon on the pachinko parlour goes out for good at about nine. The ramen lantern stays lit. The cake box stays on the windowsill.

---

It goes on like that for a long time.

Then, sometime after midnight, it changes.

He can't say how. The curses don't stop. But they stop coming *at* anything. Before, there was a direction to them, a push, like a tide. Now they just wander. They come out of the alleys and stand around under the lights like people who've forgotten why they came into a room. They're easier. They're also sadder, which he doesn't want to notice, so he doesn't.

The radio gets quieter. Fewer voices. Longer silences.

His forearm has stopped bleeding. His shoulders have gone past aching into a sort of humming. His left heel, the shoe he still hasn't had fixed, has finally given up and flaps a little every time he steps.

*Grade 1 sorcerer,* he thinks. *Saving Shinjuku with half a shoe.*

At around three he realises he hasn't felt anything cross the circle for twenty minutes.

He stands there anyway. Another twenty. Then he lets the circle go, slowly, the way you let go of a heavy door, in case it swings back.

It doesn't.

---

The sky does what it does in December. It goes from black to a sort of dark grey that's hardly different from black, and stays like that for a long time, and then suddenly it's morning and you missed the change.

The Christmas lights are still on. In daylight they look cheap and tired, like someone still wearing a party hat on the train home.

He sits down on the kerb.

He doesn't decide to. His legs make the decision for him, and he lets them, because they've earned it. The kerb is very cold. His trousers are wet from something he'd rather not think about. The sleeve of his coat is stiff and brown from the elbow to the wrist.

The street is a mess. A shop window lies in pieces across the pavement, glittering. A bicycle is bent into a shape bicycles shouldn't make. Someone has left a single high-heeled shoe in the middle of the road, standing upright, as if its owner just stepped out of it and kept going. One of the two vending machines on the corner has been crushed flat against the wall. The other is fine. Lit up, humming, every button glowing.

He looks at it for a while.

Then he gets up again, which is worse than sitting down was, and digs in his pocket for coins, and buys a can of hot coffee. The can drops into the tray with a loud clunk that echoes off the empty buildings.

He sits back down on the kerb with it. He holds it in both hands first, without opening it, just for the heat.

Then he opens it and drinks.

It's sweet and thin and tastes mostly of the can. It's the best thing he's had in his mouth all year.

*The machine survived the Night Parade of a Hundred Demons,* he thinks. *Someone should give the machine a medal.*

In his pocket, his hand finds the mandarin. He'd forgotten it. He peels it on the kerb, the peel coming away in one long cold curl, and eats it a segment at a time. It's sour. He eats all of it anyway, and puts the peel in his other pocket, because there's nowhere else to put it and the street is untidy enough.

---

People start to come back into the street as the light grows. Auxiliaries with clipboards. Cleaners, the real kind and the other kind. Two constables in reflective jackets, who look at him on the kerb, decide he's somebody else's job, and walk on.

A Grade 2 he knows by sight comes down the street from the station end. Tanaka, or Tanabe. Thirties, broad, a split lip, his jacket missing a sleeve. He's walking the way people walk when they've just heard something and need to tell it to someone. He stops by the kerb.

"You heard?" he says.

"No."

"The school," Tanabe says. "This lot was a distraction. All of it. Shinjuku, Kyoto. Geto wasn't here at all. He went to the school."

Kusakabe holds the can.

"For the Okkotsu kid," Tanabe says. "The first-year. The one with the special grade. He wanted the curse that follows him around. The girl."

Kusakabe has seen Okkotsu. A thin, quiet boy in the corridors with a sword bag and a way of apologising with his shoulders. And something behind him, sometimes, that the air didn't like. He kept his distance. Most people did.

"The other first-years were there," Tanabe says. "The Zen'in girl. Inumaki. The panda. Geto went through them. They're hurt. Bad, I heard. Alive."

He waits for Kusakabe to say something. Kusakabe doesn't.

"And the kid beat him," Tanabe says. "Okkotsu. Sixteen years old. Beat Suguru Geto. Then Gojo got there and finished it." He shakes his head, and laughs, the way people laugh when something is too big. "That's what they're saying. Okkotsu and Gojo. The heroes of the night."

"Okay," Kusakabe says.

Tanabe looks at him. Looks at the sleeve. Looks at the street, the glass, the shoe in the road, the crushed machine.

"Long one," Tanabe says.

"Yeah."

Tanabe nods and goes on down the street, to tell it to the next person.

Kusakabe drinks his coffee.

He thinks about the panda he's seen from the staff-room window, crossing the yard on its hind legs like a large man in a costume nobody is allowed to mention. He thinks about the Inumaki boy, scarf over his mouth. The girl with the sharp voice. First-years. Not his. He knows them by sight and they don't know him at all.

*Alive,* he thinks. He holds onto that part.

He thinks about Okkotsu, sixteen, in a courtyard he knows, against a man who said a thousand curses the way other people say good morning.

And Gojo, who was somewhere west of the station until he wasn't.

*Heroes of the night,* he thinks. *Fair enough.*

He doesn't feel cheated. He checks, the way you check a pocket, and there's nothing there. It was their night. It was always going to be. Somebody has to be the story. Somebody else has to stand on a corner by a ramen shop in a bad shoe and make sure the story has a city left to be told in.

He doesn't think it in those words. He thinks something more like: *Fine.* And: *My arm hurts.*

---

His phone has been on silent all night. He takes it out.

Two messages. Both from Ono Haruka.

The first is from 18:40. *She asked where you were. Told her work. She said "he's always working."*

The second is from 06:12. *Overnight done. She slept. I've signed the sheet for double. Merry Christmas.*

He reads that last part twice. He doesn't think Ono says Merry Christmas to anyone. He thinks she typed it because it was on the sheet as the date, and her thumb went on without her.

He types: *Thanks.*

He looks at it. Then he sends it, which is more than he usually does.

The coffee's gone. He holds the empty can for a while. It's still a little warm in the bottom.

The auxiliary with the fogging map comes past. She's lost the map. She has a smear of something across her forehead and she looks about twelve. She sees him, checks his face, checks his name off a list in her head, and keeps walking.

That's the debrief.

*Nobody's going to file this,* he thinks. *Grade 1, east of the station, held the corner. Nothing to add.*

He looks at the ramen shop. The lantern has finally gone out. The cake box is still on the windowsill, a little damp, the string still in its neat loop.

He doesn't know where the family went. Home, he hopes. Somewhere with the lights on. A little girl telling her mother about the blue trees and not about anything else, because there wasn't anything else. Not for her. He made sure of that.

They'll never know there was a man.

*She'll remember the blue trees,* he thinks. *Decent trade for a sleeve.*

---

He crushes the can in one hand. It folds easily, thin metal, a small dry crunch.

He drops it in the bin by the surviving vending machine. He puts the sword back in its bag. He stands, slowly, his back clicking in two places. He walks to the station with his heel flapping, past the blue trees still lit in the daylight, past people starting to come out for Christmas morning who don't look at him at all.

The train is nearly empty. He sits in the corner seat with his sword bag between his knees and his head against the glass and doesn't sleep, quite.

At home he takes off his coat, looks at the sleeve, and puts the whole coat in a plastic bag to deal with another year. He drinks a glass of water standing at the sink. He doesn't make coffee. He doesn't put the television on.

He lies down on the bed, on top of the covers, in his clothes, and looks at the ceiling for about four seconds.

Then he sleeps.

He sleeps for fourteen hours. Through Christmas morning. Through Christmas lunch, wherever people are having it. Through the afternoon and the short winter light and the dark coming back down. Somewhere in that time the phone buzzes once and he doesn't hear it. Somewhere the upstairs neighbour runs a bath.

When he wakes, it's night again, and Christmas is nearly over, and nobody has thanked him for anything.

He lies there and thinks about that, briefly. Then he turns over.

Nobody needs to.

---

# Part Two: The Year Between

## Chapter 7. Homeroom

Yaga tells him on the last Friday of March, in the principal's office, without looking up from a felt bear.

"Second-years," Yaga says. "From April. Homeroom."

Kusakabe stands just inside the door. He hasn't been asked to sit, and he wouldn't anyway. Sitting turns a sentence into a meeting.

The bear has one ear. Yaga is sewing on the other one with a curved needle and very small stitches, the way a surgeon would, if surgeons were the size of a vending machine and wore sunglasses indoors.

"Gojo has the first-years," Yaga says. "Okkotsu is abroad for the year. That leaves three."

"I know who it leaves."

"Then you know them."

He does, by sight. Everybody does. Last year they were the first-years he walked past on the way to the car yard. The girl with the glasses and the long staff, who trained like the staff owed her money. The panda. The quiet boy with his collar zipped up over his mouth, who never said anything you could use.

"I don't teach," Kusakabe says.

"You've taught for six years."

"Odd jobs. Fill-ins."

"Now it's one job." Yaga bites the thread. "They need someone who turns up."

*I don't turn up. I arrive, eventually. It's different.*

He doesn't say it. He stands there and his hand goes into his jacket pocket on its own and finds nothing, and checks the nothing anyway.

Yaga turns the bear round to look at its face. Satisfied, or not. It's hard to tell with Yaga. He sets it on the desk, on a pile of what looks like budget papers, where it sits looking at Kusakabe with its two black buttons.

"Monday the ninth," Yaga says. "Nine o'clock."

"Fine."

That's all. Yaga picks up another piece of felt. Kusakabe lets himself out.

In the corridor he stands for a moment with his hands in his pockets.

*Six years of avoiding a class of my own. Ended by a bear.*

---

Monday the ninth, he's eleven minutes late.

He doesn't plan it. He leaves at the usual time and walks the usual way, and somewhere behind the shrine the morning just gets longer than it should. It's a talent. Some people can't be late if they try.

The training yard is at the back of the school, down a slope from the main buildings. A long flat rectangle of packed earth, grass worn away to nothing in the middle and left alone at the edges. Stone steps lead down to it from the path. There's a storehouse at one end with its doors open, and a line of cherry trees at the other, dropping the last of their petals onto everything.

His class is down there. More or less.

Maki Zen'in is in the middle of the yard, swinging a staff in long flat arcs, her glasses pushed up hard on her nose, her ponytail whipping round a half-second after her head does. She's been at it a while. There's sweat on her neck and a scuff in the earth around her where her feet have been turning.

Panda is lying on the grass at the edge, on his back, paws folded on his stomach, looking at the sky. He is very large and very round and very much a panda. In the sun he looks like something that would be in a brochure.

Toge Inumaki is sitting on the bottom step with his knees up and his collar zipped to the bridge of his nose. He's watching a petal on his shoe.

"Salmon," he says, to nobody.

Kusakabe comes down the steps.

"You're late," Maki says. She doesn't stop swinging.

"Yeah."

"Why?"

"Traffic."

The staff stops. She looks at him over her glasses.

There's no traffic between his flat and the school. There's a back road with nothing on it but a man who sweeps leaves, and a path through the trees that ends at the side gate. She's seen him come up that path. Everybody has. He's been coming up it at twenty past nine for six years.

She knows he walked. He knows she knows. For a second he thinks she's going to say it.

She doesn't. She snorts, once, through her nose, and starts swinging again.

That's the first day of the arrangement. He doesn't explain himself. They don't push. Nobody signs anything.

"Morning, sensei," Panda says, from the grass, without lifting his head.

"Morning."

Inumaki raises one hand, palm out, and lowers it again.

Kusakabe stands at the bottom of the steps and looks at the three of them, and they look back at him, or at least two of them do. Maki's still swinging.

He realises he doesn't have a plan.

He's had six weeks to make one. He had a notebook out for it twice. Both times he wrote the date at the top and nothing under it.

*Homeroom teacher,* he thinks. *Eleven minutes in charge of three people and I've already lied to one of them.*

"Right," he says. "Carry on."

Panda turns his head on the grass to look at him. "Carry on doing what?"

"What you were doing."

"I was lying here."

"Then you're doing well."

Panda considers this, and seems to find it fair, and goes back to looking at the sky.

Kusakabe sits on the third step from the bottom, two steps above Inumaki, in the strip of shade from the storehouse roof, and watches Maki work.

---

It goes like that for a few days.

He's not sure what the others expected. Something, probably. Last year they had Yaga in the mornings and Gojo when Gojo felt like it, and between them that's a lot of man. A lot of noise and size and opinion. Now they have him, sitting on a step.

He watches.

That's most of it, the first week. He watches Maki with the staff, and then with a pair of short batons, and then with something long and curved from the storehouse that he's fairly sure isn't on the school's inventory. She goes through weapons the way other people go through pens. Everything she picks up, she learns. Everything she learns, she drills until it stops being interesting, and then she drills it some more, to punish it.

She has almost no cursed energy. He knows that much without anyone telling him. You can feel it, or rather you can feel the lack of it, a kind of flatness around her, like a room with the heating off. She can't even see curses without the glasses. Everything she does, she does with her body and with the tools she carries. Nothing handed to her. Nothing she was born with that anyone wanted.

He doesn't think about that for long. It's too close to something.

On the Wednesday, he sees it.

She comes in off a feint with the staff, a good feint, low and fast, and just before the real strike her back heel comes up off the ground. Not much. A finger's width. It's there for maybe a tenth of a second, and then her weight goes forward and the staff comes round.

He watches her do it four more times, to be sure.

"Your back heel lifts before you strike," he says. "They'll see it coming."

She stops. She looks at her feet, then at him.

She grunts.

The next time she comes in, the heel stays down. And the time after. The strike comes from nowhere now, out of a body that was standing still a moment ago.

She doesn't say thank you. He doesn't wait for it. He sits back on the step.

*That's a day,* he thinks. *One sentence. Paid in full.*

---

There's paperwork, too. There's always paperwork.

On the Thursday, Okabe finds him in the staff room and puts a blue binder on his desk. Okabe is in a brown cardigan today, which is new, or new to Kusakabe. It doesn't seem to have made him happier.

"Homeroom register," Okabe says. "Attendance, daily. Mission eligibility, monthly. Progress notes, end of each term. A paragraph per student is sufficient."

"A paragraph."

"Sufficient," Okabe says, "is the minimum."

Kusakabe opens the binder. Four pages, one per student, each with a photograph clipped to the corner. Maki, glaring at the camera as if it had said something. Panda, with what he would swear is a smile. Inumaki, collar up, eyes only.

The fourth page is Okkotsu Yuta. Thin face, dark hair, a look of someone apologising for being photographed. Across the top, in red stamp: *Overseas assignment. Not resident.*

He's heard the name, of course. Everybody has, since December. The boy who stood in the middle of all that and came out the other side. The hero of the night, people say, in the same breath as Gojo. Now he's somewhere far away doing something nobody will tell Kusakabe about.

*Fine by me. One less person to be late for.*

"The attendance for this week was due yesterday," Okabe says.

"It's Thursday."

"Yesterday was Wednesday."

Kusakabe looks at him. Okabe looks back. Neither of them is going to win this, and both of them know it, and somehow that doesn't stop either of them.

"Tomorrow," Kusakabe says.

Okabe makes a small note on his clipboard. Then he goes, and Kusakabe sits with the binder open in front of him, looking at three faces that are all, in their different ways, looking at him.

He fills in the attendance. Present, present, present. It takes forty seconds. He doesn't know why he didn't do it yesterday. He never knows.

---

Inumaki says "Salmon" a lot.

That's the second thing Kusakabe learns. The first is that the boy almost never says anything else. Rice-ball fillings. That's his whole vocabulary, out loud. Salmon. Bonito flakes. Kelp. Tuna mayo. It's not a joke, or not only one. His technique is in his voice. The marks on his face, when the collar slips, say so: a little curl at each corner of the mouth like a seal on a letter. If he tells a person to stop, they stop. If he tells them something worse, that happens too. So he says salmon, and kelp, and the words can't hurt anyone.

Panda translates, sometimes, unasked.

"Kelp is hello," Panda says, on the Friday. "Mostly. Salmon is yes. Or fine. Or that's good. Or he's agreeing with something nobody said out loud."

"Bonito flakes?"

"No. Or don't. Or that's a bad idea." Panda thinks. "Or he wants a rice ball."

Inumaki, on the step, gives Panda a long look over his collar.

"Bonito flakes," he says.

"See," Panda says. "That one's no."

Kusakabe nods.

He finds he doesn't need much translating, in the end. Inumaki's words are like weather. You don't need the forecast. You can look out of the window. Salmon on a bright morning means the morning is bright. Salmon to a falling petal means the petal's doing well. The boy says it to nobody, often, the way some people hum.

On the Friday afternoon, Kusakabe comes back from the vending machine with a can of coffee and sits on his step. Inumaki is two steps down. He doesn't turn round.

"Kelp," he says.

"Afternoon," Kusakabe says.

That's the whole conversation. It's one of the better ones he's had this year.

---

The second Monday it rains, so they have to use the classroom.

It's on the first floor of the old wooden building, at the end, with windows down one side that look out on wet trees. Four desks in a row. Somebody has pushed the fourth one against the wall, chair tucked in, nothing on it. Okkotsu's, presumably. Nobody sits at it and nobody mentions it.

Kusakabe stands at the front. There's a teacher's desk, and a blackboard with the ghost of last year's writing on it, and a timetable pinned to the wall that says *Mon. 1st period: Theory (Homeroom)*.

*Theory,* he thinks. *I have one theory. It's that I'd rather be outside.*

He has a textbook, at least. It's the one the school issues, blue cover, the grading system for curses laid out in tables. He opens it at the chapter on assessment in the field and reads out the first paragraph. He reads it slowly, so it takes longer.

Panda raises a paw.

"We did this last year."

"Do it again."

"We did it again in February."

Kusakabe closes the book.

The rain gets heavier. It runs down the windows in sheets, and the room goes dim, and for a while nobody says anything at all. It's not uncomfortable. It's the most restful lesson he's ever taught. He suspects that isn't the goal.

"How'd you get Grade 1?" Maki says.

She's sitting back with her arms folded, chair on two legs. She's not looking at him. She's looking at the window, at the rain, as if the question just happened to fall out of her.

"Slowly," he says.

"No technique."

"No."

"Nothing from a clan."

"No."

"So how?"

He thinks about how to answer that. There's a long answer. It has years in it, and the same drill done ten thousand times in an empty dojo, and a lot of nights he doesn't want to describe to a sixteen-year-old in a wet classroom.

"Practice," he says.

She looks at him then. Properly. Through the glasses, with her chin down, the way you'd look at a door to see if it's locked.

"That's not an answer."

"It's the only one there is."

She lets the chair come down onto four legs with a bang. She doesn't say anything else. But he sees her file it, the way she files a new weapon, and he knows, with a small sinking feeling, that it's going to come back.

Inumaki, at the end of the row, looks from one of them to the other.

"Tuna mayo," he says, thoughtfully.

"What's that one?" Kusakabe asks Panda.

Panda shrugs. "Depends."

---

Panda finds him on the grass in the second week.

It's a Tuesday. Maki has gone to the storehouse to argue with a chain. Inumaki is asleep on the top step with his head on his bag, which is either a skill or a technique. Kusakabe has come down off his step for once, because the shade has moved and he couldn't be bothered to move with it. He's sitting on the slope at the edge of the yard with his legs out in front of him.

Panda drops down beside him. The ground shakes slightly. Panda lies back with a long contented sound, like a sofa being sat on.

They look at the sky. There's a single cloud in it, going nowhere.

"Sensei."

"Mm."

"You always sit with the gate in front of you."

Kusakabe doesn't answer.

"The steps. The bench by the office. The end of the table in the staff room." Panda says it lightly, like a man listing birds. "Gate in front. Wall behind."

*Of course the panda notices. The panda has nothing to do all day but notice.*

"Habit," Kusakabe says.

"Okay."

That's the thing about Panda, he's learning. Panda says *okay* and means it, and also doesn't, both at once, and leaves you to decide which. He's put together out of cloth and cursed energy and a lot of patience, and he's made by the same pair of hands that made a thing Kusakabe doesn't think about at school. Not ever. Kusakabe looks at the cloud instead.

"Sensei. You ever think about quitting?"

"Every day."

"Why don't you?"

He means to say something flat. Something that closes the door. Instead, for some reason, he actually thinks about it. Out on the grass, in the sun, with the panda breathing beside him like the sea.

Money, mostly. His sister. The carer's invoice, higher now. The debt, which isn't the kind of thing you can pay from a different job. The fact that he's good at exactly one thing, and the thing is this.

"Haven't found anything else that pays like this," he says, "and lets me complain this much."

Panda laughs. It's a big, warm sound, the kind that makes people at the far end of a train carriage look round and smile.

Kusakabe doesn't laugh.

It wasn't entirely a joke. He thinks Panda knows that. Panda turns his head on the grass and looks at him for a moment, the round black eyes giving nothing away, and then turns back to the cloud.

"Same, kind of," Panda says.

"You don't get paid."

"No. But I complain a lot."

Up on the top step, without waking, Inumaki says, "Salmon."

---

Maki starts asking on the Wednesday of the second week.

"Spar with me."

He's on his step. She's standing at the bottom of it with the staff upright beside her like a flagpole.

"No."

"Why not?"

"Busy."

She looks at the can of coffee in his hand, and the step, and the general shape of his afternoon. She doesn't say anything. She doesn't need to. She goes back to the middle of the yard and hits the air very hard for twenty minutes.

On the Thursday she asks again. "Properly, this time. Not watching. Fighting."

"Later."

"When's later?"

"Not now."

On the Friday morning she doesn't ask. She just watches him come down the steps, eleven minutes late again. Her eyes follow him all the way to his step, and he can feel them, like a draught on the back of the neck.

He knows what she wants. It isn't complicated. She's spent her whole life being told what she can't do by people who were born with more than her. Now someone's put a man in front of her who has no technique and a Grade 1 anyway. She wants to see it. She wants to stand inside it and find out how it's done, so she can take it apart and use the pieces.

He understands that. He'd want it too.

He still isn't going to do it.

There's no half version of what he does. That's the problem. He could swing a practice sword at her, slow and polite, and she'd know in a second it was a lie, and she'd hate it more than this. And the other thing, the real thing, the circle, doesn't know the difference between a student and a curse. It doesn't do practice. It answers. He's not putting her inside it to find out how hard.

*And she'd see,* he thinks. *That there's nothing else. Just the circle and a lot of years. No secret. Nothing to take apart.*

He doesn't know which of those two reasons is the real one. He doesn't look too hard.

On the Friday afternoon she asks the last time.

It's late, the light going long across the yard. Panda is sitting up for once, doing something careful with a bandage on his paw. Inumaki is on the bottom step, eating a rice ball, which feels like it might be a statement. Maki has been working with a bokken all afternoon, and she walks over now and stops in front of the steps, and holds it out to him handle first.

"Seriously," she says. "Not one sentence from a step. Get down here."

"No."

"Why?"

"Don't want to."

The yard goes quiet. Panda's paws stop on the bandage.

Maki lowers the bokken. She looks at him the way she looks at a weapon she's decided isn't worth learning.

"You know what you are?" she says. Not loud. She doesn't need loud. "You're lazy. That's all. Everybody says you're good, and maybe you are, I don't know, because nobody's ever seen you try. Grade 1. Sitting on a step with a can of coffee. That's all you'll ever be."

"Maki," Panda says.

"Bonito flakes," says Inumaki, quietly, through rice.

She doesn't look at either of them. She's looking at Kusakabe, waiting for something. For him to get angry. For him to stand up. For a reason.

He looks back at her.

"Probably," he says.

Something closes in her face. She turns round and walks back into the yard and starts working the bokken again, harder than before, every strike landing on nothing with a sound like a door slammed in another building.

Panda looks at him. Kusakabe drinks his coffee. It's cold. He drinks it anyway.

*That's fine,* he thinks. *She's allowed. She's right about the step.*

It's fine. He keeps telling himself that it's fine, and his face keeps agreeing, and that's the important part. The face is the part that's on show.

Underneath, though, something has gone in. Small and sharp. Not deep. Just there, in a place he can't quite reach to pull it out.

He's been called worse. By clan men, by officials, by curses, if you count the noises they make. None of it stuck, because none of it was from anyone who'd watched him. She's watched him for two weeks. She's taken one of his sentences and put it in her feet. And she's looked at all of it and decided *lazy*.

*Not a bad word for it,* he thinks. *From the outside.*
