He stays on the step until the end of the day, because getting up and leaving now would be its own sentence, and he doesn't want to say it.

---

That night he does the register.

He does it at the kitchen table, which he never does. The binder open, a pen, the lamp on. Present, present, present, for the Friday. He signs the bottom.

Then he turns to Maki's page and looks at the space for progress notes. End of term, Okabe said. A paragraph is sufficient. It's April. There's no need to write anything for months.

He writes: *Back heel. Fixed.*

He looks at it. It isn't a paragraph. It isn't sufficient. It's two words, and one of them is a part of a foot.

*Lazy,* he thinks, and finds the word is still there, sitting where she put it. He turns it over. He doesn't argue with it. It's hard to argue with something you've been practising for years.

He doesn't put the TV on. He sits at the table for a while with his hands flat on either side of the binder, looking at the photograph of a girl glaring at a camera.

He doesn't feel guilty, exactly. He doesn't feel much of anything he could name. Just the small sharp thing, not going anywhere.

At eleven he closes the binder and goes to bed and lies there, and the water stain in the corner is too dark to see, which is something.

---

Monday he's late.

Nine minutes, this time. It's an improvement nobody will notice. He comes down the path from the trees and through the side gate and down the stone steps, and he doesn't know, until he's on them, what he's going to find at the bottom.

Maki is in the middle of the yard with the staff. Panda is on the grass. Inumaki is on the bottom step, collar up, watching a beetle cross the stone.

"You're late," Maki says, without stopping.

"Yeah."

"Why?"

A beat. The staff keeps going round.

"Traffic," he says.

She snorts. Once, through her nose.

That's all. She doesn't look at him. She doesn't look away from him either. She just keeps working, and the arrangement, which he'd half thought was over, turns out to be still standing, a little bent, a little scuffed, like everything else in this yard.

He doesn't know what that means. He decides not to ask.

He goes to his step. The third from the bottom, in the shade. The gate in front of him. The wall behind.

"Kelp," says Inumaki, without turning round.

"Morning."

---

Late in the afternoon, the light comes in low under the cherry trees and lays itself across the yard in long gold strips.

Maki and Panda are sparring. Not hard, not soft. Panda is enormous and patient and much faster than anything that shape has a right to be, and Maki goes at him with the staff like a woman cutting a path through a hedge. Inumaki is down off the step for once, circling them, darting in when there's an opening, slapping Panda on the back and dancing away. Panda roars, mostly for show. Maki laughs, a short, surprised sound, as if it got out without permission.

Kusakabe watches from the step.

Maki comes in off a feint. Low, fast. The back heel stays down.

The staff catches Panda square in the stomach, and he sits down hard on the grass with a sound like a beanbag dropped from a height. Inumaki says "Salmon!" very loudly, delighted, to all of them at once.

A few petals come down off the trees and land in the dust and stay there.

Kusakabe sits with his elbows on his knees and a can of coffee going warm between his hands.

For about the length of a breath, he doesn't want to be anywhere else. Not his sofa. Not a hot spring. Not some other job in some other city where nobody's ever heard of him.

Just here. This step. These three.

Then Maki looks up at him, sees him watching, and glares, and the moment goes.

He drinks his coffee.

*Still lazy,* he thinks. *Still here, though.*

---

## Chapter 8. Rice Ball Words

The job comes in on a Thursday in the middle of May, on a sheet of paper with two names on it.

*Kusakabe A. (Grade 1, supervising). Inumaki T. (Semi-Grade 1, second year). Field experience.*

The dispatch coordinator hands it to him across her desk without looking up. Her headset is round her neck. She's eating a rice cracker very quietly, as if somebody might take it off her.

"Kita ward," she says. "Karaoke building by the station. Closed six weeks. Staff kept hearing singing from empty rooms. Grade 2, probably."

"Probably."

"It's always probably."

He reads the sheet again. The second name stays where it is.

"Why him?"

"Principal's list. Second-years need hours in the field with a supervisor. You're their supervisor." She takes another bite. "That's what homeroom means."

*Nobody told me homeroom meant anything. I thought it meant a room.*

"Car's at one," she says.

---

Inumaki is already in the car yard when he gets there, which is new. Kusakabe is used to arriving at things after they've started.

The boy is leaning on the black sedan with his hands in his pockets and his collar zipped up to the bridge of his nose. Uniform, school bag on one shoulder. He doesn't straighten up when he sees Kusakabe. He just lifts his chin a fraction.

"Kelp."

"Afternoon."

Ijichi gets out of the driver's side, bows to both of them, and opens the back door. Then he hesitates over whether to open the other back door as well, decides not to, half changes his mind, and gets back in.

They sit in the back, one each side, with the empty middle seat between them like a third passenger nobody wants to talk to.

The car pulls out. Ijichi drives through the trees and down the hill and into the city, and for the first ten minutes nobody says anything.

It's the best ten minutes Kusakabe has had all week.

He's spent a month now watching the second-years. He knows Maki's footwork better than his own. He knows Panda lies on the grass in a slightly different place each day, following the sun like a cat. He knows less about Inumaki than either of them, because there's less to know from the outside. The boy sits on the bottom step. He eats rice balls. He watches things. He says salmon, and sometimes kelp, and once in a while, if something's going wrong, bonito flakes, and that's the whole public record.

From the outside, Kusakabe thinks, they probably look much the same. Two people who don't say much, sitting as far apart as a car allows.

It isn't the same, though. He knows that much. Kusakabe doesn't talk because he doesn't want to. Inumaki doesn't talk because his words can make people do things. Tell someone to stop and they stop. Tell them to sleep and they sleep. Tell them something worse and it happens, all the way. So the boy has cut his language down to fillings. Words that can't hurt anyone. Kelp. Salmon. A whole personality, built out of a convenience-store fridge.

*One of us is being careful,* Kusakabe thinks. *The other one's just tired. Hard to tell which from the back seat.*

Ijichi clears his throat at a red light.

"The building's managed by a company," he says. "They've been told to stay out. The auxiliary on site has the keys. There's a cordon at the front." He pauses. "It's a small cordon."

"Fine."

Inumaki takes a small brown bottle out of his bag, unscrews the cap, and drinks a capful of something that smells like cherries and paint. He screws it shut and puts it back. Then he takes out a flat tin, opens it, and shows it to Kusakabe across the empty seat. Lozenges, wrapped in clear plastic, the colour of honey.

Kusakabe shakes his head.

Inumaki shrugs, takes one, tucks the tin away and pulls his collar down just far enough to put it in. The marks at the corners of his mouth show for a second, the two small curls. Then the collar goes back up.

"Throat," Kusakabe says. Not a question.

Inumaki nods.

"Every day?"

The boy thinks about it. He tilts one hand side to side.

"Tuna mayo."

*Depends.* Kusakabe nods. That's fair. Most things do.

---

The karaoke building is five storeys of tired orange tile, wedged between a shop that sells phone cases and a dentist. A sign on the roof spells out SING in letters as tall as a man. The G has been dead for a while. Below it, a banner says CLOSED FOR RENOVATION, and below that, a strip of yellow tape runs from a lamp post to a drainpipe, which is the cordon.

The auxiliary is a young man with a clipboard and a very new suit. He gives Kusakabe the keys and a floor plan.

"Singing," he says. "Mostly on four. Mostly at night. Sometimes now."

They stand on the pavement and listen.

At first there's just the street. A scooter. A woman calling a child. Then, very faint, from somewhere above, a voice comes down through the closed windows. Then another, under it. Then a lot of them. Not singing together. Each one singing something different, all of them slightly flat, all of them coming to the end of the same line and starting it again.

*Six weeks of that,* Kusakabe thinks. *I'd have closed it too.*

Inumaki looks up at the windows. Then he looks at Kusakabe.

"Stay in my reach," Kusakabe says. "Behind me, left. If I say go, go."

The boy nods once.

"Don't use it unless I tell you."

A slightly longer look over the collar. Then a nod.

Kusakabe unlocks the glass doors.

---

Inside, it smells of old smoke and carpet shampoo and the inside of a microphone. The reception counter is covered in a dust sheet. The lights are off. A rack of plastic tambourines stands by the lift with a sign saying PLEASE RETURN AFTER USE.

They take the stairs.

On the second floor, the corridor is narrow and very long, with doors down both sides. Each door has a small glass window at head height and a number. The rooms behind them are dark. In one, a television screen is on with nothing on it, a blue square glowing in the black.

The singing gets louder.

He lets the circle out on the third-floor landing, not far, just to the end of his sword and a little past. He feels the stairwell, the cool rail, the boy one step behind and to the left, breathing through his nose. He feels the building above them, not with the circle, which doesn't reach that far, but with the back of his neck. Something up there is big. Bigger than probably.

*It's always probably,* he thinks. *Until it isn't.*

Fourth floor.

The corridor is the same as the others, except every door is open.

The voices are coming out of the rooms. All of them. Twenty doors, twenty songs, none of them finished. A love song in a man's voice that cracks on the high note every time. A girl doing an idol song, a beat behind the music that isn't there. Somebody drunk, doing a ballad, very sincerely. It's like walking into the inside of a hangover.

He walks down the middle of the corridor with the sword still in its bag on his shoulder and his right hand resting on the zip.

At the far end, the corridor turns left. Past the corner, a door is shut. Light under it. Not blue. Yellow.

*That's not a television.*

Then the circle tells him two things at once.

The first is that something is coming down out of the ceiling behind them. Not fast yet. Heavy, and wet, and made of all the voices at once.

The second is that there's a man in the room with the light.

---

It goes the way these things go. Faster than he can tell it.

The ceiling tiles behind them sag and drop, and the curse comes down through the gap in a long grey pour, like dirty water through a broken gutter. It has no shape he can name. Mouths, mostly. Open ones. Some of them still singing. It hits the carpet and doesn't stop. It goes past them, low and quick, round the corner towards the yellow light.

Towards the man.

The door of that room opens. A man in a cardigan comes out with a tape measure in one hand and a phone in the other. He's frowning at the noise, which to him must be just a noise, or nothing at all. He can't see it. He's looking straight at it, and he can't see it.

The curse rears up in the corridor in front of him, all mouths.

It's seven metres away. The circle is less than two.

Kusakabe is already moving. He knows, the way you know a dropped glass is going to break, that he's not going to get there. Seven metres. Half a second too many. His feet know it. His hand on the zip knows it.

Beside him, the boy pulls his collar down.

"*Don't move.*"

It isn't loud. It's worse than loud. It goes down the corridor like a hand pressing down on everything, and everything stops.

The curse stops, reared up, all its mouths open and silent for the first time in six weeks. The man stops, mid-step, mid-frown, tape measure out.

Kusakabe doesn't stop. It wasn't meant for him, and he was already gone.

Four metres. The curse shudders. It's strong. It's pushing back against the words, and he can feel it pushing, like a tide coming in under a door.

"*Don't. Move.*"

The second time is rougher. Something tears in it.

Two metres. The circle touches the curse and the sword is out, and his hands do the rest. One cut, rising. One cut, across. The mouths go out one by one, like rooms going dark at closing time, and the corridor is suddenly, completely quiet.

The man falls over.

He was frozen mid-step, and when the words let go of him, his weight was in the wrong place. He goes down sideways onto the carpet with a short surprised sound and lands on his wrist. The tape measure snaps back into its case with a little *clack*.

Behind Kusakabe, Inumaki starts coughing.

---

He turns.

The boy is bent over with one hand on the wall, coughing into the other. It isn't a normal cough. It goes on too long and comes from too far down. When he straightens up and takes his hand away, there's blood on his palm. Not much. Enough. A dark smear across the lifeline. There's more on the inside of his collar.

Kusakabe's hands have stopped being useful. They want a curse to cut. There isn't one.

"Sit," he says.

Inumaki sits on the carpet against the wall. He gets the brown bottle out with a hand that's mostly steady and drinks straight from it this time, not bothering with the cap. He makes a face. Then he leans his head back on the wall and shuts his eyes.

Kusakabe crouches in front of him.

"Bad?"

Inumaki opens his eyes. Thinks about it. Lifts a hand and tilts it.

*Depends.*

"Liar," Kusakabe says.

Something happens at the top of the collar that might be a smile.

Down the corridor, the man in the cardigan is sitting up, holding his wrist, staring at both of them.

"What," he says. "What the hell was that. What did he *do* to me?"

---

The man's name is Kanda. He's the new manager. The company told him the building was being fumigated, and he believed them, mostly, and he needed measurements for the new carpet by Friday, and he had his own key. He came in through the back at twelve. He didn't see a cordon. He didn't hear anything strange except some sort of music, which he assumed was the fumigators.

He tells all of this to the auxiliary on the pavement, holding his wrist against his chest, very loudly. Then he tells it again, to Ijichi, who has come out of the car with a first-aid kit and is saying "I'm so sorry" at intervals, like a man bailing a boat.

"I couldn't *move*," Kanda says. "That kid shouted at me and I couldn't move. I couldn't move my *legs*. What is he, what kind of, is that a drug thing? Is this a cult?"

Nobody answers that.

Kusakabe stands a little way off, by the dead G's shadow on the pavement, with the sword back in its bag. He doesn't say anything. There's nothing to say that Kanda would believe, and the true thing would only make it worse. *A curse made of everybody's bad singing was about to eat you. You couldn't see it. A boy used up some of his throat so you'd keep your head.*

It's not the kind of sentence that helps.

The auxiliary is writing it all down. Kanda's wrist. Kanda's legs. Kanda's question about the cult.

Inumaki is sitting on the kerb by the car with his collar up and his hands between his knees. He doesn't look at Kanda. He looks at the gutter.

"Clinic," Kusakabe says to Ijichi.

"Yes. Yes, I've called them." Ijichi glances at the auxiliary's clipboard and lowers his voice. "Kusakabe-san. Because there's a civilian injury, and because it was a, a restricted technique, it'll go to headquarters. Someone will want to talk to you."

"When?"

Ijichi looks like he'd rather be driving.

"I think," he says, "today."

---

The clinic the school uses is a small grey building in a residential street, three floors, a car park the size of a tablecloth. No sign on the front that means anything. The doctor who sees to throats is an old man with very clean hands who doesn't ask how anything happened. He takes Inumaki into a room at the end of the corridor and shuts the door.

Kusakabe sits in the waiting area on a plastic chair. There's a fish tank with one fish in it. The fish is orange, and old, and appears to be thinking about something.

*Same,* he thinks, and then wishes he hadn't, because it's a fish.

He looks at the dried blood on his own cuff instead. Not his. The boy's, from when he crouched. He leaves it.

He keeps seeing the boy pull the collar down. Not the words. The half second before them, when Inumaki already knew what it would cost and did it anyway, without looking at him for permission. Kusakabe had said *don't use it unless I tell you*, and meant it, and the boy had heard it, and weighed it, and put it down.

*Good,* he thinks. *Somebody had to.*

He doesn't know yet what he's going to say about that, or to whom. He hopes nobody asks.

Inspector Sakaguchi arrives at ten past five.

He's younger than Kusakabe expected. Late thirties, maybe. Navy suit, no tie, rimless glasses, a slim leather folder under his arm and a small black recorder in his breast pocket, the kind journalists use. He walks like someone who's already decided where he's going to sit. He shows Kusakabe a card. Headquarters. Office of Field Conduct.

"Kusakabe-san. Thank you for waiting."

Kusakabe hadn't been waiting for him. He'd been waiting for the boy. He lets it go.

The receptionist finds them a side room with a desk and two chairs and a poster about hand-washing. Sakaguchi sits behind the desk. Kusakabe takes the other chair. A moment later the door opens and Inumaki comes in, collar up, a fresh white dressing taped under his jaw where the doctor has had a look at something. The doctor has found him a third chair from somewhere. The boy sits on it.

Sakaguchi turns on the recorder and sets it on the desk between them.

"I'd like to start with the student," he says.

He opens the folder. He has the auxiliary's notes already, typed. Kanda's name. Kanda's wrist. The word *restricted*.

"Inumaki-kun. At approximately two fifteen this afternoon, you used Cursed Speech in a building where a civilian was present. The civilian was affected. He's been injured. I need you to tell me, in your own words, what happened, and why you made that decision."

Inumaki looks at him.

He doesn't move. He doesn't reach for the collar. After a moment he looks at Kusakabe, just with his eyes.

"He can't," Kusakabe says.

Sakaguchi glances at the dressing. "Is he injured?"

"He's been talking."

"I'm sorry?"

"His throat. Using it costs him. He used it twice today." Kusakabe stops there. That's enough. It should be enough.

Sakaguchi takes his glasses off, looks at them, and puts them back on.

"Then he can write it down." He turns a blank sheet round on the desk and puts a pen on top of it. "In full, please. Times, distances. Who gave the instruction."

Inumaki looks at the paper.

Kusakabe looks at it too. It's a big sheet. It's very white. And he knows, the way he knows where the gate is when he sits down, what Sakaguchi wants on it. Not what happened. A version where a sixteen-year-old with a restricted technique got frightened, or excited, or careless, and opened his mouth. A version that fits in the folder.

The boy picks up the pen.

*Don't,* Kusakabe thinks. *This one's mine.*

He hates this. He wants that noted, somewhere, even if it's only in his own head. He hates explaining. Every sentence you give somebody is a sentence they get to hold up to the light and turn over and find the crack in. He's built most of his adult life around not handing people sentences.

But the boy is holding a pen over a sheet of paper, and there's blood on the inside of his collar, and Kusakabe is the one with *supervising* written next to his name.

"I'll do it," he says.

Sakaguchi looks at him. "You'll give a statement on his behalf."

"Yes."

"Go ahead."

Kusakabe puts his hands on his knees. He looks at the recorder, which is easier than looking at Sakaguchi, and he says it the way he'd cut something. Once, in the right place.

"The building was supposed to be clear. It wasn't. The man came in by the back with his own key. Nobody knew. The curse was stronger than the file said. It went for him. He couldn't see it. It was seven metres from him. I was too far. I'd told Inumaki not to use his technique unless I said so. I didn't get the chance to say so. He did the right thing anyway. Two words. The man has a sprained wrist. Without those two words, he doesn't have a wrist. Or an arm to put it on."

He stops.

The room is very quiet. Out in the waiting area, the fish tank pump is going.

"That's what happened," he says.

Sakaguchi writes for a moment. His handwriting is small and neat and doesn't slope at all.

"You told the student not to use his technique," he says, "and he used it."

"Yes."

"Without your instruction."

"There wasn't time for one."

"So, without your instruction." Sakaguchi looks up. "You were supervising, Kusakabe-san. You let a student with a restricted technique get into a position where he felt he had to use it on a civilian. That's either a failure of supervision or a failure of discipline. I'm trying to establish which."

*There's a third one,* Kusakabe thinks. *It's called a curse.*

He doesn't say it. He's already said more today than he usually says in a week, and he can feel it, the way you feel a long climb in your legs the next morning.

"He saved the man's life," he says instead. "Put that in."

"The man doesn't believe his life was in danger. The man believes a teenager drugged him. He's talking about a lawyer." Sakaguchi taps the folder. "And, frankly, I can only write down what I can verify. I can verify a civilian was affected by a restricted technique. I can verify he was injured. I can't verify a curse nobody but the two of you saw."

"Then don't write it. Write that I said it."

Sakaguchi looks at him for a long moment.

"I'll write that you said it," he says.

He switches off the recorder. He puts the pen and the blank sheet back in the folder, and the folder under his arm. He stands up.

"I'll be filing a formal complaint about the supervision on this mission," he says. "It'll go on your record, Kusakabe-san. And there'll be a note on the student's file regarding unauthorised use. That's procedure. I'm sure you understand."

"Fine."

"The note on the student's file." Sakaguchi pauses at the door. "You might want to consider whether you could have prevented that."

He nods to both of them, the exact same depth of nod for each. Then he goes out. Through the half-open door Kusakabe watches him stop at reception, sign something, and say thank you to the receptionist in a pleasant voice. Then the glass front door opens and closes, and he's gone.

*He's not wrong,* Kusakabe thinks. *That's the annoying part. Nobody's wrong. The man's wrist is sprained and the boy's coughing blood and the form only has the one box.*

He sits there for a while. He doesn't feel brave. He feels like he's put down something heavy and his arms haven't noticed yet.

Beside him, Inumaki takes out his phone.

---

The boy types something with his thumb. Not long. He stops, looks at it, deletes something, types again.

Then he turns the screen round and holds it out.

*thanks*

Lower case. No full stop. Just the one word in the middle of the white screen, the cursor blinking after it as if it might have more to say and has decided not to.

Kusakabe looks at it.

There are a few things he could say. *It's my job.* *Don't mention it.* *You did the work.* All of them true. All of them more than the moment needs.

He nods.

Inumaki looks at him a second longer, as if checking that the nod was the whole reply. It was. He seems to find that fine. He puts the phone away.

---

The doctor wants to look at the boy again in an hour, to see whether the swelling's gone down. Ijichi has gone to move the car, and then, by the look of it, to apologise to someone about where he moved it.

There's a bench outside the clinic, by the car park, under a small tree that doesn't know what kind of tree it is. The evening is warm. The sky over the houses has gone the soft grey-pink of the inside of a shell. Someone a few gardens away is practising the recorder, badly, the same five notes.

Kusakabe sits at one end of the bench. Inumaki sits at the other.

There's a vending machine by the clinic door. Kusakabe bought a can of coffee from it on the way out without thinking about it. The machine had three kinds and he picked the worst one, also without thinking about it. It's too sweet and not hot enough and tastes faintly of the tin, and he drinks it slowly anyway, because holding it gives his hands somewhere to be.

Inumaki takes the flat tin out of his bag. He unwraps a lozenge, pulls his collar down an inch, puts it in, and pulls the collar back up. The wrapper crackles. He folds it into a tiny square and puts it in his pocket instead of on the ground.

Neither of them says anything.

The recorder down the street gets to the fifth note, fails, and starts again.

Kusakabe thinks about the complaint. It'll be on his file by Monday, next to *Froze?* and a late report or two. It'll be read by somebody he'll never meet, who'll form a picture of him from it, and the picture will be wrong in a way that's mostly right. He thinks about Kanda, and Kanda's lawyer, and how long it takes for that kind of thing to go away. He thinks about the note on the boy's file, the one he couldn't keep off it.

Then he stops thinking about it, because there's nothing to do about any of it tonight, and the coffee is going cold.

Beside him, the boy swings his feet a little. The lozenge clicks against his teeth.

It's quiet. Not the empty kind, the kind that sits in his flat in the evenings with the TV on. A different kind. Two people who've said what needed saying, which wasn't much, and are now not saying anything else. Nobody filling it. Nobody expected to.

*I could get used to this,* he thinks. *I won't. But I could.*

A moth finds the light over the clinic door and starts patiently hitting it.

After a while, without turning his head, Inumaki says, very quietly, in a voice like gravel in a bucket:

"Salmon."

Kusakabe drinks the last of his coffee.

"Yeah," he says.

---

## Chapter 9. The Clan Problem

June comes in wet and stays wet.

The rainy season sits on the school like a lid on a pan. Everything is damp. The steps, the grass, the paper in the staff room, which curls at the corners if you leave it out overnight. The hydrangeas along the storehouse wall have gone a blue so deep it looks deliberate. The practice swords in the rack have swollen in their grips, and Maki has started wrapping hers in white tape.

He sits on the third step anyway. The storehouse roof keeps off most of it.

On the second Tuesday of the month, Okabe comes down the path with an umbrella and stops at the top of the steps. That's as far as Okabe ever comes. The yard is not a place for cardigans.

"A visitor on Thursday," Okabe says. "From the Zen'in family."

In the middle of the yard, Maki doesn't stop. But the rhythm of the staff changes. Half a beat. Kusakabe hears it the way you'd hear a clock miss a tick.

"For what," he says.

"The family's annual review of its member." Okabe looks at the clipboard under the umbrella. "Zen'in Masaru. Branch house. He'll observe training, then meet with the homeroom teacher."

"Which homeroom teacher."

Okabe gives him the look he keeps for late reports. "You."

*I knew that. I just wanted to hear it out loud. It sounds worse out loud.*

"I've got a job Thursday."

"You haven't," Okabe says. "I checked the board."

Kusakabe has nothing to add to that. It's rare for Okabe to get there first. He doesn't seem to enjoy it, which is almost decent of him.

"The family asked for the teacher. In writing." Okabe pauses. He seems to be searching for a word, the way a man pats his pockets for keys. "Please be," he says, and finds it, "present."

Then he goes back up the path, the umbrella bobbing between the trees until it's gone.

Kusakabe looks at the rain.

*Present. That's the bar. I can probably get over that.*

---

Panda tells him the rest, because Panda tells people things.

He comes and sits on the step below, which creaks, or would if it weren't stone. He smells of wet fabric. Most things do, this month.

"Masaru," Panda says. "He came last year. And the year before."

"You know him."

"He brings his own tea. In a flask. He doesn't drink ours." Panda thinks about it. "I don't think he drinks anyone's."

"What does he want."

"To make sure she's still Grade 4."

Kusakabe looks over at Maki. She's working through a sequence with the staff, low and then high and then low again, every strike landing on the same spot in the air as if there's a nail in it.

Grade 4. He's watched her take apart a curse in the woods behind the school that a Grade 2 would have been glad to walk away from, and then complain about her glasses steaming up.

"She's not Grade 4," he says.

"No," Panda agrees. "But she is. She's been Grade 4 since she got here. She'll be Grade 4 when she's ninety."

"Why."

"The family writes letters."

That's enough. Kusakabe knows how it works. A letter on good paper goes to the right desk, and the right desk is grateful to be written to, and a recommendation that was going to go through doesn't. Nobody has to do anything.

"Sensei," Maki calls, from the middle of the yard.

He looks up. She hasn't turned round. She's facing the cherry trees, the staff level across her shoulders.

"Thursday," she says. "Don't do anything."

"Wasn't going to."

"Good."

She goes back to the nail in the air.

Panda, below him, says nothing at all. Which, for Panda, is a lot.

---

He knows the look. That's the trouble.

He thinks about it on Wednesday night, on the sofa, with the light off and the rain going steadily on the window. Not in a big way. In the small way you think about a tooth that's started to ache.

He knows the look a clan man gives a person with no technique. It's not hatred. Hatred would at least be interest. It's the look you'd give a chair in a waiting room. You'd sit on it if you had to. You'd never ask where it came from.

He's had it from old men at headquarters, reading his file with one finger. He had it in November from a nineteen-year-old Kamo with a strap of blood packs across his chest. He's had it from people he's saved.

Nobody with power has ever wanted him for anything. Not as an heir. Not as an ally worth a letter on good paper. He built what he has out of a style nobody fashionable learns any more, and a lot of hours. Nobody ever sent a man in a crested coat to check he was still where they'd left him.

*Lucky, really,* he thinks. *Being nobody's means nobody comes to inspect you.*

Maki is somebody's. That's her problem. Born into the biggest house there is, with almost no cursed energy, needing glasses to see what everyone around her could see from birth. A family like that doesn't let go of something it's ashamed of. It keeps it close, so it can keep it small.

It's not his business.

That's the other thing he knows. Clan business is a swamp. Better-connected sorcerers than him have walked into it and come out with their careers missing a leg. You stand on the bank and say yes and no, and the man in the crested coat goes home, and nothing changes, and nothing gets worse either.

She asked him not to do anything. That's the easiest instruction he's ever been given.

*Sit on the step. Answer in single words. Let him go past like a train you're not catching.*

He goes to bed at one. The rain goes on without him.

---

Thursday, Masaru comes in through the gate at ten past two.

Kusakabe sees him first. He always sees people first. That's what the gate's for.

A man in his late fifties, short and well-fed, in a dark grey haori with the family crest small and white on the chest. Hakama. White tabi, already spotted with mud from the path, which seems to offend him personally. A young attendant walks half a step behind and to the left, holding a large black umbrella over Masaru's head. The attendant's own right shoulder is soaked through. He doesn't seem to have noticed. Or he noticed a long time ago, and stopped.

They come down the slope. The attendant unfolds a small wooden stool at the top of the steps, under the overhang, and Masaru sits on it as if it had been waiting for him all his life. A steel flask appears. Tea goes into its lid. The umbrella stays up, even under the roof. Nobody tells the attendant to stop.

"Kusakabe-kun," Masaru says pleasantly.

*Kun.* Like a schoolboy. Kusakabe is thirty-something and a Grade 1. Masaru knows that. He has a voice like a man who has never once had to raise it. Soft and rounded at the edges. Every word comes out of it exactly where he put it.

"Zen'in-san."

"Please. Don't get up."

Kusakabe hadn't been going to. He stays on the third step. Masaru is three steps above him and to the side, so that Kusakabe has to turn his head to see him, and doesn't.

Below them, the yard. Maki in the middle of it, working. Panda on the grass at the edge, not lying down for once, sitting up with his paws on his knees like a schoolboy outside an office. Inumaki on the bottom step, collar to his nose, hood up against the drizzle.

Masaru watches Maki for a while, sipping his tea.

"You're the swordsman," he says eventually. "The one without a technique."

"Yes."

"How suitable. They've given her someone she can look up to without straining her neck."

Kusakabe says nothing.

*There it is. Two minutes in. Not bad.*

---

It goes on like that for half an hour.

Masaru talks the way some men smoke, as something to do with his mouth while the afternoon passes. The weather, which is bad for his knees. The drive from Kyoto, and a service station on the way, which has declined. And in between, lightly, he talks about Maki.

"She still wears the spectacles."

"Yes."

"Remarkable. A Zen'in who has to put something on her face to see what she's for." He sips. "We had hopes for the pair of them, you know. Briefly. Twins are an unlucky thing in a house like ours, but there were people who said, well, perhaps this time. Perhaps two halves would make something." He smiles at the yard. "They didn't."

Maki comes in off a feint, low and fast. Her back heel stays down. The strike comes out of nowhere.

"The other one, at least, has the sense to be modest about it," Masaru says. "In Kyoto. She knows her place. This one has never known anything of the kind. She thinks if she hits things hard enough, the family will have to look at her."

He laughs, softly.

"We look at her once a year. That's quite enough."

Kusakabe sits.

The damp seeps through the seat of his trousers. The can of coffee goes warm in his hand. His face is holding, the way it always holds.

*Not your business. Not your family. She said don't.*

He has been the man on the end of this voice. Not this one exactly. Others like it, a hundred times, in corridors and briefings and the back seats of cars. He knows the exact weight of it. It doesn't hurt in the moment. It goes in sideways and finds somewhere to sit and stays there for years, paying no rent.

He'd quite like to be on the sofa.

Maki doesn't look up. Not once. She keeps working, and she keeps working well, and he understands that this is how she's always done it. Not by answering. By being something the voice can't do anything about.

He has to admire it. He doesn't want to. He does.

Inumaki shifts on the bottom step. His hand goes to the zip of his collar and stays there.

"Inumaki," Kusakabe says. Quietly. Not looking.

The hand stays a moment longer. Then it drops.

Masaru doesn't notice any of this. Masaru isn't a man who notices boys on steps.

---

"Now," Masaru says. "The forms."

The attendant produces a folder. Masaru opens it on his knee and turns pages with one finger.

"Mission eligibility. Your monthly assessment. You've written here," and he holds the page a little further away, to read it, "'Eligible for Grade 2 accompaniment.'"

"Yes."

"The family would prefer Grade 4 work only. For her safety." Masaru closes the folder. "You'll amend it."

It isn't a question. It's said the way you'd tell a waiter you'll have the fish.

He feels the shape of the answer in his mouth before he's decided anything. Masaru goes home. The form goes in a little different. Maki does Grade 4 work for another month, which she was doing anyway. Nothing changes and nothing gets worse.

"I'll look at it," he says.

"Good." Masaru hands the folder back over his shoulder without looking, and the attendant takes it without being looked at. "I knew you'd be practical. Men like you usually are. You haven't the luxury of being otherwise."

He finishes his tea. The attendant takes the cup and screws it back onto the flask.

Kusakabe thinks that's it. He thinks the train has gone past.

Then Masaru leans down a little, towards him, and lowers his voice. Not much. Just enough to make it private. Just enough to make it friendly.

"Between ourselves, Kusakabe-kun," he says. "You take these children out on jobs. Things happen on jobs. If something were to happen to her, on one of your little outings, nobody in the house would think the worse of her teacher. Some would think rather better of him. It would close a file that's been open for sixteen years." He smiles. "I'd bear that in mind. You're not paid enough to try very hard."

---

Kusakabe stands up.

He doesn't decide to. He'd like to know when it happened. One moment he's sitting on the third step with a warm can of coffee. The next he's on his feet, turned round, and the can is on the step by his shoe, upright. He didn't put it there.

He's below Masaru, still. Three steps down. It doesn't seem to matter.

He doesn't touch his sword. It's on the step beside the can, in its bag, and his hand doesn't go near it. He doesn't draw the circle. He doesn't need it.

He looks at Masaru. Not hard. Just looks. The way he'd look at a corridor he was about to walk down.

"If anything happens to her," he says, "I'll come and ask you about it. Personally."

That's all.

It isn't loud. The rain is louder. Down in the yard, Maki's staff goes on swinging, the same steady rhythm, and he doesn't think she can have heard it from there.

Masaru's face does something it probably hasn't done in a long time. Nothing big. The pleasantness stays on it. But behind the pleasantness something goes very still, like a man on a stair who has felt it shift under his foot.

The attendant's umbrella dips. Just an inch. Then it comes back up.

"Was that a threat, Kusakabe-kun?"

Kusakabe doesn't answer. He picks up the can of coffee, and sits back down on the third step, and turns to face the yard again.

He can feel Masaru looking at the back of his head. He lets him. There's nothing there to see.

Then the creak of the stool being folded. Footsteps going back up the slope, two pairs, one of them muddy and offended and a little quicker than before.

The gate.

Gone. Twenty minutes early.

Kusakabe drinks his coffee. It's warm and bad. His fingers have gone stiff around the can, and he has to put it down and pick it up again before they'll let go of the shape.

*Well,* he thinks. *That was stupid.*

He waits to feel sorry about it. It doesn't come. Something else comes instead, quieter, and sits down next to him, and he doesn't look at it too closely.

On the grass, Panda has turned his whole head round to look at him. Panda's ears are good. Panda heard every word. He doesn't say anything. He looks at Kusakabe for a long time with his round black eyes. Then he turns back to the yard and lies down, slowly, in the wet grass, like a man settling into a bath.

On the bottom step, Inumaki takes his hand off his collar for good, and puts it in his pocket.

Maki doesn't stop.

Maybe she heard. Maybe she didn't. She finishes the sequence, and starts it again, and her back heel stays down the whole way through.

---

It takes four days. Which is quick, for paper.

On Monday, Yaga's door is open, and Yaga's voice comes out of it as Kusakabe walks past. "In."

He goes in. He stands just inside the door.

Yaga is at his desk with a letter in front of him, thick creamy paper with the crest at the top in grey. His hands are flat on either side of it, as if it might get up.

"The Zen'in have written," Yaga says.

"I'll bet."

"'Openly threatened a representative of the family in front of students.'" Yaga reads it flatly, without any expression at all, which is how Yaga reads everything. "'Conduct unbecoming. Wholly unacceptable from a teacher entrusted with a member of the house.' There's a page of this."

"They weren't in front. They were in the yard."

Yaga looks up.

"Did you threaten him?"

Kusakabe thinks about it. Honestly. It seems only fair.

"I told him I'd ask him a question."

The sunglasses stay on him for a long time. Somewhere behind the dark glass, Kusakabe suspects, Yaga is doing a very small sum, and arriving at an answer he doesn't intend to share.

"Headquarters wants a formal reprimand," Yaga says at last. "On your record. Twelve months."

"Fine."

"Your grade allowance review is deferred. A year."

This time he does the sum.

It does itself, quickly, the way it didn't in the autumn at his sister's flat. The review was due in August. Some part of him had already spent it, on Ono's hours and the thickening powder and the reed diffuser by the shoe cabinet.

Not small. Not ruinous. Just a hole, the size of something he'd been going to stand on.

"Fine," he says again. It comes out the same. That's what the face is for.

"They'd also like a written apology."

"No."

Yaga doesn't argue. He picks up a pen and writes something on the corner of the letter in small square characters.

"I'll tell them you're drafting it," he says. "Drafts take time."

"Thanks."

Yaga makes a sound that might, in a smaller man, have been a laugh. It isn't one, quite. He folds the letter back in three.

"Kusakabe."

"Mm."

"Don't make a habit of it."

"Won't need to."

In the corridor, his hand goes to his pocket and checks the thing that isn't there. A little more owed. With a debt that size, it's hard to tell whether a bit more makes any difference. He suspects Yaga knows exactly how much.

---

Okabe has the copy by the afternoon.

He brings it to the staff room in a clear plastic sleeve, like evidence, and lays it on Kusakabe's desk next to the radiator that ticks.

"Acknowledgement of formal reprimand," Okabe says. "Sign at the bottom. It goes in your file."

"Next to the other one."

"Next to the complaint from May, yes." Okabe watches him sign. "That's two this term."

"Is there a prize."

"There's a meeting," Okabe says. "In September."

Okabe checks the signature and tucks the sleeve under his clip. Then he hesitates, which isn't like him.

"For what it's worth," Okabe says, and stops.

Kusakabe waits. Okabe looks at the clipboard as if the rest of the sentence might be on it.

It isn't. Okabe nods once, to nobody, and goes.

*Huh,* Kusakabe thinks. And then, because there's nothing else to do with it, he gets on with the attendance.

---

Maki never mentions it.

Not on Tuesday, when the rain stops for a few hours and the yard steams in a sudden fierce sun. Not on Wednesday. She doesn't look at him any more than she did before, which was mostly when he was late.

He doesn't mention it either. It happened on a step, in the rain, and then was over.

On Thursday afternoon he comes down to the yard at the usual time and his step is occupied.

Not by a person. By a practice sword. One of the swollen ones from the rack, its grip freshly wrapped in white tape, the tape still bright. It's lying across the third step, exactly where he'd sit, so that to sit he'd have to move it.

Maki is in the middle of the yard with another one, her own, held loosely at her side. She isn't looking at him. She's looking at the cherry trees. Waiting, in the way that a person waits who's prepared to do it all day.

Panda is on the grass. Inumaki is on the bottom step. Both of them are looking very hard at something else.

Kusakabe stands at the top of the steps and looks at the sword.

All his reasons are still there. He counts them, like change. The circle doesn't know a student from a curse. There's no practice version of it. If he puts her inside it, it'll answer, and he doesn't want to find out how hard. And the other reason, the one he doesn't look at: that she'll see there's nothing else. No secret. Just a man and a lot of years.

All true. All still there.

He goes down the steps and picks up the sword.

The grip is damp from the air and the tape is rough under his fingers. It's lighter than his own. Everything is.

He walks out into the middle of the yard. She turns to face him. Neither of them says anything.

He keeps the circle folded away. Not drawn. Not even close. He keeps it the way you'd keep your hands in your pockets in a shop full of glass. What's left is just the old stuff, the stuff under the circle. Feet. Distance. Eyes. Breathing. The things he learned before he learned anything worth having.

She comes in fast.

She's very fast. It's different from here. The first strike is at his head and he isn't there. The second is at his ribs and his sword is there instead, and the shock of it goes up his arm into his shoulder and his teeth. The humid air is like breathing through a wet towel. His knees have opinions about the ground. He is, he realises, about twice her age, and every one of those years is in his calves.

She hits his forearm on the fourth exchange. Properly. A crack, and then a hot line of pain, and the bruise already forming underneath like a word on the tip of the tongue.

He doesn't say anything. He resets his feet.

On the seventh exchange he sees it. Not her heel. That's fixed. Her eyes. Just before she strikes, she looks at where she's going to hit. Only for an instant. A flick. But it's there.

He lets her come in again. When her eyes go to his left shoulder he's already somewhere else, and the tip of his practice sword touches her side, just below the ribs, light as a finger.

She stops.

"You look at where you're going to hit," he says.

She stands there breathing hard, sweat running down from under her glasses. She looks at him for a long moment. He can't tell what she's thinking. He's not sure he'd want to.

Then she grunts, and goes back to her mark, and lifts her sword.

They do ten more minutes. Then he goes back to his step, and sits on it, and rubs his forearm, and doesn't look at anyone.

Panda, on the grass, says, "Huh."

Inumaki says, "Salmon." Not to nobody, this time.

---

It becomes a thing. Nobody decides that it will.

Every afternoon, after the drills, Maki leaves a practice sword on the third step. Every afternoon he looks at it for a moment, as if it might be somebody else's, and picks it up. Ten minutes becomes fifteen. Fifteen becomes twenty, on a day when the rain holds off and his knees forget to complain.

He never draws the circle. She never asks him to. She takes what she can get from him, which is feet and distance and the small cruel economy of a man too tired to move more than he has to. She takes it apart in front of him and puts it back together inside herself, piece by piece.

She stops looking at where she's going to hit. It takes her four days.

He has bruises up both forearms by the end of the month. On Sunday his sister asks if he's been fighting. He says yes. She seems pleased, in a vague way, as if she always suspected.

Maki doesn't say thank you. She doesn't say anything about the man in the grey coat, or the letter, or the twelve months on his file. She doesn't need to.

He has a feeling she's saying it anyway. Every afternoon, with a practice sword, about twenty times.

He doesn't say *you're welcome.* He gets hit instead. It seems to cover it.

---

The last Friday of June, the rain comes back in the late afternoon. Not hard. A fine grey drift that makes everything look further away than it is.

They've gone longer than usual. His shirt is soaked through, and there's a fresh bruise on his left wrist that he'll feel tonight when he turns the tap. She caught him twice today. He caught her more, but not by as much as last week.

*She's going to be very good,* he thinks. *Whatever grade they write on her.*

He lowers his sword. He's done. He turns, and walks back to the steps, and sits on the third one in the shade of the storehouse roof. He lets the practice sword drop on the stone beside him. It slides off and lands in the wet grass at the bottom with a soft clack.

He leans back on his elbows and closes his eyes and listens to the rain.

*That's enough for one week. That's enough for one month. That's enough for a year.*

Footsteps on the grass. He opens his eyes.

Maki is standing at the bottom of the steps. She has his practice sword in her hand. She's picked it up from the grass. Her glasses are fogged at the edges and there's a smear of mud on her cheek and she doesn't look tired at all, which seems, in the circumstances, deeply unfair.

She looks at him. Then she tosses the sword to him, underhand, an easy arc through the rain.

His hand comes up and catches it by the grip without his asking it to.

"Again," she says.

He looks at the sword. At her. At the rain.

He sighs.

"Fine."

---

## Chapter 10. Two Tired Men

Nanami Kento is already there.

He's standing in the lobby of an empty office building in Shinagawa, at four minutes past nine on a Thursday night in October, under a ceiling light that hums. Pale suit. A tie with a small, busy pattern on it. Glasses that are closer to goggles, tinted, the frames patterned too, worn the way other men wear a wristwatch. Under one arm, something long and flat wrapped in cloth.

He's looking at his actual wristwatch when Kusakabe comes through the revolving door.

"Four minutes," Nanami says.

"Yeah."

"I'm not complaining. I'm noting it."

*Fair. It's a long list.*

---

The sheet came that morning, across the dispatch coordinator's desk, between a rice cracker and a mug.

*Kusakabe A. (Grade 1). Nanami K. (Grade 1). Shinagawa. Commercial premises, vacant. Manifests after midnight. Possible Grade 1.*

"Two of us," he said.

"Twenty-two floors," she said. "The file doesn't say which one. Car at half eight."

"I'll get the train."

She shrugged, and wrote something next to his name, and that was the end of it.

He knows Nanami the way he knows most of the people at the school. From corridors. From the staff room, at the far end of the table, reading a newspaper folded into quarters. They nod. They've never worked a job together. Kusakabe has heard things, the way you hear things. That Nanami left jujutsu after he graduated and went to work at a company, an ordinary one, with a desk and a pension. That he came back. That nobody knows why, and nobody asks, because Nanami has a face that makes asking feel like a bad investment.

That he goes home at six. Every day. Whatever's happening.

*So that's two of us who'd rather be somewhere else,* Kusakabe thought on the train. *At least nobody has to pretend.*

---

The building belonged to a freight company until August. Then the company moved to Saitama, where the rent is cheaper and the air is worse, and left twenty-two floors of grey carpet behind it, waiting to be sold.

The night guard is called Iwata. Sixties, a uniform jacket a size too big, reading glasses on a cord. He's been here eleven years. He stayed on with the building, the way some people stay on with a house after the family's gone.

He gives them the briefing in the lobby, standing, because there are no chairs left.

"The lifts first," he says. "About a month ago. They go up on their own after midnight. Fourteen, every time. Then the phones on fourteen. All of them at once. There's no line in. The company cut it in August."

"Fourteen," Nanami says.

"Accounts." Iwata touches the cord of his glasses. "They were famous for it, upstairs. Lights on all night. You'd do your rounds at three and there'd be twenty of them up there, eating out of the vending machine."

"And your colleague," Nanami says.

Iwata nods slowly.

"Fukuda. Young lad. I found him on fourteen at half three in the morning, a fortnight ago. Sitting at a desk in the dark, typing." He stops. "The computer wasn't plugged in. There wasn't a computer. Just the keyboard. He'd been at it a while. His fingers."

He doesn't finish that.

"He's alive," Iwata says. "He doesn't remember any of it. His mother rang me to say thank you, and I didn't know what to say to her."

Kusakabe doesn't say anything either. Neither does Nanami. It's a good silence, as these things go. It doesn't try to fix anything.

"I don't go above ten after midnight now," Iwata says. "I'll be honest with you."

"That's sensible," Nanami says. "Stay down here. Keep the doors locked. If you hear anything, don't come up."

"What will you be doing?"

Nanami looks at his watch again.

"Waiting," he says.

---

They take the stairs. Fourteen floors. Nanami doesn't trust the lifts, and Kusakabe doesn't trust anything, so it's unanimous.

Fourteen is a long open floor of desks with nothing on them. Grey partitions at shoulder height. Grey carpet with paler squares where filing cabinets used to stand. A whole wall of windows on the far side, and through them the city doing what it does at night, all those lit rectangles where other people are still at work.

On every desk, a phone. Beige. Cord coiled. Unplugged.

The air on fourteen is wrong in the way a room is wrong after an argument. Nothing there. Everything there. Kusakabe lets the circle out a little, just to the length of his arm, and the wrongness sits right at the edge of it, everywhere at once, not coming in.

"Well," Nanami says. "It's here."

"It's everywhere."

"Yes. That's what makes it tiresome."

At the end of the floor, by the lifts, there's a break room. A sink, a round table, four chairs that didn't go to Saitama, and a vending machine still plugged into the wall, lit up like a shop window, the only bright thing on the floor.

Nanami looks at it for a while.

"Someone forgot to turn it off," he says.

"Or it's the only one still working."

Nanami glances at him. It isn't quite a smile. It's the place where a smile would go if he were a different man.

They sit down at the round table to wait.

---

It's half past nine. The file says after midnight. Nobody knows how long after.

Kusakabe has done a lot of waiting in his life. He's good at it. You find a wall to put your back against and a door to face, and you let the body go quiet, and you don't think about anything you'd have to get up to deal with.

Nanami waits differently. He sits very straight. He takes the cloth-wrapped thing from under his arm and lays it on the table in front of him, parallel to the edge. He takes off his jacket, folds it once along the seam, and hangs it on the back of the chair. He looks at his watch.

"It's past my hours," he says.

"Since six."

"Since six." Nanami turns his wrist so the watch face sits on the inside. "I want to be clear that I find this objectionable."

"Noted."

"Not this job. The principle." He considers the dark floor beyond the doorway. "Overtime is the moment a company tells you what it actually thinks of your life. That it's a thing it can have more of, if it asks politely. Or doesn't ask."

"The company's a curse, tonight."

"Then it's the most honest employer I've had."

Kusakabe snorts. It surprises him. Nanami doesn't react, which is somehow the right reaction.

---

Ten o'clock. Nanami gets up and goes to the vending machine.

He stands in front of it with his hands in his pockets, reading it the way some men read a menu in a restaurant they don't trust. Then he feeds it coins. Two cans drop.

He puts one in front of Kusakabe without asking.

It's coffee, black, in a brown can with a picture of a mountain on it. Kusakabe opens it. It's hot, which is the best that can be said about it. It tastes of burnt sugar and a little of the inside of a pipe.

Nanami drinks his and looks at the can.

"That's dreadful," he says.

"Yeah."

"I worked in a building like this for four years. Different company. Same machine, I think. I think they make one machine and move it around." He sets the can down, exactly on the ring it came from. "I used to drink three of these a night and tell myself it was a choice."

Kusakabe waits. He doesn't ask. It seems to be the right thing, because Nanami goes on.

"Labour is shit," he says.

He says it calmly. The way you'd give a measurement.

"It takes your time and gives you money, and then you spend the money on things that help you recover from losing the time. Food you don't have the energy to cook. Rest that isn't rest. A bigger flat to be tired in." He turns the can a quarter turn. "It's a closed loop. Very elegant, if you're not inside it."

*He's right,* Kusakabe thinks. *I'd say it if I could be bothered to say things.*

"So you came back to this," he says. "Where it's worse."

"It isn't worse. It's the same shit." Nanami thinks about it. "But at the end of the day, sometimes, something is different because I was there. At the company, the only thing that was different at the end of the day was the date."

He doesn't say any more than that. Kusakabe has a feeling there's a story under it, small and specific, the kind of thing a person doesn't tell twice. He doesn't go looking for it.

"Someone thanked me once," Nanami says, as if he's read that. "For something very small. I hadn't budgeted for how much it would matter."

Then he picks up the can again, and the subject is closed, the way a drawer closes when you lean on it.

---

Eleven. Out on the floor, nothing. The lit city in the windows. A helicopter, somewhere, going over without stopping.

Nanami takes a paper bag out of his briefcase. It's folded over twice at the top and creased flat with a thumbnail. He opens it on the table. Inside, wrapped in waxed paper, a sandwich cut in half: thick white bread, something breaded and dark in the middle, a smear of mustard.

He looks at the two halves. Then he moves one of them a few centimetres across the table, towards Kusakabe.

"I can't eat both," he says. "And it won't keep."

"I'm fine."

"You haven't eaten. You came straight from somewhere."

That's true. Kusakabe can't think how Nanami knows it. Possibly it shows. Most things do, on him, if anyone bothers to look.

He takes the half.

It's good. It's very good. The bread is soft and dense at the same time, and the crust gives under his teeth with a small, definite crackle. He eats it slowly, which isn't like him, and when he's finished he finds he's looking at the waxed paper as if there might be more of it.

"Where's that from."

Nanami tells him. A bakery, a small one, near a station, open at seven. The woman who runs it starts at four. He's been going there for two years. He describes the bakery the way he hasn't described anything else all night: carefully, with pleasure, with the hours and the name of the street.

"The melon bread is overrated," he says. "Everyone gets the melon bread. Get this."

"Okay."

*I'll go one Sunday,* Kusakabe thinks. *On the way back. I'll get round to it.*

He folds the waxed paper into a square and puts it in his pocket, because there's nowhere to throw it, and then forgets it's there.

---

At a quarter to twelve, Nanami says, "I've been looking after a student."

Kusakabe looks up.

"One of Gojo-san's first-years," Nanami says. "He lends them out. Like an umbrella you don't expect back." He turns the can. "This one's earnest. Painfully. He eats a lot. He thanks people for things. He asks questions I stopped asking some time ago, and I find I don't have good answers for them."

He says it without much expression. But something sits differently in his shoulders when he says it. Like a man describing a dog he didn't want and has started walking twice a day.

Kusakabe knows which first-year. Everyone at the school knows which first-year. There are words people use about that boy in the staff room, quietly, when Gojo isn't in it, and none of them are *earnest*.

He doesn't want those words at this table.

"Your machine's still got change in it," he says. "In the return. Someone left a hundred yen."

Nanami looks at him for a moment. Not long. Long enough.

Then he says, "Then it's yours. You found it." And lets it go, the whole thing, without a mark on it.

Kusakabe fishes the coin out of the return slot. It's cold. He puts it in his pocket, the other one, the one without the waxed paper. His hand doesn't check anything. It's just a coin.

---

Just before midnight, he walks the floor. It's something to do, and Nanami is rolling back his shirt cuffs, exactly two turns each.

He goes slowly down the rows between the partitions with the sword bag on his shoulder and the circle held close. The company took the computers and the chairs and the filing cabinets. It didn't take everything.

A wall calendar, still on August, with a photograph of a lighthouse. A mug in the corner of a desk with a name written on it in marker, half washed off. A sticky note on a partition at eye level: *Back in 5 min.* The ink has faded. Whoever wrote it never came back for it.

On one desk, near the windows, the beige plastic of the phone has a dip worn into it where a hand rested on the receiver. Day after day. Year after year. Waiting for it to ring, or hoping it wouldn't.

The wrongness is thicker here. It isn't angry. That's the strange part. It's tired. It's the most tired thing he's ever felt through the circle, and he's felt it from the inside, on bad mornings.

*Fourteen floors up. Lights on all night. Eating out of a machine.*

*It didn't come from nowhere. It came from them.*

He stands at the window for a moment. Below, a taxi waits at a red light on an empty junction, patient, with nobody to see it be patient.

He goes back to the break room. Nanami looks up.

"Anything?"

"It's tired."

Nanami nods slowly, as if that were a technical finding, and writes nothing down.

---

Midnight comes and goes. Nothing.

Half past twelve. Nanami checks his watch and doesn't comment on it, which is a comment.

One o'clock. The lifts don't move.

It's the hour when a room goes thick. When the hum of the vending machine starts to sound like it's coming from inside your own head. Kusakabe sits with his back to the wall and the doorway in front of him and the circle held in, folded, ready.

"What would you do," Nanami says, "if you could stop?"

He says it to the window. Not to Kusakabe. It could be a question for anybody.

Kusakabe doesn't answer.

Nanami seems to expect that. After a while he answers it himself.

"Malaysia," he says. "Kuantan. On the east coast. There's a beach there that goes on for longer than seems necessary." He looks at the dark glass. The reflection of the break room floats over the city, the two of them at a round table under a strip light. "A house near the sea. Not a big one. I'd sell everything that needs a key. I'd take the books I've bought over the years and never read. There are a great many of them. I keep buying them. It's a kind of optimism, I suppose."

"How many."

"I stopped counting at a hundred and forty," Nanami says.

"That's a lot of optimism."

"It's a lot of evenings I thought I'd have." Nanami takes off his glasses and cleans them on a handkerchief, slowly, one lens and then the other. Without them his eyes are tired and quite ordinary. "I'd read them in order. Badly. In a chair. With the window open. And when I got to the end, I'd start buying new ones, and never read those either."

He puts the glasses back on.

"That's all," he says. "It's not much of a plan. I don't need it to be."

Kusakabe looks at the window too. He sees it for a second, more clearly than he'd like: a chair, a window, a long flat beach, the sea going in and out without needing anyone to watch it.

"You?" Nanami says.

"Nothing."

"Nothing."

"Nothing. Somewhere quiet. Nobody asking."

"That's not nothing," Nanami says. "That's a very precise description of a good life."

Kusakabe doesn't answer that, either.

He drinks the last of the coffee instead. It's gone cold. It's worse cold, which he wouldn't have thought possible. He finishes it anyway, out of something like loyalty, and puts the can down next to Nanami's, not quite on its ring.

Nanami looks at the two cans. He doesn't move them. Kusakabe gets the feeling it costs him something.

---

At eleven minutes past two, every phone on fourteen rings at once.

It isn't a ring, exactly. It's the memory of one, coming from forty dead handsets, all slightly out of time. Under it, the sound of keys. Hundreds of keys. Someone typing very fast, everywhere, on nothing.

Kusakabe is on his feet. He doesn't remember standing. The sword is in his hand and the bag is on the floor. The circle is out, and the cold has gone into his fingers the way it always does, quick and specific, like a tap turned on.

Nanami stands up beside him. He loosens his tie. He pulls it free of his collar, and winds it round his right hand, twice, and pulls it tight with his teeth. Then he unwraps the cloth from the thing on the table. A blade, broad, squared off, more like a cleaver than a sword, with a dull edge.

"From here," Nanami says, "this is overtime."

Out on the floor, between the partitions, something stands up.

It's the shape of a back at the end of a long day. Very large. Grey, stooped, with a hundred thin arms hanging off it like coat sleeves off a rail, and the ends of the arms are fingers, and the fingers are moving. It turns towards the light of the break room. It doesn't have a face. It has the place a face would go if anyone had ever looked up.

It comes for Kusakabe first. He's nearest. Things always are.

The arms cross the edge of the circle and the sword answers. He doesn't decide the cuts. He hears them: four, five, short and dry, and fingers falling on the carpet like dropped pens. The thing jerks back and comes again, wider, trying to go round him. It can't. Wherever it reaches, he's already there, a step left, a step back, the circle moving with him, and every arm that comes in comes off.

His heart is going like something trying to get out of a box.

*Keep it busy. Just keep it busy.*

Behind him, Nanami says nothing at all. Kusakabe can feel him in the circle, standing quite still, looking at the thing the way a man looks at a plank before he cuts it. Measuring. Seven parts here. Three parts there.

Then Nanami steps past him.

One stroke. Not at the thing. At a place on the thing, low on the left side of that great tired back, where there's nothing to see at all. The blunt blade goes in like it was expected.

A sound like a load-bearing wall deciding it's had enough.

The curse folds in half, the wrong way, and comes apart in grey flakes that drift down over the empty desks like ash from a paper fire. The phones stop. The keys stop. One by one. The last phone, by the window, rings once more on its own, a long way after all the others, and then it stops too.

The floor goes still.

Kusakabe lets the circle go. It takes him a moment. It always does.

---

They don't talk for a while after.

Nanami sits back down at the round table. He doesn't unwind the tie from his hand. He rests that hand on the table, palm down, and looks at it. Kusakabe sits opposite. He puts the sword away and then puts both hands flat on his knees, and waits for them to be his again.

"Thank you," Nanami says. "For holding it."

"It came to me."

"You let it."

Kusakabe shrugs. That's what the circle's for. You stand still and let things come to you, and then they stop coming.

"It was a clean job," Nanami says. "I don't say that often. Most people make a lot of noise."

"You didn't."

"No." Nanami looks at the flakes still settling on the desks outside. "Neither did you."

That's all. It's enough. It sits on the table between them with the two empty cans.

Kusakabe rings Iwata on the lobby line, and then rings dispatch. The car for him won't be here till four. Nanami will take the first train. There's an hour and a half of nothing in front of them, and nowhere to put it.

So they sit.

---

It's three in the morning. The worst hour. The city in the window has thinned out to a few hundred lights. Somewhere down there people are getting up for early shifts, and somewhere else people are still at work, and in this one room on the fourteenth floor, two men are doing neither.

Kusakabe doesn't know why he says it. Possibly the hour. Possibly the sandwich. Possibly that Nanami asked him a question three hours ago and then didn't make him answer it, and some debts get paid whether you mean to or not.

"I've got a sister," he says.

Nanami doesn't move.

"I see her on Sundays."

That's the one thing. He's said it now. It's out on the table with the cans and the cloth and it can't be put back. Nobody at the school knows that. Not Ijichi. Not Gojo. Not Okabe, who knows everything that can be written on a form.

Nanami is quiet for a moment. He doesn't ask where she lives, or what's wrong, or why Sundays. He doesn't make the face people make. He looks at the window, and nods, once, as if Kusakabe had told him the time.

"Then don't work Sundays," he says.

"I don't."

"Good. Keep it that way. Write it in somewhere. Make them ask you." He turns his wrist and looks at the watch. "Everything else they'll take."

*He's right,* Kusakabe thinks. *Again. That's twice in one night. I'm not sure I like it.*

He thinks, briefly, that this is what people mean by a colleague. Not a friend. Something a bit less than that and a bit more useful. Someone who sits at the same table at three in the morning and doesn't need you to be interesting.

He thinks they might do this again. Another job, another long night in a dead building, another can of something dreadful. He finds he wouldn't mind. He files that away somewhere he won't have to look at it.

---

At ten to four they go down the stairs. Fourteen floors. Nanami carries his jacket over his arm and the cloth-wrapped blade under it. Neither of them says anything about the lifts.

In the lobby, Iwata is standing by the reception desk with his glasses on, as if he's been standing there the whole time. He probably has.

"It's done," Nanami tells him. "It won't come back."

Iwata looks at the ceiling. He looks at it for a long time.

"I'll tell Fukuda's mother," he says.

Then he bows, deeply, to both of them, in a way that's clearly been waiting a fortnight to happen. Kusakabe bows back, less deeply. He's never sure what to do with his hands at a moment like this. Nanami bows exactly as deep as Iwata, no more and no less, as if he'd measured that too.

---

Outside, the air has gone cold and clean. It's still dark. The street is wet from a rain neither of them noticed falling. Down at the end of it, a delivery van is unloading crates of milk outside a convenience store, the bottles clinking.

Kusakabe's car isn't here yet. He stands on the pavement with his sword bag on his shoulder and his hands in his coat pockets. One pocket has a folded square of waxed paper in it. The other has a cold hundred-yen coin.

Nanami puts his jacket on. He unwinds the tie from his hand, slowly, and smooths it once between his fingers. Then he turns his collar up, and loops the tie round his neck, and knots it without looking, the way a man does who has done it every morning for most of his life.

He checks his watch.

"The first train's in nineteen minutes," he says. "If I walk, I'll be on time."

"Course you will."

"One of us should be." Nanami looks at him through the tinted glasses. Something in his face eases, very slightly, and then puts itself back. "Get some sleep before Sunday, Kusakabe."

"Yeah."

Nanami adjusts his tie. A small movement. A finger and thumb at the knot, setting it straight.

"See you," he says.

He turns and walks off down the wet street, towards the station, pale suit going grey in the dark, not hurrying, exactly on time.

Kusakabe is watching the milk van. He raises a hand without looking.

---

## Chapter 11. The Offer

The September meeting takes eleven minutes.

It's in a room on the second floor at headquarters that has a number on the door and nothing else. A table. Four chairs. A window onto the back of another building, where someone has left a single work glove on a ledge. Okabe sits on one side with his clipboard. Next to him is a woman from Personnel with a laptop and reading glasses on a chain. Kusakabe sits on the other side. Nobody has put his chair in the middle of the room this time. He notices that, and is annoyed at himself for being grateful.

"Formal reprimand," the woman reads. "Twelve months from June. Status: active."

"Yes."

"Complaint from the Office of Field Conduct. May. Status: on file."

"Yes."

"Grade allowance review, originally scheduled for August." She scrolls. "Deferred to August next year. Subject to conduct."

"Yes."

She looks up over the glasses. She seems to have expected him to say something else. People often do.

"Is there anything you'd like to add to the record?"

"No."

She types something. It's short. He wonders what it is. *Subject declined to add anything. Subject is very good at it.*

Okabe turns a page on his clipboard and turns it back, which is what Okabe does instead of talking. When the woman closes the laptop, Okabe says, "Thank you, Kusakabe-san," and it comes out almost kind, and then he seems to hear it and straightens his cardigan, which is green today.

That's all. Eleven minutes, and nothing in them he didn't already know.

He knew it in June. He did the arithmetic in Yaga's office with the Zen'in letter on the desk. But a thing said in June is a thing that might still change. A thing typed into a laptop in September has stopped moving. It's set, like concrete.

*A year. Fine. It's only money.*

He walks down the stairs. He tells himself that again on every landing. It doesn't get truer, but it gets quicker to say.

---

There's a man waiting on the front steps.

Kusakabe sees him from inside the glass doors. Fifties, silver hair cut very short. A navy suit that fits him the way suits fit people who have them made. A furled umbrella with a wooden handle hooked over one arm, though the sky is clear and has been for a week. He isn't looking at his phone. He isn't looking at anything. He's just standing, the way people who used to do this job stand. Weight even. Hands free.

He's waiting for someone. Kusakabe has a small cold feeling that it's him.

"Kusakabe-san," the man says, as he comes out.

*San.* Not *kun*. Not just *Kusakabe*. It's a small thing. He hates that he notices it.

"Yes."

"Kurosawa." A card, held out with both hands. Thick paper, cream, a name in plain black type and under it *Hoshino Security*. No logo. No crest. Money that doesn't need to tell you it's money. "I wonder if I could buy you a coffee. Ten minutes. I'll keep to it."

"Who told you I'd be here."

Kurosawa smiles, a little ruefully. "Someone who talks more than they should. It's a small world, ours." He corrects himself. "Yours. I'm out of it."

Kusakabe looks at the card. Then at the street. There's no reason to go. There's no reason not to. He has nothing until four.

*Ten minutes. I'll keep to it.* People only say that when they know they won't need to.

---

The coffee shop is round the corner. Chalkboard menu, wooden counter, a young man with a beard who weighs the beans. Kurosawa orders without looking at the board. Kusakabe says, "Whatever's quick."

It comes in a white cup with a saucer. He drinks some. It tastes of something. Not of the can, not of the jar. Of whatever coffee is supposed to taste of, presumably, when nobody is cutting corners. He doesn't know what to do with it. He puts it down.

"I was a Grade 2," Kurosawa says. "Fourteen years. Kyoto, then Tokyo. Never very good. Good enough." He stirs his cup, though he hasn't put anything in it. "Then one day I stopped. Nobody minded. That's the thing they don't tell you. Nobody minds."

Kusakabe says nothing. Kurosawa doesn't seem to need him to.

"I run security for the Takamine family now. You may know the name."

He does, vaguely. Shipping, a long time ago. Property now. A name on the side of buildings near the bay.

"Old money brings old grudges," Kurosawa says. "And old grudges bring what you'd expect. The family has a house in Hayama, by the sea. Another in the city. The grandmother is sensitive. Not a sorcerer. Just sensitive. She sees things at the edges, and she doesn't like it, and she can afford not to like it." He sets the spoon down. "We keep a resident sorcerer. The current one is retiring. His knees."

"Mm."

"We'd like it to be you."

There it is. Laid on the table between the cups, quite gently.

Kusakabe looks at it.

*Why me,* he wants to ask, and doesn't, because he can guess. A Grade 1 with no clan to object. A Grade 1 with a record getting crowded. A Grade 1 whose allowance has just been put in a drawer for a year. Somebody at headquarters has been talking, and Kurosawa has been listening, and here they are.

"Someone pencilled a question on your file last year," Kurosawa says, as if he'd heard. "About a tunnel in Gunma. I've read the incident. I don't think you froze."

It lands somewhere Kusakabe didn't know was open. He keeps his face where it is.

Kurosawa takes a folded sheet from his inside pocket and puts it beside Kusakabe's saucer. He doesn't push it across. He just leaves it there, within reach.

Kusakabe unfolds it.

A salary. It's a little under twice what he makes now. A flat in Hayama, staff accommodation, rent covered. A car. Medical cover, comprehensive, *for the employee and any dependants*. Four days on, three off, on a rota.

"Incidents," Kusakabe says.

"Four in six years. Nothing above Grade 3. You'd be bored."

"I'm good at bored."

"I know," Kurosawa says. "That's partly why I'm here."

He lists the rest the way a doctor gives good news. No students. No higher-ups. No headquarters. No panels. "You'd answer to me. I answer to Mrs. Takamine. Mrs. Takamine answers to nobody, which is restful, once you get used to it."

Kusakabe reads the sheet again. The number doesn't change. He half expected it to.

He thinks of Ono's invoice, which comes on the fifth. He thinks of the thickening powder, which costs more than it has any right to. He thinks of the reed diffuser by his sister's shoe cabinet, which he replaces every two months, and which suddenly seems like a very small thing to have been worried about.

*Any dependants.*

"We understand you may have family commitments," Kurosawa says. Lightly. Not prying. Just leaving a door open.

"No," Kusakabe says.

It comes out flat and immediate, the way it's supposed to. Some things you don't put on a table in a coffee shop. Not even a good one.

Kurosawa nods, as if that were a perfectly ordinary answer, which perhaps it is.

And then, for a moment, it's very easy.

He can see it. A flat by the sea. A car he wouldn't drive. A grandmother who sees things at the edges, and a man with a sword who stands near her so she doesn't have to.
