Nobody waiting for his attendance. Nobody's file to sign. Ono paid. The powder paid. Everything paid, and something left over, and no meeting next September.

His mouth opens.

"When would I—"

He stops.

He doesn't know why. Nothing happens. Nobody comes in. The young man with the beard is still weighing beans. His hand has gone to his coat pocket on its own and is resting there on nothing, the way it does.

"End of the month," Kurosawa says, quite as if the sentence had been finished. "There's no hurry. Take a few weeks. Think about it properly."

"I'll think about it."

"That's all I ask." Kurosawa stands, and leaves money on the counter that more than covers it, and picks up his umbrella. "I'll send you my number. My own, not the office."

At the door he turns back, as if he's just remembered. "One thing. The family entertains at weekends. Sundays can't always be guaranteed."

Kusakabe hears it. He puts it somewhere, and doesn't look at it.

Kurosawa keeps to the ten minutes. Kusakabe checks. Nine and a half.

His phone buzzes before he's reached the corner. *Kurosawa. Hoshino Security. Whenever you're ready.*

He saves it. He tells himself that's just manners.

---

That night he makes a list.

He doesn't make lists. He's not a list man. But he finds the Midori Home Care envelope from last month on the kitchen counter, empty, and turns it over, and there's a pen in the drawer that works on the third try.

He draws a line down the middle.

On the left he writes the number. Then he crosses it out and writes it again, more carefully. The first time he wrote it the way he wanted it to be and the second time he wrote it the way it was. It's the same number. He just wanted to be sure.

Under it: *Flat. Rent free.* Under that: *Car.* Under that: *Medical. Dependants.*

Under that, after a while: *No Okabe.*

He looks at that one for some time. It seems petty. He leaves it.

*No meetings. No panels. No Froze.*

On the right he writes nothing at all for a long time.

The fridge hums. Somebody in the next building is watching a quiz show with the window open. He can hear the buzzer, and a studio audience making the sound audiences make when someone gets something wrong.

On the left, at the bottom, he writes: *No students.*

He looks at it. It's true. It goes in the left column. That's where it obviously goes.

He crosses it out. He doesn't put it on the right, either. It doesn't seem to belong in either.

At one in the morning the envelope has six things on the left and none on the right, and one crossed out in the middle. He goes to bed with the feeling of a man who's worked very hard on something and got it wrong in a way he can't find.

---

Ono rings on Tuesday at half past twelve.

He answers on the first ring. He has done that since December. Nobody has mentioned it, including him.

"Ono."

"Yes."

"Doctor came this morning. About the legs." Ono never starts with hello. She starts with the thing. "He says physio would help. Keep the joints moving. Stop it getting worse."

"Okay."

"Twice a week, he says. The agency has a woman. She's good. I've seen her work." A pause, which for Ono is a long speech. "It's not on the plan. It'd be extra."

"How much."

She tells him. Per session. He multiplies it by two, and then by four, and then by twelve, standing in the corridor outside the staff room with one hand on the wall. Somewhere behind him, a printer starts up and then thinks better of it.

"Can I let you know."

"Yes." Then, after a moment: "She'd like it, I think. She liked the last one. In hospital. Before me."

He didn't know there'd been a last one. He didn't know she'd liked anything, in hospital.

"Okay," he says. "Thanks."

Ono hangs up. She always hangs up first. It saves time.

That night he takes the envelope out again and writes on the left: *Physio. Twice.*

Then he sits with the pen and the number and does it all over again, properly this time. Tax. Insurance. The rent he wouldn't be paying here. The rent he'd still be paying at his sister's. Ono's hours. The night carer. The physio woman, twice a week, every week, for as long as there are weeks.

It comes out good. It comes out good with tax, and good without the car, and good even when he pretends the flat isn't free. He tries it three ways. It's good every time.

*That's the problem with a good number. You can't argue with it. You just have to look at it.*

---

Maki hits him on Wednesday.

That isn't news. She hits him most days. But on Wednesday she hits him on the second exchange, high on the shoulder, before he's even properly set. He feels it go all the way down his arm and out of his fingertips.

He steps back. Resets.

She doesn't come in again. She stands there with the practice sword low, looking at him.

"You're somewhere else," she says.

"I'm here."

"You're not." She tilts her head. Her glasses catch the light. "Where are you?"

*By the sea, apparently. With a grandmother.*

"Thinking," he says.

"About what."

He considers several answers and gives her none of them. He lifts his sword.

She looks at him a moment longer, the way she looks at a fault in a weapon she can't find yet. Then she comes in, and this time he's there for it, and the next ten minutes go the way they're supposed to. But she was right, and they both know she was right, and when he goes back to the third step his shoulder has already started to stiffen.

On the grass, Panda rolls his head towards him. "Sensei's got a face on."

"I've always got a face on."

"A different one."

Inumaki, on the bottom step, looks up at Kusakabe and then away. "Tuna mayo," he says, and tilts his hand. It could go either way.

*Even the boy who doesn't talk can tell.*

He doesn't tell them. It's not theirs. It isn't even, yet, a thing that has happened. It's a sheet of paper in his inside pocket that he keeps meaning to take out and leave at home and doesn't.

---

The nights go like this.

Thursday: the envelope, the pen. He adds the cost of moving. He adds a deposit he wouldn't need. He takes it off again. Two in the morning.

Friday: he doesn't take the envelope out. He lies in bed and does it in his head instead, which is worse, because in his head there's no line down the middle. The numbers just go round.

Saturday: he looks up Hayama on his phone. Photographs of a small harbour. White boats. A long beach with the sea coming in very flat and very blue. A mountain in the background that might be Fuji on a clear day, and is, in the photographs, always a clear day. He looks for a long time. He doesn't know what he's looking for. Then he looks up the route from Hayama to his sister's flat.

Bus to Zushi. Train. Change. Train. Change. An hour and fifty-two minutes, if everything runs.

From here it's twenty. Six stops.

He puts that on the right side of the envelope. *Twenty minutes.* It's the first thing on the right.

He looks at the two columns. Six things, seven now, on the left. One on the right. It ought to be easy. The left is heavier. Anyone could see that.

He sits there and finds that it isn't easy at all.

---

On Friday afternoon Yaga stops him in the corridor by the stairs.

"Kusakabe."

"Mm."

"Second-years' eligibility forms."

"Friday."

"It is Friday."

"Tonight, then."

Yaga nods once. That's the whole conversation. He goes on down the corridor, big and slow, in his dark glasses, with a cardboard tube of something under his arm. He doesn't look back. He never does.

Kusakabe stands at the top of the stairs and watches him go.

His hand goes to his pocket. The thing that isn't there is there.

*If I go, he won't say a word.*

He knows that the way he knows the weight of his own sword. Yaga will sign whatever needs signing. He'll find someone else to turn up for the second-years. He won't mention his sister, or Takeru, or a kitchen, years ago, or anything else. He's never mentioned it. He's not going to start now.

*That's worse,* Kusakabe thinks. *If he'd ask for it, I could say no.*

The debt doesn't come with terms. There's nothing written down. There's no number. He could put it on the envelope, and it wouldn't fit in either column, and he wouldn't know what to write. He's tried. On Thursday night he wrote a *Y* on the right side, small, and then scribbled it out until the pen went through the paper. Some things don't go on paper. Not even on the back of an envelope that's going in the bin.

But he knows this much. You don't owe a man that much and then go and live by the sea.

Not because Yaga would mind. Because he'd be somewhere else. And the debt would be here. And that's not how it works. You stay where the thing is owed. You stay where it can be called in, even if nobody ever calls it.

He doesn't think it in those words. He doesn't think it in words at all. He just stands at the top of the stairs with his hand in his pocket. Then a first-year comes up past him and says *excuse me*, and then he goes and does the eligibility forms.

---

Sunday. Ono hands over at the door.

"Physio," she says. "You decided?"

"Not yet."

She nods and writes something in the rabbit notebook. He'd like to know what. He doesn't ask.

His sister is in the window chair. The lavender is where it always is. The car park is full of Sunday cars. He makes the tea, thickened, too weak, and holds the cup while she drinks. She makes a face at the powder as she does every week, and he agrees with her as he does every week.

It's a good day. She knows him from the door.

He sits with her through the afternoon. She talks about the dog upstairs, which has got worse. He listens. He tells her the dog is probably lonely. She says, "Everyone's lonely. They don't all bark about it."

Then she looks at him properly. Her eyes are very clear today.

"You're doing sums," she says.

He blinks. "How can you tell."

"Your mouth goes small." She purses hers, to show him. It's not flattering. "You've done it all afternoon."

He doesn't say anything. He has nothing ready for this.

She turns back to the window. Outside, a man is trying to get a pushchair into the boot of a car and losing.

"We should go to the sea," she says, out of nowhere.

Something in his chest goes very still.

"Too much sand," he says.

"There's always too much sand. That's how you know it's the sea." She watches the man and the pushchair. "Not now. When it's warm. You could push me along the front."

"Maybe."

She nods, satisfied with *maybe*, and in ten minutes she's forgotten it. He can see it go. One moment it's there in her face, the sea, the front, the warm. The next her face is just looking at the car park.

He hasn't.

He sits there with the cup going cold in his hands. An hour and fifty-two minutes, if everything runs. Four days on, three off, on a rota. And Kurosawa at the door of the coffee shop, umbrella on his arm, almost as an afterthought: *Sundays can't always be guaranteed.*

He looks at it now.

Some Sundays, she'd be in this chair and he wouldn't be in that one. Ono would tell her *work*. She'd forget it. Next week she'd forget it again. And somebody would pay for all of it, lavishly, very well, and the chair would be empty anyway.

He could bring her to the sea. In theory. With the money. With a car he doesn't drive and a carer who'd come, and a flat with a ramp, and a window chair moved to another window.

Ono's rule. *Don't move her from the window.* Ono said it like a statement about herself. *I don't do that.*

He doesn't either.

On the train home he takes out the envelope and writes on the right, under *Twenty minutes*: *Sundays.*

That's two.

---

He calls Kurosawa on the last Thursday of the month, at lunchtime, from the bench outside the old archive room, where nobody comes.

He's been ready to call for a week and a half. Every day he's been ready. He's not sure what took so long. Probably nothing. Probably just him.

"Kusakabe-san." Kurosawa sounds pleased to hear from him. He sounds like a man who is pleased to hear from most people, and means it, and is good at his job.

"It's no," Kusakabe says.

A pause. Not a long one.

"I'm sorry to hear that. May I ask why?"

"No."

Kurosawa laughs, a small real laugh. "Fair enough. That was always going to be the answer to that one." Then, more quietly: "The position's open until the end of the month. After that, I'll have to look elsewhere. If anything changes."

"It won't."

"Then I wish you well. Truly." A beat. "Look after your knees."

He hangs up. Kusakabe sits there with the phone in his hand.

He waits to feel something. Relief, maybe. Or the other thing. He gets neither. He gets a bench, and a radiator that's warm at one end, and a window with a crack in the corner he's never noticed.

After a while he makes the other call.

Mr. Hasegawa at Midori answers on the fourth ring, sounding like a man with a mask under his chin. Kusakabe asks for the physio. Once a week. Thursdays. Starting next week.

"The doctor's note says twice," Hasegawa says. Not unkindly. Just reading.

"Once," Kusakabe says. "For now."

"For now," Hasegawa agrees, and writes it down. Kusakabe can hear the pen.

That's what the left column was. Not a flat by the sea. Not a car. One afternoon a week of a woman moving his sister's legs, so that they don't get worse. He could have bought the second one. He could have bought both and a good deal more. He's just decided not to.

He goes through his own outgoings that evening looking for something to cut, so it could be twice. There isn't much of him to cut. A phone plan. Lunch. He cuts the phone plan to the cheapest one. It isn't enough. It's a start.

*Once a week,* he thinks. *She'll have to make do with half as much not getting worse.*

---

At eleven o'clock that night he's lying in bed in the dark, and it opens again.

He doesn't open it. It just opens, the way a cupboard door does in an old flat, on its own, with a small click.

The position's open until the end of the month.

It's the twenty-seventh.

He lies there and goes through it all again. The number. The flat. The physio twice. No Okabe. No September. The coffee in the white cup that tasted of something.

Then the other side. Twenty minutes. Sundays. A *Y*, scribbled out until the pen went through.

*Two things,* he thinks. *Against six. Seven.*

*But they're heavier.*

He isn't sure that's true. He's sure, and then he isn't. He can feel himself starting to do the sum again, his mouth going small in the dark, and it occurs to him that he could do this every night for the rest of his life. He could lie here and add it up and take it away and add it again, until the number was a ghost he kept in the flat, like the stain. Until it had a corner of its own.

He reaches for his phone on the floor by the bed. The screen lights his hand, white.

Contacts. K.

*Kurosawa. Hoshino Security.*

His thumb hangs over it. Call. Message. Edit. It would be so easy. *Sorry for the late hour. I've reconsidered.* Kurosawa would be gracious. Kurosawa would be gracious at midnight. That's what you'd be paying for.

He holds his thumb there for a long time.

*Decided. Done. No more.*

He doesn't feel decided. He does it anyway.

Edit. Delete contact. *Are you sure?*

He isn't. He presses it.

The name goes. The list closes up over the gap as if nothing had ever been there. Kawamura, Kimura, the dispatch line. Nobody misses anyone, in a contacts list.

He puts the phone face up on the floor. After a few seconds the screen goes dark on its own.

It's still a lot of money. It's still physio once a week instead of twice. It'll still be a lot of money tomorrow, and in August, and next September in a room with a number on the door. Nothing about that has changed. He's just stopped adding it up.

Through the gap in the curtain, the streetlight lays a thin pale line across the ceiling. He follows it into the corner, above the wardrobe.

The stain is there. The same shape. Exactly where he left it.

He lies on his back and looks at it for a long time.

---

## Chapter 12. Rumours

The first time he hears it, it's a wet Tuesday in June, and it comes from Ogawa.

Ogawa is a Grade 2 in his late fifties who has been at the school longer than the radiators. He teaches basic warding to whoever is put in front of him, keeps a toothpick in the corner of his mouth that he never seems to use, and knows everything first. Nobody knows how. Kusakabe's theory is that he doesn't go home.

"Sendai," Ogawa says, to the staff room, which at this hour is Kusakabe and a kettle. "High-school boy. Ate one."

Kusakabe is at his desk in the corner with a monthly eligibility form. He doesn't look up.

"Ate one what."

"A finger."

That's when he looks up.

---

Everyone in this line of work knows about the fingers. You learn about them early, the way children on the coast learn where the high ground is.

Twenty of them. Sukuna's. The King of Curses, a thousand years dead and still not finished with anyone. Nobody could destroy the fingers, so they were sealed and scattered, and some of them are used as wards, because something that poisonous keeps smaller things away.

Nobody eats one. It isn't a thing people do. It would be like drinking bleach to see what colour it is.

"He's dead, then," Kusakabe says.

"No." Ogawa moves the toothpick to the other side. "That's the thing. He's fine. Walking around. Talking. Sukuna's in him, and he's fine." He says it with the bright, unhealthy pleasure of a man who has news nobody else has yet. Not pleasure about the boy. Pleasure about the news. "Gojo's got him."

*Gojo's got him.*

Kusakabe has heard that sentence about a lot of things. In his experience it means the thing is now better and worse at the same time.

He goes back to the form. Maki: eligible for Grade 2 accompaniment. He looks at the line for longer than it needs.

---

The rest of it comes over the next few days, a piece at a time, from people who weren't there.

The higher-ups sentenced the boy to death. Straight away. Nobody argued, apparently, because there was nothing to argue about. A child with the King of Curses inside him is not a child. It's a door.

Then Gojo argued.

Nobody knows exactly what Gojo said. Ogawa thinks he shouted. The dispatch coordinator, who heard it from someone at headquarters, thinks he didn't have to. Either way the sentence wasn't cancelled. It was suspended. The boy will be allowed to live until he has eaten all the fingers. Then they'll kill him, and Sukuna with him, all at once.

"Waste not," Ogawa says.

Kusakabe walks home that evening slower than usual.

*So that's the plan,* he thinks. *Feed a schoolboy the King of Curses one piece at a time. Then kill him. On a schedule.*

*And that's the version somebody fought for.*

He can guess what the other version was. Old men who don't raise their voices. Over by the weekend.

He finds he can't decide which version he'd rather work next to.

---

The boy is fifteen. His name is Itadori Yuji. He's enrolled. He's a first-year, in Gojo's class, with the Fushiguro boy and a new girl from the countryside who is said to carry nails and a hammer and to have opinions about Tokyo.

Yaga tested him. That's the story. Sat him down in the office with one of the dolls, the kind that hit you if your cursed energy wavers. The boy didn't flinch and didn't waver, and Yaga let him in.

"He passed," Ogawa says.

"Course he did," Kusakabe says. "He's got the best cursed energy in the country inside him."

Ogawa laughs. Kusakabe wasn't joking.

That night he lies on his back and does what he always does with a new job. He works out the grade.

Grade 1 is a lot. People forget that, because of Gojo, because of how easily some things happen around Gojo. A Grade 1 can walk into most places and walk out again. Special grade is different. It isn't the top of the ladder. It's the thing that kicks the ladder over.

And now one of them is sitting in a classroom on the first floor, behind a fifteen-year-old's face, being taught theory.

*He's not even in the room. He's in a boy who's in the room.*

*That's not better. That's just more walls.*

---

He sees the boy once, that June. Or he sees the hair.

It's at the far end of the long corridor in the old building, past the noticeboards. Gojo is walking ahead, talking with his hands. Beside him, a head of pink hair, short, bright as a warning label. A school uniform with a hood. A voice that carries, laughing at something Gojo said, the easy laugh of someone who hasn't yet learned what this place is.

Kusakabe stops.

There's an empty classroom on his left. He goes into it. He stands just inside the door, by a stack of chairs, and reads a timetable from April until the voices have gone past and down the stairs.

His circle is out. He didn't let it out. It's sitting at the length of his arm, the way it does on jobs.

He pulls it back in. It takes a moment longer than it should.

*It's a corridor,* he tells himself. *You're in a corridor at school, hiding from a first-year.*

*Yes. Correct. That's what's happening.*

---

At the start of July, Ijichi drives him to a job in Chiba and doesn't talk for forty minutes, which is normal, and then talks, which isn't.

"I drove them," Ijichi says.

Kusakabe waits. He's heard the outline already. Everyone has. A first-year dead on a job, ten days ago. He hasn't heard it from anyone who was there.

"The first-years. Last month. To the detention centre." Ijichi's hands are at ten and two on the wheel, exactly. "There was a womb. In the building. Turning into something. They said Grade 1, possibly more. They sent the three of them. First-years. Three weeks in."

"Who sent them?"

Ijichi doesn't answer. That's an answer.

The rest comes out in short pieces, between junctions. A juvenile detention centre, the inmates gone or dead. The building twisted inside. The thing in it was special grade by the time anyone looked properly. The boy with the finger in him sent the other two out and stayed. Then something happened that Ijichi only knows from the reports. Sukuna came up, and fought, and won, and wouldn't give the body back. And the boy, from inside, made him give it back anyway. The only way he could.

"His heart," Ijichi says. "Sukuna took it out. Before he went under again. So the boy came back to a body with no heart in it."

He indicates left, carefully, though there's nobody behind them.

"He's dead," Ijichi says. "Itadori-kun. He's dead."

Kusakabe looks out of the window. Rice fields. A heron standing in one of them, on one leg, as if it's been told to wait there and has no plans to ask why.

He waits to feel something. Something comes.

It's relief.

It's not large. It's the feeling of a hand he didn't know was clenched coming slowly open. A boy is dead, and Kusakabe's shoulders come down half an inch, and he notices them do it, and doesn't like noticing it, and they stay down anyway.

*Fifteen,* he thinks. *Good kid, probably. Bad luck. And I'm sitting here glad the door shut.*

He doesn't say anything. Ijichi doesn't seem to need him to.

"Gojo-san was away," Ijichi says, after a while. "When it was decided. He came back and found out." He swallows. "He said he'd talk to me about it later."

"Has he?"

"Not yet." Ijichi turns into a car park. "I've been waiting. It's been quite a long later."

---

The summer goes on.

That's the strange part. A boy dies, and the summer doesn't notice. The cicadas start. The training yard dries out and goes hard under the feet. Maki leaves the practice sword on the third step every afternoon and he picks it up, and gets hit, and occasionally doesn't.

The Fushiguro boy and the girl with the hammer walk round the school with faces like closed shutters. Kusakabe gives them the space you give anyone who's carrying something heavy up a stair.

Panda tells him, one afternoon on the grass, that he never got to meet the boy.

"Everyone says he was nice," Panda says.

"Yeah."

"I'd have liked a new first-year. Someone to be older than."

Kusakabe doesn't say anything. There's nothing in him he'd want Panda to hear.

The only person in the building who seems untouched is Gojo. Which, Kusakabe thinks, is either the worst thing about Gojo or the most Gojo thing about him, and he can't tell which. Gojo walks the corridors humming. He buys snacks in bulk. Once, in August, Kusakabe passes him at the gate with a carrier bag from a rental shop, the plastic stretched square around a stack of DVDs.

"Film night," Gojo says, cheerful, without stopping.

"Right," Kusakabe says.

He doesn't think about it again. Later, he'll wish he had. It would have saved him a surprise. Not much else.

---

In September there's something in Kanagawa.

It arrives as less than a rumour. A school. An assembly hall. A curse that does things to people's shapes. A boy who died, or a lot of people who died, depending on who's telling it. Nanami's name is on the file, and the file is sealed.

Ogawa doesn't know anything about it. That's how Kusakabe knows it's bad.

---

The Goodwill Event with Kyoto is in September too. Two days. The Kyoto students come to Tokyo, and the two schools spend the first day trying to beat each other in the woods behind the school, and everybody calls it an exchange.

His three are on the team. Maki has been training for it as if it's personal, which, since her sister is on the other side, it is.

He's supposed to watch. Every teacher in the building is supposed to watch.

The week before, the dispatch coordinator puts a sheet in front of him. A dam in Ibaraki. Something in the overflow tunnel. Two days, possibly three.

"Somebody has to cover the region," she says, "while everyone else is watching children hit each other."

"Fine," he says.

She looks at him as though she's waiting for him to argue. He doesn't. He takes the sheet. Walking away, he notices that he's not disappointed, and that this is not because he dislikes watching children hit each other.

*It's the woods,* he thinks. *Two schools. One of them with him in it. No.*

*Wait. He's dead. He's not in it.*

*Still no.*

---

The thing in the overflow tunnel is slow and stupid and takes most of a day to find. He cuts it in the dark with water running round his ankles.

At four in the afternoon, the young auxiliary driving him back gets a call. She listens. Her face goes very still.

"There's a curtain over the school," she says.

"Whose?"

"Nobody knows. Curse users. Curses." She's already turning onto the expressway. "It's over the woods. They can't get in. Gojo-san can't get in."

That last part sits in the car with them for a while.

It's two hours to Tokyo. By the time the radio says it's over, they're still forty minutes out. When they come up the hill, the gate is open and there are cars everywhere, and lights in the trees. People are walking in pairs with the slow, careful steps of people who've been running.

Behind the school, where the woods go up the slope, something is missing.

He stands at the top of the path and looks at it in the last of the light. A line has been taken out of the forest. Straight. Clean. Trees on either side, and between them nothing, a long bare gap running up the hill, as if someone had laid a ruler across it and pressed down.

*Gojo,* he thinks. *Got in eventually, then.*

He can smell it. Raw wood, and turned earth, and something underneath that he doesn't have a name for and doesn't want one.

---

The pieces come in over the next three days, from everyone, all at once and none of them the same.

A special grade. Here. In the woods behind the school, with the students. A curse made of trees. Or flowers. Or both. Ogawa says it had branches growing out of its eyes. A Kyoto auxiliary says no, the eyes were fine, it was the arms. The dispatch coordinator says it didn't matter what it looked like, it nearly killed a Kyoto third-year and the boy.

"Which boy?"

She looks at him.

"Itadori," she says. "You didn't hear?"

---

He hears it from Panda, in the end. On the grass, the next afternoon, Panda flat on his back with patches of fur missing on one side and a bandage on one ear.

"He's alive," Panda says.

"Who."

"Itadori." Panda turns his head. "Gojo-sensei hid him. All summer. Training him. Nobody knew. Not even Fushiguro. Then on the first day of the event he wheeled a big box into the meeting, and Itadori jumped out of it and said surprise."

Kusakabe looks at him.

"He jumped out of a box."

"It was very awkward," Panda says happily. "Gojo-sensei was supposed to explain first. He didn't. So Itadori just stood there holding the box lid, and the Kyoto lot thought it was a trick, and Kugisaki cried and then pretended she didn't."

"Right."

"He's fun," Panda says. "You'd like him."

"Doubt it."

"You haven't met him."

Kusakabe watches the cherry trees, which are just starting to go yellow at the edges.

"Let's keep it that way," he says.

Panda doesn't laugh. He looks at Kusakabe for a moment, the way he sometimes does, as though he's turning something over to see the label. Then he turns his face back to the sky.

---

What Panda doesn't tell him, Ogawa does.

Inumaki used his voice on the special grade. Told it to go away, or to stop, or both. Against something that size, it was like shouting at a lorry. It went. He coughed up enough blood that somebody had to help him walk.

Kusakabe finds him the next morning on the bottom step, collar zipped to his nose, a fresh white dressing visible under the line of his jaw.

He sits down on the third step. He doesn't ask. Asking would mean Inumaki having to answer.

After a while, Inumaki takes the flat tin of lozenges out of his pocket and holds it up, sideways, and gives it a small shake. It rattles. Half full.

"Yeah," Kusakabe says. "Good."

That seems to be the whole report. It's better than most he reads.

Maki, who fought her sister in the woods, says nothing about any of it. Kusakabe doesn't ask her either. She leaves the practice sword on his step that afternoon, the same as always, and hits him rather harder than usual, and he lets her.

---

The staff room talks about nothing else for a fortnight.

A Kyoto third-year called Todo, the size of a door, fought the special grade alongside the boy and now calls him *brother*. Nobody knows why, including, apparently, the boy.

The curse users took things from the vaults while the curtain was up. Sealed things. Some say wombs. Some say fingers. Ogawa says both. Nobody from headquarters has said anything, which, again, is how you know.

The curtain was made to keep one person out, and it was Gojo, and it worked for a while.

The second day of the event was baseball. Somebody tells Kusakabe this as though it's an ordinary thing that happened after the other thing. Tokyo won.

And there's the other rumour. The ugly one. That before the event, the old man who runs the Kyoto school told his students to find the vessel in the woods, now that he was alive again, and kill him. Quietly. An accident in a training exercise. Nobody would have been surprised.

Ogawa tells this one in a whisper, which is unlike him, and then looks at the door.

Kusakabe sits with it at his desk afterwards.

He knows what he's supposed to think. That it's disgusting. Old men sending children to kill a child. And it is.

But underneath that, in the place where he keeps the things he doesn't say, there's a smaller voice, and the smaller voice is saying: *I understand them.*

Not the method. The fear. Somebody looked at the King of Curses walking around a school in a hoodie, and did the sum, and didn't like the answer.

He doesn't like that he understands it. He goes on understanding it anyway.

---

Gojo finds him at the end of September, by the vending machines on the ground floor, which isn't hiding, but is near it.

"Isn't it great?" Gojo says.

He doesn't say what. He doesn't have to. He's practically glowing. Blindfold on, hands in his pockets, rocking slightly on his heels like a man waiting for applause.

"Great," Kusakabe says.

"He's good, you know. Really good. He's going to be really, really good." Gojo leans on the machine. "You should come and watch him train sometime. He's got legs like you wouldn't believe. Threw a shot put halfway across a stadium, back at his old school. Before any of this."

"I'm busy."

"You're always busy."

"Yeah. It's a lot of work."

Gojo grins. It's not quite the grin he had last winter, handing out praise in the staff room. There's something underneath it that's harder and doesn't move.

Kusakabe hears himself say, "And the other one."

Gojo doesn't pretend not to understand. That's something.

"The other one stays where he is," Gojo says.

"Until."

"Until nothing." Gojo pushes off the machine. "I'm here."

He says it lightly. He says it as though it's a fact about the building, like the stairs or the roof. And the thing is, it is. Kusakabe has seen what happens when Gojo is here. He saw a line taken out of a forest.

Gojo pats him on the shoulder and walks off, humming.

Kusakabe stands by the machine and doesn't buy anything.

*That's the whole plan,* he thinks. *"I'm here."*

*It's a good plan. It's probably the best plan anyone's got.*

*It only has the one part.*

---

October. The boy is at the school now, properly. Not hidden. Not in a box.

Kusakabe starts to know where he is the way you know where a draught is coming from in a room. Without looking.

He knows the first-years train on the far side of the yard in the mornings. He knows the boy eats in the canteen at twelve sharp and goes back for more. He knows it the way he knows the layout of a job.

He keeps his distance. It isn't hard. A school is mostly corridors, and a corridor is mostly a choice about when to walk down it. He leaves the staff room two minutes later. He comes back by the outside stairs. Once, at the end of the long corridor, he sees pink hair coming the other way. He stops at a noticeboard and reads a fire drill procedure from start to finish, until the voices have gone round the corner.

*Fire drill. Assemble by the bins.* He's worked here six years and never known that.

His circle doesn't come out on its own again. He's careful about that. He keeps it folded small and tight.

But he can feel himself doing it, all day. Like holding a door shut with his shoulder, in a room where nobody else is pushing.

---

Ogawa, at the kettle, mid-October.

"Funny thing," Ogawa says. "I sat in on one of his theory lessons. Covering for Gojo. He asked me what happens to curses after you exorcise them. Where they go."

"What did you say?"

"I said nowhere. He said that was sad." Ogawa moves the toothpick. "The King of Curses is sitting in that boy, and he thinks it's sad what happens to curses."

Kusakabe waits for the rest. There isn't any. Ogawa just shakes his head, as if the boy is a puzzle that's got a piece missing, and pours his tea.

---

It's not panic. That's the thing he'd say, if anyone asked, which nobody does.

He sleeps. He eats. He takes the jobs on the board and comes back from them. He spars with Maki and goes home and sits on the sofa and gets up again.

The fear is just there. Steady. Quiet. Like the sound of your own breathing, which is always going and which you only hear when you think about it. And then for a while you can't stop hearing it.

A fifteen-year-old boy with a thousand-year-old monster folded up inside him, asleep, or pretending to be. And the only thing between the monster and everything else is the boy's own will, and Gojo.

Two things. A boy and a man.

He's seen what can happen to a boy. He heard about it in a car in July.

On Sunday, at his sister's, he checks the lock on the window twice.

She watches him do it from the chair.

"Expecting someone?" she says.

"No."

"You did that twice."

"Yeah."

She turns back to the window. "You always were a worrier," she says, which isn't true, and which he lets stand.

---

Nanami tries to tell him about the boy once, at midnight in an empty office building in Shinagawa. Kusakabe finds a reason to change the subject.

He's built a picture of Itadori Yuji in his head, piece by piece, over five months. He could draw it for you.

Pink hair. Fifteen. From Sendai. Eats a lot. Thanks people for things. Strong legs. Jumped out of a box. Died once. Fought a special grade in the woods. A Kyoto giant calls him brother. Thinks it's sad what happens to curses. Laughs loudly in corridors.

Every one of those pieces came from someone else's mouth.

He could pick the boy out of a crowd by the hair. He couldn't tell you what colour his eyes are. He couldn't tell you what his voice sounds like when it isn't laughing, or whether he's left-handed, or which foot he puts his weight on when he's frightened.

He knows how to read a person in about four seconds. He's never let himself have the four seconds.

*It's fine,* he thinks. *You don't need to know the shape of a door. You just need to know what's behind it.*

---

Once, in late October, it rains properly for the first time in a month, and he changes his route.

His usual way from the staff room to the second-years' classroom goes along the covered walkway between the buildings. Dry, short, and past the first-years' door.

That morning, as he comes out of the staff room, he hears the first-years ahead of him on the walkway. The girl, loudly, complaining about something. The Fushiguro boy, flat, saying one word. And the third voice, louder than both, laughing.

He stands in the doorway for a second.

Then he turns round and goes the other way. Down the back stairs, out the side door, along the outside of the old building where there's no roof. Round the storehouse. Up the slope. It takes five minutes longer. It's raining the whole time.

He comes into the classroom with his hair flat and his shoulders dark with water.

Maki looks up from her desk. She looks at his hair. At the window. At the covered walkway outside it, which is dry.

"You're wet," she says.

"Rain."

She looks at the walkway again. Then at him. For a moment he thinks she's going to say something.

She doesn't. She goes back to her book.

Panda, who is lying across two chairs at the back, says nothing either. But he's watching. Kusakabe can feel it the way you feel someone reading over your shoulder.

He sits down and opens the textbook to a page he's taught twice.

*Not in front of the kids,* he thinks. *Not like that.*

He doesn't do it again. Not that particular way. He gets better at timing instead.

---

The last week of October. Clear and cold and very blue.

He's crossing from the staff room to the car yard with his sword bag over his shoulder. A job in Saitama, small, done by evening. Ijichi will be waiting.

The path takes him along the edge of the courtyard in front of the old building. There's a ginkgo in the middle of it that drops its leaves all at once every year, on one particular night, as if it's agreed something with itself. It hasn't yet. It's gold all the way through and holding on.

Under it, three first-years.

The girl is sitting on the low wall, swinging one foot, explaining something with her hands. The Fushiguro boy is standing with his hands in his pockets, looking at the sky, pretending not to listen. And between them, his back half turned, a boy with pink hair, in the hooded uniform, bent over, laughing so hard he has to put his hands on his knees.

It isn't a big laugh. It's just a kid laughing at something his friend said. It carries across the courtyard, very clear in the cold air, and the girl throws a ginkgo leaf at him, and he laughs harder.

Kusakabe stops at the edge of the courtyard.

He could walk straight across. It's twenty metres. The path goes right past the tree. He could nod, the way he nods at everyone, and keep walking, and that would be that. Four seconds.

He stands there long enough to know he isn't going to.

The boy straightens up, still grinning, and starts to turn this way.

Kusakabe turns first, and takes the long way round.

---

# Part Three: Shibuya

## Chapter 13. Halloween

The job sheet says *Standby.*

One word, in the dispatch coordinator's small square handwriting. Under it: *Shibuya area (north). 31 Oct. 18:00 until crowds disperse. Kusakabe A. Panda (second year).*

Every big crowd night, the school puts a few people out. Crowds make things. Drink and noise and too many bodies, a lot of feeling in a small space with nowhere to go. Most nights, nothing comes of it. You stand on a corner for six hours and go home.

Standby is his favourite kind of work. You get paid to be somewhere.

*Grade 1 sorcerer,* he thinks. *Very highly trained. Paid to lean on a railing.*

The others in his homeroom are on other people's lists tonight, on other corners of the city. Only Panda is on his.

Panda has read the sheet over his shoulder in the staff car yard. Panda doesn't say anything at first. Then he does a small, contained thing with his whole body, like a dog that's heard the word *walk* and is trying to be dignified about it.

"Halloween," Panda says.

"I know what day it is."

"In *Shibuya*, sensei."

"It's a job."

"It's the one night of the year," Panda says, "I can walk down the middle of the street."

---

Ijichi drives them in at half past five, through traffic that thickens until it's less traffic than a car park that occasionally moves.

Ijichi has a list on his knee. Five standby points round the station, a pair at each. A number to call. He goes through it twice, in the same words both times, and Kusakabe lets him, because it's clearly helping one of them.

"It's a precaution," Ijichi says. "There's been nothing specific."

"Fine."

"Some unusual readings. Earlier in the week. Around the station. But nothing you could put on a sheet." He looks at Kusakabe in the mirror. "I'd have put it on the sheet."

"I know."

"I'm sure it's crowds."

"It's always crowds."

Ijichi lets them out on Meiji-dori, at the Harajuku end, near the Meiji-Jingumae station steps. North of everything. The far edge of the map.

*Good,* Kusakabe thinks. *The edge of the map is where I live.*

---

The street is already full.

Not full like a morning train. Full like a party nobody organised. People drift. People stop for no reason. People shout to each other across three metres of air as if it were a valley.

And everyone is someone else.

Witches. A great many witches. Nurses with fake blood down their aprons. A row of young men all dressed as the same plumber, moustaches drawn on, one of them already sitting on the kerb with his head between his knees. A man in a full shark suit, the head open at the front, his real face peering out of the teeth like a man looking out of a garage. A boy in a cardboard box painted to look like a vending machine, with a slot, which people keep putting coins into.

*At least he's earning,* Kusakabe thinks.

He finds a railing outside a closed shoe shop and leans on it. Sword bag over his shoulder. Dark coat. He looks, he assumes, like a tired man waiting for someone who's late.

Panda does not lean on anything. Panda walks out into the crowd like a man walking into warm sea.

It takes about four seconds.

"Oh my God."

"Is that real?"

"It's so *good*."

Three girls in matching devil horns surround him with their phones up. Panda poses. Panda knows how to pose. One paw on his hip, the other raised, and the girls scream, and then the plumbers want one, all of them, including the one on the kerb, who gets up for it.

"How do you see out of it?" one of the devils asks.

"It's a very good costume," Panda says.

"It *talks*."

"Battery," Panda says.

Kusakabe watches from the railing. Nobody asks him for a photograph. That suits him.

A woman in a witch's hat stops in front of him, though, a can of something sweet in her hand. She looks at the sword bag. She looks at his face.

"What are you meant to be?"

He thinks about it.

"A teacher," he says.

She laughs, delighted, as if it's the scariest thing she's heard all night, and goes on into the crowd repeating it. *A teacher.*

*Not a bad costume,* he thinks. *Took me years.*

Panda comes back after a while, slightly out of breath, with a small plastic pumpkin full of sweets that someone has given him.

"Sensei." He holds it out. "They think I'm a person in a panda."

"They think a lot of things."

"It's nice," Panda says. He says it simply, the way he says most things. "Walking about. Nobody looking twice." He thinks. "Well. Everybody looking twice. But nicely."

Kusakabe takes a sweet. It's a small orange chew shaped like a pumpkin and it tastes faintly of soap.

"Masamichi would hate this," Panda says, looking out over the street with great contentment.

At the name, the coin in Kusakabe's pocket gets a little heavier. It always does. He doesn't check it.

"Yeah," he says. "He would."

---

The circle stays folded. He doesn't need it out. He can feel the street the ordinary way, the way anyone could if they were paying attention, which almost nobody here is.

Noise. A sound system somewhere playing something with a lot of bass and not much else. A police officer up on a little platform with a loudspeaker, asking people politely, over and over, to keep moving, which nobody does. The convenience store on the corner with a queue out of the door and a handwritten sign: *NO TOILET.*

And underneath it, very faint, a kind of pressure. Like the air before weather. He notices it the way you notice a fridge humming only when it stops.

Except it doesn't stop.

*Crowds,* he tells himself. *It's always crowds.*

He looks at his phone. 18:51. Nothing from Ijichi.

He puts it away and watches a man dressed as a banana try to buy a crêpe with a credit card that doesn't work.

---

At seven o'clock, the pressure stops being faint.

He feels it before he sees anything. A drop in his stomach, like a lift going down too fast. Panda's head comes up at the same moment. Panda is looking south, down Meiji-dori, over all the hats and horns and ears.

"Sensei."

"I know."

Then he sees it.

Far down the street, over the rooftops, something is coming down out of the sky. Not falling. Being lowered. A darkness, slow and smooth, like ink poured very carefully into water and somehow staying in one piece. It spreads as it comes. It finds the tops of the buildings. It keeps going.

It's a curtain. He's put up a few hundred himself, in car parks and stairwells and empty schools, small black lids over small bad jobs.

This one is not small.

It goes on sinking until the station is gone under it, and everything round the station. He can't see the edge of it in either direction. Only a wall of black, curved like the top of a bowl turned over the city. The lights inside it go out. Not one by one. All at once, as if someone has simply decided they were never there.

The street goes quiet for a moment, the way a classroom does when a teacher walks in.

Then a few people laugh, because it must be part of it. Some kind of show. A man in a vampire cape takes a photo.

Then the screaming starts, somewhere down near the edge of it, and comes up the street towards them like something being passed hand to hand.

---

His phone rings. Ijichi.

"Kusakabe-san—" A burst of noise. "—curtain, over the station, it's—" Static. "—four hundred metres, roughly, they think—" Gone. Back. "—not ours. Not one of ours. Please hold your—"

The line drops.

He calls back. It rings out.

A second phone, Panda's, buzzing in Panda's paw. Panda reads it, holding it very close to his face. "Group message. 'Everyone stay where you are.'" A pause. Another buzz. "'Ignore last. Await instructions.'"

An auxiliary he doesn't know comes pushing up the pavement against the crowd, a young man in a suit with a radio clipped to his collar that's talking to itself in short bursts of nothing.

"—repeat, civilians cannot—"

"—who's at the east side, who's—"

"—sorcerers can pass, confirm, sorcerers can pass, civilians can't—"

"—a name, they're all saying a—"

The auxiliary sees the sword bag and stops. His face is very white. "Are you—?"

"Kusakabe. Grade 1."

"They're saying a name," the boy says. "The people stuck inside. At the edge. They're pressed up against it and they're all saying the same thing. Over and over." He swallows. "They're saying 'Bring Gojo Satoru.'"

"They know who that is?"

"No," the boy says. "That's the thing. They don't."

Then his radio says something that might be his name, and he goes, back towards the black.

*Civilians in, can't get out,* Kusakabe thinks. *Sorcerers can walk in. And somebody wants Gojo.*

*Somebody has built a very large room, and set a table, and invited one man.*

He notices his palms are cold. He wipes them on his coat. It doesn't help. It never helps.

The fear is at his ankles now, like water on a basement floor. That's how it usually starts. A cold line, low down. Something you could step out of, if you went now.

---

The crowd turns into a different thing.

Some of it runs north, past them, costumes coming apart as they go. A witch's hat in the road. A shark head, empty, rolling on its side. Some of it runs the other way, towards the curtain, because there are people inside it. Friends. A sister. A boyfriend who went to buy drinks.

Some of it just stands there and films.

Police whistles. Sirens, far off and getting closer and then, somehow, not arriving.

Kusakabe and Panda end up doing the only thing anyone has told them to do, which is stay where they are. That turns out to mean standing at the top of the station steps, keeping people from going down them all at once. It isn't sorcery. It's a job anyone with arms could do. He finds he's grateful for it.

"Don't run," he says, to a group of boys in matching zombie make-up. "Walk."

They walk. They look at Panda and walk faster.

A girl in a nurse's costume is on the steps, sitting, crying into her phone. "She's in there," she says, to nobody, to Kusakabe because he's nearest. "She went to the station to get cash. She's in there."

He doesn't know what to say. He never knows what to say.

"Stay off the steps," he says.

She looks at him as if he's said something unforgivable. Maybe he has. She gets up and goes. He doesn't watch where.

His phone buzzes. A text, not from Ijichi. From the dispatch line. *ALL STAFF HOLD POSITION. DO NOT ENTER. FURTHER ORDERS TO FOLLOW.*

Two minutes later, from the same number: *Disregard. Await team assignment.*

*Nobody knows,* he thinks. *That's what this is. A lot of people who don't know, telling each other.*

---

At 20:14, the radios all say the same thing at once, for the first time all night.

Gojo is here. Gojo has gone in.

Alone.

Kusakabe hears it from the radio, and from Panda's phone, and from a second auxiliary who comes running up the hill to tell them in person, out of breath, as if it were good news.

"Alone?" Panda says.

"Into the station," the auxiliary says. "That's what they wanted. They wanted him."

There's a feeling, when you hear Gojo has turned up. He's had it before. Everybody in the job has had it. It's like standing in a hospital corridor and seeing the senior doctor walk past. The thing is still bad. The thing is now somebody else's.

He waits for the feeling.

It comes. It's thinner than usual.

*He's the strongest there is,* he thinks. *He'll be fine. He's always fine.*

*Then why did they build a room for him.*

He doesn't like that thought. He puts it somewhere and doesn't look at it.

Panda is looking south, at the black dome, very still, his round head tilted a little to one side.

"He'll be fine," Panda says.

"Yeah."

"He's always fine."

"Yeah," Kusakabe says. He wonders which of them that was for.

---

The orders, when they come, come all at once, and from Ijichi, whose voice is very calm in the way it only gets when nothing is.

The curtain isn't the only one. There are others, inside it. Smaller ones, layered, like a set of boxes. Some of them let sorcerers in. Some of them don't. Somebody inside is holding them up: curse users, on the upper floors, at the exits, keeping them standing. Curses too. A lot of curses. Nobody knows how many. Gojo is inside the station and whatever is happening to him is happening where nobody can see.

The teams are to go in and take down the curtains. Find whoever is holding them. Open the way.

"Kusakabe-san, you and Panda-kun from Meiji-Jingumae. Down into the subway. Follow the line south, towards Shibuya. Clear what you find. There'll be—" Static. "—at the—" Static. "—on the radio as you go."

"Ijichi."

"Yes."

"How many are in there?"

A pause. A long one, for Ijichi.

"I don't know," Ijichi says. "I'm sorry. Nobody does."

The line holds, for once. Behind Ijichi, someone is shouting a set of coordinates and someone else is shouting that they're wrong.

"Okay," Kusakabe says, and hangs up.

He looks at the top of the steps going down into the station. The tiled walls, the lit sign, the escalator still running for nobody.

*I could be on a railing,* he thinks. *I was on a railing. I was good at it.*

The fear is at his knees.

"Sensei?" Panda says.

He unzips the sword bag. "Down," he says.

---

The station is lit and empty and wrong.

All the lights are on. The ticket gates are open, the little barriers folded back as if the whole system has given up asking for money. A newsagent's shutter is half down, magazines still on the rack under it.

They go down to the platforms. Nobody. A train standing with its doors open, all its lights on, a paper bag on one of the seats. A screen telling them, in cheerful letters, that the next service is delayed.

Panda jumps down onto the track bed first and offers a paw. Kusakabe climbs down by himself, which takes longer, and Panda waits without comment.

Then they walk south. Into the tunnel. The rails gleam a little way ahead in the work lights, and then don't.

It's a long walk. Most of a kilometre. His shoes on the gravel. Panda's feet, softer. The radio an auxiliary clipped to Panda's collar, saying things in pieces, none of them about here.

"—Dogenzaka side—"

"—can't get through, it's keyed against—"

"—anyone on the Fukutoshin—"

"That's us," Panda says, and presses the button, and says, "Panda. We're on the Fukutoshin. In the tunnel." The radio doesn't answer. Panda waits, and then lets go of the button. "They'll have heard."

"Sure."

Somewhere ahead, the tunnel is darker than a tunnel should be. A flat black across the whole round mouth of it, from the track bed to the roof. The edge of the big curtain, reaching down through the ground the way roots do.

They stop in front of it.

*Sorcerers can pass,* he thinks. *That's what the boy said.*

He puts his hand out. The black is cool and gives like the skin on cold milk. Then his hand is through, and his arm, and he follows it.

On the other side, the air is thicker. Like a room where too many people have been breathing for too long. And there are sounds now, ahead, that aren't the radio.

He lets the circle open.

---

They come along the roof of the tunnel first.

Five. Six. Low things, flat, many-legged, moving fast and close together. Grade 3s, maybe 4s. Small. A lot of them. His circle feels the first one cross the line before his eyes find it, and his hands answer. The sword is out and back, and the thing comes down on the rail in two parts and goes to smoke.

Then the others are in the circle all at once, and there's nothing to think about. That's the one mercy of it. Edge, weight, turn. Something wet across his cheek. Something grabbing at his coat and letting go because it has no hand any more. Behind him, a sound like a sack of rice hitting a wall, and then another. He knows without looking that it's Panda, and that Panda is fine, and that the things Panda is hitting are not.

It takes about forty seconds.

When it's done he's breathing hard and there's a nick on the back of his left hand that he didn't feel arrive. The tunnel smells of burnt hair.

"Sensei. You okay?"

"Fine."

He wipes his cheek with his sleeve. His hand is shaking. Only a little. Only the left one.

*Small ones,* he thinks. *That's the bad news. Small ones means there's a reason they were here. Small ones run in front of things.*

The fear is at his waist. He can feel it there, cold and level, and he can feel that it isn't going to go back down.

---

There's a service door in the tunnel wall, half open, light behind it. Beyond it, a concrete passage. The passage is not empty.

Civilians. Twenty, thirty, sitting against the wall, some still in costume. A man in a gorilla suit with the head off, holding it in his lap. A girl dressed as a cartoon cat, with a cut on her forehead. A woman in office clothes who was just going home.

They look up at Kusakabe. Then they look at Panda.

Nobody says *is that real* this time.

"Are you police?" the woman in office clothes says.

"Something like that."

"We came down from the street when it went dark. We thought we could walk out along the tracks." She points back at the tunnel. "But there's a—" She doesn't know the word. Nobody does. "It's black. Down there, and up on the stairs. You can't go through it."

"I know."

"What is it?"

He thinks about what he could say. *It's a barrier made of cursed energy, keyed to keep you in. Somebody upstairs is holding it up. They're doing it to catch a man who's stronger than anyone alive, and nobody can tell me what's happened to him.*

"We're dealing with it," he says.

It's a terrible answer. It's the only one he's got. She nods as if it's a good one, because she needs it to be.

Panda crouches by the girl with the cut. He gives her the plastic pumpkin of sweets. She holds it in both hands and doesn't eat any.

---

The stairs at the end of the passage go up into black. Not the big curtain. A smaller one, cut across the stairwell at the half landing, flat and dull as a blackboard.

Kusakabe puts his hand on it.

It doesn't give.

He pushes. It's like pushing a wall. Not hard, exactly, more like the thing simply has no interest in him at all. *Keyed against.* That's what the radio meant. This one's not for keeping people in. This one's for keeping people like him out.

"Someone's holding it," Panda says.

"Yeah."

"Up there."

"Yeah."

He makes himself feel past it, with the circle, as far as it'll go, which is not very far. One arm and one sword. That's all it's ever been.

But at the very edge, just at the turn of the stair, on the other side of the black: something. A person. Cursed energy, thin and steady, like a tap left running.

Panda goes back along the passage and comes back with a fire extinguisher off the wall.

"Panda."

"It's not a sorcerer," Panda says. "It's an extinguisher."

He throws it. It doesn't go through. It hits the curtain and stops and drops, the way a ball drops off a net. But on the other side, at the edge of his circle, the person flinches. Moves. Comes down a step to look.

And the curtain, for a second, thins.

Kusakabe goes through it the way you go through a door that's about to close. He doesn't decide to. He's just on the other side.

A young man. Twenties. A hooded jacket with a skull on it, which might be a costume and might not. A strip of paper in his hand, covered in writing, that's what the curtain's coming from. He sees Kusakabe and his mouth opens.

The sword goes once. Not through the man. Through the paper, and the two fingers holding it.

The man screams. The curtain comes apart behind them, like smoke in a draught.

Then Panda is there, and the man is on the floor, and not moving, and breathing. Kusakabe checks that. He checks it twice.

He stands on the stairs with two fingers on the step by his shoe and the man's blood on his blade. Not a curse's. A person's.

*He'd have killed every one of them down there,* he thinks. *He'd have stood here and held the door shut until whatever's up there came down.*

It doesn't help much. He wipes the blade on his coat.

Below them, the civilians are getting up. The woman in office clothes is at the bottom of the stairs, looking up at him.

"Wait," he says. "Then come up behind us. Not before."

She doesn't say thank you. She doesn't need to. She nods.

The fear is at his chest. It's in his breathing now. Every breath, he can feel it there, sitting on the top of the lungs.

---

They go up first. The stairs come out in the basement of a shopping building, shutters down, mannequins in a window in their Halloween capes, and an escalator up to the street, still running.

The exit at the top is held. He feels them before he sees them, from the bottom of the escalator: three, four, crowded into the light at the head of it like something waiting for a bus. Bigger than the ones in the tunnel. One of them much bigger.

The radio on Panda's collar says: "—upper levels, all exits, repeat, curses and users on all—" and then nothing.

"Thanks," Panda tells it.

He goes back and tells the civilians to sit on the stairs. They sit. It's strange how often people do what you tell them when you have a sword and they don't.

Then he goes up, and Panda goes up beside him.

He doesn't remember much of it after. The big one's weight. The way it came down the escalator steps and the steps kept moving under it, so that for a moment it was walking and staying in the same place, like a man on a treadmill. The circle snapping round him tight as a belt. His blade going in somewhere soft and catching and having to be pulled. Panda's shoulder hitting the thing from the side. A noise from his own mouth that he doesn't recognise. Then the cold, hard stop of it, and the grey, and the escalator still running, carrying a little drift of ash up to the top and tipping it off.

He's on one knee at the top. He doesn't remember kneeling.

"Sensei."

"Yeah."

"Sensei. You're bleeding."

He looks. There's a line across his forearm, through the coat, not deep. It's the same arm as last time. *Consistent,* he thinks. *At least I'm consistent.*

"It's nothing."

Panda looks at it, then at his face, for a long moment. Then Panda nods, and holds out a paw.

Kusakabe looks at it. Then he gets up by himself, one hand on the rail, which takes longer. Panda lowers the paw and waits, and doesn't say anything about it.

---

They wave the civilians up. They go out past the two sorcerers in a long, quick, frightened line. The gorilla holding his head. The cat girl holding her pumpkin. The woman in office clothes holding the hand of someone she didn't know an hour ago. Out into the street, towards the auxiliary he can see waving a torch at the next corner. Still inside. Everyone is still inside.

Kusakabe and Panda come out last.

They're inside now. Properly inside. On the street, in the middle of it all, somewhere north of the station.

The noise is the first thing. Far off, but not far enough. Sirens with nowhere to go. Something like thunder, under the ground, that he feels in his feet. A long scream that stops. Then the smell. Smoke, and something sweeter under it that he doesn't want to name.

The street is full of the leftovers of a party. Cans. A broken plastic sword. A food stall on its side, its paper lanterns still lit. Somewhere, a sound system is still playing the same bass line, to nobody.

His phone has no signal. Panda's radio hisses and says nothing.

*Nobody knows,* he thinks. *And now we're in it, and we still don't.*

The fear is at his throat. He swallows, and it's still there. He thinks if he opens his mouth it'll come out, and so he doesn't open his mouth. That's all right. He's never been much of a talker.

Panda stands beside him, enormous and calm and very still, fur spiked with something dark across one shoulder. He isn't looking at the street. He's looking up.

Kusakabe looks up too.

There's no sky. There are no streetlights above the second floor. The tops of the buildings go up into nothing and stop. No glow of the city. No aircraft. No moon. Just the curtain, from one side of the world to the other, flat and close and perfectly black.

Like a sky that someone has taken all the stars out of. Carefully. One by one. And not told anyone.

---

## Chapter 14. B5F

The position is a staircase.

That's what the radio calls it, the last time the radio says anything with all its words in the right order. *North teams, hold the north entrances. Keep civilians moving out. Do not go further in.* Ijichi's voice, or someone doing a very good impression of Ijichi's voice. Calm, in that particular way.

So they hold a staircase.

It's on the Meiji-dori side, north of the station, where a covered stairway goes down from the pavement into the underground concourse. Grey tiles. A steel handrail down the middle, polished by a million hands that weren't thinking about it. A sign with a little train on it and an arrow pointing down, and under the arrow, in smaller letters, the names of the lines. Fukutoshin is the last one.

Down is the station. Up is the street. They stand at the top, between the two.

*Promoted,* he thinks. *From railing to staircase.*

---

For a while it's people.

They come up out of the concourse in clumps of five and ten, blinking, as if they've been in a cinema and the film went wrong. Some are still in costume. Most of the costumes have stopped being funny. A man dressed as a mummy comes up trailing a long loose bandage from his ankle and keeps treading on it, and doesn't seem to notice, and keeps going. A boy carrying a girl on his back, both of them wearing the same rabbit ears. A woman in a business suit with a pumpkin painted on one cheek, only one, as if somebody had started on her and been interrupted.

"Up the street," Kusakabe says. "Left at the corner. Keep going."

They go. Nobody asks him anything. He likes them for it.

Panda walks the slow ones to the corner and comes back. Walks some more. Comes back. He's quick about it and patient at the same time, which is a combination Kusakabe has only ever seen in Panda and a few nurses. People flinch when they first see him. Then they take his paw. It's a very big paw and there's something about it that makes people hold on.

Between clumps, it's quiet. Not real quiet. There's the hum under the ground that he can feel through his shoes, and every so often something heavier, far off, like furniture being moved in a flat three floors down. Smoke somewhere. Panda's radio, clipped to his collar, coughing up pieces of sentences.

"—Dogenzaka, can anyone—"

"—confirm, the users are on the—"

"—no, the other—"

He doesn't try to make it into anything. You can't make one sentence out of the ends of twenty.

His forearm has stiffened where the coat sleeve stuck to the cut. He bends it now and then to keep it honest.

Panda's shoulder is still dark where the fur spiked in the escalator fight. Some of it might be the curse's. Some of it might be Panda's. Kusakabe has never asked what Panda has where other people have blood, and he isn't going to start tonight. Panda doesn't seem to notice it. He keeps touching the radio on his collar instead, as if it's a sore tooth.

---

The first thing to come up the stairs that isn't a person comes up at nineteen minutes to nine.

He feels it before it turns the landing. A touch at the edge of the circle, light and cold, like someone laying the back of a finger on his wrist.

Then it's in sight. It comes up the steps on too many legs, pale and quick, and it's wearing faces. That's the first thing he sees and the only thing he'll remember. Rubber faces, the cheap kind from the discount shops, witches and skulls and grinning old men, stacked one over another all down the length of it. They slide as it moves. Under each face is another face. Under that one, nothing he wants to know about.

*Halloween,* he thinks. *Even the curses dressed up.*

It comes at the top step where he's standing because he's what's standing there.

The line breaks. His hands answer. One cut across, low, where the legs meet the rest. The weight of it coming on anyway, the way a falling thing keeps falling after it's dead. He turns his shoulder and lets it go past him, and it goes over the handrail and down into the stairwell in two pieces, faces coming off it as it goes, slapping on the tiles.

Then the grey. Then the smell of hot rubber.

A witch's face lies on the top step by his foot, grinning up at him with nothing behind it.

He nudges it down the stairs with his toe.

His heart is going. It always goes. He waits for it the way you wait for a kettle to stop rattling after you've turned it off.

"That's three tonight," Panda says, coming back from the corner. "Of the big ones. I'm counting."

"Don't."

"Okay." A pause. "It's three, though."

---

Tanabe comes up the stairs at ten to nine.

Tanabe, or Tanaka. Ten months since Shinjuku, a dozen times passing in corridors, and he still doesn't know which. It was too late to ask the first time. It's much too late now.

Tanabe has someone over his shoulder. A young auxiliary, a woman in a black suit with one shoe, her arm round his neck, her head down. Tanabe's left eye is swollen shut. There's blood dried in his eyebrow like rust on a gate. He gets to the top step and stops there, breathing, and looks at Kusakabe with the eye that still works.

"Kusakabe."

"Tanabe."

He doesn't correct it. So that's something, or it's nothing. Either way it's settled.

"Where's the medical point?"

"Up. Corner. Left. Someone'll have a van."

Tanabe nods. He shifts the auxiliary on his shoulder. She makes a small noise and goes quiet again.

Panda is fifty metres up the street, with a mother and two small boys in matching dinosaur suits, and has his back to them.

"Don't go past the concourse," Tanabe says.

"Wasn't going to."

"I mean it." He wipes his good eye with the back of his wrist. "B5F. The Fukutoshin platform. Five floors down. Right at the bottom of all of it." He swallows. "There's special grades down there. Not one. More than one. Gojo's down there with them."

The word *grades*, with the *s* on the end, goes into Kusakabe like a cold key dropped down the back of a collar.

"Who else?"

"Nobody, that I know of. Some went down early, before it all closed up. Nobody's come back up from that level to say anything." Tanabe looks at the stairwell behind him, at the arrow on the sign. "The radio was talking about it. Then it wasn't. That's all I've got."

"Okay."

"I'm not going back down."

"No."

"I've got her." Tanabe moves the woman's arm a little higher on his neck, as if she's the reason, and maybe she is. "Hold your stairs."

"Yeah."

He goes. Up the street, past Panda, towards the corner. He doesn't look back. Kusakabe watches him go and thinks about calling something after him, his name, the right one, and doesn't.

---

Panda comes back without the dinosaurs.

The radio on his collar wakes up as he comes. A woman's voice this time, high and too fast, half of it gone to static.

"—repeat, B5F—special grade—Gojo-san is—platform level—anyone who can—"

Then nothing.

Panda stops on the top step. He's very still. He looks down at the radio on his own collar, which is difficult for him, because of the shape of his head, so he looks at it sideways, the way a dog looks at a noise.

"Sensei. Did you hear that?"

"Yeah."

"B5F."

"Yeah."

"That's where Satoru is."

"Yeah."

"Special grades," Panda says. "She said special grades." He looks at the stairwell. The arrow. The word *Fukutoshin* at the bottom of the list. "That's this line. That's straight down from here."

Kusakabe says nothing.

"We should go down," Panda says.

He says it simply. Not bravely. That's the worst of it. He says it the way he'd say it's raining, or the way he'd hand someone a sweet. There are people down there. Satoru is down there. The stairs are right here. So.

And Kusakabe feels it.

A cold sliding feeling in his stomach. Something that was sitting on a shelf inside him, heavy, minding its own business, and has started to slide off the edge. Slowly, and then not slowly.

*Special grades.* Plural. Things that don't care about a circle. Things that don't care about a sword, or how long you practised, or how good your feet are. Things that have looked at men like him for longer than there have been men like him, and been bored.

Five floors down. In the dark. With whatever built the curtain.

He thinks, for no reason at all, of his sister's window lock. Whether he checked it on Sunday. He did. Twice.

And then he hears his own voice.

"I heard they've already got people down there."

It comes out easy. That's what he'll remember afterwards. How easy. Not a stammer, not a pause. Level and tired and a little bored, his ordinary voice, the one he uses for *Traffic*. As if the lie had been sitting ready in his mouth all night, already shaped, waiting for the door to open.

Panda turns his head and looks at him.

It's a long look. Panda's face doesn't do much. It isn't built to. There's no eyebrow to raise, no mouth to tighten. But Panda has spent two years reading Inumaki off three words and a hand, and Kusakabe can feel himself being read now, the way you'd feel a torch on the back of your neck.

He doesn't look away. That's the other thing he'll remember. He's good at not looking away. He's had years of practice at not looking away from people while not telling them anything.

The radio hisses on Panda's collar. Somewhere under the street, a long way down, something heavy shifts.

"Okay, sensei," Panda says.

He turns back to the stairs.

---

The relief comes first.

It comes fast and it comes warm, all through him, like stepping out of the wind into a shop doorway. His shoulders go down. He didn't know they were up. His hands, which have been cold all night, are suddenly not cold. He breathes all the way to the bottom for the first time since seven o'clock.

*We're not going down.*

*We're staying here. On the stairs. With the small ones.*

It's a good feeling. That's the trouble. It's a really good feeling, and he has it for nearly two seconds.

Then the other thing comes in underneath it. Smaller. Colder. Not a feeling so much as a fact he steps on in the dark, like a stair that isn't there.

*You just lied to a student.*

*To keep yourself off a floor.*

He looks at Panda's back, at the top of the stairs, at the dark patch across one shoulder where something got him in the tunnel. Panda is looking down into the stairwell. Not at anything. Just down.

*Nobody's going to know,* he thinks.

It's true. Tanabe's gone. The radio's gone. Whoever's down there, if anybody is, will never hear about two people on a staircase on Meiji-dori deciding not to come. There'll be no report. No margin. Nobody pencilling a word next to his name.

Nobody's going to know.

He waits for that to make him feel fine.

It doesn't. He doesn't feel fine. He feels small. Not frightened-small. He knows that kind. This is a different size. The size of a man standing at the top of some stairs, telling himself nobody saw.

---

He does the sum anyway. He can't help it. It's what his head does when it's been told not to.

The concourse first. One floor down. Ticket gates, shops with their shutters half down, the long white corridors that all look like each other. Then the next level, and the next. Escalators going down into escalators. Each one a little longer than you'd think. Each landing a place where something could be waiting round the corner, outside the reach of one arm and one sword, until it isn't.

Five of them. Five floors of that, with Panda going first because Panda always goes first, and the air getting thicker the lower you get.

And at the bottom, the platform. A long lit room under the whole city, with the tracks running off into the dark at both ends. And in it, things that make the radio say *grades* with an *s* on the end.

He gets as far as the third escalator in his head and stops there. He doesn't let himself see the platform. He's not sure he could if he tried. He has never stood in a room with a special grade. He's spent his whole working life arranging not to.

*Years,* he thinks. *Years of very careful arranging. And it works. Look. It's still working.*

He looks at the stairs. They go down to the first landing and turn and go out of sight. That's all he can see of them. That's all he needs to.

---

The radio keeps going, in pieces.

"—B five—"

"—can't, it's keyed—"

"—anybody on the—"

He reaches over and turns it down. Not off. Down. Panda looks at the dial and then at him.

"Battery," Kusakabe says.

Panda doesn't say anything. He sits down on the top step, his back to the street, his feet two steps below, and the stair takes his weight with a small complaint from the handrail fixings. He rests his chin on his paws and watches the landing.

Kusakabe stands behind him. Sword out. Circle open, a little. The street at his back. People still coming up now and then, fewer each time, stepping round Panda on the stairs and then stepping round Kusakabe, saying sorry to both of them.

*That's two lies now,* he thinks. *The second one was smaller. That's how they do it. The second one's always smaller.*

---

The curse user comes from the street side, a little after nine.

He feels him before he hears him. Not a curse. A person, with cursed energy coming off him in a hard, steady line, like heat off a road.

A big man. Thirties. A tracksuit with the zip done up to the chin and something wound round his right forearm that might be a chain, and might be something that used to be a chain before somebody did something to it. He's walking down the middle of Meiji-dori in no hurry, through the dropped hats and the cans, and two things are walking with him. Low things. Long. They go on all fours, at his heels, with their heads down.

"Is this the bear?" the man says. He's looking at Panda. "They said there'd be a bear."

Panda stands up.

"Panda," Panda says. "Not a bear. Common mistake."

The man laughs and lets the things go.

The fighting is short. It's always short. It's the waiting that's long.

Panda takes the man. Kusakabe takes what's left. The first of the low things comes in along the ground and meets the line, and his blade is already there, and it isn't any more. The second one doesn't come for him. It goes wide. Round. Along the wall of the stairwell, very fast, towards the place where Panda is standing with his whole back open to it, trading blows with the man in the tracksuit.

Panda's back is outside the circle.

Kusakabe doesn't decide anything. He steps. Two steps, sideways and in, until Panda's back is inside the line. The low thing crosses it. His hands answer. Something hot and thin goes across the back of his knuckles and then the thing is in pieces at Panda's heels, smoking.

Behind him there's a sound like a car door slammed very hard. The man in the tracksuit is sitting against a shop shutter on the other side of the pavement, with the shutter bent round him in a shape it wasn't made in. He isn't getting up. The chain on his arm has gone slack.

Panda looks round at what's smoking by his feet. Then at Kusakabe.

"Thanks."

"Mm."

His knuckles are bleeding a little. Same hand as the tunnel. He seems to be keeping all the damage on the one side tonight, like a man with a system.

*Easy,* he thinks. *Minding a back you can see. Anyone can do that.*

*If we'd gone down, there'd be nobody up here for this.*

He tries that thought on. It almost fits. It's close. It's the right size for the shape of a reason, and if he doesn't move about too much in it, nobody would notice it isn't his.

He takes it off again. He knows where he got it.

---

They tie the man's hands behind him with the cord from somebody's dropped costume. A long red sash from a devil, or a vampire. Panda does the knot. It's a very good knot.

"Where'd you learn that?"

"Masamichi," Panda says. "He ties up all his dolls for the post. Every one, separately. You can't get them open after."

The man in the tracksuit comes round while they're doing it. He looks at Panda, and then at Kusakabe, and then at the stairwell.
