"You're going to get it," he says. He sounds pleased about it, the way people are pleased about weather that will ruin someone else's day. "All of you. You've no idea what's down there."

Kusakabe doesn't answer. He doesn't need to. The man's quite right.

Panda looks at him, though. Just for a second. Then he looks away.

---

Afterwards, his knuckles won't stop.

It isn't much. A thin, steady seep across the back of the left hand, running down between the fingers and making the grip slippery. He shakes it. It keeps coming. He wipes it on his trousers. It keeps coming.

Panda holds out a paw. There's a strip of black cloth in it, torn from the bottom of a cape somebody left on the pavement.

"I'm fine."

"You're dripping on the stairs," Panda says. "People have to walk on them."

He holds out his hand.

Panda wraps it. Three times round, and a tuck. His paws are much too big for this and he does it anyway, carefully, with his whole attention, the way he does the knot, the way he does everything. He doesn't pull it too tight. He doesn't make a joke about it. He holds Kusakabe's hand in both paws for a moment after he's finished, the way you'd hold something to check it isn't going to come apart.

Then he lets go.

"There."

"Thanks."

It comes out easily too. Which is strange. Of the two things he's said easily tonight, this is the one that's true, and it's the one that's hard to look at.

---

A small one comes up the stairs at twenty past nine. Barely a curse. Grade 4 at a push. A thing the size of a cat, made mostly of noise, the kind that lives in station toilets and drinks bad moods.

Panda flicks it off the step with the back of a paw, the way you'd flick crumbs off a table, and it bursts on the wall.

"That's five," Panda says.

"I said don't count."

"You said don't. You didn't say why."

---

At some point the radio stops talking about B5F.

Kusakabe doesn't notice it happen. He just notices, sometime after half past nine, that it's been a while. The voices are still there, in pieces. Exits. Users. Someone asking for a medic at a crossing whose name he doesn't catch. Someone counting heads, slowly, and losing their place, and starting again.

But nobody says *B five* any more.

He doesn't know what that means. It could mean it's over down there. It could mean it's over down there.

He doesn't ask.

Panda is sitting on the top step again. He has his head turned a little, the way he does, towards the radio. He's noticed too. Kusakabe can tell from his ears.

"Sensei."

"Yeah."

"The people down there. From ours." Panda doesn't turn round. "Who are they?"

Kusakabe looks at the back of Panda's head. The round ears. The dust in the fur.

"Didn't say," he says.

That's true. Tanabe didn't say. Tanabe didn't say because there was nothing to say. It's the truest thing he's said all night, and it's the worst one, and he can feel the difference between those things like a stone in the hand.

*Building a house out of technically,* he thinks. *Very sound construction. No windows.*

"Okay," Panda says.

---

They don't talk for a while after that.

The street empties. The last group up the stairs is three teenagers who've lost their friend and want to go back down for her, and Panda talks them round, quietly, in the end by promising. Kusakabe doesn't hear what. He only hears Panda's voice, low and even, and then the three of them going up the street, the middle one looking back twice.

Then there's nobody.

Just the stairs, and the light in the stairwell, which is the white, honest light of every station in the city. Advertising boards along the wall going down. A face cream. A language school. A smiling family on a train, going somewhere with mountains.

"Toge's out here somewhere," Panda says, to the empty street. "On another list. And Maki."

"Yeah."

"They'll be all right."

Kusakabe doesn't say *yeah* to that one. He finds he can't. He's run out of the easy ones.

Panda doesn't seem to need it. He nods, once, as if somebody has agreed with him.

Kusakabe sits on the step behind Panda. Higher up. He puts the sword across his knees.

"You scared, sensei?"

Panda doesn't look round when he asks.

Kusakabe thinks about it. He finds he can't be bothered to lie about this one. It would be one too many. There's a limit, apparently. He's found it.

"Yeah."

"Me too."

"You don't look it."

"Neither do you," Panda says. "We're both very good at faces."

Somewhere under them, far down, a long way below the concourse, something gives a single deep knock, like a door being shut in the bottom of a building. Then nothing.

Panda's ears come up. Then, slowly, they go down.

*He knows,* Kusakabe thinks. And then, because he's tired, and the step is cold, and there's no one to see: *He doesn't know anything. He's a student. He believed me.*

He isn't sure which of those he's hoping for.

---

The order comes at ten to ten.

The radio, turned down as it is, still manages it. A man's voice, clear for once, as though he's standing right next to a working aerial.

"All north teams. All north teams. If you're clear, move south. Regroup at the crossing below the station. Repeat. North teams, regroup south."

South. Further in. Not down.

Kusakabe finds he can tell the difference with great precision. He could draw it on a map.

Panda stands up. He does it all at once, the way he does everything, as if gravity is a suggestion he's decided to go along with. He brushes the dust off his knees, and some of it comes off.

"Come on, sensei."

"Yeah."

Kusakabe gets up. His knees tell him about it. He slides the sword home and leaves his hand on the hilt, because his hand wants to be there.

At the top of the stairs he stops, for half a second, and looks down. The landing. The tiles. The arrow on the sign, and the list, and the last name on it.

*Five floors,* he thinks. *That's all. Five floors.*

Then he turns away from it.

Panda has already started down the street.

He walks in the middle of Meiji-dori, on the white lines, where cars are supposed to be. There's nobody to stop him now and nobody to photograph him. He steps over a witch's broom snapped in half. He steps round a child's light-up wand lying in the road, still blinking pink, on and off, for nobody. He doesn't hurry and he doesn't look back, because he doesn't need to. He knows his teacher is behind him.

His back is very broad. The fur is matted across one shoulder, dark and stiff. The radio is clipped at his collar, hissing softly to itself. The round head, the round ears. The plain, steady, rolling walk of someone who believes what he's been told.

Kusakabe looks at it.

He looks at it for about as long as he can. Which isn't long.

Then he looks at the road instead, a step at a time, the white lines going under his feet, and follows.

---

## Chapter 15. Stay Still

South is downhill.

Meiji-dori runs down towards the station in a long, slow slope, and if you walk it in the middle, on the white lines, you can feel it in the backs of your legs. The road wants you to go faster. He doesn't.

Panda walks ahead. Kusakabe walks behind. The gap between them stays the same, about three metres, as if somebody measured it.

The street is nearly empty now. Shops with their lights still on and nobody in them. A bicycle on its side with the back wheel turning, very slowly, in no wind. A ramen place with the stools all spun round to face the door, the way people leave them when they get up in a hurry. Above the second floor, nothing. The curtain sits on top of everything, and the streetlights shine up into it and don't come back.

The radio on Panda's collar is still turned down. It still talks.

"—say again, the—"

"—Gojo-san has been—"

"—no, sealed, she said sealed—"

"—that's not possible, say again—"

Then a long hiss.

Panda stops walking.

He doesn't turn round. He stands in the middle of the road with his head tilted towards his collar, very still, the way he stood on the stairs.

"Sensei."

"Yeah."

"Did they say sealed?"

Kusakabe thinks about it. He tries to hear it again, the exact shape of it, and finds he can't. It's gone, the way the end of a dream goes when you sit up too fast.

"Don't know," he says.

That one's true. He checks it, the way you'd check a step before you put your weight on it, and it holds. He doesn't know. Nobody on that radio knows. They're all just passing the word round in the dark, and the word gets heavier every time somebody touches it.

*Gojo doesn't get sealed,* he thinks. *Gojo is what you put on top of everything else so it stays shut.*

Panda stands there a while longer. Then he starts walking again. The gap goes back to three metres.

---

There's a pedestrian bridge further down, the kind with blue railings and a sign telling cyclists to dismount. Somebody has hung a costume over the railing, a full-length rubber dragon with the zip open down its back, as if the person inside just stepped out of it and kept going. It moves a little in the air coming up from the street. It looks more alive than anything else on the road.

They walk under it.

"You'd fit in that," Panda says.

"No."

"You would. You'd have to bend at the knees."

"I'm not getting in the dragon."

"I'm just saying. If you needed to blend in."

*He's talking,* Kusakabe thinks. *He's talking because the radio said sealed and he doesn't want to hear it say it again.*

The coin is in his pocket. He doesn't check it. He knows if he checks it, it'll weigh something, and he doesn't want to find out how much.

---

It starts a little after ten.

He's watching the white lines, and then the white lines are wrong. Not moving. Nothing's moving. But the air on the back of his hands goes thick, all at once, the way air goes thick in a room when someone opens an oven door. Except it isn't hot. Not yet. It's just heavy. The weight of it settles on his shoulders and the top of his head and inside his ears, and his ears pop, like a train going into a tunnel.

And the circle is up.

He didn't put it there. He didn't think the word, or move his feet, or touch the hilt. It's just there, all round him, the reach of one arm and one sword, already drawn and humming. It came up the way your hand comes off a hot pan. Nobody decided anything. The body heard something he didn't, and answered.

Three metres ahead, Panda has stopped.

Kusakabe sees the fur go up along the back of his neck. All of it, at once, from the collar to the round of his head, like a field when the wind goes over it.

"Sensei," Panda says. Very quietly.

"Yeah."

"What is that?"

He doesn't answer. He's trying to find the edge of it. With a curse there's always an edge. You feel it come in at the line, small or large, and you know where it is and roughly what it weighs, and your hands get ready for that weight.

This hasn't got an edge.

It isn't coming in at the line. It's everywhere past the line, all of it, the whole street, the way weather is everywhere. He can't find the outside of it to hold. He reaches for it the way he'd reach for a wall in the dark and his hand just goes on reaching.

*Oh,* he thinks. Very calmly, from somewhere a long way back in his head. *Oh, no.*

---

Then it splits.

Not the pressure. His sense of it. The weight separates into two weights, the way a sound you thought was one note turns out to be two when you listen properly.

One of them is hot. He feels that one clearly now. It's off to the south-west, beyond the station, somewhere in the streets that go up the hill. It burns. Not like a fire burns. Like the inside of the ground. It's angry and it's very old and very proud of itself, and it's so much stronger than anything he's ever stood near that his teeth hurt.

The other one he can't feel at all.

That's the strange part. That's the part that's going to stay. The other one has no heat and no shape and no edge. He only knows it's there because of what it does to everything around it. The way you'd know there was something very heavy on a bed by the way the whole mattress bends towards it. The street bends towards it. His circle, his small humming circle, leans.

And under the leaning, very faint, there's something he recognises.

He's spent five months knowing where that thing is in the building. Five months of corridors and timings and long ways round. He knows it the way you know your own front door in the dark. He's never once been this close to it with the lid off.

"Sensei." Panda has turned round. His face can't show it. But his voice has gone up at the end, a little, which it never does. "Sensei, that's Yuji."

Kusakabe looks at him.

"That's Yuji's," Panda says. "I know his. I've stood right next to him. That's—" He stops. He listens, with his whole head, the round ears turned. "No. That's not. It's coming from where he'd be. But it isn't him."

"No."

"It's the other one."

"Yeah."

Panda doesn't say the name. Neither does Kusakabe. It feels like the kind of name you don't say in a street with no sky, in case something hears it said and looks round.

---

Away to the south-west, the curtain lights up.

He sees it before he hears anything. The black overhead goes orange in one place, low down, beyond the buildings, the way the underside of a cloud goes orange over a city. Then it goes white. Then the sound gets to them, and it isn't one sound. It's a whole street of glass going at once, a long falling ringing that goes on much too long, like someone dropping every plate in a restaurant down a staircase.

The air comes after the sound.

It comes up Meiji-dori from the south in a wall. Hot. Dry. It smells of stone and burnt plastic and something under that, a smell like a struck match held too close to his face. It pushes his coat flat against his legs. The dragon on the bridge behind them fills up all at once and stands out straight from the railing, empty and fat and furious. Then it tears loose and goes tumbling away up the road.

The circle holds. Of course it holds. It isn't made for this. Nothing's crossing the line. Nothing's in his reach. There's nothing for his hands to answer. The circle just sits there round him, perfectly drawn, guarding a space about the size of a bus shelter against something the size of a district.

*Look at it,* he thinks. *Look how neat it is.*

Another flare. Closer, or bigger. Hard to tell. The orange goes up the underside of the curtain in a long sheet and for a second he can see the whole bowl of it overhead, lit from below, the inside of a lamp.

Then a sound he doesn't have anything to put next to. Not glass. Not a bang. A sort of tearing, very long and very fine, as if the air itself has been cut down the middle with a blade and the two halves are still deciding whether to fall apart.

He knows what a cut sounds like. He's made a lot of them.

He has never made one that sounded like that.

---

"We're going back," he says.

Panda looks at him. "The order was south."

"I know."

"The crossing's that way."

"I know where the crossing is." He hears his own voice. It's level, and it's tired. It's the easy one, the same one that told Panda there were people down there, and he hates it. So he stops using it. He makes himself say the next part in a different one. Flatter. Worse. True.

"I'm scared," he says. "That's the first reason. The second reason is there's nothing down there we can do. Not you. Not me. We go south, we're just two more things in the road. So we go back. We find whoever's still on this street and we take them north with us. That's what we're for."

It's the longest thing he's said all day. Maybe all week. It comes out in pieces, not smooth. It doesn't sound like an order. It sounds like a man reading out his own bill.

Panda looks at him for a long moment.

Then the ground shakes. Not much. A shiver up through the soles of his shoes, as if the whole road had been tapped once from underneath.

"Okay," Panda says. "Yeah. Okay."

He turns round. So does Kusakabe. They go back up the hill.

*That's the first honest order I've given tonight,* he thinks. *And it's a retreat. Well. Start with what you're good at.*

---

They find people.

Not many. A side street off the main road, narrow, bars and a hair salon and a shop that sells only hats. In the doorway of the hat shop, behind a half-pulled shutter, there are six of them. A man in a security jacket with a torch he isn't using. Two girls in matching sailor costumes, holding hands so hard their knuckles have gone yellow. A woman in a white bed sheet with eyeholes cut in it, a ghost, holding the sheet up off her face with one hand so she can see. An old man in ordinary clothes. And a young auxiliary in a black suit, a boy really, with a radio exactly like Panda's that he keeps turning off and on again.

"They told us to wait," the auxiliary says, when he sees them. "Someone said wait here. I don't know who."

"Well, now someone's saying come with us," Kusakabe says. "North. Now."

"Okay," the auxiliary says. "Okay. Yes."

They come out from behind the shutter. The ghost has to bend down to get under it.

There's another flare, behind them. No glass this time, there's no glass left. Just the light, and the air, and a sound like a long breath being let out by something with lungs the size of a station.

The sailor girls start crying. Not loudly. Just at the same time.

"This way," Panda says, in his low, even voice. "Stay near me. I'm very easy to see."

They go.

---

They get as far as the end of the side street.

It stops.

The fire, the light, the tearing sound. One second it's all there, the whole south-west of the curtain alive with it. The next second it isn't. The orange on the underside of the black goes out, all at once, like a hand closing over a candle. The hot weight he's been feeling, the angry, ancient, proud one, simply isn't there any more. He reaches for where it was and there's only a hole the shape of it, cooling.

The other weight is still there.

It hasn't changed at all. That's the worst of it. It's not tired. It's not hurt. It isn't even pleased. Whatever just happened over there, it happened to the other one. This one just carries on being exactly as heavy as it was before, bending the street towards it, the way it's probably been bending streets towards it for a very long time.

And then it moves.

He feels it start. Not fast. Not slow. Just coming.

It's coming this way.

"Sensei," Panda says.

"I know."

The auxiliary has stopped. The civilians have stopped behind him, bunched up, because he stopped. They're at the mouth of the side street, where it meets Meiji-dori.

There's nowhere to put them. The street behind is a dead end of bars and hats. The main road is wide open both ways. It's too late to run and it was always too late to run. He did the sum and the sum came out to here.

His hand is on the hilt.

He doesn't remember it going there.

---

It comes down out of the dark.

Not along the road. Down. Off a roof, or off the air. One second there's nothing in the middle of Meiji-dori but the white lines and a burning bin. The next there's a figure standing on the lines, about thirty metres off, as if it's been there the whole time and he just hadn't thought to look.

A boy.

That's what it is, first. A boy in a school uniform, the dark high-collared kind, the one the first-years wear, mostly torn off him now and black at the edges where it's burnt. Bare arms. Pink hair, pushed back off the forehead, as if someone ran a hand through it hard.

Kusakabe has spent five months not looking at this boy's face.

He looks now. He can't not. And it isn't the face.

There are marks on it. Black lines, clean and dark as if they've been painted on with a brush, across the cheeks and up over the brow. And under the eyes, under the ordinary eyes, a second pair. They're open. They're looking. Four eyes in a boy's face, red, all of them, and every one of them bored.

Behind him, one of the sailor girls makes a small sound.

The four eyes come round.

They don't hurry. They take the street in, the whole of it, the way you'd look round a room you'd stopped renting a long time ago. The bins. The bridge. The burning thing in the road. The crowd at the end of the side street.

"Don't move," it says.

The voice is the boy's voice. That's what's wrong with it. It's a fifteen-year-old's voice, a bit hoarse, the voice that laughs in the courtyard. Somebody else is using it. Somebody who's said this sentence before, to a great many people, and is tired of it.

"I'm not done," it says. "I don't want to be bothered. Stay where you are."

And that's all. It doesn't explain. It doesn't threaten. It doesn't need to. It says it the way you'd tell a cat to get off a table.

---

Kusakabe stays where he is.

He stays where he is the way a wall stays where it is. He doesn't decide to. There isn't anything left in him to decide with. Every part of him that usually has a vote has gone very quiet, and the part that's left is very simple, and only knows one thing.

*Don't breathe too loud.*

He breathes through his nose. Slow. Small. Not to the bottom. Just enough.

*Don't be interesting.*

His face does nothing. It's good at that. Thirty-something years of practice. He lets it go slack and plain and tired, the face of a man waiting for a train that's late. He doesn't look at the four eyes. He doesn't look away from them, either. Looking away is a movement. He looks at a point just below the boy's chin, at the burnt edge of the collar, and keeps his eyes there.

*Be furniture.*

The circle is still up. He can feel it all round him, drawn and ready and humming, the way it's been since the air went thick. It's the only thing about him that's moving. And it suddenly seems very loud. Like someone in a library whistling.

He thinks about letting it go.

He doesn't dare. Letting it go is a movement too. So it stays, and he stands in the middle of it, and hopes it's too small to notice. Hopes it's like a candle in a window across a valley. There, and lit, and nothing to do with anyone.

Next to him, Panda is still.

Really still. Not the way Panda's still on the grass, loose and heavy and half asleep. This is something else. Every bit of him set, from the round ears down. Kusakabe can feel him in the circle, the edge of one shoulder just inside the line. Panda isn't breathing at all. Kusakabe doesn't know if Panda needs to. It's one more thing on the list of things he's never asked.

Panda's paw, at his side, is closed. Tight. The way a hand closes when it's stopping itself from reaching for something.

*He wants to say the name,* Kusakabe thinks. *He wants to say Yuji. And see if anyone's in there.*

*Don't. Please. Don't be the one who's interesting.*

Panda doesn't.

---

The four eyes go along the crowd.

The auxiliary. The man with the torch. The ghost, still holding up her sheet with one hand, frozen like that, her elbow in the air. The old man. The sailor girls, who have stopped crying, because crying is a movement.

Panda. The eyes stay on Panda for a moment. Not long. A flick of something, the way a cat's ear moves towards a sound and then decides against it. Then they move on.

Then they come to him.

He feels it arrive. It doesn't come in at the line. It doesn't come in anywhere. It just lands on him, the whole weight of being looked at by that, and his circle doesn't so much as twitch, because there's nothing in it to answer. Nothing has crossed. Nothing needs to. The circle is built to catch things that come in. It has nothing at all to say about something that simply looks.

There have been men like him for a thousand years.

He knows that, suddenly, standing there. Men with a sword and a bit of training and good feet. Men who practised a small trick until it was very good. Men with a sister somewhere, and a debt, and a sofa. There have been thousands of them. This thing has met them all. It has killed some of them, probably, on days when it had nothing better to do. It doesn't remember a single face.

It isn't going to remember his.

The eyes go through him. Not past him. Not over him. Through, as if he were a pane of glass, and on the other side of the glass there was something faintly more worth looking at. A wall. A drainpipe. The hat shop.

It doesn't see a Grade 1. It doesn't see a sword, or a circle, or a man. It sees a street with some things in it, and he's one of the things.

*Good,* he thinks. *Good. That's right. That's all I am.*

*Keep thinking that. Mean it.*

The four eyes move on.

They go up, to the black where the sky should be. To the south-west, where the fire was. They narrow. Not at anything here. At something further off, something it's thinking about, and Kusakabe, very far back inside himself, understands that he has already been forgotten.

The boy's mouth does something. Not quite a smile. The shape a mouth makes when it's been told a joke it already knew.

Then it's gone.

He doesn't see it go. Nobody does. There's no jump, no step, no flare. The space in the middle of the road where it was standing is just a space. The white lines run through it, and a scrap of burnt cloth turns over once on the tarmac and lies still.

The weight goes with it. Not all at once. It drains off south and west, bending other streets now, someone else's air, someone else's problem. He feels it go the way you feel a lorry go past on a bridge you're standing on. The whole thing shaking. Then less. Then a hum. Then just the bridge.

---

Nobody moves.

That's the thing he'll remember. Long after it's gone, nobody moves. Not the auxiliary. Not the ghost with her elbow up. Not the old man. They all stand exactly where they were, in the shapes they were in, as if they've been told not to and nobody's come back to say they can stop.

Nobody's going to come back and say they can stop.

The ghost is the first. Her arm comes down. Slowly, as if it's very heavy. The sheet falls over her face again and she doesn't lift it. She just stands there, a white shape, breathing under it. Kusakabe can see the sheet go in and out where her mouth is.

Then one of the sailor girls sits down on the kerb. Not falls. Sits. Very carefully, as if the kerb might not be there. Her friend sits down next to her. They're still holding hands.

Panda breathes out. Or does something that sounds like breathing out. His paw opens, at his side. The fur on his neck is still up.

"Sensei."

Kusakabe doesn't answer.

"That was Yuji's face," Panda says.

"No," Kusakabe says. "It wasn't."

It's the first thing he's said since it spoke, and his voice comes out wrong, too dry, like paper. Panda looks at him. Then Panda looks back at the empty road.

"He's not in there right now," Panda says. "Is he."

Kusakabe doesn't say anything to that. There isn't anything. Five months of corridors. Five months of the long way round so he'd never have to look at the boy's face. And tonight he finally looked. And the boy wasn't home.

*So you still don't know what he looks like,* he thinks. *Well done. You managed it after all.*

---

They move, eventually.

The auxiliary's radio decides to work. A voice he doesn't know says there's a van at the top of Meiji-dori, north, for civilians. Panda takes them. The ghost, the sailor girls, the man with the torch, the old man. Panda walks in front, slowly, his round back very easy to see.

"Coming?" Panda says, over his shoulder.

"In a minute."

Panda looks at him. Then he nods, and goes on up the hill with his small crowd behind him.

Kusakabe stands at the mouth of the side street.

The circle is still up. He finds that out the way you find out you've been holding your breath. It's still drawn round him, perfectly round, perfectly neat, humming. Guarding nothing. He lets it go. It goes slowly, like something that isn't sure it's allowed.

The road is very quiet. The bin has burnt down to a red glow. The white lines run off south into the dark, towards the station, towards the crossing where they were supposed to regroup, where he'd have been standing if he'd followed the order.

He isn't ashamed of standing still. He checks, the way he checked the other things tonight, and it holds. That one's just true. There was nothing to do but stand still, so he stood still. A man who moved would be lying in the road now. He stood there like a coat rack and he's alive and so are six other people and a panda. He'll take that. He'd take it again.

It's only that his hand won't come off the sword.

He notices it the way you notice a sound that's been going on for a long time. His right hand is on the hilt. It's been there since the side street, since before the thing came down out of the dark. The fingers are wrapped round the grip so hard the knuckles have gone white, four small white hills in a row. His left hand, the bandaged one, is holding the scabbard up by the mouth, and the black cloth Panda tied round it is dark again in one place where the cut's opened.

He never drew. Not an inch. The blade is still in the scabbard. All night, the one thing that's been asked of him, and he did it perfectly, and it was to not do anything at all.

He tells the hand to let go.

It doesn't.

He tells it again. It stays. It isn't frightened, exactly. It's just not finished. It heard the order, *don't move,* and it believed it more than the rest of him did, and nobody's come back to tell it it can stop.

Up the road, a long way off now, Panda's back goes round the corner under a streetlight, broad and slow, and the little crowd goes after it, and the street is empty.

Kusakabe stands in it.

Hand on the sword. Knuckles white. Long after there's nothing left in the road to be still for.

---

## Chapter 16. Under the Rock

He gets his fingers off the hilt in the end.

One at a time. The little finger first, which goes easily. Then the ring finger. The first two take much longer. They've been closed for so long that they've stopped thinking of themselves as a hand, and they open stiffly, with a small ache in every joint, like a gate nobody has used all winter.

He stands there with his hand open at his side and looks at it.

*Still got all of them,* he thinks. *Good. I'll need them for the forms.*

The street is very quiet. Smoke somewhere to the south, low and brown. Nobody running. Nobody shouting. The kind of quiet that isn't peace, just everything holding its breath at once.

He doesn't know where Panda is. He'll go and find out. In a minute.

Then the air changes again.

---

He doesn't fully understand what happens next. Nobody does, at the time.

There are two of them now. He can feel them from here, without trying, the way you can feel two trains in a tunnel through the soles of your feet. One of them is the thing that walked past him. He knows that one. He'll know it for the rest of his life.

The other is new. It's huge, and it isn't a curse he has a grade for, and it isn't anything he has a word for. All he gets of it is a sense of something turning. Slow and heavy and patient, round and round, like a mill wheel in a river that has never once stopped.

They meet somewhere to the south.

*Not near,* he thinks. *Not near enough to matter.*

He's wrong about that.

What he has afterwards is in pieces.

The ground goes first. Not a shake. A drop, as if the whole street has been let down a few centimetres on a rope and caught.

Then the circle. He doesn't draw it. It's just there, all the way out to the edge, and everything is crossing it. Not one thing. Everything. From every side at once, thin and fast and without end, as if the air itself has been filled with edges and set going. His hands try to answer. There's nothing to answer. You can't cut what's already cutting everything.

Across the road, a shop sign comes down in four neat pieces. A lamp post leans, and keeps leaning, in slices. The glass in the windows doesn't shatter. It slides out of its frames in clean sheets, as if it's been told it can go home.

*This isn't for me,* he thinks, with total clarity. *None of this is for me. I'm just standing in it.*

Then fire. A line of it from the south, so bright the street loses all its colours. White, and the black shapes of things in the white. The heat comes a moment after the light, as though it had further to walk.

Then something worse than fire. A pressure, pushing outward from one place, flattening whatever it meets. The building behind him makes a long low sound, almost an animal sound. Concrete splits along lines he can hear.

The world comes down.

---

When he opens his eyes, it's dark.

Not night dark. Night has streetlights in it, and windows, and that faint orange the city puts on the underside of the clouds. This dark has nothing in it at all. He blinks to check his eyes are open. They are. It makes no difference.

He's on his back.

He works that out slowly, from the weight of his own head and the cold under his shoulder blades. Grit under him. Something hard under one hip, not hard enough to matter.

Dust. In his mouth, thick and dry, tasting of chalk. In his eyes, in his nose, in the creases of his neck. The smell of burnt stone, which is not a smell he knew stone could have. He tries breathing through his nose and that's worse. He breathes through his teeth instead, carefully, like a man drinking soup that's too hot.

He lifts his left hand. It goes up about the length of his forearm and stops against something flat.

Cold. Rough. Concrete.

He lays his palm on it and feels along. It goes on further than his arm does. A slab. A big one. It isn't lying on him. It's over him, at an angle, low above his feet and higher above his head, caught at the top on something he can't see. Leaning, the way a sheet of board leans against a wall in a builder's yard.

Under it, there's a pocket of space. Just enough for one man lying down. Not an inch more.

*Somebody measured that,* he thinks. Then: *Nobody measured that.*

On the back of his left hand, under his own fingers, he can feel cloth. The black strip from the cape. Three times round, and a tuck. He finds the tuck with his thumb. Still there. Still holding.

His right hand is still gripping his katana.

He finds that out last. The fingers are closed so hard round the hilt that he'd stopped counting them as his. The blade lies along his side, bare, the tip somewhere down past his knee. He can feel the cold of it through his trouser leg.

He doesn't remember drawing it. He doesn't remember choosing to hold on. A minute before the world came down he was opening those fingers one at a time. Now here they are, closed again, as if nobody had ever asked them to let go.

He didn't choose this. The body did. The body always does.

---

He lies there.

He could move. He tests a few things, small, without meaning anything by it. Toes: yes. Knees: they bend a little, then meet stone. His left side aches in a deep, ordinary way. The cut on his forearm has opened again, warm and sticky under the sleeve. Nothing that's going to kill him. Not quickly.

He could push on the slab and find out how much of it there is. He doesn't.

He could shout.

He thinks about that one properly. He fills his chest to see whether it would work, and the dust comes in with the air, and he coughs. A short dry bark that goes nowhere. The sound stays in the pocket with him. It's very small.

He doesn't try again.

There's a phone in his inside pocket. It has a light on it. He knows exactly where it is. He could reach it with his left hand if he turned his wrist the right way. He doesn't. He has a fair idea of what he'd see, and seeing it wouldn't change anything except how much he'd have to think about it.

Something presses into his right thigh through his trouser pocket. Hard edges on a ring.

Keys. Only two that matter. His own front door. And a ground-floor door twenty minutes away by train, where he always rings the bell first anyway.

---

After a while, because there's nothing else to do with it, he lets the circle out.

Not far. It can't go far. It goes out the reach of one arm and one sword and meets stone everywhere. Stone above, stone below, stone on both sides, close enough to touch. The whole of his circle, every bit of it, full of rock that isn't going anywhere.

Nothing crosses the line. Nothing can. There's no room for anything to come in.

He lies there and feels it for a long moment. It's the safest his circle has ever been.

*Look at that,* he thinks. *Finally got it how I like it.*

He lets it go again.

---

*So this is it,* he thinks.

Not dramatically. He's not built for dramatically. Quietly, the way he'd read a line off a form.

*Grade 1. No technique. Lied to a student to avoid a basement. Stood still while the King of Curses walked past. Got buried under a building I didn't even get to fight in.*

He goes through it again, in case he's left anything out. He hasn't. It's quite a short list.

*What was any of it for?*

He doesn't mean it as a big question. He means it the way you'd mean it about a drawer full of old receipts. All that keeping. For what.

---

He thinks about his sister.

He sees her at the window, because that's where she is. Always. In the high-backed chair with the blue cover she chose and has since forgotten choosing.

It's Wednesday. Halloween. She won't know that. She'll be asleep by now, or awake and not minding which.

Tomorrow's Thursday. The physio comes on Thursdays. That'll happen whether he's under a building or not. He's already paid for it.

Sunday's in four days. Three, if it's past midnight. He doesn't know if it's past midnight.

On Sunday he'll ring the bell and then use his own key. Or he won't.

And if he doesn't, who tells her?

He goes looking for the answer, and there isn't one.

Nobody at school knows she exists. He made very sure of that. Not Ijichi. Not Okabe, with all his files. Not Gojo, who has been through everybody's desk. He told one man, once, at three in the morning, in a break room on the fourteenth floor of an empty building. That's the whole list. One man.

So it would be Ono who noticed. Ono notices everything and mentions almost none of it. She'd ring him, and he wouldn't answer. She'd ring again. After the third time she'd write something down in the notebook with the rabbit on it, and then she'd ring the agency. And Hasegawa would sit at his desk above the dry cleaner's, with the steam coming up the stairwell, and try to work out who you call about a man nobody can tell you anything about.

And his sister would sit at the window on Sunday and look at the door.

Or she wouldn't.

Some Sundays she calls him by their father's name. Some Sundays it takes her until half past four. Maybe she wouldn't know who was missing. Maybe all she'd know is that the tea was different. That somebody else had made it, and made it properly, the way she doesn't like it, and she'd frown at the cup and not know why.

*That's the one,* he thinks. *That's the one I can't do anything with.*

He lies there with it. It doesn't get any smaller.

---

He thinks about Takeru on her lap.

He doesn't think it, exactly. He sees it. The boy sitting the way small children sit once they've decided a lap is theirs, with all of their weight and no apology. And her right hand, the one that can still lift a little, lying flat on his back. Not holding. Just there. Resting on the fact of him.

He has never once said out loud what that was worth. He's never had a number for it. He's tried, sometimes, on trains. There isn't one.

---

He thinks about Yaga.

The debt is where it always is. He checks it, out of habit, here in the dark. He always checks it. He's never once found it gone.

It's there. Of course it's there. It's the only thing in here that hasn't moved.

*Still unpaid.*

Yaga has never mentioned it. Not once, in all these years. Not in a corridor, not in his office with a half-sewn doll on the desk, not when he handed Kusakabe a homeroom. Kusakabe used to think that was tact. Lying here, he isn't so sure. Maybe Yaga just knows what Kusakabe knows. That there's no amount. That a thing like that doesn't get paid back. It only gets carried, for as long as you can carry it, and you hope that counts for something.

If he stays down here, it doesn't get paid. It doesn't get cancelled either. It just stops. Halfway across the room, still in his arms.

*He'd hate this,* Kusakabe thinks. *Losing a perfectly good debtor to a building.*

---

He thinks about his flat.

The stain in the corner above the wardrobe. Two years. Mr. Sato's email address, which he has never once typed.

Somebody will have to clear the flat. That's how it works. Somebody always does. A man from the school, probably, with flattened boxes under his arm and a list. He'll let himself in with a spare key from some office. He'll see the jar of instant coffee by the kettle, and the bag with the sword cleaning kit in it, and the sofa with the shape of one person worn into the left end. He'll look up, the way people do in a stranger's room, and see the stain. And he'll think, *nobody ever reported that,* and he'll be right.

The sofa.

He thinks about the sofa for longer than he means to.

The left end. The cushion gone flat from one particular weight. All the days it's had off him. Not the nights. The nights were fine; everybody sits down at night. The days. Whole days, sat down in at ten in the morning and got up from in the dark. The five in a row last November. The Saturdays. The bank holidays. The afternoons after jobs, when he'd come in, and sit, and that was it, that was the afternoon.

He used to call it rest.

It wasn't rest. He knows that, lying here. They were just days he didn't want to put anything in, in case whatever he put in them asked for something back. So he left them empty. And they went anyway. One after another, quite happily, without him. Nothing in them to hold them down.

He tries to think what he'd have done with them instead. What he'd have put there.

He can't think of anything.

That's the worst part. Not that he wasted them. That he still can't think what they were for.

---

*I avoided everything,* he thinks. *And it still found me.*

It isn't self-pity. He checks. It's more like reading the total at the bottom of a bill.

Because he's good at it. He's very good at it. If there were a box for it on the forms, he'd tick it. A fire door propped open with a mop bucket. The long way round behind the shrine. A phone lying face down on the arm of the sofa while it rang. A covered walkway on a dry morning, taken round the outside, in the rain. A courtyard, and a boy laughing, and the long way round again.

Every one of them felt like a decision. That was the thing. Every one felt like a hand on the wheel. Like he was the one who decided where things went, and what got in, and what didn't.

And tonight, the best one of all. The cleanest work he's ever done. A staircase, a sign with an arrow on it, five floors down. One sentence, in his ordinary voice. *I heard they've already got people down there.* And it worked. It worked perfectly. He kept them both off that floor.

He went south instead. Further in. Not down.

And the floors came down to him.

He nearly laughs. The dust stops him. It comes out as a breath through his nose, and that puts more dust in.

*It felt like steering,* he thinks. *All of it. It was never steering. The world doesn't care which floor you didn't go to. It comes down where it comes down.*

*And you end up here. On your back, in the dark, holding a sword you never got to use. Wondering what you saved yourself for.*

---

He waits to feel frightened.

It would make sense. A man under a building ought to be frightened. He knows exactly what his fear feels like. He's had it all night. Cold palms. The heart going. The circle pulled in tight.

He waits for any of it.

It doesn't come. Fear needs something to spend, and he's got nothing left. He's been spending since seven o'clock.

What's left isn't fear. He turns it over in the dark for a while, trying to find the right word for it, the way he'd look for the right word on a report and then write something shorter instead.

*Lost,* he thinks. *That's the one.*

Not lost like a man in a strange city with the map the wrong way up. Lost like something that's fallen down behind the back of a heavy piece of furniture. Still there. Still exactly itself. Just somewhere nobody's going to think to look.

---

Time goes. He doesn't know how much.

Sounds come through the stone, far off and changed by it, soft at the edges, the way sounds are when you're under the water in a bath. A long rumble that might be another building going. Something that might be screaming, a long way away, that stops. A crack, very sharp. Then another. Then nothing, for a long time.

Then voices.

Two of them, or three. Somewhere up above and to the left. People moving over rubble, loose stuff sliding under their feet. A shout. A name, but not his. He can hear the shape of the words but not the words themselves. They get closer.

He lies still and listens to them, and holds the sword, and doesn't make a sound.

They get quieter. Then they're gone.

*You could have,* he thinks.

*Yeah.*

He tells himself he doesn't know why he didn't. That isn't true. He knows exactly. If he'd called out, they'd have come, and dug, and found him. And then they'd have asked. Are you hurt. Where. Who were you with. Where were you when it happened. Why were you there. And he'd have had to lie there in the light of their torches, with dust in his mouth, and answer.

He'd rather have the dark.

That's not a good thing to know about yourself. He knows it anyway. He's had a lot of practice knowing things about himself and then leaving them where they are.

Dust settles on his face, very lightly, one grain at a time. He doesn't wipe it off.

He closes his eyes. It makes no difference to the dark. But it's a different dark. His own.

*Maybe if I lie here long enough,* he thinks, *someone else will have dealt with it.*

All of it. Whatever turned. Whatever burned. Whatever's still down at the bottom of those five floors. The radios and the stretchers and the lists of names. Somebody will deal with it. Somebody always does. Somebody stronger, or braver, or just better at being found.

And then he'll come out, afterwards. When it's done.

Or he won't. From in here, it's hard to feel much difference.

---

Then the slab moves.

A grinding sound, right over his face. Stone on stone, long and low. A fine trickle of grit across his cheek and into his ear.

He opens his eyes.

Light.

A thin line of it at the high end, above his head. Grey and hard and so bright after the dark that it hurts, and he squints. The line gets wider. Dust pours through it in a sheet, lit all the way down, like something being sieved. The concrete over him shifts. Tilts. And lifts.

He looks up through the dust into a grey sky.

Panda is standing over him.

He's holding the slab above his head with both arms. It's a big slab. Much bigger than it felt from underneath. Panda's legs are set wide on the rubble and his arms are straight and shaking very slightly with the weight. His fur is grey all over with dust and dark in patches with blood, so much of both that he looks like a statue of himself somebody left out in the weather. One ear is torn at the edge. Whatever was on his shoulder in the tunnel is lost under everything that's happened since.

"Sensei," Panda says. "You alive?"

For a moment, Kusakabe can't answer.

He just looks up at him.

The student he lied to. Three hours ago, or four, at the top of a staircase, in his ordinary voice, so that neither of them would have to go down. The student who said *Okay, sensei,* and walked ahead of him down the middle of the street, believing it.

A cursed corpse. Made by the same man, with the same patient hands, as the boy on his sister's lap. The same forbidden work. The same impossible kindness. Standing in the ruins of Shibuya with its arms over its head, holding a building off him.

Panda, who didn't have to come looking.

Who, by the look of him, has been looking for some time.

"Yeah," Kusakabe says. His voice is rough with dust. It sounds like it belongs to somebody older. "Yeah. I'm alive."

"Good," Panda says. "Hang on."

He shifts his feet. Then he turns at the waist and tosses the slab aside like it's a sofa cushion. It goes over with a crash and a great slow cloud, and slides a little way down the heap, and stops. A grey slab of something that used to be a wall, lying on its back, looking at the sky.

Then Panda holds out a paw.

Kusakabe looks at it.

It's grey with dust, like the rest of him. There's blood dried into the pad, dark in the creases. It isn't shaking any more. It's just there. Held out at the height of a man lying on his back. Waiting, with no particular hurry, to see what he'll do. The way it has waited before.

He takes it with his left hand. The bandaged one. Panda's work, three times round and a tuck, still holding.

And he lets himself be pulled up.

---

Standing is worse than lying down.

His knees complain. Nothing under his feet is level. Dust slides off his shoulders and out of his hair, and he coughs, and spits grey, and stands there swaying a little, the sword still in his right hand, and looks.

There's no street.

There's nothing he'd call a street. Where the buildings were there's a slope of broken things going away in every direction, grey on grey, with steel rods coming out of it at angles, like the grass that grows on waste ground. Nothing standing above two floors, as far as the dust lets him see. Small fires here and there, quiet, minding their own business. A traffic light lying on its side, still trying to change.

The shapes the city had are gone. All of them. He doesn't know which way the station is.

He looks at Panda.

Panda is looking at him, head tilted a little, the way he does. Waiting.

"How'd you find me?" Kusakabe says.

Panda thinks about it.

"Kept lifting things," he says.

Kusakabe looks past him, back the way he must have come. The rubble is turned over in patches, a long untidy line of them, slabs pulled up and dropped aside, going back further into the grey than he can see.

He looks at Panda again.

He almost says something.

*Sorry about B5F.* It's right there. Three words. It would be easy, almost.

*Thank you.* Two.

*I didn't deserve that.* Four. The truest, and the worst.

He opens his mouth. There's dust in it. He closes it again.

He doesn't say any of them. They'd come out wrong, or they'd come out right, and he doesn't know which of those he'd be less able to stand there and hear. That's not how he works. He's never been able to make it work that way, and standing in the wreck of Shibuya with grit in his teeth is not the place he's going to learn.

So he says the only thing he's got.

"Let's go."

Panda looks at him for a moment longer. Whatever he sees, he keeps it.

"Okay, sensei."

---

They walk.

There's no road, so they make one. Panda picks the way, over the flat slabs and round the ones that move, testing each step with one foot before he gives it his weight. Kusakabe follows.

Not the way he followed down Meiji-dori, a few hours and a whole city ago, with his eyes on the white lines. Closer than that. Half a step behind, at Panda's left shoulder. Near enough to put out a hand. He doesn't. But he could.

The sword is still in his right hand. He hasn't put it away. He isn't sure he can yet, and he doesn't try.

He looks at Panda's back.

It's grey now, all of it, the same grey as everything. Grey fur, grey dust, the round grey shapes of the ears. He looks at it for a while. Longer than before. Not as long as he'd like.

Once, at the top of a heap of broken concrete, Panda stops and looks back over his shoulder. Not at the way they've come. At him. Just a glance, the kind you'd give a door you've already locked.

Kusakabe is there.

Panda turns round again and goes on down the other side.

Around them, nothing moves but the smoke.

Two shapes in all that grey. One big, one not. Walking carefully, because there's nothing else to do, over what's left of the place.

The smaller one half a step behind. Keeping up.

---

## Chapter 17. The Count

The first thing still standing is a postbox.

Red, square-shouldered, a little crooked on its post. Grey on top with dust, like everything, but red underneath, and upright. It's the first thing in a long time that's the shape it's meant to be.

Kusakabe looks at it as they go past.

*Last collection, seven forty-five,* he thinks. *Missed it.*

After the postbox, a wall with a window in it. Then a building with all its floors. Then a lamp post that's on, the light coming down in a cone full of drifting grey, as though it's snowing very slowly and only there.

Panda stops under it and looks up.

"Power," he says.

"Some."

They go on. The ground starts being a road again. There's glass on it, and stones, and a lot of paper, and one shoe, but it's flat, and it goes somewhere. Kusakabe's legs notice before he does. They stop checking every step.

The sword is still in his hand. He looks down at it, and then he puts it away. It goes into the scabbard first time. He's a little surprised by that.

---

The van is at the top of Meiji-dori, where the radio said it would be. There are three vans now, and two cars with their headlights on, angled in across the road to make a sort of room out of light. Inside the room are people. Some standing. More sitting. More than that lying down.

Nobody's shouting. That's the first thing he notices. Earlier tonight everyone was shouting. Now people talk in ordinary voices, quite low, the way they talk in a hospital corridor when the doors are open.

On the side of the nearest van, somebody has been writing in marker pen.

Names. Two columns of them, down the white panel, in thick black capitals. The left column is long and goes all the way to the wheel arch. The right column is shorter. There's no heading on either.

A young auxiliary is standing at the van with the pen. Black suit, white shirt gone grey, a cut along his jaw that someone has closed with three strips of tape. He's writing the name of a woman sitting on the kerb. She spells it for him. He writes it in the left column.

Then he sees Kusakabe.

"Name?"

"Kusakabe. Grade 1."

The auxiliary writes it. KUSAKABE. Not very straight. He's run out of room at the bottom and has started a second line beside the first.

He looks at Panda. He opens his mouth. He looks at the pen.

"Just Panda," Panda says.

"Right. Sorry." He writes PANDA. He looks at it for a moment, as if it might need a surname.

"Inumaki," Panda says. "Toge. Second year. Have you got him?"

The auxiliary runs the cap of the pen down the left column. Then, more slowly, the right one.

"No," he says. "Not on here. Doesn't mean." He doesn't finish it.

"Zen'in Maki?"

"No. Sorry. People are still coming in."

Panda nods. He stands there looking at the van, at the two columns, for longer than reading them would take.

Kusakabe doesn't read the right-hand column. He knows what it is. He doesn't need it to have a heading.

---

The radio inside the van is on. Somebody has turned it up so everyone can hear, and nobody can, quite. It comes in pieces.

"—Dogenzaka, two more, need a—"

"—the one at Shibuya Hikarie, is anyone—"

"—Ijichi-san, repeat, Ijichi-san was—"

Kusakabe looks up.

"—stabbed, repeat, he's out, they got him out, he's—"

A long hiss.

The young auxiliary has gone still with the pen in the air. He waits. Everybody near the van waits.

"—alive. He's alive. Over."

The auxiliary lets out a breath. He puts the cap on the pen and takes it off again.

"He drove me here," he says, to nobody. "This afternoon. He said sorry about the traffic."

*Of course he did,* Kusakabe thinks. *He'll apologise for this too. To the nurse. For bleeding on her.*

It's not funny. It's the nearest thing to it there is, so he keeps it.

---

"You."

A woman is pointing at him. Fifties, short grey hair, a lamp strapped round her head that makes it hard to see her face. Blue gloves. Blood on the gloves up to the wrist.

"Can you lift?"

"Yeah."

"Then lift." She points the other way, down the road, where the dark starts. "They're bringing them up from the edge. We're short of hands. Go with him."

*Him* is a man in a fire brigade jacket that doesn't fit, holding one end of a folding stretcher.

Kusakabe looks at Panda.

"I'll go and dig," Panda says. "There's a team going back in. They want someone who can move the big pieces."

"Okay."

"You all right?"

"Yeah."

Panda looks at him the way he does. Then he goes, round the back of the vans, towards the grey. He moves differently out there. Faster. As if, now that he's got something to lift, the rest of him knows what it's for.

Kusakabe takes the other end of the stretcher.

Before they go, he looks south.

From up here, at the top of the slope, he can see it. Not all of it. The dust is still hanging, and there's no proper light. But enough. Enough to see where the buildings stop being buildings. Where the city just ends, and the grey begins, and goes on.

It isn't ragged. That's what he can't stop looking at. You'd expect it to be ragged, the edge of a thing like that. Bits standing, bits fallen, the way a sandcastle goes when the tide gets it. It isn't. The edge runs away from him on both sides in a long, clean curve. As if somebody had stood in the middle with a length of string and a pencil and drawn it.

A circle. Someone else's. A great deal bigger than his.

Everything inside it, cut. Everything outside it, mostly fine. A postbox. A lamp post. A van with names on its side.

*So that's what it looks like,* he thinks, *from outside.*

He doesn't like the thought. He puts it down with the others.

"Ready?" says the fire brigade man.

"Yeah."

---

It's easy work. That's what's wrong with it.

You walk down to where the light runs out. Someone has already got a person out of the rubble and onto the ground. You put the stretcher down beside them. You don't lift them; the medics lift them, with a board and a count. One, two, three. Then you pick up your end.

Then you walk back.

A person is heavier than they look and lighter than you're ready for. The weight comes through the poles into his hands, and up his arms, and settles in his shoulders, and it moves. It shifts when they breathe. He can feel it through the handles. It's the only way he knows, sometimes, that they are.

The fire brigade man is called Mizuno. He says so on the first trip, over his shoulder, as if it's a thing you ought to exchange. He's an auxiliary. The jacket was on the back seat of a fire engine with nobody in it, and he was cold. He doesn't say whose it is. He doesn't seem to want to know.

Mizuno walks in front. Kusakabe walks behind, where he can see the face.

He doesn't look at the faces. He looks at the man's back, and the road, and the place where the light starts again.

Fifty-two steps from the edge to the vans. He counts them on the first trip without meaning to. On the second trip he counts them on purpose. Fifty-two. On the third it's fifty-four, because they go round a car.

The man on the third stretcher is awake. He keeps saying he's fine and trying to sit up, and Mizuno keeps saying, "Lie down, sir," in a voice so patient it might be a recording. The man lies down. Then he tries to sit up again.

"Where's my phone," he says. "My wife's going to ring."

"Lie down, sir."

Kusakabe walks.

*Fifty-four.*

They put him down by the vans with the others. The woman with the head-lamp is already there, kneeling, and she doesn't look up.

"Again," she says.

They go again.

---

It's on the fifth trip, or the sixth, that the other names start.

Not on the radio. In the road. People pass each other carrying things, and as they pass they say whatever they've got, quickly, the way you'd hand someone a bag on a staircase.

"—the Zen'in girl, the one with the glasses—"

He's walking backwards over a kerb. He doesn't turn round.

"—burnt. Bad. The fire one got her. The one with the head like a—"

"—she was with the Zen'in men, the old one, they were all—"

"—took her out in the first car. She was talking. She was swearing, actually."

Then they're past, and it's just the road again, and the weight in his hands.

*Swearing,* he thinks. *Good. That's good.*

He tries to see it and stops himself. He's carrying someone. He needs his eyes for the kerb.

But he sees one thing anyway, without asking to. The third step from the bottom, at the yard. The practice sword lying across it, handle towards where he sits, the tape on the grip gone grey with her hands.

There won't be one there tomorrow.

He doesn't know when there will be.

*Fifty-two.*

---

Somewhere around one o'clock, the radio says a name he knows.

He's at the van when it happens, putting his end down. Mizuno has gone to get water. The radio is talking about a team in the south, and a street, and a list, and then a voice comes in over the top of it, a different voice, tired and very clear.

"—confirm Nanami, Grade 1, Nanami Kento. Confirmed. Over."

That's all.

No one near the van reacts. Most of them don't know the name. A medic carries on cutting a sleeve. The woman on the kerb has her eyes shut.

The young auxiliary, at the side of the van, looks at his pen.

Then he steps along to the right-hand column. He has to crouch a little; it's further down. He writes slowly, in the same thick capitals as all the others. NANAMI K.

He stands back and looks at it.

Kusakabe looks at it too.

"Sure?" he says.

The auxiliary turns. He seems surprised anyone asked.

"They said confirmed." He swallows. "Someone saw. It was a curse. The one that does things to people. The shapes of people." He stops. "That's all I've got. Sorry."

"Okay."

Kusakabe stands there.

Six letters and one. Black on white, on the side of a van, in a stranger's handwriting. Not very straight, because the man is tired and the van isn't flat.

*See you.*

It's just there. Not as a memory. As a sound. A finger and thumb at a knot, and two words, and a pale suit going off down a wet street. As if somebody had pressed play on it, in his head, without asking.

*See you.*

He's waiting for something else to come after it. He doesn't know what. It doesn't come. It's just the two words, and then the space where they were, and then the two words again.

"You," says the woman with the head-lamp, from somewhere behind the light. "Are you lifting or not?"

He turns round.

"Lifting."

He picks up the end of the stretcher. Mizuno is back. They go down the road.

---

*Fifty-two.*

He thinks about the bakery.

He didn't mean to. It just arrives, the way the two words arrived. A small place near a station. Opens at seven. The owner starts at four. The breaded sandwich. Not the melon bread.

He was going to go. One Sunday. He never said so, out loud, to anyone. He'd just had it in mind, the way you have a thing in mind for later, and later is a place you think will always be there.

*You could still go,* he thinks. *It's still there. The bakery didn't die.*

He knows he won't. He knows it the way he knows the number of steps.

They put down the stretcher. They pick up another.

*See you.*

He thinks about a beach that's longer than it needs to be. A house near the sea. More than a hundred and forty books that somebody was saving for evenings. He wonders who gets them now. Whether they'll be read. Whether anyone will even open the boxes, or whether they'll just go on to the next place, still waiting, a hundred and forty evenings nobody's going to have.

*Then don't work Sundays.*

He doesn't. Almost never. It's past midnight now, the first of November, and Sunday is three days off, and he means to be at a ground-floor door on Sunday, ringing the bell before he uses his key. He's kept that one. He'll keep it.

The man who told him to is a name on a van.

*Everything else they'll take.*

He's walking backwards over the kerb again. He gets it right. He's had practice.

And there's another thing, under all of it, smaller and colder, that he doesn't want to look at and looks at anyway. He told one person. Once. At three in the morning, in a break room, by a vending machine. *I've got a sister.* The only person at the school who knew.

Now nobody does.

He's back where he started. Nobody knows anything about him at all.

It should feel like safety. That's what he built it for.

It doesn't feel like anything. It just feels like the list got shorter.

*Fifty-four.*

---

Panda comes back a while later.

Kusakabe is sitting on an upturned plastic crate behind the second van, because the woman with the head-lamp told him to sit for ten minutes, and he did, because it was easier than arguing. He has a bottle of water he hasn't opened. His arms have that light, buzzing feeling they get after carrying, as though they're expecting the weight to come back.

Panda's fur is darker than before. Wetter. There's something dark on his forearms that isn't dust. He walks round the van slowly and stops in front of Kusakabe, and doesn't sit.

"I found someone who carried Toge," he says.

Kusakabe looks up.

"An auxiliary. A woman. She was on the team that got him out. Before. Near the station." Panda's voice is level and low. "He's alive. They've taken him. They've got him somewhere they can work on him."

"Okay."

"His arm," Panda says.

He stops.

Kusakabe waits.

"The left one," Panda says. "It's gone. It was the—" He makes a small movement with one paw, a flat, sideways movement, in the air between them, and then stops making it. "When everything came down. When it all got cut. He was in it."

Kusakabe doesn't say anything.

There isn't anything. He goes looking, the way he went looking under the rock, and there's nothing there to say. *Okay* is wrong. *Yeah* is wrong. Every word he's got is wrong and most of them are short.

So he sits.

Panda sits too. Not on a crate. On the road, a few metres off, with his back against the van's rear wheel. Not next to him. Near.

*The boy who says little,* Kusakabe thinks.

He doesn't finish the thought for a while.

He thinks about a bench outside a clinic. A tin of lozenges. The wrappers. Inumaki folds the wrappers when he's finished, into small careful squares, smaller and smaller, and puts them in his pocket. Kusakabe has watched him do it a dozen times. Two hands. You can't do it with one. He's never thought about that before. He's never had to.

*Says little. And now has less to say it with.*

Inumaki doesn't use words to talk. He uses rice-ball names, and a phone, and his hands. A hand tilted for *depends.* A zip pulled down to the chin when it matters. A thumb on a screen, typing *thanks* in lower case. Half of everything that boy says, he says with his hands.

Now he's got half the hands.

Kusakabe sits with that.

It doesn't do anything. It doesn't get bigger or smaller. It just sits there with him, on the crate, behind the van, the way the stretcher weight sits in his shoulders after he's put the stretcher down. Still there. Still the shape of what he was carrying.

Panda doesn't say anything either.

That's something he'll think about later, much later. That Panda could have said a great many things just then, and didn't. Could have said *we should have been there.* Could have said *if we'd gone south.* Could have said *if we'd gone down.* He could have said a lot of things, and every one of them would have been fair.

Panda sits against the wheel with his paws on his knees, and looks at the road, and says none of them.

After a while the radio says something about a team at Dogenzaka needing someone strong.

Panda gets up.

"That's me," he says.

"Yeah."

Panda stands there a moment. He looks at the unopened water bottle in Kusakabe's hand. Then he goes back round the van, towards the grey, and doesn't look back.

Kusakabe opens the bottle. He drinks half of it. He sits a little longer.

Then he gets up, and goes round to the side of the first van.

The young auxiliary is still there with the pen. He's been there, as far as Kusakabe can tell, the whole night. He's writing a name in the left column, and his hand is shaking a little, so the letters lean.

Kusakabe holds out the bottle. Half full.

The auxiliary looks at it as if he's never seen one.

"Drink that," Kusakabe says.

"I'm fine."

"I know. Drink it anyway."

The auxiliary drinks it. All of it, in one go, with his head back, like a child. Then he looks at the empty bottle, embarrassed.

"Thanks."

Kusakabe takes the bottle back from him, so he won't have to work out where to put it. He doesn't look at the right-hand column on his way past. He knows how long it is. He knows one name on it.

Then he goes back, because the ten minutes are over, and the woman with the head-lamp is already looking for him.

---

They run out of stretchers before they run out of people.

So he carries a door instead, with Mizuno, an office door with the handle still on, a woman lying on it holding the handle with one hand as if it might open. Fifty-two.

He holds a torch for a medic who's putting a line into a man's arm, because her head-lamp has died. He holds it very still. She doesn't thank him. She says, "Left a bit," twice, and then, "There," and then, "You can go."

He walks a boy of about twelve, in a skeleton costume, up and down the line of people sitting on the kerb, looking for his mother. They don't find her. The boy doesn't cry. He just says, "She was wearing a crown," to every woman they pass, as though that should be enough. Kusakabe leaves him with an auxiliary who has a list of children. The boy gives his name to her very politely. KUDO. She writes it down.

He finds Tanabe, or Tanaka, sitting on the bumper of the third van with a dressing over the swollen eye.

Tanabe looks up at him with the other one.

"You held your stairs," he says.

It isn't a question. Kusakabe doesn't know what it is.

"Yeah," he says.

Tanabe nods. He looks at the right-hand column on the side of the first van. He's quiet for a while.

"I didn't go back down," he says. "In the end."

"No."

"Nobody did. Nobody could have. You hear that?" He's still looking at the van. "That's what they're saying."

Kusakabe hears it. He doesn't know what to do with it, so he leaves it where it is.

"Eye all right?"

"It's there." Tanabe touches the dressing. "That's about all I'm asking of it tonight."

That's all. Kusakabe goes and picks up his end of the next thing.

*See you.*

---

Somewhere after two, the woman with the head-lamp sends him off the line.

Not to rest. To look. There's a side street running east off Meiji-dori, a block down, where the power's still out. Somebody thinks they heard someone. Nobody's been down it since the ground stopped moving.

"Look," she says. "Shout if there's anyone. Don't be a hero."

"Wasn't planning to."

She looks at him for the first time all night, properly, under the lamp. Whatever she sees, she doesn't say.

"Good," she says. "We've got enough of those in the right-hand column."

He goes.

---

The side street is dark.

Not the dark under the rock. This dark has edges. Shapes of buildings, still mostly up. Shop shutters, some down, some half down, one ripped out of its rails and lying in the road like a dropped page. A bicycle rack with no bicycles. Glass underfoot, so much of it that every step sounds like walking on sugar.

He walks the middle of the street. He lets the circle out, a little. Not to fight anything. Just to listen.

Nothing crosses it. Nothing alive. He walks the length of the street and back again, and calls once, "Anyone?" and his voice goes off between the buildings and doesn't come back with anything.

*Nobody,* he thinks. *Good. Or not good. Just nobody.*

He's turning to go when he sees the light.

Halfway down, set back in the doorway of a closed shop. Pale, steady, very white. It's the only thing on the whole street that's lit.

A vending machine.

It's standing there in the dark as if nothing has happened. Front panel glowing. Two rows of small cans behind the glass, each one with its little price underneath. A row of buttons, lit red for hot. The glass is cracked across one corner, a long thin line. Otherwise it's fine. It's humming. He can hear it from the middle of the road.

Somewhere under all this, a cable still runs to it. Or a battery. Or it's just too stubborn to stop. He doesn't know how they work. He's never once wondered.

He walks over to it.

He stands in front of it, in its light. It lights him up, too, the front of his coat, his hands, the dust in the creases of his knuckles. The black cloth round his left hand, three times and a tuck, filthy now.

He puts his hand in his coat pocket for change.

There's paper in there. Folded, soft, a square of something waxy. He doesn't take it out. He knows what it is. He moves his fingers past it.

In the other pocket, coins. He picks them out by feel. Some small ones. And one that's bigger, and colder than the others, as though it's been in that pocket longer. It has. He knows which one it is. It's just a coin.

He leaves that one where it is, and feeds in the small ones.

He presses the button for coffee. Hot, black, the one with the plainest can. Something drops behind the flap with a heavy, ordinary clunk.

Then his hand goes back to the coins.

He watches it do it. It goes into the pocket, finds the cold one, and puts it in the slot, and adds two small ones, and presses the same button again. As if this is just what happens. As if this is what you do, at a machine, at night, at the end of a long job.

He's never bought two in his life.

Clunk.

He bends down and takes them out. One in each hand. They're hot, almost too hot to hold, the way they always are at first. He stands there in the doorway with the machine humming at his back.

He opens the first one with his thumb, and drinks some. It's bad. It's very bad. It tastes of the can and of sugar he didn't ask for, and it's the right temperature for the night, and he drinks it slowly.

The second one he doesn't open.

He holds it.

It's hot in his left hand, through the cloth. He can feel the heat going into the cut on his knuckles and staying there. He doesn't put it down. He doesn't put it in his pocket. He doesn't know who it's for.

Down at the end of the street, past the dark, there are headlights, and voices, and the van with the two columns on its side. Someone will be looking for him soon. The woman with the head-lamp. *Again.*

He'll go in a minute.

He stands in the light of the machine, in the dead street, drinking a bad coffee out of one hand. In the other, the second can slowly stops being hot. Then warm. Then it's just the temperature of his hand.

He holds it anyway.

---

## Chapter 18. Uzumaki

The second can goes in his coat pocket in the end.

He can't drink it. He can't give it to anyone. He can't put it back in the machine, because machines don't work that way, and neither does anything else tonight. So it goes in the left pocket, where it sits against his ribs, the same temperature as he is, like something he's been carrying for years.

The side street has gone quiet in the way it does between things. The machine hums in its doorway. Somewhere up the hill a van door slides shut. The curtain overhead, close and black.

It's after two. The first of November. Nobody has said so. He worked it out from his watch, which came through the building. That's more than the building did.

Panda finds him there, standing by the machine.

"Sensei."

"Yeah."

"Yuji's still going." Panda's head is turned south, towards the station, his torn ear flat. "Him and the big one from Kyoto. Against the patchwork one. The one that changes people."

Kusakabe knows which one. A school, an assembly hall, a curse that changes people's shapes. A sealed file with Nanami's name on it. The thing that killed Nanami tonight, if the auxiliary had it right. He heard that an hour ago, in pieces, and he hasn't put the pieces together yet. He isn't going to do it here.

"Radio says anyone who can," Panda says.

Kusakabe looks up the street, towards the vans and the stretchers. Then at Panda, who isn't asking him anything. Who's just telling him where he's going, in case he wants to know.

*Somebody should go back and carry,* he thinks.

Somebody is. The medic has hands enough. She said so, more or less, when she sent him down here.

"Fine," he says.

---

They meet her two streets on.

She comes round a corner at a run, a sword in a black scabbard held tight against her hip, and nearly goes into Panda. Her thumb pushes the guard up an inch before she sees who it is. Then it slides back down.

"Panda-kun." Her whole face lets go. Then she sees Kusakabe, and it pulls itself straight again, the way a student sits up when a teacher walks in. Which one has. Sort of.

She's young. Blue hair to her shoulders, the Kyoto uniform, dust down one side of it as if she's been knocked over and got up and not had time to brush off.

"You're Kusakabe-san," she says. "From Tokyo. New Shadow Style."

He has no idea how she knows that. Then he looks at her hands.

Left hand on the scabbard just below the guard. Thumb ready. Right hand loose, a little above the hilt, not touching it. Weight forward on the balls of the feet. It's how they teach it. Exactly how they teach it. A different dojo, probably, a different teacher, ten or fifteen years on. The same thumb.

"Miwa," Panda says. "Kasumi Miwa. Kyoto, second year. She's nice."

"I'm looking for Todo," she says. "And Itadori-kun. Somebody said they were fighting the special grade. The patchwork one. Down there." She points south with her chin, because her hands are busy.

"Same," says Panda.

She falls in on Panda's other side, and the three of them walk.

She's shaking. Not much. A fine steady shake in the hands, the kind you only see because the scabbard tip moves. She sees him see it, and grips harder to make it stop. It doesn't stop. It just moves up into her wrists.

He knows that one. He's done that one for years.

"I should say," she says, after a bit, to the road. "I'm not very strong. Just so you know. Before anything. I'm mostly good at holding the line."

Nobody answers. She seems to need to fill the space, so she does.

"I only do this for the money, really. That sounds bad. It's just, there's my brothers, and..." She stops. "Sorry. That's not relevant."

Something in him sits up at that, and looks at her, and then sits back down.

*It's relevant,* he thinks. He doesn't say it.

"Do you think they're all right?" she says. "Itadori-kun and Todo?"

"Don't know."

She nods, as if that was a proper answer. It's the only one he's got.

---

The ground opens out where a block of buildings used to be.

Fires here and there, low. Pale concrete. A crater with soft edges, as if something had been pressed into the city with a thumb. It's lighter here than it should be under the curtain, because so much is burning.

He sees the boy first.

Pink hair, grey with dust. Kneeling, his back to them, head down, shoulders going up and down. That's all. No face. Kusakabe has gone a whole night, a whole year, without seeing that face, and it seems he'll go a bit longer. Something in him is grateful for that. Something in him isn't.

Against a heap of rubble to the left, a very big young man is sitting with his legs out in front of him. One of his arms ends at the wrist in a wad of red cloth. He's awake. He's grinning, which seems wrong, and also seems to be the kind of thing he'd do.

And in the middle of the open ground, a man in a monk's robes is bending over something on the ground.

The something is grey. Patched. It's trying to crawl away, and it isn't getting very far. Whatever it was earlier tonight, it has been beaten down to something about the size of a dog, dragging itself through the dust on hands that keep changing shape.

The man straightens up.
