Kusakabe sees his face, and his stomach goes cold and flat.

He knows the face. Not from life. From a file. From a photograph that was pinned up in the dispatch office for a whole year. The corners curled and the colour went orange, and nobody looked at it because everybody already knew. Suguru Geto. Special grade curse user. The man who declared war on everyone, and then sent the Night Parade to collect.

Dead. Last Christmas Eve. Everybody knows that too. Gojo finished it himself.

Same long black hair, tied up. Same heavy earrings. The same robes, the dull gold and black of them. A pleasant face. Calm and pleasant, like a man who's come to talk to you about your insurance.

And across the forehead, from one side to the other, a line of black stitches. Neat. Small. Like a seam on a coat.

*Dead men don't have stitches,* he thinks. *Dead men don't need them.*

Panda has gone completely still beside him.

"That's Geto," Panda says, very low. "And Geto's dead."

"Yeah."

That's all either of them can manage. It's like looking at a house you know has been empty for a year and seeing a light on upstairs. Not frightening, at first. Just wrong. Wrong in a way that gets into the back of your knees.

The man looks down at the crawling thing with something like fondness.

"You did well," he says. "You really did."

The voice fits the face exactly. Kusakabe has never heard it before, and he'd know it anywhere, and that's worse.

The man holds out his hand. The grey thing on the ground stops crawling. It folds. It goes in on itself, over and over, smaller and darker, until it's a ball about the size of a plum, sitting in his palm.

He puts it in his mouth and swallows.

Kusakabe's throat moves with it, the way your throat moves when you watch somebody take a pill. He wishes it hadn't.

The boy on his knees doesn't look up.

---

The man turns. He isn't looking at them, particularly. He's looking at the boy.

He lifts his hand again, palm out, and the air starts to turn.

"Maximum," he says. Pleasantly. As if reading out the next stop on a train. "Uzumaki."

It comes out of nothing, in front of his hand. Everything he just swallowed, and everything he's ever swallowed before that, curses folded into curses folded into curses, pulled in to one point and wound tight. Black, and every dark colour there is, turning on itself. Tighter. Tighter. The way a wet towel tightens when you wring it, until you think it'll tear.

Kusakabe feels it before he's properly seen it.

The circle comes up on its own, and the whole edge of it is leaning. Grit drifts sideways across the ground. The flames on the low fires bend towards it like a crowd bending towards something that's fallen in the road. His coat tugs at him, gently, the way a child tugs. The air itself twists, and there's a sound in his ears that's mostly pressure, a high thin note, like a finger on the rim of a glass, getting louder.

He knows what it's for. It's for the boy on his knees.

And between the boy and the man, there's Miwa.

He didn't see her go. She's just there, twenty metres ahead of him, in front of the kneeling boy, her sword out. She's in the stance. Her own circle is up, small and round and properly drawn, a good one. Somebody taught her well.

And she's stopped.

Not waiting. Stopped. Knees locked straight. Shoulders up round her ears. Her face is white, and her eyes are fixed on the thing turning in the man's hand, and she isn't going to move out of the way. She can't. He can see it from here. He can see it in her knees.

Her circle is going to be a sheet of paper held up in front of that.

One tenth of a second.

That's what there is. Not a moment. Less than a moment. Not enough room for a thought. Not enough room for a sentence. Not *I heard they've already got people down there.* Not *Traffic.* Not *in a bit.* Nothing he's ever said to get out of anything would fit in the gap.

So nothing does.

He's there.

He doesn't remember the twenty metres. He's just there, in front of her, between her and the turning dark, and the circle is around both of them, and the blade is out.

The spiral comes into the circle.

And his body answers it the way it's answered everything for twenty years. Not a decision. A habit. The same draw he's done ten thousand mornings, alone, in a cold room, until his shoulders ached and then until they didn't. The same small step. The same angle. Every bit of it was put there a long time ago, one morning at a time, by a man who wasn't thinking about tonight at all.

He doesn't try to cut it. You don't cut that. Nobody could.

He meets it at the edge of the circle, at an angle, with the flat of everything he's got, and he turns it.

Like a stone in a fast river. The stone doesn't stop the river. The stone doesn't fight it. The stone just stays exactly where it is, and is exactly the shape it is, and the river, all that weight of it, finds that it would rather go round.

The spiral goes round.

It goes past his shoulder with a scream like metal being torn, past Miwa's head, close enough to lift her hair, and on into the dark behind them. Something there that was part of a building isn't any more. The sound of it comes back to him a long moment later, a deep soft crump, and then a rain of small stones.

Then it's quiet.

He's still standing.

His arms are shaking. Not his hands. All of it, from the shoulders down, the way a rope shakes after a weight has come off it. His palms are buzzing like a struck bell. He can't feel his fingers. He looks down and they're still closed round the hilt, both hands. The blade is still in one piece, which he finds hard to believe. He can't tell from feeling alone whether he's holding it or it's holding him.

Behind him, Miwa sits down in the dust. Not falls. Sits. All at once, like a coat sliding off a hook.

He doesn't turn round. He can feel her there. Inside the circle. Breathing.

---

The man in the robes is looking at him.

Properly. Not through him. Not past him at the thing behind. At him, with real attention, his head on one side.

It's the first time tonight anything that size has really looked at him. He'd thought being looked through was the worst of it. He was wrong. This is worse. This is being noticed.

"Simple Domain," the man says.

He sounds pleased. Genuinely pleased. Like a man who's gone back to a street he knew and found a shop still open that he'd been sure had closed years ago.

"And New Shadow Style, at that. Well."

Kusakabe says nothing. His arms are shaking too hard to say anything with.

"It's rare," the man says. "Somebody of your generation, using real sorcery. Proper sorcery. The old kind." The stitched forehead creases, very slightly, like a man reading the small print. "I'm impressed. I mean that."

*Impressed,* Kusakabe thinks. *A dead man is impressed with me. Put it on the file. Next to Froze.*

The man looks at him a moment longer, as though he might remember the face. Then he doesn't seem to need to. He's already looking somewhere else.

---

Kusakabe stands there, and the fear arrives.

Late. All at once. The way the pain from a stubbed toe arrives a full second after the noise, and then makes up for lost time.

His legs go loose at the knee. His heart is going so hard he can feel it in his teeth. His mouth is full of a taste like pennies. He's standing in the open, in front of a dead man with a sewn-up head, with a girl from Kyoto he met twenty minutes ago sitting in the dust at his heels.

*What am I doing here?*

No. That's not the question. He knows what he's doing here. Panda said anyone who can, and he said fine.

*Why didn't I avoid that?*

That's the one.

Because he would have. Any other night of his life. He's the best there is at it. He'd have seen the spiral and his feet would have found the way out before his head had finished reading the situation. He'd have been behind a wall. He'd have been at the edge of things, looking at the ground, being nobody in particular. He's been doing it all night. He was doing it at the top of a staircase five hours ago, in his ordinary voice.

So where was that?

He goes looking for it. For the moment where he chose. The way you look for a step on a staircase you don't remember climbing.

The spiral. The girl's knees. Then the turn.

There's nothing in between. Not a gap. Not a door. Nothing he could have stood in and thought it over.

It didn't come to the part of him that decides things. The part that says *Traffic*, and turns the radio down, and takes the long way round. It never got that far.

*She was already inside,* he thinks. *Inside the circle.*

That's as far as he gets with it. It doesn't turn into anything. It just sits there, a small hard thing he hasn't got anywhere to put.

He puts it down for now. Somewhere he'll find it.

---

Someone else arrives, fast, from the side.

A young man. Pale, thin, his hair tied up in two short bunches, a dark band of something across the bridge of his nose like a stripe of paint. He's shouting before he's even stopped moving. Something about brothers. Something about *you*, at the man in the robes, in a voice that's been torn open.

There's blood in the air around him. Not spilled. Moving. Drawn out of his hands in long lines, hard and straight as wire, bending where he tells them to.

Kusakabe has seen blood used like that once before. A boy from a minor branch of the Kamo, nineteen, pleased with himself, in a railway tunnel. This is the same thing done by someone who means it with every part of him.

The man in the robes laughs, and turns to meet him, and the two of them go at each other in the middle of the open ground.

Kusakabe doesn't follow it. He couldn't, and it isn't his. Lines of red flicking across the edge of his circle. A turning darkness. The ground jumping once under his feet.

He reaches back without looking and gets a fistful of Miwa's collar and pulls her up and behind him. She comes. She's light. She doesn't argue. Panda is on her other side already, big and grey and solid, his shoulder touching hers.

"Back," Kusakabe says.

They go back. A step. Two.

He means to keep going. He means it quite strongly.

---

The cold comes first.

He thinks it's his hands. They've been numb since the spiral, so he doesn't trust them. Then he breathes out, and the breath goes white.

He looks down. Frost on the rubble. Spreading. Fine and fast and very pretty, white feathers growing out across the grey stone in every direction, the way frost grows on a car windscreen in January, except you can see it happen.

There's someone standing near the man in the robes who wasn't there before.

That's the part that gets him. It didn't cross his circle. It didn't come from anywhere. It was simply there, the way a word is suddenly on a page you've already read.

White hair, cut short. A pale face. Pale, quiet clothes. He can't tell how old. He can't tell if it's a man or a woman. After a moment he stops trying. It doesn't matter. The white-haired one is looking at the three of them now, without hurry, the way you'd look at a mess on a table that someone else ought to have wiped up.

The white-haired one says something to the man in the robes. Quiet. Not to them. He doesn't catch it.

Then the cold arrives properly.

It comes up from the ground. His feet first. One moment they're cold, and the next he can't feel them, and when he looks down there's ice up to his ankles, clear and thick, with dust caught in it.

He tries the circle. The cold crosses it everywhere at once, the way the air does. There's nothing to meet. Nothing to turn. You can't move a stone out of the way of the weather.

It doesn't climb so much as close, like a hand. He feels Panda go stiff beside Miwa, mid-word, a sound starting and not finishing. He feels Miwa's hand close on the back of his coat and stay closed.

His breath stops being white, because it's stopped going anywhere.

*Save a kid,* he thinks, with the last of his face, *get turned into an ice sculpture. Very dignified.*

Then everything is white.

---

It's not dark, at least. He's had enough dark for one night.

It's white. A cloudy, bluish white, with light coming through it from somewhere, and shapes on the other side that might be fire, or might be people, or might be nothing at all.

He can't move.

That's fine. He's had practice. He stood still for a king, earlier, because a king told him to. He lay still under a building because he didn't want to be found. Now somebody else has done the holding for him.

*Third time tonight,* he thinks. *At least this one isn't my idea.*

He can't feel his hands. They had a head start.

Sound comes through the ice very slow and very far off, like sound through a wall in a hotel. A crack. A rush of something. The man's voice, still pleasant, saying something long. The other one, the young one, shouting. Then not shouting. Then a long low noise from somewhere a long way away, as if the whole city has let out a breath.

He thinks about Miwa's hand on the back of his coat. He can't feel it. He decides it's still there.

He thinks, quite calmly, that if this goes on much longer he'll be very cold, and then he'll stop minding, and that'll be that. He thinks it the way he'd note the forecast.

Then he thinks about the lavender by her door. It'll want replacing in a fortnight. Nobody else knows that. It isn't written down anywhere.

He doesn't like that thought. He keeps it anyway. It's something to hold.

---

He hears the engine before anything else.

A deep, rough, rising roar, coming up the street. He thinks it's a motorbike. A big one. Somebody riding a motorbike into the middle of this, which seems like the act of an idiot, or someone who's never been frightened of anything.

It stops. It wasn't a motorbike. He doesn't know what it was.

Then a voice. A woman's voice, loud and warm and completely relaxed, as if she's walked into a bar where she knows everybody.

"So," she says, to someone. "What kind of woman is your type?"

He can't see who she's asking. He doesn't think they answer.

*Who asks that,* he thinks. *Here. Now.*

There's only one person he's ever heard of who would. A special grade who never comes to meetings. Who's never on any dispatch board. Who's been abroad for years, doing whatever she likes, and answers to nobody. People at school say her name the way they talk about the weather in another country.

*Yuki Tsukumo,* he thinks. *The only sorcerer in Japan who's better at not turning up than I am.*

And she's turned up.

Then a lot of noise he can't make into anything. Then quiet.

Then a knock, right in front of his face. A sharp one. The white in front of him goes grey, then cracks across in a long line like a frozen pond. Then it's falling away in plates, and there's air, cold and full of smoke, and light.

A woman is standing in front of him. Very tall. Long fair hair. A jacket. A grin like she's just been told a good joke and is saving it for later.

"Morning," she says. "Hold still a sec."

He would laugh, if his face worked.

"Been doing that," he says. His voice comes out cracked down the middle.

She does laugh. Loud, with her head back. Then she puts the flat of her hand on the ice round his chest and it breaks, all of it, at once. He falls forward out of it onto his hands and knees on the rubble.

---

Dawn.

He doesn't know when the curtain came down. Some time while he was inside the ice. When he gets his head up, there's sky above Shibuya again. A real sky, with nothing over it.

It's grey in the west, still. In the east it's going pale. Then a thin wash of yellow along the broken edges of what's left of the buildings. Smoke going up straight into it, in a lot of thin lines, because there's no wind.

He's sitting on a slab with someone's blanket round his shoulders. He doesn't know whose. It smells of engine oil.

Thawing is worse than freezing. Nobody tells you that. Freezing just takes things away. Thawing gives them back, all of them, at once, with interest. His feet burn. His knees ache in the bone. Water runs out of his hair and down the back of his collar. The pins and needles in his hands have got so bad they've gone all the way round to feeling like fire.

Panda is a few metres off, shaking himself like a dog out of a river, spraying meltwater in a wide ring. He stops. Shakes again. Looks at Kusakabe.

"Cold," Panda says.

"Yeah."

"You okay, sensei?"

"Ask me later."

Panda nods, as if that's fair, and sits down very heavily on the rubble beside him, and steams gently in the first of the sun.

Miwa is on his other side. Someone has given her a blanket too. She's holding a cup of something hot in both hands and not drinking it. Her knees are pulled up. Her sword is across them, sheathed. Her eyes keep going back to the place where she stood.

"Kusakabe-san," she says. "Thank you. I couldn't, I just, I couldn't make myself..."

She doesn't finish. Her face goes tight.

He thinks about what to say. There are a lot of things. Most of them are long.

"Keep your knees soft," he says.

She looks at him.

"When it's big. They lock. Keep them soft. Then you can move."

She looks down at her knees. Then back at him. Then she nods, once, very seriously, as though he's given her something she's going to put somewhere safe.

That's fine. That's a good one. One sentence. Two, if you're counting.

Yuki Tsukumo comes over the rubble towards them, hands in her jacket pockets, picking her way along as if she's walking on a beach. She stops in front of him and looks down, interested, the way someone looks at a dog that's done something clever.

"You're the sword guy," she says. "With the circle. They told me."

He doesn't ask who.

"So," she says. "What kind of woman is your type?"

He considers it. He's very tired.

"One who lets me sleep."

She laughs again, the big laugh, and claps him once on the shoulder hard enough to hurt. Then she goes off across the rubble towards the pink-haired boy, who is sitting now, somewhere off to the left, with his head in his hands. Kusakabe doesn't look over. Not yet.

He puts his hand in his coat pocket.

The can is still there. Frozen solid. The side of it has split open, and there's a fat brown ridge of coffee ice bulging out of the split, like toothpaste out of a tube someone stepped on. He takes it out and looks at it. Then he puts it down on the slab next to him, very carefully, standing up. Somebody might want it. Nobody is going to want it.

The sun clears the top of something broken. Light comes across the ruins, low and gold, and lands on him.

His teeth are chattering. He can't make them stop. He doesn't try.

He holds his hands out in front of him, in the light, and looks at them.

Red, and grey with dust, and shaking. A blister coming up across one palm where the hilt was. The black strip of cape still round the knuckles of the left, wet through, three times round and a tuck.

He turns them over. Turns them back.

He looks at them for a long time, the way you'd look at a pair of hands on somebody else. Somebody he doesn't know very well. Somebody braver.

---

# Part Four: After

## Chapter 19. The Corridor

Okabe is waiting at the top of the stairs with the form already in his hand.

That's the first thing that's different. Okabe doesn't wait for people. People come to Okabe's desk and stand in front of it, and Okabe finishes the line he's writing before he looks up.

The second thing is the jacket. A dark suit jacket, buttoned, a little tight across the middle. No cardigan. Kusakabe has known him for years and has never once seen him without a cardigan. He looks like his own photograph in a newspaper.

"Kusakabe-san."

"Okabe."

"Statements." He holds out the form. "Everyone who was inside the curtain. The Office wants them by five."

Kusakabe takes it. Two pages, stapled at the corner.

"Today," Okabe says.

"I know what by five means."

Okabe nods. His eyes go to Kusakabe's right sleeve, where the dressing on the forearm makes a small square shape under the cloth. Then to his left hand. The black strip from the cape is gone. It's white clinic tape now, neat and anonymous. Okabe doesn't say anything about either. He writes something on his clipboard. Kusakabe can't see what.

"Room six is free," Okabe says. "Second door. There's tea." A pause. "It isn't good."

"Fine."

"I'm told everyone's is late. This week." He says it to the clipboard. Then he goes down the stairs, one hand on the rail, carefully, like a man who hasn't slept in his own bed for several nights and has stopped expecting to.

*Five days,* Kusakabe thinks. *That's what it took to get Okabe out of a cardigan. Five days and a district.*

---

Room six has a table, four chairs, a whiteboard with the ghost of somebody's diagram on it, and a flask of tea on a tray with paper cups.

He pours one. It comes out the grey-brown of a puddle in a car park and tastes of the flask. He drinks it anyway. He sits with his back to the wall and the door in front of him, and puts the form on the table, and reads it.

*Account of Movements, 31 October.*

Name. Grade. Assigned position. Time of entry to the curtained area. Times at each subsequent position, with reasons for any change. Persons encountered. Injuries sustained. Injuries observed. Information received during deployment. And at the bottom, a large empty box with *Further Remarks* printed over it in a small, polite font, as if it doesn't want to put anybody under pressure.

He fills in his name. That part he's good at.

*Grade 1.*

*Standby, Shibuya (north). With Panda (second year).*

Time of entry. He thinks about that one. The tunnel, the train standing with its doors open. He writes a time that's roughly right and means nothing.

Then the positions. The tunnel. The service passage. The stairs up into the shopping building. The staircase on Meiji-dori. South. North again. The street with the hat shop. The rest he puts down in the order it happened, which isn't the same as the order he remembers it in.

*Reasons for any change.*

He writes *Ordered* three times, and it's true three times.

He writes *Withdrew* once. He looks at it for a while. It's true as well.

*Persons encountered.*

He writes *Panda (second year)* again. Then a curse user, tied up with a sash. Then Tanabe, or Tanaka. He writes *Tanabe (Grade 2)* and leaves it. If it's wrong, someone will put a line through it, and then at least one person in the building will know the man's name.

He notices, about halfway down the second page, that Panda's name is in every box. Every position. Every change. Every list. It runs down the form like the stitching down the side of a bag.

*Information received during deployment.*

*Radio. Fragments.*

He stops there with the pen on the paper.

There was a man with blood in his eyebrow, at the top of some stairs, who told him exactly where not to go. That isn't a fragment. That's a sentence. It had a floor number in it.

He doesn't write it down.

He lifts the pen and moves on to *Injuries sustained*, and doesn't look back up the page, which is the same as writing it down, only quieter.

*Forearm, cut. Left hand, cut. Left side, bruising.*

Then, because there's no box for it and he has to put it somewhere: *Frozen (ice), duration unknown. Released by special grade Yuki Tsukumo.*

He reads that back.

*Grade 1 sorcerer,* he thinks. *Account of Movements. Movements: none, for a while. I was a block of ice.*

*Further Remarks.*

He leaves it empty. The box looks relieved.

---

The tray on Okabe's desk is already full when he puts his form on top of it. Thirty of them, maybe more, squared off at the corners. Every one of them two pages. Every one of them someone's whole night, in the order they decided it happened.

His goes on the top. It doesn't look any different from the others. That's the idea.

The lift on the second floor has a handwritten sign taped to it. *Out of order. Please use the east stairs.* The handwriting is careful and very upset. He goes along the corridor towards the east stairs.

It's a long corridor. Grey carpet down the middle, polished wood either side of it, doors on the left with numbers and no names, windows on the right looking down on a courtyard where nobody ever goes. The radiators are on too high. The whole building smells of radiators being on too high, and of paper, and under that, faintly, of something medical, as though half the people in it have come straight from a clinic. They probably have.

At the far end, by the last window, two men are standing with their backs half to him.

One is old. Grey hair cut close, a dark wool coat folded over one arm, a face that's been pinched in at the mouth for so many years it's set that way. The other is younger, forty perhaps, with a cardboard file box held against his stomach like a tray of drinks.

They're talking quietly. Not quietly enough. The floor is the kind that carries.

Kusakabe doesn't slow down. He doesn't speed up either. He keeps the same pace, the one he's been walking at all his life, the one nobody remembers.

"Signed this morning," the old man is saying. "All of them. Nobody held back."

"Death, then."

"What else would it be."

"For Geto."

"For conspiring with Geto Suguru. For Shibuya. For half a city." The old man turns his coat over on his arm, as if it's the coat that's bothering him. "You were in the room on Friday. You heard."

"I heard what they said. I didn't hear what they had."

The old man doesn't answer that.

Kusakabe passes a door. Then another. The carpet takes his footsteps and gives nothing back.

"They have what he can do," the old man says, after a moment. "That's enough. Not the dolls. The other kind. Cursed corpses that don't need him. With their own souls in. That get up and walk about and make up their own minds. Nobody else alive knows how, and he's never once told anyone. Not in twenty years of being asked nicely."

"So it's the method they want."

"It's the method they're frightened of. An army that doesn't need paying and doesn't need feeding and doesn't need him. You'd be frightened too."

*With their own souls in.*

Kusakabe's feet keep going. Left, right. The same pace. He's quite proud of them, in a distant way, the way you'd be proud of a dog that sits when the doorbell goes.

Twenty minutes away by train, on a ground floor, by a window, there's one of those. Sitting on a lap.

He keeps walking.

"And he's run."

"Before the order got to him. Walked out of his own office."

"So how do they get him?"

"The panda." The old man says it like a word for an object. "They've got it downstairs. In the old rooms, sealed. He made it. He's been sewing its ears back on for years. He'll come for it."

"And if he doesn't?"

The old man shrugs. The coat moves with him.

"Then it's evidence," he says. "One of his. The only one they've got their hands on. They'll open it up and see how it's done."

They notice Kusakabe then. He's three metres away. Two.

The younger one shifts his box. The old one gives him the look the old ones always give him, the one that starts at his face, finds nothing on the list, and goes back to the window.

Kusakabe nods.

"Afternoon."

They nod back. Neither of them knows who he is. A Grade 1 with no technique, a coat, a sword bag, a bandaged hand. One of thirty forms in a tray. It's taken years to be that unremarkable. It's the one thing he's built that's never once let him down.

He goes past them and through the door to the east stairs, and lets it close behind him on its slow arm.

He goes down the stairs at the same pace he came along the corridor.

He doesn't stop on the landing. He doesn't stop at the bottom. He doesn't stop at the door, or on the front steps, or at the gate. A guard he's never seen before checks the photograph on his pass against his face and waves him through.

He doesn't stop until he's two streets away, at a red light, with nothing to do but wait for it.

Then he stands there and finds his jaw aches from holding it shut.

*Not my business,* he thinks.

The light changes. He crosses.

---

The trains are running, mostly.

The line through Shibuya isn't. A sheet of paper taped to the ticket machines explains why without explaining anything. *Service suspended between the following stations until further notice. We apologise for the inconvenience.* Somebody has drawn a small sad face in biro in the corner. Somebody else has crossed it out.

He takes the long way round, two changes, and gets off a stop early and walks.

The city has changed its walk. That's the only way he can put it. People still go to work. They still carry bags and look at their phones. But they go faster, and closer to the walls, and they look up more. A woman at a crossing keeps glancing at the sky over the rooftops as if it might be about to go dark at four in the afternoon. It's the second time Kusakabe has seen someone do that today.

A small boy in a yellow school cap waits at the same crossing, holding a fold of his mother's sleeve. When the green man comes on he doesn't move until she does. Nobody tells him to. He's just learned it, this week, the way the whole city has.

An electronics shop has a wall of televisions in its window, all on the same channel. Footage from a helicopter. Grey. A great untidy field of grey with smoke going up from it in thin lines, and the ticker running along the bottom too fast to read. From up there it doesn't look like anything. It looks like a quarry.

He doesn't stop to watch. He knows what it looks like from underneath.

A convenience store has a sign on the door. *Water: two per customer.* Inside, the shelf where the bottled water goes is bare metal. The boy at the till is reading the back of a cup of noodles as though it might have the news on it.

There are photographs on the lamp posts. He sees the first one and then he can't stop seeing them. A man in a suit at a wedding. A girl with a cat on her shoulder. *Have you seen.* A phone number written very large. He keeps his eyes on the pavement after the third one, and counts the lamp posts instead, and loses count.

*Not my business,* he thinks. *Not my fight.*

He says it again at the next corner, in case it didn't take the first time.

---

His flat is exactly where he left it.

Same door. Same lock that sticks if you turn the key too fast. Same smell of a room nobody's been in all day. The jar of instant coffee by the kettle. The sofa.

He puts the sword bag down against the arm of the sofa and sits.

He doesn't take his coat off. He'll do that in a minute.

The clock on the cooker says five to five.

*Right,* he thinks. *Let's go through it.*

He goes through it.

Yaga is a grown man. Older than Kusakabe, and harder, and better at nearly everything. He's known for twenty years, at least, that what he does is the sort of thing they kill people for. He knew it in Kusakabe's kitchen. He knew it at the window, at that flat on the ground floor, when he was very clear that it was not the boy back. He did it anyway. That was his choice. He made it with his eyes open. He's not a man who needs rescuing. He's a man who walked out of his own office before the order got there. Wherever he is now, he'll have thought about this longer and better than Kusakabe can on a sofa.

Not his business.

Panda can lift a building. Kusakabe's seen him do it. Panda is strong and quick and patient, and he's lived in that school his whole life, and people like him. Somebody will speak up for him. Gojo would, if Gojo were anywhere. Somebody else will.

Not his business.

And the other thing. The thing under everything else.

There's a reprimand on his record with seven months left to run. A complaint from May. A letter on cream paper from the Zen'in. A pencil note in a margin that says *Froze?* That's what's on the file. That's a file that gets read carefully. A man with that file doesn't get a reprimand for this, or a meeting in September, or a deferred allowance. He gets the other thing. The thing the old man in the corridor said in two words.

And then who pays Ono. Who rings the bell and then uses his own key. Who makes the tea wrong.

And under that, smaller and colder: the method. They want the method. They're frightened of anything that has its own soul in it and wasn't made by them. If anyone, ever, starts asking questions about what Yaga made, and who for, and where it is now, then the safest thing a man can be is somebody nobody's ever looked at twice.

He's spent his whole life becoming that. He's very good at it. He just proved it in a corridor.

*Not my business. Not my fight.*

He sits back.

It's a good argument. He's checked it from every side. There's nothing wrong with it.

He sits there with it.

---

The light goes.

It goes slowly, the way it does in November, without making any announcement about it. The window turns from white to grey to the dark blue of a school uniform. He doesn't put the lamp on. Across the road, a light comes on in somebody's kitchen, and a woman moves back and forth in it, cooking something, with her back to the street.

Upstairs, somebody drags a chair across the floor. Stops. Drags it back.

His coat is still on. The sword bag is still against the arm of the sofa. His hands are in his lap. He looks at them in the last of the light and they look like hands.

The clock on the cooker says five forty.

Then six.

He doesn't think about anything in particular. He's very good at that too. The argument is finished. It's been put away. It just stays out on the table, in the dark, where he can see it.

And somewhere in that hour, without asking, his back remembers the cold.

---

Not a thought. A feeling, low down, between the shoulder blades. Grit. The flat cold of stone under him. Dust in the back of his throat, the chalk taste of it.

Then the dark. The dark with nothing in it.

Then the grinding.

He doesn't try to stop it. He's sitting on his own sofa with his coat on, and he's also lying on his back under a building, and both of those are happening at once and he lets them.

The line of light. The slab tilting. The grey sky.

And Panda's face, above him.

Upside down, from where he was lying. Dust caught in the fur round the eyes, so thick it had gone into little grey lines, like the creases in an old man's face. The torn ear. The mouth set flat with the weight of what his arms were holding. And the eyes, looking down. Not angry. Not anything, really. Just looking, to see if there was still somebody there.

*Sensei. You alive?*

He's gone over it since. He's had five days. And there's one thing about it he keeps coming back to, the way your tongue keeps coming back to a chipped tooth.

Panda didn't ask him anything.

Not one question. Not where he'd been when it came down. Not why he hadn't shouted. Not why the man who was supposed to be in charge of him had been lying in a hole in the dark with his sword drawn. Waiting for someone else to deal with it. Not what had really been on that radio at ten to nine.

He just lifted the rock. And then he held out his paw. And then he walked in front, in case.

*They'll open it up and see how it's done.*

Kusakabe's hands close in his lap.

---

He thinks about Takeru.

He doesn't mean to. It just comes in behind the rest, the way it always does, as if it was waiting at the door for its turn.

The boy on her lap. A Sunday afternoon, the window bright behind them. His feet hanging over her knees, nowhere near the floor, knocking against her shin now and then when he forgot himself, and her not minding. Her not minding. Her face, which most days doesn't know what day it is, knowing exactly what that was.

Yaga's work. The other kind. Exactly the thing the old man in the corridor is frightened of. With its own soul in.

Yaga never asked for anything for it. Never once. Not money. Not a favour. Not a word in a corridor. He just did it, and went back to his office, and sewed ears on bears, and gave Kusakabe a homeroom as though it were a thing anyone might be given.

And now his work is in a room downstairs, sealed, with a sign on the door. Waiting for someone senior to come down with a list and some instruments.

The debt is where it always is.

For years it's been something he carries. A weight. A thing to check on trains, and in the dark, to make sure it's still there. He's never once thought of it as something he could hand over. There was never anywhere to hand it.

He sits there in the dark with his coat on and feels the shape of it.

It's exactly the shape of a sealed door.

---

*Damn it,* he thinks.

He says it out loud, quietly, to the room.

"Damn it."

The room takes it without comment.

He sits for one more minute, because he's earned that much. He looks at the argument on the table. It's still a good argument. Every word of it is true. It just doesn't seem to be the part of him that's deciding.

Then he stands up.

His knees take a moment. His left side takes longer. He's still got his coat on. He's been wearing it for an hour and a half on his own sofa, which probably says something about him, and he doesn't stop to ask what.

He picks up the sword bag. He puts the strap over his shoulder.

At the door, he stops, and turns, and leaves the lamp on. He doesn't know why. So the room looks like somebody lives in it, maybe. So there's something to come back to, if he comes back.

He locks the door behind him. The key sticks. He takes it more slowly, and it turns.

He goes out.

---

In the morning, Panda is free.

Kusakabe learns it at twenty to seven, standing at the window in a T-shirt, from his phone, which buzzes once on the windowsill beside the cup.

It's Ogawa. Ogawa doesn't use punctuation and doesn't go home.

*bear's out. last night sometime. nobody knows who. upstairs going mad*

He reads it twice. He puts the phone back on the windowsill, face up.

The kettle clicks off behind him.

He makes the coffee the way he always makes it, which is badly. One spoon, a bit more than one. Water just off the boil, because he never waits for it to settle. A stir that's mostly for something to do. It comes out the colour it always comes out, and smells the way it always smells, and tastes, when he tries it, exactly as bad as it did last Tuesday. He's grateful for that in a way he couldn't explain to anyone. Some things you want to stay exactly where they are.

He stands at the window with it.

The street is quieter than it was before Shibuya. Fewer people. The ones there are walk faster. A woman in a padded coat puts a bag of recycling out by the post, looks up at the sky the way everybody does now, and goes back in. A delivery van stops, thinks about it, and goes on without delivering anything. Two crows are working on a split bin bag on the corner with the patience of men who are paid by the hour.

The phone buzzes again. Not a message this time. A call.

*Okabe.*

He lets it ring twice, which is fair, and picks up.

"Kusakabe-san." Okabe's voice sounds the way the jacket looked. "I'm sorry to ring so early. I'm working through a list."

"Okay."

"Can you tell me where you were last night? From about six."

"Home."

A pause. He can hear a pen.

"Is there anyone who can confirm that?"

"No."

The pen again. One word, he thinks. Short.

"Thank you, Kusakabe-san," Okabe says. Then, after a moment, in a different voice: "It's a long list."

Kusakabe doesn't know if that's meant to be a comfort. He doesn't think Okabe knows either.

"Okay," he says.

Okabe rings off first. That's never happened before. It's a morning for things that haven't happened before.

He puts the phone down on the sill again and drinks some more of the coffee.

---

There it is, then. He looks at it the way he'd look at the street.

If anyone ever finds out, that's the end of him. Not a margin note. Not a letter on cream paper, or a meeting in a room with a number on the door. The end. The kind they write down once, with a date beside it, and carry out quietly, by somebody senior, and never mention again. He knows exactly what it is. He heard it in a corridor yesterday, in two words, from a man holding a coat.

He looks at that for about as long as it takes to drink half a cup of very bad coffee.

Then he drinks the other half.

It doesn't feel like much of anything. No drums. No speech. It feels like Monday morning. Like paying the bills at the convenience store at seven, before anyone's in, the way he always does. Card in. Card out. The receipt in the bin by the door. Done. Nothing to show for it, and nothing to show anyone.

Panda is out. Somewhere. Wherever Panda would go. That's Panda's business now.

Nobody knows who.

Nobody's going to.

Somewhere at headquarters, in a tray on a desk, there's a form with his name at the top and Panda's name in every box. Under *Further Remarks*, nothing at all. It's the most accurate thing he's ever filed.

On the corner, one of the crows gets the bag open at last and pulls out something long and white, and the other one immediately wants it.

---

He finishes the coffee standing up.

There's a ring of it dried in the bottom of the cup, the way there always is. He takes it to the sink and runs the tap until the water comes warm. He rinses the cup, and rubs the ring out with his thumb, and the water runs down over his wrist and over the white tape on his left hand and he lets it.

He turns off the tap.

He shakes the cup once, and looks at his hands.

Yesterday, in the dark, on the sofa, they looked like hands. A few days ago, at dawn, in what was left of Shibuya, with his teeth going, they looked like somebody else's. Somebody braver. He didn't recognise them at all.

He recognises them now.

He puts the cup upside down on the rack.

His hands are steady.

---

## Chapter 20. Gakuganji

It's Ogawa who tells him. It's always Ogawa.

A Tuesday in November, a little after two. The staff room has been half empty since Shibuya. Desks with nobody at them. A jacket on the back of a chair that nobody has come back for, and that nobody moves.

Kusakabe is at his desk in the corner, by the radiator that ticks and doesn't work. He's filling in a form about stretchers. Somebody at headquarters wants to know how many were used on the night of the thirty-first, and by whom, and whether they were returned. He has written *Some* in the first box. He's looking at the second.

The door opens. Ogawa comes in, shuts it behind him, and stands by the kettle without filling it.

The toothpick goes from one side of his mouth to the other. Then back.

*Twice,* Kusakabe thinks. *He's got something.*

There are two other people in the room. A woman from Records, asleep with her chin on her hand. A young Grade 3 with headphones in, eating crisps one at a time, as if he's been told they're the last crisps. Ogawa looks at both of them. He looks at the door. Then he comes over and sits on the edge of the next desk, which isn't his.

"You heard," he says.

"Heard what."

"This morning. Early." Ogawa takes the toothpick out and turns it in his fingers. "It's done."

Kusakabe doesn't ask what. He doesn't need to. There's been only one thing in this building since the arrest order went out and found an empty office. People make tea round it. They nod round it in corridors, and nobody says it, and it gets bigger every time nobody says it.

He puts the pen down.

"Who," he says.

"Kyoto's old man. Gakuganji." Ogawa puts the toothpick back. "He found him. Out in the hills somewhere, in the trees. They wanted someone with a title. Someone old enough that nobody could say it was personal."

"Was it?"

Ogawa thinks about that, which is unusual for Ogawa.

"I don't know," he says. "I wasn't there. Nobody was there that I'd ask." He looks at the door again. "It's done, anyway. That's all I've got."

He goes back to the kettle and fills it this time, and stands with his back to the room while it boils.

Kusakabe looks down. The second box on the form says *Returned? Yes or no.*

He sits there for a while. Then he writes *No*, because most of them weren't, and puts the form in his tray, and goes out.

---

Okabe catches him in the corridor.

Okabe is coming the other way with a stack of paper against his chest, one sheet per person. He's back in a cardigan, after a week of suit jackets. A black one. Kusakabe has seen grey, brown, green, and the one the colour of weak tea. Never black. He has a feeling it was bought for this. Possibly yesterday.

Okabe stops and holds out the top sheet.

Three lines, typed, centred, on headquarters paper.

*The sentence passed on Yaga Masamichi, formerly Principal of Tokyo Jujutsu High, was carried out this morning. Staff are reminded that matters relating to the case are not to be discussed. Further guidance will follow.*

*Formerly,* he thinks. *They had that ready.*

He reads it twice. It doesn't get longer.

Okabe hasn't moved on. He's holding the pile very square against his cardigan, looking at a point on the wall just past Kusakabe's ear.

"He always signed in the right box," Okabe says.

Kusakabe looks at him.

"Leave forms. Requisitions. Fourteen years. He never once signed in the box for the countersignatory." Okabe's thumb moves along the edge of the paper, squaring it again. It's already square. "You'd be surprised. Most people don't read the boxes."

"No," Kusakabe says. "I wouldn't."

Something happens at the corner of Okabe's mouth. It isn't a smile. It's more like a man pressing on a bruise to see if it's still there.

Then Okabe nods, once, as if they've agreed something in a meeting, and goes on down the corridor with his pile. Kusakabe hears him knock on the next door, softly, though it's open.

---

He doesn't go back to his desk.

He walks. Down the stairs, out the side door, along the path behind the old building, to where his feet always end up when nobody's told them otherwise.

The training yard.

It's empty. It's been empty since Shibuya. Maki is in a hospital bed with her burns dressed. Inumaki is in another one, somewhere else, learning to do things with one hand. The packed earth has a thin skin of leaves on it that nobody's raked. The storehouse doors are shut.

He sits down on the third step from the bottom. Gate in front. Wall behind.

*He couldn't stop it.*

He thinks that first, and then he checks it, because he doesn't trust a thought that comes that quickly and makes him feel that much better.

So he goes through it properly. Room by room.

He could have gone to headquarters. Sat in the empty chair in Conference Room B with his hands where everyone could see them and said *don't*. They'd have written it down. Then they'd have done it anyway, and added his name to a list.

He could have gone looking for the old man. Gakuganji. Stood in his way. A Grade 1 without a technique, standing in front of the principal of Kyoto and the whole of the higher-ups behind him, with a sword and a small circle. He tries to picture it and it doesn't even get as far as a picture. It's just a man in a corridor, and then it isn't.

Gojo could have stopped it. Walked into the room where they decided it and said *no*, in that bored voice. But Gojo is sealed in something nobody can open. That's the whole reason the room got to decide.

He runs down the list of people who could have said no and had it count, and the list is one name long, and the name is in a box.

*Nobody could,* he thinks.

He waits to see if it's an excuse.

It isn't. He's built enough excuses in his life to know the shape of one. An excuse has a door in it somewhere, a *technically*. This hasn't. This is just a wall, and he's walked the length of it, and there's no gap.

It doesn't make him feel better. That's how he knows it's true.

---

He thinks about Gakuganji.

He's seen him once. At headquarters, a year or two ago, at the far end of a corridor. A very old man in formal dress, white hair, the slow upright walk of someone who has never been in a hurry, because things wait for him. Two younger men behind him. And on his back, in a black case, an electric guitar.

Nobody mentioned it. It was the most interesting thing Kusakabe had seen at headquarters in years, and he never asked.

He knows the type, though. Not the guitar. The rest. The ones who believe in the rules the way an old wall believes in standing up. They don't need to like them. The rules are holding up the roof. Somebody tells them a thing has to be done in a certain way, by a certain kind of person, and they put on their good clothes and come up on the train and do it.

The same man told his students to kill a boy, in the summer. Ogawa said so. Kusakabe remembers being not much bothered by that, at the time.

He'd like to hate him. It would be simpler.

He tries it, sitting on the step. It won't take. Like a damp match. Hate needs feeding, and he hasn't got anything to feed it with: one corridor, a guitar case, a white head going round a corner. You can't keep a fire going on that.

And under that, the thing he doesn't want to look at. That an old man who does what the rules say, because the rules say it, isn't so very different from a man who signs under someone else's version because it's quicker. Just older. Just further along.

*Fine,* he thinks. *Don't hate him, then. You don't have to do anything about him at all. That's always been your favourite.*

---

His hand goes to his pocket.

The coin's there.

He's been half expecting it not to be. As if the debt might go with the man. As if a debt were the kind of thing you could bury.

It's there. But it's lighter.

He can't point to how he knows. It's the way a bag is lighter after you've taken one thing out of it. Still the same bag. Still heavy. Still on your shoulder. But you know.

He knows what came out.

He doesn't think about a night, or a door, or what he did with his hands. He's not going to, not here, not ever, because a thing you don't think about can't come out of your mouth by accident. He only thinks: there's a panda somewhere who is sitting in the open air, and not in a sealed room. That's all.

That's what came out of the bag.

It isn't nothing. It isn't the whole thing, either. Not close. A boy on his mother's lap, years ago, in a chair by a window. Against that, one night's work and one risk, which might still come back for him. He can do the sum this time. It doesn't come out even.

*We're closer to even, old man,* he thinks.

Not even. Closer.

He'd never have said it to his face. Yaga would have hated it. He'd have looked at Kusakabe over the top of those glasses, the way he looked at a doll with a crooked seam, and gone back to his sewing. He'd have said nothing, and the nothing would have been an opinion.

Kusakabe finds he's almost smiling. Not quite.

He didn't like him much. That was true, and it's still true. Yaga was stubborn and proud and difficult to talk to, and he made things in meetings, and he never asked for anything back. Not once. Not one look in a corridor. Not even at the end, when asking would have made sense.

You don't have to like a man to respect that. You just have to be tired enough to see it.

He's tired enough.

---

Panda comes at four.

Kusakabe feels him before he sees him, something large crossing the edge of the circle. He doesn't turn round. He knows the weight of the walk.

Panda doesn't stop by him. He goes on down past the third step, past the second, and sits on the bottom one, a little to the side. Where Inumaki sits. He sits the way he never sits, all the way down, with his knees up and his arms round them.

They look at the yard.

A crow lands on the storehouse roof, looks at them both, and decides against it.

"Ogawa?" Panda says, after a while.

"Yeah."

"Me too." Panda's voice is quite normal. That's the worst thing about it. "He found me. He said he thought I'd want to hear it from a person."

A pause.

"I'm not really meant to be anywhere people can see me. So I came here." He looks at the empty yard. "Nobody looks here."

Kusakabe doesn't say anything. He doesn't have anything that would sit right next to that.

The light goes down a little. The cold comes up out of the step.

"He was going to teach me to sew," Panda says. "Properly. Not just stuffing. The faces. He said when I was older."

A pause.

"I don't get older. That's sort of the thing about me." He tilts his head. "I think he knew that. I think it was a joke. He didn't make many."

*He never made one I laughed at,* Kusakabe thinks.

*That one, though.*

He doesn't say that either.

He doesn't put a hand on Panda's shoulder. He doesn't say *I'm sorry*, or *he was a good man*, or *at least*. Panda had a father. Kusakabe had a debt. One of those is a great deal bigger than the other, and it isn't his, and there's nothing he can put into it from three steps up that would make it any smaller.

So he sits.

That's the one thing he's always been good at. Not doing anything, very steadily, for a long time. For once it's the right tool.

The lights come on along the path. The crow leaves. Somewhere up at the main building a door bangs, and then another, people going home.

Past five, he realises he'd normally be gone by now, and that he isn't going until Panda does. He doesn't decide it. It's just what's happening.

At ten to six Panda gets up. He brushes leaves off the back of himself, carefully, though there aren't any.

"Night, sensei."

"Night."

Panda goes up the steps, past him, close enough that his fur nearly brushes Kusakabe's shoulder. He doesn't look down. Kusakabe doesn't look up.

He waits until the steps are quiet. Then a bit longer. Then he goes home.

---

The rest of the week goes on as if it hasn't noticed.

It hasn't, mostly. People who've been told not to discuss something don't. They discuss stretchers, and who's covering what, and whether the second-floor kettle is broken or just slow. The thing sits in the middle of every conversation like a table nobody's allowed to put anything on.

Nobody asks Kusakabe anything.

He keeps expecting them to. Every time a door opens behind him, some part of him waits for his name. *Kusakabe-san, a moment.* It doesn't come on Wednesday. It doesn't come on Thursday. He doesn't take that to mean it won't.

*It's not over because nobody's said anything,* he thinks. *It's not over until the file's closed. And nobody ever tells you when the file's closed.*

He does his jobs. A Grade 3 in a multistorey in Nerima, small and stupid and fast, two cuts. A residual check on a shrine.

On Thursday he goes to the staff pigeonholes for his post, and there's something in the one next to his.

The pigeonholes run in names, in order. His is near the bottom. The one next to it, for reasons of the alphabet, says YAGA on a strip of tape that has gone yellow at the ends.

There's a catalogue in it. Thin, glossy, a mail-order company. On the front, stacks of felt in squares of colour, every colour, like a paintbox. Underneath, smaller: *Wool felt, stuffing, safety eyes. Trade prices.* It's addressed to Yaga by name, on a printed label, with the word *Principal* after it.

Just a company, somewhere, sending a catalogue to a good customer, the way it always does.

He doesn't take it out. It isn't his. He doesn't think it's anybody's, now.

On Friday it's gone. So is the tape. There's a clean pale strip on the wood where it was, and the pigeonhole is empty, and the one below has already started to use a bit of it for overflow.

He takes his own post and goes.

---

Sunday.

Same train. Same six stops. A carrier bag between his feet. Soft rice crackers. A new lavender stick, because it's been two months. A tin of the thickening powder, because it was on the list Ono texted him on Friday: *Powder. One tin. Not the big one, she doesn't like the big one.* He doesn't know how anyone can tell. He bought the small one.

He rings the bell. Then the key.

The lavender reaches him first. Faint now, the old stick nearly done. He takes it out of the little bottle on the shoe cabinet and puts the new one in, before he's even got his shoes off. First thing in.

"You're early," Ono says, from the kitchen.

"Train."

She doesn't ask. She's at the counter in her black puffer jacket, which she's never once taken off in this flat, writing the last line in the rabbit notebook. She turns it round on the counter so it's facing him, and puts the pen down beside it, square.

"She's had a good week," she says. "Mostly."

He reads it standing up.

Ono's handwriting is small and upright and wastes nothing. No full stops if she can help it. *Tue: ate all. Wed: physio Thurs moved, physio sick. Thu: confused PM, thought it was the hospital. Fine by dinner. Fri: good.* And then, under Thursday, squeezed in afterwards in the margin:

*Asked twice about "the principal." Big man, glasses. Said I don't know him. (True.)*

He reads that one a second time.

Ono is putting her bag on her shoulder. She's also watching him.

"She means a man who visited," he says. "Years ago. Before your time."

"Okay."

That's all. She doesn't want the rest. He's grateful in a way that sits in his chest like something warm swallowed too fast.

"The TV was on," Ono says, at the door. "Night man had it on. News." She zips the puffer jacket up another centimetre. "I turned it off. She didn't see much."

"Thanks."

"Mm." She opens the door. Ono says goodbye to his sister once, in the room, properly, and never shouts it down the hall. "Tuesday," she says to him.

"Tuesday."

The door shuts.

He stands in the hallway a moment in the new lavender. Then he goes through.

---

She's in the window chair.

The light is November light, low and clean and a bit thin, as if it's been rationed. It lies across her blanket and her two hands, the one that works and the one that doesn't, and the side of her face. Outside, the car park. A man washing a white car in a sweater, very thoroughly, as if somebody important is going to inspect it.

"Hey," he says.

She turns her head. Her eyes go over him slowly, deciding.

"Oh," she says. "It's you."

He waits to find out who *you* is.

"Atsuya. You've got leaves on your coat."

He has. A wet yellow one, stuck flat to his sleeve. He peels it off, doesn't know what to do with it, and puts it in his pocket.

"Ginkgo," she says. "That's a ginkgo. It'll smell."

"It's fine."

"It'll smell in your pocket."

"I'll take it out later."

She looks satisfied. She looks back at the man and his car.

He sits down on the stool beside her. The TV in the corner is off. He gets up and turns it a little more towards the wall anyway, and sits down again.

*It's off,* he thinks. *It can't show anything. Fine.*

---

He makes the tea.

The cheap green tea, in a bag. The water in. The bag dipped. One. Two. Three. Out.

The colour of almost nothing.

Then the powder, one level scoop, stirred in until the tea goes slow and thick. It took him weeks to stop the lumps. Now his hand does it while he watches the window.

He makes his own with the same bag. No powder. He drinks some standing at the counter. It tastes, as it always tastes, of hot water that once heard about tea.

He carries hers through and holds the cup for her. She drinks. Slow sips. Some of it goes down her chin and he wipes it, and neither of them mentions it, which is the arrangement.

She doesn't say it's too strong.

She doesn't say anything at all about it. She drinks half the cup, and then turns her head away, which means enough, and goes back to the window.

He takes that as a review. It's the best one he's had.

---

The afternoon goes.

He does the small things. Sunday's pills, crushed now and folded into a spoon of something soft, which is the doctor's idea, and which he hates a bit, and does anyway. He checks the toilet handle, which sticks again. He fixes it with the handle of a teaspoon, which is the right tool, it turns out, and always was. He rubs hand cream into her fingers, the unscented one, and she lets him.

She talks to the room. The dog upstairs. Something about a bus. The sea, again, the red roof and the watermelon, in the same order, and he listens to it in the same order, the way you'd walk a path you know.

At about three, she stops talking for a while. He thinks she might be asleep. Then she says, not turning her head:

"How's the nice principal?"

He's holding the hand-cream tube. He keeps holding it.

"The big one," she says. "With the glasses. He came here. Didn't he come here?"

"Yeah," he says. "A long time ago."

"He took his glasses off to talk to me." She frowns a little, getting it right. "Indoors he kept them on. Then he sat down, there, and he took them off. Nobody does that. People talk to the chair." She lifts her right hand an inch off the blanket, at the room, at everyone who's ever come in it and talked to the chair. "He talked to me."

Kusakabe looks at the spot she means. The floor by the low table. There's nothing there. A bit of carpet, worn flatter than the rest, from the stool and the years.

He could tell her.

One sentence. He's very good at one sentence. And she'd hear it, today, with her eyes this clear. Then she'd forget it in an hour, and ask again next week, and he'd have to decide again. Every week a new first time, for her. For him the same one, over and over, worn a bit flatter each time, like the carpet.

Or she wouldn't forget. That would be worse.

*No,* he thinks.

Not because it'd be hard for him. It would be. That's not why.

Because this room is about the size she can manage. A chair. A window. A car park. Some futons, when it's dry. A man on Sundays who makes the tea too weak. That's her whole world now, and it holds. It's been holding for years. And there's no news from outside it that would make it bigger. Only heavier.

He's let in enough. He knows exactly what's outside these walls this month. He's not bringing any of it through the door.

"Is he well?" she says.

"Haven't seen him for a bit," Kusakabe says.

It's true. It's the kind of true that he's been using all his life, the kind that has a fact in it and nothing else. *Traffic.* *Battery.* He knows how it works. He hates a little that it works so well, here, on her.

She nods.

"Busy man," she says.

"Yeah."

"Tell him I said hello. When you see him."

The hand cream is warm in his fist now. He puts the cap back on it. He puts it on the little table, very neatly, next to the cup.

"Okay," he says.

And that's another one. Another thing in the pocket. Small, this one. He's carrying worse.

She seems pleased. She turns back to the window. The man with the car has finished, and is standing back from it, looking at it, the way you'd look at something you made.

---

At four she asks for the radio. He finds old songs, no talking, and keeps his thumb near the dial. The news comes on at the hour. He turns it off before the second sentence, and on again at five past.

"There was a fire," she says, a little later. Out of nowhere. "The lady said. The night lady. On the television. A big one."

The night carer is a man. He doesn't correct her.

"Somewhere else," he says. "A long way off."

"Was anyone hurt?"

"People are seeing to it."

*Not much of an answer,* he thinks. *It's the one there is.*

She accepts it. He's her brother, and he's sitting on the stool, and the tea was right, and the lavender's new, and there is nothing in this room that is on fire.

That's the size of it. He can hold that size. Arm's reach, more or less. The chair, the window, the cup. Anything bigger, he can't promise. But this he can keep. He's been keeping it for years. He'll keep it a bit longer.

---

The light goes.

It goes the November way, early and all at once, the sky over the car park turning the grey of a pigeon's back and then darker. Windows come on across the way. Somebody on a balcony across the way takes their washing in, in a hurry, as if they've only just remembered it.

He doesn't turn the lamp on yet. She likes the dusk. She's always liked it.

He sits on the stool with his hands between his knees. In his coat pocket, on the hook by the door, there's a ginkgo leaf going soft, and a coin that weighs a little less than it did. He can feel both from here, which isn't possible, and he feels them anyway.

His sister starts to hum.

Quietly. Not to him. To the window, the way she talks to the room. A tune that goes up and comes down again and goes up a little further the second time, like someone climbing stairs and stopping on each landing to see if anyone's following.

He doesn't know it.

He listens for it to turn into something. A song from the radio. Something from the guest house with the red roof. It doesn't turn into anything. It just goes on being itself. Up, and down, and up a bit further.

He could ask her what it is. She might even tell him.

He doesn't ask.

Outside, the last of the grey goes out of the sky. The room gets darker round them, small and warm, the right size.

She hums it through once, and then again from the beginning.

He doesn't know the song.

He listens anyway.

---

## Chapter 21. Execute the Boy

Sakaguchi has the same recorder.

Small, black, the size of a packet of gum. He puts it on the table between them and turns it so the little red light faces Kusakabe. The way he did in May, outside a clinic, with a boy coughing into his collar in the next room.

Same rimless glasses. Same navy suit. No tie.

"Kusakabe-san. Thank you for coming in."

"Fine."

It's a Thursday. Mid-November. A room at headquarters on the third floor, with a number on the door and no name. A window that looks straight at the wall of the next building, near enough to touch if it opened.

"This isn't about your conduct," Sakaguchi says. "I want to say that first. I know the last time we spoke it was."

"Okay."

"The Office is taking accounts from everyone who saw the vessel on the night of the thirty-first. While it was under the King of Curses' control." He opens the leather folder. "You're down as having been on Meiji-dori. With Panda."

He says *Panda* carefully, the way you'd step round something on a floor. Panda is not, this month, a name anyone at headquarters says comfortably.

"Yeah."

"Can you tell me what you saw."

Kusakabe tells him.

It doesn't take long. A street. A burning bin. Something dropping out of the dark. Four eyes. Two orders. Then nothing in the road.

"Did it harm anyone in your presence?"

"No."

"Did it speak to you directly?"

"It spoke to the street."

Sakaguchi writes that down. His handwriting still doesn't slope.

"And you didn't engage."

"No."

"Why not?"

Kusakabe looks at him.

Sakaguchi looks back, quite mildly, and waits. He isn't trying to catch anything. He just has a box on the form and the box wants a word in it.

"It told us not to move," Kusakabe says.

Sakaguchi writes that down as well.

---

Then he turns the page.

"There's a second part," he says. "The Office is recording the views of Grade 1 staff who were witnesses. On the sentence."

Kusakabe already knows. It went round the school on Monday in four versions, and by Tuesday they'd agreed with each other.

The boy was sentenced to death in June. Gojo stood in front of it and said *later*, and *later* held, because Gojo was the one saying it. Now Gojo is sealed in something nobody can open, and *later* has turned out to belong to him, and not to the boy. The sentence is back. It never really went anywhere. It was just waiting in a drawer for the man holding it shut to leave the room.

"It's been reinstated," Sakaguchi says. "You'll have heard."

"Yeah."

"Some staff have asked for it to be deferred. On the grounds that the vessel wasn't in control at the time." He says this in the same voice as everything else. "Others haven't. I'm asking each witness whether they support the sentence being carried out. And, if so, on what timescale."

He waits.

Kusakabe looks at the window, at the wall of the next building. A pigeon is sitting on the one bit of ledge the spikes don't cover, looking very pleased with itself.

He thinks about Shibuya.

Not the whole night. One thing. The edge he saw from the top of Meiji-dori at one in the morning, where the ruin stopped. Round. Deliberate. All those streets, inside that line. All those people who'd come out for one night in a costume.

That came out of a boy. Through his hands. While he was somewhere else.

And it could come out again. Any time. The thing that stood in Meiji-dori and looked at all of them as if they were weather is still in there, waiting for a door to be left open. It doesn't care about any of them. It barely noticed they were there.

The next time, it might notice.

"Yes," Kusakabe says.

Sakaguchi writes. "You support it."

"Yes."

"Timescale?"

"Soon."

Sakaguchi's pen stops above the paper. "Can I put a little more than that?"

Kusakabe thinks about it. Not about the answer. About how many words it needs.

"Before it happens again," he says.

Sakaguchi writes that. Then he looks at it on the page for a moment, and Kusakabe can't tell what he thinks of it, because Sakaguchi gives everything the same depth of look. It's the one thing about him that's always been fair.

"Most of the people I've seen have said the same," Sakaguchi says. Not as comfort. As information. "Almost word for word."

"Then it'll be easy to file."

Sakaguchi's face doesn't change. It never does. That might be agreement. It might be the opposite.

"Yes," Sakaguchi says. "It will."

He reaches out and turns the recorder off. The red light goes. Kusakabe hadn't noticed how much he'd been looking at it.

---

He walks back to the station the long way, round two blocks he doesn't need, and goes through it on the way.

*Not cruelty,* he thinks. *I don't want anything to happen to a boy. I don't want anything to happen to anyone. That's the whole point.*

That's true.

*One life against the next Shibuya. That's not a hard sum. Anyone would do it.*

That's true as well. Almost everyone did.

It's a decent argument. It's got numbers in it. It's the kind of argument people sit in rooms and nod at.

He walks a bit further, and then, at a crossing, with nothing to do but wait, he does the thing he's always been bad at, which is look at it from underneath.

It's the same feeling.

That's what he finds under there. Not the argument. The feeling the argument is standing on. The cold sliding thing in his stomach. He had it at the top of a staircase on Meiji-dori, with a man with one eye swollen shut telling him a floor number. He had it in a corridor in June, at the first flash of pink hair, ducking into an empty classroom. He had it when he stood in a road and was very carefully furniture.

*Avoid B5F.* That's all it ever says. *Don't go near it. Make it so you never have to.*

Now it's put on a better coat. Something with a collar. It says *public safety*, and *timescale*, and *the next Shibuya*, and every word of it is true. But it's the same thing underneath, wearing it.

*Make it so you never have to be in a room with that again.*

The light changes. He crosses.

He doesn't change his mind. That's the thing. He looks at it from underneath, and sees exactly what it is, and it doesn't make any difference. He still wants it done. He'd still say *soon*. Seeing the fear doesn't make the fear wrong.

*It might be both,* he thinks. *Scared and right. People manage both all the time.*

He gets on the train. He doesn't sit down.

---

It's the woman from dispatch who tells him about Okkotsu.

She's writing on the board in her small square hand. Most of the board is empty now.

"They've brought Okkotsu back," she says, without turning round. "Did you hear?"

"No."

"Special grade. They flew him in. He's got it."

She doesn't say what. Nobody has to say what, this month.

Kusakabe stands by the board with his hands in his coat pockets.

Okkotsu. He remembers him the way you'd remember weather. A thin first-year in a white uniform, a year ago and more, going along corridors with his shoulders up round his ears, as if the corridor were something he'd borrowed. And something with him that you felt in your teeth. Kusakabe used to wait for him to pass.

Then the Night Parade, and his name at dawn. Then abroad. Then a photograph in the homeroom binder, stamped in red.

He'd be seventeen now.

*They've sent a boy,* Kusakabe thinks. *To do it to a boy.*

It sits wrong. He notices that it sits wrong. He also notices, right behind it, quick and small and very relieved, the other thing.

*Not me, though.*

They didn't ask him. Of course they didn't. A Grade 1 with no technique, a reprimand, a pencil note in a margin. He's the last name anybody would put on that list, and he's never been so glad of his file.

*Better him than me.* He thinks it, and then he hears himself think it. He doesn't take it back. There's no one to take it back to.

"When?" he says.

The woman from dispatch caps her pen. "He has to find him first." She looks at the board, at the gaps in it. "The boy didn't come back. After. Someone says he's still out in it. In Shibuya. On his own."

*On his own.*

"Hm," Kusakabe says.

He goes back to his desk. He sits down and tries, for no reason he can name, to picture the boy's face.

He can't.

He's had a whole year. He's been in the same buildings, on the same paths, under the same roof. And what he has is this: pink hair across a courtyard. A hood. The back of a head, in the grey at dawn, bent forward over its own knees. And a face in Meiji-dori, with black marks across it and two extra eyes, that wasn't the boy's at all.

The only time he's seen it up close, somebody else was using it.

He doesn't know what colour the boy's eyes are when they're his. He doesn't know what his voice sounds like up close. He doesn't know if he's frightened now.

*You don't need to know the boy,* he thinks. *You only need to know what's in him.*

That's what he told himself all summer. It held up all summer.

It holds up now, too. It just takes a bit more leaning on.

---

On Monday the ginkgo in the front courtyard has let go of everything.

It does it once a year, in a single night. You come in and the tree is bare and the ground is gold.

He crosses the courtyard through it. It's the courtyard where the girl threw a leaf, three weeks ago, and somebody laughed, and he turned round.

Maki is back.

He sees her from the top of the path above the training yard. She's standing in the middle of the packed earth with her staff, and for a moment he thinks she's training, and then he sees she's leaning on it.

She's never leaned on anything.

The left side of her face is dressed. A square of gauze from the cheekbone down, taped at the edges, and more of it going down her neck into her collar, where he can't see the end of it. Her left forearm is bandaged to the wrist. What he can see of the skin round the edges of it is shiny, and pink, and the wrong kind of smooth. Her glasses are on. One arm of them has been bent and bent back.

They let her out early. Or she got dressed and went.

Panda is on the bottom step.

He's sitting the way he used to, legs out. He sees Kusakabe on the path, and lifts a paw, and then puts it down again, and looks at Maki.

Maki has a sheet of paper.

---

She doesn't wait for him to sit.

She comes across the yard to the foot of the steps, slowly, the staff taking some of each step. She holds the paper up to him, at arm's length, so he has to come down two steps to take it.

He takes it.

*Request for Deferral of Sentence.* Then four paragraphs, typed. He reads the first one. *The undersigned, students of Tokyo Jujutsu High, respectfully request.* He doesn't read the others. He doesn't have to.

At the bottom, two names.

*Zen'in Maki.* Fast, hard, the pen almost through the paper.

*Inumaki Toge.* Slower. Careful. A signature written by someone who's had to learn which hand is going to do things now.

Panda's isn't there. Panda doesn't officially exist this month.

"Ogawa says it'll go in a drawer," Maki says. Her voice is rough. Smoke, maybe. Or just the dressing pulling when she talks. "He's probably right. It goes in a different drawer if a Grade 1 signs it. One they open."

She holds out a pen.

He looks at the pen. Then at the paper. Then at her.

"No," he says.

The pen stays where it is, for a moment. Then she lowers it.

"Why?"

"I already gave them my view."

Panda, on the bottom step, has gone very still. Not frozen-still. The other kind. Listening.

"What view," Maki says.

He could not answer. He's good at that. He could say *it's complicated*, which is a sentence that's never once been true when anyone said it. He could say *Traffic*.

He doesn't. He finds he doesn't want to lie about this one. It's the one thing he's sure of.

"That it should be quick," he says.

---

Nobody says anything.

Up at the main building, somebody is trying to start a leaf blower. Kusakabe hands the paper back. Maki takes it without looking at it, and folds it once, and holds it against her side.

"He's fifteen," Panda says.

He says it quietly, from the step. Not to argue. As if it's a fact Kusakabe might have mislaid.

"I know."

"It wasn't him. In Meiji-dori. You were there. You saw."

"It was his body."

"That's not the same."

"It's the same body the next time."

Maki laughs. One short breath through the nose, no sound in it. It isn't a laugh.

"You've never spoken to him," she says.

He doesn't answer.

"Not once. Not all year. You went round the outside in the rain so you wouldn't walk past his door." She looks at him through the bent glasses. "I saw you. You were soaked. You said it was the rain."

"It was raining."

"The walkway was dry."

He doesn't say anything to that. There's nothing to say to it. It was dry.

"You hid from him since June," Maki says. "And now you want him dead before you have to stop."

That isn't fair. It's also not wrong. He stands on the step with both of those things and finds he can't put either of them down long enough to answer.

"It's not about me," he says.

It comes out flat. He hears how it sounds. Like a man telling you there's nothing in his pockets while holding them shut.

Maki hears it too.

"No?" she says. "Do you remember Masaru? The one with the flask. On the stool. In the rain."

He remembers.

"He said it'd be tidier. If I had an accident. Save everybody a file." She shifts her weight on the staff. Something in her face moves under the gauze, and she waits for it to stop. "You stood up. I didn't hear what you said. Panda did. He told me after. He said it was the only time he'd ever seen you get up quickly."

Panda doesn't say anything.

"So tell me how this is different," Maki says. "A kid nobody asked for. Something in him the grown-ups are scared of. It'd be tidier."

*It is different,* he thinks. *You didn't kill a district. You don't have a thousand years in you looking out through your eyes.*

And then, right behind it: *He didn't kill a district either. Something in him did, while he was gone.*

And behind that: *She didn't ask to be born a Zen'in. He didn't ask to eat a finger. He did it to save somebody. Ogawa told you that in June. You heard.*

He doesn't say any of it. He could say the first one. It's the strongest. But he can feel the other two standing right behind it. If he opens his mouth, they'll come out as well, in the wrong order, and he'll have argued her side for her.

"It's different," he says.

"How."

He looks at the yard. The storehouse. The line of bare cherry trees at the far end, black against the white sky.

"It just is."

Maki looks at him for a long time.

It isn't the look from April, when she called him lazy. That one had heat in it. There was something she wanted from him, and she was angry he wasn't giving it. This one doesn't want anything. She's finished asking. It's the look you'd give a name on a list, just before you put a line through it.

"Okay," she says.

She turns round and goes back across the yard. Slowly. The staff taking some of each step.

---

Panda stays where he is a moment longer.

"Sensei."

"Yeah."
