"He'd probably agree with you." Panda is looking at his own paws. "Yuji. If you asked him. He'd say they were right. He'd say it was his fault." A pause. "That's sort of the worst part. He'd let them."

Kusakabe doesn't know what to do with that. He hadn't thought about what the boy would say. He hadn't thought about the boy saying anything. In his head the boy has never once been a person who talks. He's been a door.

"You'd like him," Panda says. "I said that before. You said let's keep it that way."

"I remember."

"You kept it that way."

Panda gets up. He does it all at once, the way he does. He brushes off the backs of his legs. He doesn't look at Kusakabe.

"See you, sensei."

He goes across the yard to where Maki is standing under the cherry trees, and sits down on the cold ground beside her, and she leans on him instead of the staff.

Kusakabe goes back up to the third step and sits.

He could go over there. It's forty metres. He could stand in front of them and say a better thing. There is a better thing. He can feel the shape of it, somewhere about his breastbone. Something about the edge of the ruin, and his hands not being enough.

He'd have to stand in front of two students, one of them with half her face under gauze, and explain why he's frightened, and why that makes him right.

He doesn't go.

He sits there until the leaf blower stops, and then a while after, and then he gets up and goes to his desk.

---

That night he puts the TV on and doesn't watch it.

A man in a studio pointing at a map of Shibuya with a ring drawn on it. He changes the channel. Cooking. He leaves that.

*You could have signed it,* he thinks. *It goes in a drawer. Ogawa said. It wouldn't change anything.*

That's exactly why he couldn't.

If it went in a drawer, it was a lie. A small one. A man putting his name to a thing he doesn't believe so that two students would look at him the way they used to. And if it didn't go in a drawer? If it changed something? If his name on the bottom was the one that tipped it, and the boy lived, and the next time came. And the edge of the ruin was round some other part of the city. Round a ground floor by a window, say.

He'd rather they looked at him like that.

He'd rather it was him they looked at like that.

It doesn't feel brave. It feels like holding something hot because putting it down would be worse.

He turns it off and sits in the dark.

---

The distance doesn't arrive all at once. It settles, a bit more each day, like dust on a shelf nobody's going to wipe.

He teaches. They turn up. That part doesn't change. The arrangement holds. It's just got a crack in it now, the kind you only see when the light is low.

Maki comes to the yard most days, against everybody's advice. She can't train properly. She does what she can. Footwork, one-handed. Balance. Standing on one leg with her eyes shut until the burnt side pulls and she has to stop. She does it at the far end, under the cherry trees, facing away.

She speaks to him when she has to.

"Sensei. I'm going to the clinic at three."

"Sensei. Is the storehouse open."

She's never once called him that. Eight months, and it was always nothing. No name, no title. *You're late.* Just the sentence, straight at him, as if there wasn't room for anything else in front of it.

Now there's room. She's put *sensei* in it, very politely, like a bag on the seat beside her on a train.

It's the coldest thing she's ever said to him.

"It's open," he says.

---

Panda still says good morning.

"Morning, sensei." Every day. In the corridor, at the classroom door, on the path. Not cold. Panda isn't any good at cold. It's just the word, and the voice is a nice voice, and then Panda goes on past.

He doesn't come to the steps anymore.

Kusakabe doesn't notice the first day. On the second he tells himself it's the weather. By the end of the week there isn't any weather, just a dry white sky. Panda is across the yard every afternoon, lying flat out in the dust near Maki, the way he used to lie near Kusakabe. The step below Kusakabe's is empty.

It's a strange thing to miss. Panda never did anything there. Sat with his legs out. Asked a question now and then, and was content with one word back. Was large, and warm, and there, inside the circle, the way a stove is there in a cold room. You don't look at it. You just know which side of you it's on.

Now Kusakabe knows which side it's on. It's on the far side of the yard.

He doesn't call him over.

He thinks about it once, on a Wednesday, with a can of coffee going cold in his hands. *Panda. Come and sit.* Three words. He could say three words.

He doesn't. He knows what they'd sound like. They'd sound like a man asking to be let off.

---

Inumaki comes back for a day.

He's not allowed back properly. He's been somewhere else, learning how to do up a coat. He comes in the afternoon, with Panda walking beside him a little closer than he needs to, and stands at the top of the steps.

His collar is zipped to the bridge of his nose, the same as always. Under his jacket, the left sleeve is folded flat and pinned to the shoulder with a safety pin. A big one. The kind you'd use on a nappy.

Kusakabe is on the third step. Inumaki stops beside him.

Kusakabe waits for it. *Kelp*. Or *Bonito flakes*. Or the hand tilt, *tuna mayo*, with the one hand he's got. Something. In eight months Inumaki has never once passed him without a word. Even when there was nobody to say it to, he said it to nobody.

Inumaki looks at him.

He doesn't say anything at all.

It goes on for about as long as it takes to breathe in and out twice. Then he goes down the steps, past the bottom one, across the yard to the cherry trees, and Maki moves her staff so he can sit.

*Rice-ball words,* Kusakabe thinks. *He's spent his whole life making sure his words don't hurt anybody.*

*He's found out that not saying one works too.*

---

There's no news.

Or there's news, in pieces. Okkotsu was seen in Shibuya. Okkotsu wasn't. Someone says he found the boy. Someone says the boy wasn't alone. Nobody says any of it twice, or to Kusakabe directly. It goes round the staff room and past his desk and out again, like a smell from somebody else's lunch.

He doesn't ask.

He finds he's listening for it, though. The same way you listen for a phone you've decided not to answer. Every time the staff-room door opens, part of him leans to hear if the next sentence is the one. *It's done.*

He tells himself it's so he can stop thinking about it.

He doesn't look too closely at what he'll feel when it comes. He has a fair idea. He felt it once already, in July, in Ijichi's car, when he heard the boy's heart had been torn out. Relief. He's never told anyone that.

He doesn't want to feel that again.

He wants it done anyway.

Both. People manage both.

---

A Friday. The cold has come down properly now, and the light goes early.

Maki leaves at four for the clinic. Panda goes with her. They go up the far side of the yard, by the cherry trees, where there aren't any steps. Just a slope of dry grass and a path worn through it. Kusakabe has never once seen anybody use that way before. They've used it every day this week.

He stays.

He tells himself he's waiting for the cold to get bad enough to be worth going in. Nobody's going to ask.

The yard empties out the way the ginkgo did. All at once, and then it's just the ground.

He's on the third step from the bottom. The stone is cold through his coat. The storehouse doors are shut. The cherry trees have nothing on them. The packed earth has the marks of Maki's feet on it, one-legged circles at the far end, in the place she's been standing all month with her back to him.

He looks down.

The bottom step is empty. So is the second. So is the one beside him, where nobody ever sat, but which was always, somehow, part of the arrangement.

Caught in the crack between the bottom step and the next one, there's a tuft of fur. Black, with a little white in it. The wind keeps trying to take it, and it keeps not going.

Weeks, and it's still there. Nobody's sat close enough to knock it loose.

The lights come on along the path, one after another, up towards the main building.

He doesn't move. Gate in front of him. Wall at his back. The whole yard in his reach, if he wanted to draw it, and nothing in it to feel.

All those steps. All that room.

Just him on them.

---

## Chapter 22. Culling Season

The dispatch board has run out of room.

It's the long whiteboard on the wall of the office by the car yard. For six years it has held one week of jobs in the coordinator's small square handwriting, with a strip at the bottom for notes nobody reads. Now the jobs go all the way down to the frame. Someone has taped sheets of printer paper underneath and carried on, and the sheets nearly reach the floor.

Kusakabe stands in front of it with his coat on.

His name is up there seven times this week. Nerima. Itabashi. A hospital in Adachi that's been half emptied and wants to be fully emptied. An evacuation centre in a school gym in Kita. A residual check on something in Ota with a question mark after it and no grade.

Other names have lines through them. Some have nothing next to them at all, which is worse, because a line means somebody decided something.

The coordinator sits under the board with her headset round her neck and a phone against each ear. She hasn't had a rice cracker in a fortnight. He knows because he's started noticing what isn't there.

She covers one phone with her hand.

"Adachi first," she says. "The car's outside. It's not Ijichi."

"Fine."

"She drives fast."

"Fine."

She takes her hand off the phone and goes back to saying no to someone, politely, in four different ways.

---

The driver is called Sekine. She's twenty-two, has been an auxiliary for nine months, and drives as if the road has personally let her down.

"Sorry," she says, at the first corner. She doesn't slow down.

"It's fine."

"Ijichi-san said you don't like talking."

"Ijichi-san's right."

She nods, satisfied, and doesn't say another word until Adachi. He likes her already. He won't tell her.

The city goes past the window, and it's the wrong city.

Most of it is the city he knows. But it's thinner. Whole streets have a gap in them, as if someone took one building out of the row to see if the others would notice. Some of the gaps are Shibuya. Some are newer.

At a crossing in Kita, a whole school class walks past in a line, holding a rope, with a teacher at each end. Their bags have name tags and a second tag in a different colour. He doesn't know what the colour means. He has a feeling it means where to send them.

Two streets on, there's a tape across the road and a policeman in front of it who doesn't look at the car. Beyond the tape the road goes on, perfectly ordinary, into nothing in particular. The light past a certain point is a shade wrong. A shade heavier. As if the air on that side is paying more rent.

"Is that one of them?" he says.

Sekine looks.

"No. That's just closed. The real ones you can't miss."

She says it like a commuter. Nine months in, and she's already got the voice.

---

He gets the Game the way he gets everything now. In pieces, from people who are in a hurry.

From Okabe first, on a single sheet, typed, under the heading *Interim Guidance*. The sheet says there are barriers. It says they're called colonies. It says there are ten of them across the country, and two in Tokyo, and staff who are not players are not, under any circumstances, to cross into one.

*Staff who are not players.*

He reads the word twice. Players.

The rest he gets from wherever it falls. From Sekine, at a red light. From two Grade 3s at the vending machines, talking low the way people talk about money. From the TV in a noodle shop, where a newsreader says *unexplained* eleven times in a minute and a half, and the man behind the counter switches over to baseball.

It goes something like this.

The man in the robes, the dead one, the one with the stitches across his forehead, did something at the end of Shibuya. Something long prepared. All across the country, people woke up different. Ordinary people. Office workers, students, a woman who runs a florist's. One morning there's a technique in them that wasn't there the night before, and nobody asked them. And some of the people who woke up weren't people who'd gone to sleep. Old ones. Very old ones, from centuries back, who'd made some arrangement with him long ago, and were waiting, and now have somebody's body to wear.

Then the barriers came down round them, and the rules came with them.

The rules are a game. Points for killing. Points for killing sorcerers. Join, or the technique kills you. Stay in, or the rules do. Ogawa has seen them written out on a sheet, and says they read like the instructions for something you'd be given at a children's party, if the party were in hell.

"Who's in," Kusakabe says.

Ogawa moves his toothpick. "Some of ours. Not many. The young ones mostly."

"Ours."

"The first-years. The Okkotsu boy's back." He looks at the door. "Nobody's saying who else."

And the boy. The one they sent Okkotsu for. Kusakabe hears he's dead, on a Thursday, from Ogawa. On the Monday after, from Ogawa, he hears he isn't, and that he's gone into one of the barriers. Ogawa tells him both with the same face.

Kusakabe doesn't know which one he was hoping for. He's careful not to find out.

He doesn't ask who else. He finds he can do that, these days. Not ask. Stand in a corridor with a question sitting right there in his mouth, and close it, and walk on.

*Not a player,* he thinks, on the way out. *First game in my life I wasn't picked for. And I'm glad.*

It isn't quite a joke. It isn't quite not.

---

Adachi is a hospital with the patients going out of it in ones and twos, on trolleys and in wheelchairs, into ambulances that come from three other wards and go back full.

Nobody has told the staff what's in the basement. They've been told *structural survey*.

What's in the basement is small and has been feeding for a week. It's grown fat on the leaving. On every worried family in the car park, every *where are they taking her*, every phone call that ends with *I don't know, they won't say*.

He goes down alone. Sekine waits at the top of the stairs with her radio, because he asks her to, and because she's nine months in and knows the difference between brave and useful.

The boiler room hums.

He lets the circle out. It goes out to its usual edge and stops there, and the curse is just outside it, wedged between two pipes, watching him. Too many joints. A face like a bruise that hasn't decided on a colour yet.

It comes in.

His hands answer. One cut, low. The thing comes apart on the blade like a fistful of lint, and then it's ash on the warm floor, and the boiler keeps humming. As far as the boiler's concerned, nothing has happened.

His palms are cold. They always are. He stands for a while until they come back.

*Grade 1,* he thinks. *Sword. Small circle. Basements, mostly.*

When he comes up the stairs, Sekine looks at him, then at her watch.

"That was quick."

"It was small."

"They're not all small," she says, and starts the car.

---

The third week of November, he goes to his sister's on a Wednesday.

There's no reason. There's no job near her. It's simply that after the hospital in Adachi he gets back to the school at half past four, with nothing more on the board for him until morning. He stands in the car yard, and his feet go to the station.

He has done this twice before in the last ten days. A Friday. A Monday.

Ono is in the kitchen when he lets himself in, writing up the day in the rabbit notebook. She looks at him, then at the calendar on her phone.

"It's Wednesday," she says.

"Yeah."

"You came Monday."

"I was passing."

Ono knows exactly where the school is. She doesn't say so. She holds out the notebook, and he reads it.

*Wed: ate most. Physio OK. Asked about the TV. Told her it's broken. (Unplugged it.)*

He looks at her.

"She was getting upset," Ono says. "The news is all the same thing. She doesn't know what it is but she can tell it's the same thing."

"Right."

"I'll plug it back in. When it's different."

"Thanks."

She puts her bag on her shoulder and doesn't go. That's new. Ono always goes.

"My cousin's in Nakano," she says. "They moved her whole street on Saturday. Buses. Two hours' notice." She hitches the bag strap higher. "If they say move, here. Where do I take her?"

It's the most she's ever said to him in one go.

He opens his mouth to say *they won't*, and closes it, because he doesn't know that, and she'd know he didn't.

"Call me," he says.

"And if you don't answer?"

There it is. He's been waiting a year for someone to say it back to him.

"I'll answer."

Ono considers this for about as long as she considers anything, which isn't long. Then she nods, once, and writes something in the margin of the notebook. He reads it after she's gone.

*If move: call brother. He answers.*

Underlined. He stands in the kitchen and looks at it longer than it needs.

---

His sister is awake. The lamp's on already. The days are short now, and getting shorter.

"You were here," she says, when he sits down.

"When?"

"Before. Yesterday."

"Monday."

"Yesterday," she says firmly, and he lets it be yesterday.

She looks at him a while. Her eyes are clear tonight. Not always. Tonight.

"You're checking on me," she says.

He doesn't answer that.

"You've got a face," she says. "Like you're counting the windows."

*I am,* he thinks. *Two. One locks properly.*

He gets up and checks the second one. It locks properly now as well. He fixed it on Friday with a screwdriver and a piece of folded card. She watches him do it, and doesn't say anything, and when he sits down again she puts her good hand on his sleeve and leaves it there.

It stays there for the rest of the evening. He doesn't move his arm.

---

Panda is in the corridor outside the dispatch office on the last Monday of November.

Kusakabe sees him from the far end. There's no missing him. He's standing by the noticeboard with a bag over one shoulder, which is strange, because Panda doesn't carry bags. Panda has never needed anything he couldn't hold in his paws.

He's talking to the coordinator. Then he isn't. He turns and comes up the corridor towards the stairs, which means towards Kusakabe.

Neither of them slows down.

It isn't far. A corridor is never far, whatever it feels like. Grey floor. The radiators going. Somebody's umbrella drying against the wall in a small brown puddle.

"Sensei," Panda says, level, as they pass.

"Panda."

That's all. Panda goes on to the stairs. He doesn't look back. He goes down them steadily, the bag swinging, and the door to the yard bangs at the bottom.

Kusakabe stands outside the dispatch office.

The coordinator looks up at him through the glass, then back down at her phones. She doesn't tell him. She has no reason to. He reads it off the board on the way past, under a sheet of printer paper headed *Colonies. Personnel. Do not discuss.*

Panda's name is there. A little further down, Maki's.

He'd known they would. Since the first sheet from Okabe, he'd known. You don't spend a year teaching a girl like Maki to fight and expect her to stay in a yard. Panda neither. He just thought someone might say so.

*Someone did,* he thinks. *The board did. That's what boards are for.*

He goes out to the car.

He thinks about the third step on the way to Itabashi. He hasn't sat on it since the afternoon he sat on it alone. He doesn't think about why. He thinks about the road, and the car in front, and the way Sekine taps the wheel at red lights. He thinks about Nerima tomorrow. It works for most of the drive.

---

The rest rule comes back on the second of December.

Somebody upstairs has remembered it exists. Twelve days on, one day off, compulsory, for anyone who isn't currently bleeding. A sheet goes up on the board with the days blocked out in yellow highlighter. It's Okabe's yellow. Kusakabe would know it anywhere.

His day is a Tuesday.

He wakes at ten past six out of habit. He lies there. He gets up, eventually, and makes the coffee, and drinks it, and then there's nothing.

He sits down on the sofa.

He knows this sofa. He knows the way the cushion gives, and the angle you can lean back to without quite lying down. He knows which spring you can feel through the cover if you sit too far to the right.

He doesn't sit too far to the right.

At noon the ward loudspeaker plays its chime, the one that's meant to test the emergency system and has never once been used for an emergency. Twelve notes, thin and cheerful, over the rooftops. He hears it. That's all he does with it.

The phone is on the arm of the sofa, face up. It doesn't ring. It doesn't need to. Nobody's going to need him today. That's the whole idea of a rest day. The board has his name blocked out in yellow, and somewhere else a car is taking someone else to a basement.

He sits.

It used to be easy. That's what he notices first. It used to be the easiest thing in the world. He'd sit down and the day would come apart quietly in his hands, the way wet bread comes apart, and there'd be nothing left by evening, and he wouldn't miss it. That was the point.

It isn't easy now.

He doesn't know when it changed. He sits there and tries to find the old shape of it, the soft grey nothing, and it won't come. Something else comes instead.

His chest is heavy.

Not sad. Not tired, though he is. Heavy, like something's resting on it. Flat across the ribs. A weight he has to breathe round. He can taste something. Not dust. Not quite. The memory of dust, at the back of the throat.

The room is light. It's the middle of the day. But the edges of it are going dark, the way they go dark when you've been lying still too long in a small space, and your eyes have stopped bothering.

He doesn't move. Not moving is the whole idea.

And he understands, quite slowly, sitting there in the middle of a Tuesday with his hands on his knees, what this is.

It's the slab.

It's the same. It's exactly the same. Lying still and letting it all go on somewhere else, without him, and calling that safe. He's done it on this sofa a thousand times. He did it once under a building. He hadn't noticed, until now, that they were the same thing. The sofa just had better lighting.

*Maybe if I sit here long enough,* something in him starts to say, *someone else will—*

He's on his feet.

He didn't decide it. There's no resolve in it. He'd laugh at anyone who called it that. It's the thing his body does when something crosses into the circle without asking. His legs simply stop agreeing to it. That's all. Sitting still has started to feel like being under the rock, and his body, which has only ever been sensible about one thing, has decided it isn't going under there again.

He stands in the middle of the room, breathing, a little stupidly, like a man who's got up to answer a door and found nobody there.

The coffee cup is on the floor by the sofa. He picks it up and puts it in the sink.

Then he puts his shoes on.

---

He doesn't go anywhere in particular. He walks.

Down the hill, past the shrine, where the ginkgos are bare now. Along the river, where one man is fishing and nobody looks at anybody.

He walks for two hours.

His chest gets lighter as he goes. He doesn't make anything of it. It's a fact, like the cold.

Somewhere around three he finds he's at the school gate.

*Of course I am,* he thinks. *Nowhere else to go on a day off. Very healthy.*

The coordinator looks up when he comes into the office. She looks at the yellow block with his name in it. She looks at him.

"You're off," she says.

"I know."

She looks at him a while longer. Then she reaches under the board, where the printer paper goes nearly to the floor, and tears off a strip at the bottom.

"Nobody's taken this," she says. "Since Thursday. Nobody wants it."

He reads it. *Evacuation centre, Kita. School gym. Reports: people waking at night, can't breathe, something on the chest. Grade unknown. Night only.*

He reads it twice.

*Something on the chest.*

"Fine," he says.

---

The gym is full of people sleeping.

Three hundred, the warden says. Families on blue mats in rows, with bags and coats in the gaps. A whole street, and part of the next one. A radio playing quietly in a corner. A child's drawing taped to the climbing bars: a house, a tree, a sun with a face, all of it in very confident orange.

They're sleeping badly. You can hear it. Three hundred people turning over and turning back.

He sits on the stage at the end with his back to the wall and the doors in front of him. Sekine sits on the steps below. She's brought a flask. It's terrible, and it's better than anything he'd have made.

At ten past two a woman in the third row sits up and makes a sound like she's been pushed under water.

He's already moving.

It's low and flat and grey, lying across her the way a fog lies on a field, and it's been feeding on exactly what you'd expect. On three hundred people lying in a strange room, being told nothing, waiting to be told when they can go home. It doesn't care about any one of them. It just likes the weight.

It feels him coming and lifts off her, and turns, and it's in the circle.

Two cuts. That's all. One to make it let go, one to finish it. It comes apart without a sound, which is the only mercy, because nobody wakes. Not even the woman. She's already lying back down, breathing easier, frowning in her sleep at something she won't remember.

He stands in the aisle between the mats with the sword still out, and his palms cold, and three hundred people sleeping round him, more easily now, one row at a time.

He doesn't feel brave. He feels like a man who's come in to fix a draught.

*Something on the chest,* he thinks. *Yeah. I know that one.*

He goes back to the stage and sits down. Sekine passes him the flask without a word.

He drinks it. It's still terrible.

---

December goes past in pieces.

A job in Nerima. A job in Ota that turns out to be two. Paperwork he fills in at three in the morning in the back of Sekine's car with a torch in his teeth, because the forms don't stop just because the world has.

He goes to his sister's on Sundays, and on Wednesdays, and twice on a Friday. Ono stops remarking on it. The rabbit notebook gets a new line at the bottom of each page. *Brother came.* As if he were weather. As if he were the physio.

News comes the way it's been coming. From other people's mouths, in corridors, without the beginnings or the ends.

The Game goes on. The colonies hold. Some of the players are sorcerers from a thousand years ago, and some are office workers, and the points go up.

Names go on lists. Some of them are names he knows. He doesn't ask about Panda. He doesn't ask about Maki. He reads the board every morning, though, top to bottom, all the way to the floor. Their names are still on it, without a line through them, and he goes out to the car.

---

On the nineteenth, a Wednesday, the radio in the dispatch office says Gojo's out.

It says it the way it says everything now. Broken up. *Prison Realm. Confirmed. Repeat, Gojo-san is.* And then a lot of people talking at once, and then the coordinator turns it down, because everybody in the room has stopped what they're doing and she needs someone to answer a phone.

Kusakabe is by the door.

He feels it go through the building. Not the radio. The people. A whole school letting its shoulders down at once, like a room after the exam papers have been collected. Ogawa takes the toothpick out of his mouth and doesn't put it back.

*Out,* Kusakabe thinks.

He waits to feel what everyone else is feeling.

Some of it comes. Relief, real and warm. He'd be lying if he said it didn't. There's a kind of tiredness that only goes when the strongest man in the country is back on the map.

But under it, colder, there's the other thing. The thing he knows because he's spent his whole life working out where the trouble goes.

If Gojo's out, someone's going to fight him.

There's only one someone.

He finds out the rest by evening. In pieces, the way he finds out everything. The night Gojo came out, something else came loose as well. Ogawa has three versions of what, and doesn't like any of them. Something with the King of Curses. A cost. There's always a cost. Nobody in the staff room says it the same way twice, and nobody says it all the way to the end.

Kusakabe doesn't try to finish the sentences for them.

He goes home. He sleeps, which surprises him.

---

The date goes up on the board on the twenty-first.

He comes in at eight and there it is, at the top, above all the printer paper, in red. The coordinator's small square writing, gone a little larger than usual, as if her hand didn't quite trust itself.

*24 Dec. Shinjuku.*

Nothing else. No grade. No team. No *All available*, not yet. Just the place and the date. Nothing to say who it's for.

He stands in front of it with his coat on.

"They agreed it," the coordinator says, from under the board. She isn't on the phone, for once. "Gojo-san and the other one. Like a dentist's appointment." She looks up at it with him. "The twenty-fourth."

"Yeah."

"One year," she says. "Since the Parade. To the day. Did you notice?"

He'd noticed. He'd noticed before she finished the sentence. A year ago he was in Shinjuku in the dark, under a sky full of Christmas lights, and his arm was bleeding into his sleeve, and he didn't know any of their names yet. Not Panda's, not properly. Not Maki's. Not the pink-haired boy's.

*Someone's got a sense of occasion,* he thinks. *I hope it isn't the King of Curses. It's probably the King of Curses.*

"Yeah," he says. "I noticed."

He goes out to the car. It's Nerima again. Something in a laundrette. He's quite glad of it.

---

That night he's in the kitchen, waiting for the kettle.

The kitchen is narrow. He uses it for two things: the kettle, and standing in. He's standing in it now, with his back to the counter, looking at the wall by the fridge.

There's a calendar on it.

A paper one, from Midori Home Care. It came in the post with an invoice last December. A brown envelope, with a card saying *Season's Greetings from all the team*, and he hung it on the nail by the fridge because the nail was there. A photograph for each month. Mountains, mostly.

It's still on January.

He's never turned it over. Not once in a year. He's looked at it every day, standing here, waiting for the kettle, and it's been January the whole time. A mountain with a little red shrine at the bottom. The same mountain, every morning, for three hundred and fifty-something mornings.

He lifts the pages.

February. March. A lake in April with blossom on the water. He goes through the whole year in one handful. Every square empty. Every Sunday empty, though there wasn't a Sunday he missed. Every Monday empty, though he paid the bills on every one. Nobody would know from this calendar that anything happened to anyone in 2018.

December is a bridge in the snow. Two people crossing it, small, with an umbrella.

He finds a pen in the drawer. It's a cheap biro from a bank he's never used. He shakes it until it works.

He finds the twenty-fourth. A Monday.

He holds the pen over it for a moment. Not long. He's not a man who needs long.

Then he draws a circle round it. Small. Neat. Just the square, and the number inside.

He looks at it.

It's the first thing he's written on the calendar in a year. A single small circle, in blue, on a page with a bridge on it.

The kettle clicks off behind him. He doesn't turn round yet.

He leaves the pen on the counter, next to it. In case.

---

## Chapter 23. Training Yuji

The sheet is in his pigeonhole on a Monday, a week before Christmas Eve, folded once.

Not the dispatch board. Not a red tab. One page, handwritten, in a fast square hand he doesn't know, as if whoever wrote it had six of these to do before lunch and this was the fourth.

*Afternoons. Two till dark. The yard. Itadori Y. Reaction, footwork, reading. Until further notice. Kusakabe A.*

He reads it standing up, with people going past behind him.

*Itadori Y.*

He has seen that name on paper for six months. Mostly at the bottom of things. A line in a casualty list. A note on a dispatch sheet. Dead, a month ago, according to Ogawa. Then not dead, and gone into a barrier. And once, six weeks ago, at the top of a page that said what ought to be done about it, and how soon. He'd agreed with that page. Out loud.

He folds the sheet back along its crease and puts it in his coat.

He waits for the part of him that finds the back door.

It doesn't come. That's the odd thing. A year ago he'd have had three ways out before he reached the end of the corridor. A job in Chiba. A knee. A Sunday. He knows every fire door in this building and what's wedged against it. He's just stopped walking towards them. He couldn't say when. Somewhere between a slab of concrete and a paper calendar.

*Feet,* he thinks. *Fine. I can do feet.*

---

Two o'clock. He's seven minutes late.

The boy is already there.

He's alone in the middle of the yard, doing press-ups on his knuckles on the packed earth, counting under his breath. The count is somewhere past a hundred and not slowing. The leaves have been raked. Somebody's done that since last month. Kusakabe doesn't know who.

He stops at the top of the steps.

This is the thing he managed, all year, not to have. Pink hair across a courtyard. A back, kneeling in grey rubble. A face that wasn't the boy's, with too many eyes in it. He's been very good at it. A whole year of corridors timed to the minute. A whole year of the long way round.

The count stops at a round number. The boy gets up in one movement, without using his hands, brushes his knuckles on his trousers and comes over at a jog. He stops a polite distance below the bottom step and bows from the waist. Properly. All the way down, and a beat at the bottom, the way an old man teaches a boy to bow.

"Itadori Yuji," he says. "First year. Thank you for doing this."

He straightens up.

And there it is. The face.

It's ordinary. That's the first thing. Fifteen, and looks it. Broad through the shoulders, the way a boy who's played every sport going is broad. The pink hair is growing out a little darker at the roots. The uniform collar is whole. Kusakabe last looked at that collar from thirty metres away, burnt at the edge, because looking anywhere else would have been a movement.

The eyes are brown. Just brown.

Under each one there's a small line, healed pale, like a cut that closed neatly and left a seam.

Kusakabe knows what opened there.

He looks at it for exactly as long as he can stand, which turns out to be longer than he'd have guessed. Then he looks at the two brown eyes instead. They're waiting for him to say something.

*So that's what you look like,* he thinks. *Took me a whole year not to find out.*

His circle is out before he's decided anything. It always is, near a thing he's afraid of. He reaches through it, quietly, for the weight he felt on Meiji-dori. The thing with no edge that everything leaned towards.

There's a boy.

A warm, solid, heavy-boned boy, standing just past the edge of his reach with his hands at his sides. Breathing a bit fast from the press-ups. That's all there is in the circle.

He doesn't know what that means. Nobody's told him, and he hasn't asked. He lets the circle go.

"Kusakabe," he says.

"I know. Panda-senpai said." The boy nods, very serious. "He said you'd be late."

"Did he."

"He said not to ask why. He said you'd say traffic."

Kusakabe looks down at him. The boy looks back. There's nothing in his face but interest.

"Was there?" the boy says. "Traffic?"

"No."

He doesn't know why he says that. It just comes out true, the way a foot comes down where the ground actually is.

The boy grins. It takes up most of his face.

"Okay," he says. "Thank you."

*For what,* Kusakabe thinks.

He doesn't ask. He comes down the steps.

---

The storehouse doors are open. The practice swords are in the rack where they always are, the grips wrapped in white tape, the tape grey now and coming loose at the ends.

Maki wraps them. Wrapped them. Every few weeks, sitting on the floor of the storehouse with a roll of tape and a face like she was punishing them for something. Nobody's rewrapped them since October. Maki is away. Her name was on a sheet on the dispatch board under a heading that said not to discuss it, and nobody does, and he doesn't ask.

He takes one. He doesn't take one for the boy.

In the middle of the yard he drags his heel through the dirt, a short curved line, about the distance of one arm and one sword from where he's standing. Then he stands behind it.

"Come in," he says. "Touch my shoulder. Don't get hit."

"With what? Like, a hand?"

"Anything."

The boy looks at the line. He looks at the practice sword, hanging loose by Kusakabe's knee. Something goes over his face that Kusakabe has seen on a lot of first-years. It's the look of someone who has been taught a great many things very quickly by a great many people, and is trying to remember which one goes here.

Then he comes.

He's fast. Kusakabe knew he'd be fast. Everybody has a story. A shot put thrown halfway out of a stadium. Fifty metres in three seconds. A curse put through a wall with a fist. Knowing it is one thing. Standing in front of it is another. The boy covers the ground between them the way a dropped thing covers the ground between a table and the floor.

Kusakabe taps him on the forearm with the practice sword.

Not hard. Just enough to be there.

The boy stops, a step inside the line, looking at his arm.

"One more," the boy says, before Kusakabe can say anything.

He comes again. Tap, on the ribs. And again. Tap, on the outside of the knee. Then faster, with a feint off the left foot that would have put most Grade 2s on the floor. Tap, on the wrist, as the hand comes up.

"Thank you," the boy says, every time. Getting his breath. Going back to the start. "Thank you. Okay. Thank you."

Kusakabe doesn't say anything. He watches.

It isn't speed the boy's short of. He's got more of that than anyone Kusakabe has ever stood in front of who wasn't trying to kill him. It's the half-second before. Just before each step, the boy's eyes drop. Not far. A flick, down and up again. Checking. Like someone following a recipe they don't trust.

He's thinking about his feet. Every time. Somebody told him where to put them, and he's being good, and putting them there.

By the twentieth go the sun is behind the storehouse roof and the boy has a red mark on most of his visible skin. He's still grinning. His shirt is stuck to his back.

"That's today," Kusakabe says.

"It's not dark yet."

"It's dark enough."

"Can I do it a few more times? On my own?"

"There's no one to hit you."

"I'll imagine it." The boy bows again. All the way down. "Thank you. Really."

Kusakabe goes back up the steps.

There's someone at the far end of the yard, under the cherry trees. Big. Black and white. Sitting with his back to a trunk in the last of the light, the way you'd sit if you'd been there a while and hadn't wanted anyone to notice you arrive.

Panda doesn't wave. Kusakabe doesn't either.

He goes home the long way, out of habit, and then wonders who exactly he's avoiding now. The boy is behind him in the yard. Panda is under a tree. There's nobody left on the short way to miss.

---

Tuesday, the boy is there before him again.

Kusakabe's eight minutes late. He doesn't say why. The boy doesn't ask. He just says "Afternoon! Thank you," and goes to stand at the start, already up on the balls of his feet, the way a boy waits at a starting line on sports day.

Panda is by the storehouse today. In the shade, against the wall. Not near the steps.

They do it for an hour. Tap. Thank you. Tap. Thank you. The flick of the eyes, every time. Down and up.

Kusakabe stops him.

He's never been good at this part. Saying the thing. He's always thought that if you say more than one sentence you're mostly just hearing yourself talk, and the student is mostly just waiting for you to stop. So he stands there on his side of the line for a while, with the boy waiting, and finds the one that's true.

"Stop thinking about where your feet go," he says. "Feel the edge. Let the body answer."

The boy blinks.

"The edge of what?"

"Where I can reach." Kusakabe touches the line in the dirt with the tip of the practice sword. "You know where it is. Your body knows. It knew the first time. You keep checking."

"So don't check."

"Don't check."

The boy nods slowly. Not the eager nod. A different one. Kusakabe has seen it once before, on a girl from Kyoto at three in the morning in a ruin.

The boy goes back to the start. He stands there a moment with his hands loose. He doesn't look at his feet. He looks at Kusakabe's chest.

Then he comes in.

And stops.

Exactly on the line. Not over it. His front foot is a finger's width from the curve in the dirt, his weight already back on the other one. And the practice sword, which was going to be there, isn't anywhere. There's nothing to tap.

They stand like that for a second.

Then the boy slips left, inside the line, and the sword taps him on the shoulder. But it's late. Only just, but late.

The boy looks at his own feet now. Afterwards. As if they belong to someone he's only just been introduced to.

"Oh," he says.

"Go on," Kusakabe says.

It isn't every time after that. It's one in four, and then one in three. But it's there now. The flick has gone. When the boy's feet are wrong now they're wrong without being asked, which is a better kind of wrong, the kind that gets fixed.

"Thank you," the boy says, when the light goes. "That was. Thank you. Seriously."

Kusakabe nods and goes up the steps.

*One sentence,* he thinks. *Took me two days to find it. Pay's the same either way.*

---

Wednesday, more of the same. The line. The tap. The thank you. One in three, then two in five.

At the water break the boy stands with the bottle halfway to his mouth and doesn't drink from it.

"Were you in Shibuya?" he says.

Kusakabe looks at the yard.

"Yeah."

"Where?"

"North. Meiji-dori."

The boy is quiet. Kusakabe can see him working out the map. Where Meiji-dori is. What happened on it, and when, and in whose body. The grin goes out of his face completely, for the first time all week, and what's under it is older than fifteen by a long way.

"I'm sorry," the boy says.

It's the wrong way round. Kusakabe knows it's the wrong way round. He sits with it for a moment, the wrongness of it, like a coat buttoned one hole off.

He thinks of a burnt collar, thirty metres away, and four red eyes going through him as if he were a window. Not one of them brown.

"Wasn't you," he says. "I saw."

The boy looks at him for a long second. He doesn't say thank you. It's the only time all week he doesn't. He just nods, once, and drinks his water, and goes back to the start.

---

That evening he rings Ono on the walk home. He's been doing that more. He doesn't think about why.

"Fine," Ono says. No hello. There never is. "Ate. Physio tomorrow. She asked if you'd got a cold."

"I haven't."

"I told her you sound like it." A pause in which he can hear her writing something down. "Sunday?"

"Sunday."

She hangs up first.

---

Thursday, Okabe finds him in the corridor with a form.

Okabe's in a grey cardigan again. Kusakabe takes it as a sign of something. He isn't sure what. That the world's still there, possibly. That it's gone back to having forms.

"Instruction hours," Okabe says. "For the record. Anyone assigned to a student this month logs them. There's a box for each day."

Kusakabe takes the form. There are boxes for each day. Each box has a space for *From* and a space for *To*.

He writes a *2* in the first one. In the second he writes *Dark.*

Okabe reads it upside down.

"Dark isn't a time," he says.

"It is in December. Half four."

Okabe looks at him for a moment longer than he needs to. Then he takes the form back and puts it in the clear plastic sleeve on top of his pile, where the forms that have been done go.

"Half four," Okabe says. He writes it in, small, beside the word *Dark*, in pencil, in case it changes.

---

Thursday, in the yard, he moves on.

"Reading," he says. "I'm going to come at you. Slowly. Tell me where before I get there."

"Out loud?"

"Out loud."

He comes at the boy at walking pace with the practice sword low. The boy watches the sword. Of course he watches the sword. It's the thing that hurts.

"Left," the boy says. It goes right. Tap.

"Thank you. Okay. One more."

"Not the sword," Kusakabe says. "The sword's the last thing to know where it's going. Watch the middle. Here." He touches his own breastbone with two fingers. "And the shoulders. Everything starts there and goes outwards. By the time it's at the end of the blade, it's old news."

The boy looks at the middle of him. It's an odd feeling, being looked at there. Like somebody reading a letter over your shoulder.

"Left," the boy says. It goes left. "Low," he says, and it's low. "Right. No. Oh, that's mean."

"Yeah."

"Thank you."

At half three they stop for water. The boy has a convenience-store bag by the steps. He sits on the bottom step, the one that used to be Inumaki's, and eats three rice balls one after another, and then a sandwich. Then he looks into the bag with an air of deep disappointment, as if it has let him down personally.

*Earnest,* Kusakabe thinks. *Eats a lot. Thanks people.*

He knows where he heard that. A break room on the fourteenth floor, in a building nobody worked in any more, at about one in the morning. A man in a pale suit setting a can of coffee back on its ring, saying he'd been looking after one of Gojo's first-years. An earnest one. Kusakabe had known which boy, and changed the subject. To a coin in a machine.

There's nowhere to change it to now. The subject is sitting on the bottom step with rice on his chin.

He doesn't say anything about it. He drinks his water.

Panda is lying on the grass at the edge of the yard, on his back, near the cherry trees. He calls out, "Yuji. Eat slower. You'll be sick." He doesn't look at the steps.

"I'm never sick!"

"You said that last time."

"Last time was different!"

Kusakabe watches the two of them shout at each other across the yard, easy, the way people shout who've stood in the same bad places. He doesn't join in. He wouldn't know how to. And it isn't his to join.

Panda's voice stops when it gets to the steps. It's been doing that for weeks. It goes round him the way a conversation in a staff room goes round a desk.

He knows why. He was there when he earned it.

---

Friday it's cold, the dry kind, the sky very high and pale.

"Eyes shut," he says.

"Shut?"

"Shut."

The boy shuts them. He stands in the middle of the yard with his hands at his sides, and his eyes shut, and his face tipped up a little, as if he's expecting rain.

Kusakabe has a paper bag of dried soybeans from the supermarket. It cost a hundred and eight yen. It's the cheapest thing he's ever bought for work.

He can't teach the boy the circle. There are rules about the circle. Old ones. Who gets taught it, and by whom, and what they promise first. Made by men in a dojo he's never been invited to, a long time before he was born. He's spent his whole working life inside those rules without once being asked whether he minded them. He can't hand it over.

But he can show what it's for. He can show that there's an edge. That you can know where it is without looking.

He flicks a bean.

It hits the boy in the middle of the forehead.

"Ow. Thank you."

"Don't thank me. Move."

"Move where?"

"Away from the bean."

"I can't see the bean."

"That's the point."

He flicks another. Shoulder. Another. Ear. The boy flinches after each one, a half-second late, and opens his mouth. Kusakabe can see the *thank you* coming up the way a sneeze comes up. Then the boy remembers, and shuts his mouth again, and nods instead.

It takes most of the afternoon.

Then, somewhere after three, a bean goes past his ear. It doesn't hit him. He's moved. Not far. A hand's width. His eyes are still shut and his face has gone very still, the way faces go when the person inside them is listening to something a long way off.

Another bean. Another hand's width. It goes past.

The boy's mouth opens.

"Don't," Kusakabe says.

The boy shuts it again. He's smiling with his eyes closed. That's the most annoying thing Kusakabe has seen all week, and he's seen Okabe write *half four* in pencil.

*He's got it. Not the circle. He'll never have that from me. But the edge. He knows there's an edge.*

The bag runs out at quarter to four. Beans everywhere. On the packed earth, in the grass, one balanced on the boy's collar. The crows will be pleased.

---

They stop as the light goes. The boy picks up beans for a while, one at a time, because he's the kind of person who picks things up. Then he straightens and looks over at the steps.

"You don't have to stay till dark, you know," he says.

Kusakabe looks at him.

"It's fine. If you've got stuff. I know some people aren't..." The boy stops. He rubs the back of his neck. He doesn't look upset. He looks like someone reading out a fact off a sign. "Some people don't really want to be near me. After. I get it. It's fair, honestly. I'd probably be the same."

He says it the way you'd tell someone the shop on the corner closes early on Wednesdays.

Kusakabe is sitting on the third step. He doesn't remember sitting down. He hasn't sat there in weeks. He sits very still.

He wonders, not for the first time this week, whether the boy knows. Whether somebody's told him what was said in which room, and who said what about him in it. Whether that's what this is. Or whether he doesn't know, and this is just what he's like. A boy who's been told for six months that he ought to be dead, by enough people, often enough, that he's started agreeing with them politely.

He can't decide which is worse.

"Two till dark," Kusakabe says. "It says."

The boy looks at him for a second. Then the grin comes back. Not all of it. Most of it.

"Okay," he says. "Thank you."

*Stop thanking me,* Kusakabe thinks. *I wanted you dead six weeks ago. I said so. In a room. Out loud. I'd have signed it myself.*

He doesn't say it.

It sits in his mouth for a while, all of it, whole and true. He could say it. It would be the truest thing anyone's said in this yard all week. And then it would be the boy's to carry, on top of everything else, for the sake of letting Kusakabe put it down.

He keeps it.

"Tomorrow," he says. "Two."

"You'll be late."

"Probably."

"Okay." The boy bows. "Thank you."

Kusakabe gets up, and his knees tell him exactly how many afternoons this week he's spent standing on packed earth with his weight back, and he goes.

At the top of the steps he looks back once. Panda is by the storehouse, sitting against the wall. Panda is looking at him. Not at the boy. At him.

Then Panda looks away, and Kusakabe goes home.

---

At home he lies on the sofa for an hour with his arm across his eyes.

An hour. That's all it gets now. Then he gets up and eats something standing at the counter and goes to bed.

The calendar's on the kitchen wall where he can see it from the counter. He doesn't look at it. He knows what's on it.

His right arm aches from the wrist to the shoulder. Hundreds of small taps. Each one the same weight, in the same place, at the end of the same arm. Like writing your name over and over until the pen wears a groove in your finger.

He realises, lying in the dark, that he's tired in a way he isn't usually. Not the flat grey tired, the sofa tired, the kind that comes from nothing. This is the other kind. The kind from doing something.

He'd forgotten there were two.

---

Saturday he's five minutes late. The boy says, "Better!" and Kusakabe says, "Don't."

They do all of it. The line. The reading. The beans, a new bag, a hundred and eight yen. The boy stops on the edge three times in four now. He calls *left* and *low* and *right* and is mostly right. A bean goes past his ear with his eyes shut and he doesn't say anything, and doesn't smile either this time. He just goes on listening.

None of it's a technique. None of it's a new thing he can do. It's a boy who was already very fast, now wasting a bit less of it. Half a second here. A flick of the eyes there. It isn't much.

*It's what I've got,* Kusakabe thinks. *It's all it ever was.*

At four the light starts going. At twenty past, he says, "That's it."

"Can I stay?"

"There's no one to hit you."

"I know. I'll do the line." The boy's already walking back to the start. "On my own. Thank you. Really. For all week."

Kusakabe doesn't answer that. He goes up the steps and sits down on the third one from the bottom. Gate in front. Wall behind. The step is cold right through his coat.

The boy stands at the start with his hands loose. Then he comes in. Up to the line. Stops. Back. Up to the line. Stops. Back. No sword in front of him now. Nobody. Just a curve in the dirt that's mostly been scuffed away, and his body finding the edge of it anyway, on its own, without being asked.

The circle tells him first. It's been out all afternoon without his asking, thin as breath. Something big comes into it from the side, from the storehouse, unhurried, the grass hushing under it. He doesn't turn his head.

The stone takes the weight without a sound. Panda doesn't go past him this time, down to the bottom one. He sits on the third step. Next to him. Not near. Next to.

The step is wide enough. It always was.

Neither of them says anything.

Kusakabe doesn't say *sorry*. He doesn't say *I was wrong*, or *you were right*, or *I've been teaching him all week, did you see*. Panda saw. That's why he's here. There's nothing to add to it that wouldn't make it smaller.

Panda doesn't say anything either. He just sits, paws flat on his knees, like someone in a waiting room who has decided not to read the magazines, and watches the boy.

The light goes from grey to a darker grey. The cherry trees lose their edges. Somewhere beyond the wall a bicycle bell goes twice, for nobody.

In the middle of the yard the boy keeps going. His breath comes out white. Up to the line. Back. Up to the line. Back.

He doesn't look down once.

---

## Chapter 24. December Twenty-Third

The order is read out at nine on Sunday morning, in the big room on the ground floor of the main building, by a man Kusakabe has never seen before.

The man has a sheet of paper and a voice that has been told to stay level. He keeps it level. There are about thirty people in the room, on folding chairs and along the walls. Nobody takes their coat off.

Gojo isn't there. Somebody says he's resting. Somebody else says that means eating.

The man reads it out the way you'd read the terms on the back of a ticket.

Tomorrow, at the agreed time, at the agreed place, Gojo Satoru meets the King of Curses. Alone. Everybody else waits at a distance that has been worked out by people with instruments. If Gojo wins, that's the end of it.

If he doesn't, the next one goes in. Then the next. In an order. The man reads the order. It isn't long.

Kusakabe's name is on it. Not near the top. Not near the bottom. Somewhere in the part of the list where you'd only get to it if things had gone very badly for everyone above.

*Middle of the table,* he thinks. *Story of my life.*

He looks at the backs of heads. Okkotsu is on the list, high up. The boy is on it. Other names he knows from corridors, and a few he only knows from the colony sheet. Most of them are younger than his coat.

The boy is three rows in front, sitting very straight, the way you'd sit in an exam. When his name is read out he nods, once, to nobody. As if somebody had asked him whether he was free on Monday.

Okabe is by the door with a clipboard, ticking people off as they came in. He hasn't got a cardigan on. Kusakabe notices that, and wishes he hadn't.

Nobody asks a question. The man folds his paper. People stand up, and the chairs scrape, and the room goes back to being a room.

He goes out to the station.

It's Sunday.

---

Ono opens the door before he's finished ringing.

"You're early," she says.

"Yeah."

She doesn't ask. She hands him the rabbit notebook and puts her coat on while he reads it. *Sun slept OK ate half asked what day told her Sunday she said good.* No full stops. There never are. Underneath, in the same small upright hand: *Brother came.* Already written. She's started putting it in before he arrives.

He doesn't say anything about that.

"Wednesday?" Ono says, at the door.

He looks at the notebook. At the line she's going to write on Wednesday, before he gets there.

"Wednesday," he says.

She nods and writes it on the back of her hand, the way she wrote *three* once, a year ago, about the tea. Then she goes, with a word to his sister on the way past and none to him, and the door closes quietly behind her.

---

His sister is at the window.

The blue chair. The car park. Other people's balconies. On the second floor across the way somebody has put up a string of Christmas lights round the rail, the kind that run through all their colours and start again. In daylight you can barely see them. They're going anyway.

"Is it you?" she says, without turning round.

"It's me."

"Atsuya." She says it as if she's checking it against something. Then she turns her head, and looks at him, and it fits. "Ono said it was you. At the door."

"It was."

"Ono says everything first." She doesn't sound as if she minds.

He takes his coat off. He puts the bag on the kitchen counter. Bread, the small tin of thickening powder, a bag of the soft rice crackers she can manage. And a box he bought at the chemist's by the station, without stopping to think about it, because it was by the till.

He opens it in the hall.

The diffuser on the shoe cabinet is about two-thirds gone. The sticks are dark at the tips. He's been changing it every two months for years, and this one has three weeks left in it, maybe four.

He changes it anyway. Takes the old one to the kitchen. Puts the new one where the old one was, and turns the sticks over so the dry ends go in, and stands back.

It's very strong for a minute. Lavender, cheap and loud, the whole hall full of it.

"That's new," she says, from the window.

"Yeah."

"It's not time."

"It was going off."

She sniffs, thinking about it. "It smells like a hotel."

"It'll calm down."

"Everything does," she says, and goes back to the balcony with the lights.

---

He makes the tea.

The bag in, and out again before it can do anything. Then the powder, stirred until the tea gives up and goes thick. It looks like nothing at all.

He holds the cup for her. She takes three sips and says it's too strong. He says sorry. It's the most reliable conversation he has.

Then he crushes the pills on the saucer with the back of the spoon and stirs them into a spoonful of yoghurt, and she eats it, and makes the face.

He does the rest of the jobs. There aren't many left that Ono hasn't done. He wipes down the arms of the chair. He finds the spare batteries in the drawer and changes the ones in the wall clock, which was losing a minute a day. Nobody had noticed. He did.

Then there's nothing to do, and he sits down in the other chair, and they look out of the window.

The lights on the balcony go red, then green, then a blue that's almost white, then red again.

"They leave those on all night," she says.

"Do they."

"All night. Every night. Since the first." She sounds offended on behalf of the electricity. "Who's that for? Nobody's looking at three in the morning."

*You are,* he thinks. He doesn't say it.

After a while she says, "My hair's a mess."

It isn't, much. Ono will have done it this morning. But he gets the brush from the bathroom, the one with the soft bristles, and stands behind the chair.

He's not good at it. He's never been good at it. Her hair is fine and grey at the front now, and longer than it used to be, and he goes slowly, from the ends upwards, the way the nurse showed him once and he's never forgotten.

"You pull," she says.

"Sorry."

"You always pulled."

He doesn't know if that's true. He doesn't ask. He goes slower.

She closes her eyes. Her head tips back a little against his hands. Outside, the light goes down another notch, and the lights on the balcony start to mean something.

He keeps brushing long after it's done.

---

At about five she says, "Are you going somewhere?"

He stops the brush.

"Work," he says. "Tomorrow."

"Tomorrow's Christmas Eve."

"Yeah."

She thinks about that. He watches her think about it in the window, her reflection over the car park, the lights going through her shoulder.

"Somewhere cold?"

"Probably."

"Wrap up, then."

"Okay."

That's all. She doesn't ask what the work is. She never has, not once, in all the years, even on the clear days. He used to think it was the illness. He thinks now it might just be her.

He puts the brush down on the windowsill.

He doesn't say anything else. There's nothing to say that she'd keep. Anything he told her tonight, she'd have to be told again on Wednesday, and again the Sunday after, new each time. That's no kind of thing to give someone.

So he says what he'd say on any Sunday.

He tells her the man downstairs has painted his door. He tells her the upstairs dog has gone quiet, and she says "Good," with real feeling. He tells her it's going to be cold this week.

And then, because the window's there, and the car park, and the dark coming down, he says, "When it's warm. The sea."

She looks at him.

"What sea?"

"You wanted to go. You said. I could push you along the front."

She thinks about it. He can see her looking for it, somewhere inside, and not finding it, and deciding it doesn't matter.

"There's always too much sand," she says.

"Yeah."

"All right, then. When it's warm."

He nods. He doesn't know if it counts, a promise made to someone who won't remember it. He makes it anyway. Somebody should be holding it.

---

The night carer comes at seven. He never usually stays that long.

He stands in the hall with his coat on while she takes her shoes off. The lavender has calmed down. It's just the hall again.

He goes back into the room.

His sister is looking at the lights. He puts his hand on her good hand, on the arm of the chair, for about as long as he'd put a cup down.

"Your hands are cold," she says.

"Always are."

"Get some gloves."

"Okay."

"Night, then."

"Night."

He goes. In the hall, he stops with his hand on the door, and breathes in once, and goes out.

---

The train back is half empty.

People with shopping bags. A man asleep with his mouth open and a wrapped present held upright on his knees, both hands round it, as if it might try to leave. A schoolgirl going through vocabulary cards, her lips moving. On the door, a printed notice about evacuation routes that someone has already started to peel off at one corner.

Six stops. He counts them, for once. He doesn't know why. He's done this journey every Sunday for years and never once counted.

At the fourth stop the man with the present wakes up, looks round in a panic, sees it's still in his hands, and goes back to sleep.

*Good,* Kusakabe thinks. *Hold on to it.*

---

On the walk from the station he goes into the convenience store and pays the bills.

It's Sunday. He pays them on Mondays, at seven, at the store near the school. He has for years. Tomorrow at seven he won't be near the school.

The girl at the till scans them one by one without looking at him. The care agency. The flat. Electricity. Gas. Her phone. His phone. The machine thinks about it, and prints a long white receipt.

He looks at it for a moment. Then he puts it in the bin by the door, the way he always does, and goes home.

---

The pen is on the kitchen counter where he left it on Friday, next to the calendar.

*In case,* he thinks. *Well. Here's the case.*

He gets the notebook out of the drawer. The old one, with the cat on the front, that Mrs. Fujita filled with everything in case. There are pages left at the back. He sits down at the kitchen table, which he doesn't use, and writes.

Not a letter. He wouldn't know how to start one.

An address. A ground floor flat, six stops from here. Ono Haruka's number. Midori Home Care, Mr. Hasegawa. Which bank. Which bills, on which day. *Tea: count to three. She'll say it's too strong. It isn't.* The diffuser, every two months. The window lock on the right, which needs pushing up before it'll turn.

He reads it back.

Nobody at the school knows about any of this. Nobody's known since a man in a pale suit stopped being anyone. If it comes to it, somebody from the school will let themselves into this flat with a box. They'll find this. And then somebody will know.

He leaves the notebook open on the table. He puts the pen on top of it, so it doesn't close.

---

He goes to bed at eleven, because that's what you do.

*Get some sleep before Sunday,* a man said to him once, in a wet street at dawn, tying his tie. He never did get the order right. It's Sunday night now, and the sleep isn't coming either.

At one he's still awake. At two he gives up and goes and lies on the sofa instead, in the dark, with his coat over him, because the coat is nearer than the blanket.

The flat is very quiet. The fridge. Somebody's heating ticking in the wall.

Someone across the street is up late too. Their window puts a pale square on his ceiling, a bit crooked, and the top corner of it just reaches the stain.

He looks at it.

Same shape. It's always the same shape. He's been meaning to report it for two years.

He's been saying two years for over a year now. It's three. It's nearly three. Mr. Sato has probably forgotten he owns this ceiling.

He laughs. Once, quietly, at nothing. It surprises him in the dark, the sound of it.

*Grade 1 sorcerer. Going to fight the end of the world tomorrow. Possibly. Still hasn't sent the email.*

---

Tomorrow.

He doesn't have to make himself think about it. It's right there.

Gojo, and the King of Curses. Somewhere in Shinjuku, at a time agreed between them, like two men who've booked a court. A year ago, in that same city, somebody told him Gojo was west of the station, and that had been enough. You didn't have to think about anything else. Gojo was west of the station.

He doesn't know who wins. Nobody does. That's what the list is for.

And if it isn't Gojo, then the next one goes in, and the next. Okkotsu. The boy. The others. One after another, into whatever's left. Until there's nobody above his name.

He thinks about the size of what he felt in Shibuya. A weight with no edge, that everything leaned towards. He thinks about his circle, which is the reach of one arm and one sword, standing in front of that.

*One arm,* he thinks. *Against that.*

He lies there.

---

He thinks about running.

He doesn't pretend he doesn't. There's nobody here to pretend for.

It's not even hard. That's what he notices. He's spent his whole life finding back doors, and this one is the easiest he's ever seen. It's standing open.

A bag. One bag. He doesn't own much. The bank card. Cash from the machine at the station, as much as it'll give. The first train in the morning, the other way from Shinjuku, west, then north.

His sister. He'd have to go and get her. The chair folds. He's done the transfer a thousand times, the practised hug, her arm round his neck. He'd need a taxi to the station. A carriage with the space for a wheelchair. He knows which end of the platform that is. He's looked, without knowing why he was looking.

Somewhere small. Somewhere with a mountain behind it, like the photograph for January. A little red shrine at the bottom. A ground floor flat. A new agency that doesn't know him. A new carer with a new notebook, who'll write *Brother came* in a different hand.

He works out the money. He's good at the money now. It doesn't last forever. It lasts long enough.

And nobody would come. That's the plain truth of it. Who'd come looking? A Grade 1 with no technique, with a reprimand on his file and *Froze?* in the margin. Somebody would put a line through his name on a sheet of printer paper, and somebody else would move up one.

*Who'd miss me,* he thinks. Not sorry for himself. Just asking. *Okabe. For the forms.*

He thinks about it for a long time.

He thinks about it properly, the way he did with the Hayama job, in columns. He thinks about which bag. He thinks about whether she'd mind the cold up there, and decides she'd mind the train more, and that she'd forget the train by Wednesday.

He thinks about what he'd say to Ono. Nothing, probably. He'd just not ring. He knows exactly what that costs. He's done it before.

The square on the ceiling goes out. Whoever was awake across the road has gone to bed.

He thinks about it in the dark for a while longer.

---

Then he thinks about Panda holding up the rock.

He doesn't decide to. It's just there. The way it always is now, when he gets near the bottom of something. Grey light pouring in. Dust coming down. A slab he'd made his peace with, lifted off him like a lid, and Panda underneath it with both arms up, fur matted, one ear torn.

*Sensei. You alive?*

He hadn't been, really. Not in any way that counted. Panda had come looking anyway. Had kept lifting things until one of them was him.

He thinks about Miwa, behind him, alive.

He didn't decide that either. He was just there, in front of her, and the spiral went round. He can still feel her hand in the back of his coat, holding on, in the ice.

He thinks about the boy.

Earnest. Idiotic. Fifteen. A boy who carried a monster round inside him for months, and had it walk about in his face, and had to stand in a yard afterwards and find out who'd wanted him dead for it. And still bows all the way down. And still says thank you, for a tap on the wrist with a practice sword. For a bean on the forehead.

Tomorrow he goes in. He's on the list. He'll go in when his name comes up, with his feet doing what they're told now, without looking.

Kusakabe taught him that. A week. Six afternoons.

It isn't much. It's what he had.

And the others. He goes through them without meaning to, the way you'd count heads on a stretcher run.

Maki, somewhere, with half her face still healing and her name on a sheet that says not to discuss it. She'll be there if she can stand. She'll be there if she can't. Inumaki, with one sleeve pinned, who used to say *salmon* to nobody and now mostly says nothing. Panda.

Panda, on the third step yesterday, at dusk. Not near. Next to. Not one word between them, the whole time, and the light going, and the boy in the yard going up to the line and back.

He'd thought, sitting there, that it might be the first of a lot of evenings like that.

He'd still like it to be.

---

And then Yaga.

He reaches for the coin, out of habit, lying there in the dark. It's nearly nothing now. He can barely find it.

He paid most of it in one night in November, and never said, and never will. The rest went in a yard in the hills, with nobody there to say it was paid. He thought that might be the end of it. You can't owe a dead man.

It isn't the end of it. There's still a little left. He can feel the shape of it.

It's the shape of a bear who calls the old man Masamichi. It's the shape of a school the old man kept standing for years, mostly by being too stubborn to let it fall. It's the shape of a list read out this morning, and the children on it, every one of them taught in rooms Yaga kept open.

That's the last of it. That's where it goes.

*Fine,* he thinks. *Fine, old man.*

---

*The kids are going to fight tomorrow,* he thinks. *With their lives. They're children.*

*And I'm the adult.*

That's all it is.

It isn't courage. He'd laugh at anyone who called it that. He's not brave. He's never been brave. He's a man who's been frightened his whole working life and turned up anyway, mostly late.

It's just a fact. It sits in his chest like a stone, and it doesn't move, and there's nothing to argue with. You can argue with a feeling. You can talk a feeling round, give it a few days, put it on the sofa. You can't talk round a stone. It's just there, the size it is.

He lies there with it for a while.

Then he gets up.

---

The sword is on the wall by the door, on its two hooks, where it lives when he's home.

He takes it down. He sits on the edge of the sofa with it across his knees, and turns the lamp on, and draws it a hand's width out of the scabbard.

He checks the edge. Thumb across it, never along. The cloth. The oil, the little bottle that never seems to get emptier. He wipes the blade down from the guard to the tip, slowly, and holds it to the lamp, and looks along it.

It doesn't need it. He did it on Friday. He'd know if it needed it.

It's not a good sword. It's never been a good sword. It's a plain one, bought with his own money when he made Grade 2, from a man in a shop in Kanda who didn't ask what it was for. It's been reground twice. It has a small nick near the guard from a car park in Kawasaki that he's never had taken out. It's the only one he's ever needed.

He runs the cloth over the nick. Still there. Some things you leave.

That isn't why. His hands need something to do. They've always been the sensible part of him. They'd rather be doing this than lying still.

He does it twice. Then he puts it back, and it goes home with the small sound it always makes, and he sits there holding it.

Four o'clock. Five. He doesn't lie down again. He doesn't need to. Whatever the sofa used to do for him, it isn't going to do it tonight, and he finds he doesn't hold that against it.

---

The window goes grey at about six.

Not light. Just less dark. A cold, flat grey, with the buildings across the road coming out of it slowly, as if somebody were drawing them in pencil.

He makes the coffee.

It's as bad as it always is. He drinks it standing at the counter, looking at the calendar. The bridge in the snow. The two small people with the umbrella. The blue circle round the twenty-fourth.

*Today, then.*

Somewhere below, a car starts and idles. A door. Somebody's alarm going, two floors down, for a job that's still on. Monday. For most of the city it's just a Monday, the last one before the holidays, with a bit of shopping still to do.

He envies them. He doesn't mind envying them. It's an old habit, and a harmless one.

He doesn't finish the coffee. He leaves the cup in the sink, with an inch in the bottom.

*I'll rinse it later,* he thinks.

He puts his coat on. His shoes. He checks his pockets, out of habit. Keys. His flat. Hers. The phone, charged, on loud, because he said he'd answer.

He stands in the middle of the room.

*Probably going to die,* he thinks. Almost conversationally, the way you'd mention the weather to a man at a bus stop. *Probably. Not definitely.*

*Shikata ga nai.*

It can't be helped. People say it about rain, and late trains, and the price of things. He's said it about a good deal less than this.

It's never fitted anything better.

He lets the circle out.

Not because there's anything in the room. There's nothing. A sofa, a cup in the sink, a notebook open on a table. He just wants to feel it.

It goes out round him the way it always has. Quietly. To the same edge it's had for years. The reach of one arm and one sword. He can feel the sofa just inside it, and the table just outside, and the cold coming off the window. Nothing in it but him.

It's small. It's plain. There's no name in it, no clan, nothing anybody handed him. Just years. The same small circle, drawn and drawn again, until it stopped being something he did and became something he was.

It's everything he has.

He lets it go.

The notebook is still open on the table, the pen lying across it. He looks at it. He leaves it where it is. If he comes back, he'll shut it himself, and nobody will ever have read it, and that'll be fine.

He picks up the sword.

He opens the door. The cold comes in off the landing, sharp and clean, and the grey is getting lighter at the end of the corridor.

He walks out the door.

---

## Chapter 25. Sunday

There's a cupboard in the hall he doesn't open.

The narrow one by the front door. The vacuum cleaner lives in it, and an umbrella with a broken spoke, and a box of things from a desk he cleared out in some other year. Behind all of that, upright in the corner, there's a long shape wrapped in an old cleaning cloth.

Two pieces. He knows which way up they are. He put them there himself, in January, and shut the door, and hasn't opened it since.

He could throw it out. Steel is steel. A broken sword is just a length of something that used to be useful, and the bins go out on Thursdays.

He walks past the cupboard every morning on the way to his shoes. His eyes don't go to it. His hand doesn't go to the handle. There's a new sword on the wall in the other room, and he's mostly used to the weight of it now. Mostly.

*Not ready to look at it,* he thinks. *Not ready to bin it either. Very decisive man.*

---

It's April, and the letter is still on the kitchen table, where it's been for three weeks.

Thick paper. Brush script. A red seal at the bottom, pressed so hard it's left a dent in the table. It came from the dojo he was never invited to, from men he has met twice in his life, both times standing up.

It says, in a great many more words than it needs, that he is head of the New Shadow Style.

It also says the old vows are lifted. The rules about the circle. Who could be taught it, and by whom, and what they had to promise before anyone showed them anything. Struck out, all of it, by the same kind of men who wrote them, in the same kind of ink. He can teach it to anyone he likes now. Nobody has to promise anything.

He read it standing up, the first time. Then he sat down and read it again, which he has never once done with a letter.

He looks at it now, over the top of a cup of instant coffee that tastes the way it always has.

*Promotion,* he thinks. *The one thing I spent my whole life avoiding.*

It doesn't come with more money. He asked. Okabe went through two binders and said it wasn't a grade, as such. "It's more of a position."

"Unpaid."

"Honorary," Okabe said. Which is the same word with its shirt tucked in.

On Tuesdays and Thursdays now there are students in the yard who are learning the circle. Not many. They draw it too wide and drop it the second anything frightens them. He tells them that's normal. It's the most encouraging thing he's said to anyone in years, and he only says it because it's true.

Last Thursday a second-year he's still learning the name of asked him what they used to have to promise.

"Doesn't matter now."

"But what was it?"

He thought about it. "Not to show it to anyone outside the style."

"That's it?"

"That's the short version." The long version took a whole afternoon and a great deal of kneeling. He doesn't miss it.

The boy looked at him as if he'd been cheated of something. Kusakabe knows the feeling. He just never thought he'd be on the other side of it.

He's late for all of them.

---

He gets dressed by the window, because the light's better there.

Shirt. He does the buttons from the bottom up. Halfway, his fingers go over the scars without meaning to, the way you'd run a thumb along a seam to find where it starts.
